Choose disjoint coordinate blocks \(I,J\subseteq[N]\), each of size \(t=O(s_1\log(n+s_1))\), large enough that some pattern \(p\in\{0,1\}^I\) and some pattern \(q\in\{0,1\}^J\) are each absent from every member of \(Y\). Such blocks fit because \(t=o(N)\).

Let \(E\subseteq U\) be the high-complexity truth tables that disagree with \(p\) somewhere on \(I\), and let \(H\subseteq U\) be those that disagree with \(q\) somewhere on \(J\). Every \(a\in Y\) disagrees with each forbidden pattern, so some generator slice \(B_{i,a_i}\cap U\) is contained in \(E\), and some slice \(B_{j,a_j}\cap U\) is contained in \(H\). Hence \(E,H\in\mathcal F_a^{\rm can}\).

For each input coordinate \(k\), the literal slice \(B_{k,a_k}\cap U\) is not contained in \(E\cap H\). If \(k\in I\), take a high-complexity table matching \(q\) on the disjoint block \(J\) and matching \(a_k\) at \(k\); it lies outside \(H\). If \(k\in J\), use a high-complexity completion of \(p\) on \(I\); if \(k\notin I\cup J\), use either block. Such a completion exists: fixing \(t+1=o(N)\) truth-table bits leaves \(2^{N-t-1}\) strings, whereas the total number of circuits of size below \(s_2=N^\beta\) is only \(2^{O(s_2\log(n+s_2))}=2^{o(N)}\). Thus no canonical generator slice is contained in \(E\cap H\), so \(E\cap H\notin\mathcal F_a^{\rm can}\). The pair \((E,H)\) violates preservation for every canonical filter at once. The intersection \(E\cap H\) is itself nonempty: choose patterns on both blocks differing from \(p,q\); the \(2^{N-2t}\) completions include a high-complexity table by the same count.

This strengthens the earlier constant-mass obstruction for canonical-only distributions to a one-pair cover of the canonical family, including at the bit-literal level. The argument does not cover arbitrary semi-filters: a noncanonical semi-filter may contain the nonempty set \(E\cap H\), and then it preserves this pair. It therefore does not upper-bound the full promise fusion-cover complexity.

### Proposition 12.4 ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã¢â‚¬Â high-entropy majority-measure filters are covered by one pair

This tests a natural noncanonical alternative: define each semi-filter by majority under a probability distribution supported on high-complexity truth tables near its low-complexity anchor.

Let \(N=2^n\), fix \(0<\beta<1\), and use \(s_2=N^\beta\) and \(s_1=s_2/(cn)\) for fixed \(c>0\). Put \(U=\{z:\operatorname{CC}_n(z)\ge s_2\}\) and \(Y=\{a:\operatorname{CC}_n(a)\le s_1\}\). For a sufficiently large constant \(K=K(\beta)\), let \(k=\lceil K s_2\rceil\). For each \(a\in Y\), let \(\mu_a\) be uniform on the high-complexity truth tables at Hamming distance exactly \(k\) from \(a\), and define
\[
\mathcal F_a^\mu=\{W\subseteq U:\mu_a(W)>1/2\}.
\]
For all sufficiently large \(n\), these are semi-filters above their respective anchors, yet there is one pair \(E,H\subseteq U\) such that, simultaneously for every \(a\in Y\),
\[
\mu_a(E)>1/2,\qquad \mu_a(H)>1/2,\qquad \mu_a(E\cap H)<1/2.
\]
Consequently the cover number of this entire majority-measure family is at most one.

#### Proof

The number of truth tables computed by circuits of size below \(s\) is at most \(2^{C s\log(n+s)}\) for a model-dependent constant \(C\). Write \(M=\binom Nk\). Since \(k=\Theta(s_2)=o(N)\),
\[
\log_2 M\ge k\log_2(N/k)=K(1-\beta+o(1))s_2 n,
\]
whereas the number of tables of circuit size below \(s_2\) is at most \(2^{C(\beta+o(1))s_2 n}\). Choose \(K\) large enough that the latter count is at most \(M2^{-\eta s_2 n}\) for some constant \(\eta>0\). Thus, in every radius-\(k\) sphere around \(a\), a \(1-2^{-\eta s_2 n}\) fraction of tables lie in \(U\).

Before conditioning on membership in \(U\), a uniformly random point of that sphere differs from \(a\) at any fixed coordinate with probability \(k/N=o(1)\). Conditioning away a fraction at most \(2^{-\eta s_2 n}\) raises this probability to at most \((k/N)/(1-2^{-\eta s_2 n})<1/2\). Therefore every slice \(\{z\in U:z_i=a_i\}\) has \(\mu_a\)-measure greater than \(1/2\), so \(\mathcal F_a^\mu\) is above \(a\). It is upward closed, contains \(U\), and excludes the empty set, as required.

Now color every point of \(U\), independently and uniformly, with one of three labels \(A,B,C\). Set \(E=A\cup B\) and \(H=B\cup C\). For each fixed \(a\), the random measures of \(E,H,B\) under \(\mu_a\) have expectations \(2/3,2/3,1/3\). The support of \(\mu_a\) has size at least \(m=M(1-2^{-\eta s_2 n})=2^{\Omega(s_2 n)}\), and \(\mu_a\) is uniform there. Hoeffding's inequality bounds the probability that any one of these three measures deviates by at least \(1/6\) by at most \(2e^{-m/18}\). Also \(|Y|\le 2^{O(s_1\log(n+s_1))}=2^{O(s_2)}\). A union bound over all anchors and the three measures has failure probability at most \(6|Y|e^{-m/18}<1\) for large \(n\). A coloring therefore exists with \(\mu_a(E),\mu_a(H)>1/2\) and \(\mu_a(B)<1/2\) for every \(a\); since \(E\cap H=B\), the claimed pair follows. ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬â€œÃ‚Â¡

More generally, the concentration argument shows that any family of majority-measure filters indexed by \(Y\) is covered by one pair when the minimum collision-entropy effective support \(1/\sum_{u\in U}\mu_a(u)^2\) grows faster than \(\log |Y|\). The sphere construction has far more support than this threshold. This rules out that high-entropy filter strategy; it does not rule out arbitrary semi-filters or low-entropy, highly correlated measures, and it does not bound the full promise cover number.

### Proposition 12.5 ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã¢â‚¬Â the same high sphere contains constant-support majority witnesses

The entropy obstruction has a sharp practical limitation: the measures in Proposition 12.4 can be sparsified while preserving every required bit majority.

For each \(a\in Y\), draw an odd number \(t=t(\beta)\) of independent samples from \(\mu_a\), where \(t\) is a sufficiently large constant depending only on fixed \(\beta<1\). Each sample differs from \(a\) at any fixed coordinate with probability at most \(q_n=(k/N)/(1-2^{-\eta s_2n})=O(N^{\beta-1})\). For a fixed coordinate, the probability that at least half of the samples disagree with \(a\) is at most \(2^tq_n^{(t+1)/2}\). A union bound over all \(N\) coordinates tends to zero once
\[
1-(1-\beta)(t+1)/2<0.
\]
Thus, for each low table \(a\), there exist \(t=O(1/(1-\beta))\) high-complexity tables \(z_1,\ldots,z_t\), each at distance \(k=O(s_2)\) from \(a\), whose coordinatewise majority is exactly \(a\). The uniform measure on these \(t\) tables defines a majority semi-filter above \(a\) with effective support at most \(t\).

This sparse family is not defeated by the concentration proof in Proposition 12.4, whose exponent depends on effective support. It can also preserve the specific pair constructed in Proposition 12.3: for each anchor, select one coordinate in each forbidden-pattern block where the anchor differs from that pattern, and choose the samples so that more than half match the anchor at both selected coordinates. This extra event holds with probability tending to one because each sample flips either selected coordinate with probability at most \(2q_n\). Those samples lie in the intersection of the two sets in that pair, so their majority filter preserves it.

This shows that the high-entropy construction can be sparsified, and that its specific pair obstruction can be avoided. The next proposition shows why constant support still cannot yield the desired superlinear cover lower bound.

### Proposition 12.6 ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã¢â‚¬Â all bounded-support majority measures have a short cover

Let \(U\) be any finite set with \(|U|\le 2^N\), and let \(t\ge3\). Consider every majority semi-filter \(\mathcal F_\mu=\{W\subseteq U:\mu(W)>1/2\}\) whose probability measure has support of size at most \(t\) and every atom has weight below \(1/2\). There is a single list of at most
\[
O(3^t t\log(2|U|))=O(3^t tN)
\]
pairs that covers all of them.

#### Proof

First fix one such measure and write its support weights as \(w_1,\ldots,w_m\). Let \(w_1=\max_i w_i<1/2\), and put the corresponding support point in part \(A\). The remaining mass is \(1-w_1>1/2\). Since every remaining atom is at most \(w_1\), add remaining support points to part \(B\) until its mass first exceeds \(1/2-w_1\). If it hits that value exactly, add one more unused atom. This gives
\[
1/2-w_1<\mu(B)\le1/2.
\]
Let \(C\) contain the remaining support points. Then \(\mu(A)<1/2\), \(\mu(C)<1/2\), and \(\mu(B)\le1/2\). Thus the pair \(E=A\cup B\), \(H=B\cup C\) satisfies \(\mu(E)>1/2\), \(\mu(H)>1/2\), and \(\mu(E\cap H)=\mu(B)\le1/2\).

It remains to make one list work for every support and every partition of that support into three labels. A random coloring \(\chi:U\to\{A,B,C\}\) matches any fixed labeling of a support of size at most \(t\) with probability at least \(3^{-t}\). There are at most \((t+1)(3|U|)^t\) support-labeling patterns. Taking \(O(3^t t\log(2|U|))\) independent random colorings and applying a union bound gives a list that realizes every pattern. For each coloring, use the pair \(E=\chi^{-1}(A\cup B)\), \(H=\chi^{-1}(B\cup C)\). The partition above ensures one pair in the list violates preservation for each measure. ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬â€œÃ‚Â¡

For the constant \(t=t(\beta)\) in Proposition 12.5, the bound is \(O(N)\), so that sparse majority-measure construction cannot give the required \(N^{1+\varepsilon}\) cover lower bound. More generally, support sizes up to \(t\le \alpha\log_2 N\) are covered in \(N^{1+\alpha\log_2 3+o(1)}\) pairs. In any measure-based route to an \(N^{1+\varepsilon}\) lower bound, one must therefore use supports above this range or rely on features not captured by a single majority measure.

### Proposition 12.7 ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã¢â‚¬Â one heavy atom with a diffuse tail is also covered by one pair

Another apparent escape is to put mass just below one half on a single high-complexity table, with the rest spread over the large Hamming sphere from Proposition 12.4. That construction is still covered by one pair.

For each \(a\in Y\), choose \(z_a\) from the support of \(\mu_a\) in Proposition 12.4, and set
\[
\widehat\mu_a=0.49\,\delta_{z_a}+0.51\,\mu_a.
\]
For sufficiently large \(n\), the majority slices still contain \(a\): at a coordinate where \(z_a\) disagrees with \(a\), the diffuse part alone gives mass at least \(0.51(1-q_n)>1/2\), where \(q_n=o(1)\) is the coordinate flip probability from Proposition 12.5. At coordinates where they agree, the atom adds further mass.

Let \(S=\{z_a:a\in Y\}\). For each \(a\), the diffuse measure \(\mu_a\) assigns \(S\) mass at most \(|Y|/m=o(1)\), where \(m=2^{\Omega(s_2n)}\) is its support size and \(|Y|=2^{O(s_2)}\). Put \(S\) in one part \(B\), and independently split \(U\setminus S\) into parts \(A,C\) with probability \(1/2\) each. Hoeffding's inequality and a union bound over \(Y\) give one split for which every diffuse measure assigns at least \(0.1\) mass to each of \(A,C\). Then define \(E=A\cup B\) and \(H=B\cup C\). For each \(a\),
\[
\widehat\mu_a(E),\widehat\mu_a(H)\ge 0.49+0.51(0.1)>1/2,
\qquad
\widehat\mu_a(E\cap H)=\widehat\mu_a(B)\le0.49+0.51\,o(1)<1/2.
\]
Thus one pair covers the whole heavy-atom family. This excludes the one-heavy-atom repair of the high-entropy construction; distributions with many interacting heavy atoms remain unaddressed.

### Proposition 12.8 ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã¢â‚¬Â existential majority-support filters have a disjoint one-pair cover

Another noncanonical variant lets each set choose its own majority measure:
\[
\mathcal G_a=\{W\subseteq U:\text{there exists a probability measure }\nu\text{ supported on }W\text{ with }\nu(z_i=a_i)>1/2\text{ for every }i\}.
\]
These are semi-filters above a. They are upward closed, exclude the empty set, and contain U. For each literal slice `B_{i,a_i} intersect U`, take the high-sphere measure from Proposition 12.4 conditioned on matching the anchor bit at coordinate i. Since the unconditioned coordinate flip probability q_n tends to zero, all remaining coordinate marginals still favor the corresponding bits of a with probability tending to one, and eventually exceed 1/2.

There is a single partition of U into disjoint sets E and H for which both E and H belong to G_a for every a in Y. Choose a random independent fair two-coloring of U. For the uniform high-sphere measure \(\mu_a\) from Proposition 12.4 and coordinate i, let \(D_{a,i}=\{z\in U:z_i\ne a_i\}\) be the tables whose i-th bit differs from a. For either color class Q,
\[
\mathbb E[\mu_a(Q)]=1/2,\qquad
\mathbb E[\mu_a(Q\cap D_{a,i})]=q_n/2.
\]
The difference \(\mu_a(Q)/2-\mu_a(Q\cap D_{a,i})\) therefore has expectation \(1/4-q_n/2\), bounded away from zero. It is a sum of independent bounded contributions over a support of size \(m=2^{\Omega(s_2n)}\), so Hoeffding's inequality makes the probability that this difference is nonpositive at most \(e^{-\Omega(m)}\). A union bound over both colors, all \(N\) coordinates, and all \(|Y|=2^{O(s_2)}\) anchors still has failure probability less than one. For such a partition, conditioning \(\mu_a\) on either color class gives a distribution supported there with every coordinate majority equal to a. Hence both disjoint sets E and H belong to every \(\mathcal G_a\), while \(E\cap H=\varnothing\notin\mathcal G_a\). One pair covers the whole family.

This rules out defining the filter by the existence of any supporting majority distribution. The proof uses the same large high-complexity Hamming spheres as Proposition 12.4 and does not apply to arbitrary semi-filters.

### Proposition 12.9 ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã¢â‚¬Â a collision-entropy criterion for a disjoint cover

The random-bipartition argument has a useful quantitative form that does not require uniform measures. Fix, for each anchor \(a\in Y\), a probability measure \(\mu_a\) on U. Write
\[
q_{a,i}=\mu_a(D_{a,i}),\qquad d_{a,i}=1-2q_{a,i}>0,
\qquad C_a=\sum_{z\in U}\mu_a(z)^2.
\]
Thus \(d_{a,i}\) is the signed majority margin at coordinate i, and \(1/C_a\) is the collision-effective support size. If
\[
\min_{a\in Y,\ i\in[N]}\frac{d_{a,i}^2}{2C_a}>\ln(2|Y|N),
\]
then there is a partition \(U=E\sqcup H\) such that conditioning each \(\mu_a\) on either E or H preserves every coordinate majority. In particular E and H both belong to every existential majority-support filter \(\mathcal G_a\), while their intersection is empty.

To prove this, color each z independently and fairly into Q or its complement. For fixed a and i, put \(g_{a,i}(z)=1\) when \(z_i=a_i\) and \(-1\) otherwise. The signed mass in Q is
\[
X_{Q,a,i}=\sum_z \mu_a(z)g_{a,i}(z)\mathbf 1[z\in Q],
\qquad \mathbb E X_{Q,a,i}=d_{a,i}/2.
\]
Hoeffding's inequality, using squared range lengths \(\sum_z\mu_a(z)^2=C_a\), gives
\[
\Pr[X_{Q,a,i}\le0]\le \exp\!\left(-\frac{d_{a,i}^2}{2C_a}\right).
\]
The same estimate holds for the complement. A union bound over both colors and all \(|Y|N\) anchor-coordinate pairs is less than one by the displayed hypothesis. On the resulting coloring, positive signed mass means that the conditional measure on each color class has the required strict majority.

This criterion makes the obstacle explicit: a random disjoint cover works whenever every chosen witness measure has enough collision-effective support relative to its smallest majority margin. It says nothing for measures whose margins are tiny or whose collision mass is large; those remain the difficult regime for measure-based constructions.

### Proposition 12.10 - a vector-discrepancy criterion for a disjoint cover

There is also a criterion sensitive to how the anchor measures overlap point by point. For each \(z\in U\), form a vector \(v_z\in\mathbb R^{|Y|N}\) whose coordinate indexed by \((a,i)\) is
\[
(v_z)_{a,i}=\mu_a(z)g_{a,i}(z),
\]
where \(g_{a,i}(z)=1\) if \(z_i=a_i\) and \(-1\) otherwise. Let \(R=\max_{z\in U}\|v_z\|_2\), and let \(d_* = \min_{a,i} d_{a,i}\). If \(d_*>36R\), then there is a partition \(U=E\sqcup H\) such that conditioning each \(\mu_a\) on either part preserves all its strict coordinate majorities.

Indeed, the Komlos vector-balancing theorem gives signs \(\varepsilon_z\in\{-1,1\}\) with \(\|\sum_z\varepsilon_zv_z\|_\infty\le36R\). Put E equal to the positively signed points and H to the negatively signed points. For every coordinate \((a,i)\), the signed masses on these two parts are respectively \((d_{a,i}+\sum_z\varepsilon_z(v_z)_{a,i})/2\) and \((d_{a,i}-\sum_z\varepsilon_z(v_z)_{a,i})/2\), both strictly positive. Thus both conditional measures have the desired majorities.

For the uniform high-sphere witnesses, \(R\le\sqrt{N|Y|}/m\), because each atom has mass \(1/m\) in at most \(|Y|\) anchor measures. Since \(m=2^{\Omega(s_2n)}\), this criterion also proves the one-pair cover. Unlike Proposition 12.9, it can apply when each measure's collision mass is not small enough for a union-bound argument, provided no single table carries much aggregate squared mass across all anchors. The cited Komlos result is an existence theorem, not a polynomial-time construction of the partition.

### Consequence for the research plan

1. Remove the unrestricted row/column graph-cover target from the active proof plan: its cover number is at most one for the stated promise gap.
2. Keep the Boolean promise-cover target only if a construction directly uses the \(2n\) bit-literal generators and does not silently treat arbitrary sets of half truth tables as free unions.
3. Re-test every proposed filter distribution against the cofactor support rectangle. In particular, any witness whose behavior depends only on unrestricted row/column supports is defeated by this single cover pair.
4. Canonical bit-literal filters, high-entropy majority-measure filters in Proposition 12.4, the one-heavy-atom mixture in Proposition 12.7, and the existential majority-support filters in Proposition 12.8 are covered by one pair; Propositions 12.9 and 12.10 quantify two broader randomization and discrepancy regimes. Filters with support at most t have an O(3^t tN)-pair cover. Propositions 18.7-18.8 also show that selected majority witnesses with low support-overlap chromatic number have short covers, and the sampled high-sphere one-witness-per-anchor family can be made pairwise disjoint. A measure-based lower-bound route must therefore address families with high support-overlap chromatic number, tiny margins, large aggregate atomic mass including many interacting moderate or heavy atoms, multiple measures per anchor, or use non-measure semi-filters.
5. These are proved negative results about specific strategies. They are not a \(P\ne NP\) proof or a positive complexity lower bound.

---

## 13. Check of a recent public P = NP claim

The current Clay Mathematics Institute page continues to list P vs NP among the unsolved Millennium problems ([CMI problem list](https://www.claymath.org/millennium-problems/)). A public GitHub archive dated July 2026 claims a deterministic polynomial-time SAT solver, PolaritySAT, based on a ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã…â€œglobally unified variableÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã‚Â lemma ([claim and proof sketch](https://github.com/yinhao2026/P-NP--Complete-Archive)). I checked the core lemma rather than treating the claim as accepted literature.

The archive defines a variable \(v\) as globally unified if there is a unique polarity \(p\) such that, for every larger variable \(u\), all unsatisfied clauses containing both \(u,v\) contain \(v\) only with polarity \(p\). Its proof then treats the largest variable as globally unified because the condition over larger variables is vacuous. But when there are no such clauses, the condition cannot determine a unique polarity. If the definition is instead read as vacuous for either polarity, it does not imply that assigning the largest variable according to a chosen polarity preserves satisfiability.

The two-variable formula
\[
F(x,y)=(x\lor y)\land(\neg x\lor\neg y)
\]
is a direct check. It is satisfiable by both \((x,y)=(1,0)\) and \((0,1)\); neither variable is forced. With order \(x>y\), the largest variable \(x\) occurs in both polarities and has no larger variable to constrain it. Thus the archive's ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã…â€œvacuously unifiedÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã‚Â step does not establish the forced-assignment lemma. This refutes that proof step; it is not a result about the truth of P vs NP, and the repository's claim is not evidence of a breakthrough.

### A second check: the Lean-certified pedigree-polytope claim

An arXiv preprint submitted on 2 June 2026 claims a Lean-verified proof of `P=NP` through a polynomial-time membership algorithm for the pedigree polytope ([arXiv:2606.03194](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03194); [public Lean repository](https://github.com/TiruArt/Pedigree-Polytopes-Lean4)). Inspection of the actual Lean source reveals that the advertised proof chain is not self-contained and does not establish `P=NP`.

The decisive membership step is incomplete in Lean. The source marks the necessity direction, `X in conv(P_n) =>` the multicommodity-flow optimum equals `z_max`, as an axiom in [`N_MembershipCharacterisation.lean`](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/TiruArt/Pedigree-Polytopes-Lean4/main/MembershipProject/Core/N_MembershipCharacterisation.lean); the [README](https://github.com/TiruArt/Pedigree-Polytopes-Lean4/blob/main/README.md) says the attempted formal proof remains in a backup file with 16 `sorry` placeholders. Sufficiency alone proves that a maximum-flow witness certifies membership, but does not show that every member receives such a witness, so it is not enough to make the flow test a correct membership decision procedure.

The main [`N_PEqualsNP.lean`](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/TiruArt/Pedigree-Polytopes-Lean4/main/MembershipProject/Core/N_PEqualsNP.lean) route avoids that axiom by declaring `QuickProtocol` as an uninterpreted proposition and postulating `tardos_strongly_polynomial : QuickProtocol (LayeredPoint n)`. It then also assumes the transfer from that predicate to the projected polytope, and assumes the link from polynomial optimisation to STSP. The file defines `An n := Unit` as a placeholder, while the algorithmic predicates are not given operational definitions. The source also contains project-specific axioms beyond the six-item external-theorem inventory, including `membership_An_of_Pn` and `mi_objective_solves_stsp_ax`. Consequently Lean verifies a derivation from these assumptions, not the claimed end-to-end SAT algorithm. The preprint describes other external axioms as standard published theorems, but these project-specific assumptions include the disputed membership algorithm itself.

This is a concrete audit failure of the claimed Lean certificate, not a proof that `P!=NP`. The cited textbook argument for the missing necessity direction would need an independent mathematical audit before the underlying pedigree-polytope idea could be ruled out; the available formal project does not supply that verification.

The report's own P vs NP status remains unchanged: no proof has been found in this project.

---

## 14. Relevant 2026 literature check

The latest primary-source checks strengthen nearby circuit-lower-bound and meta-complexity results, but none crosses the required boundary to an unconditional NP circuit lower bound.

* Ren and Williams report a near-maximum `2^n/n` circuit lower bound for `E^prMA/1`, using Range Avoidance, PCPs, and an iterative win-win argument ([ECCC TR26-118](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/118/)). The target class has exponential time, promise-MA queries, and one advice bit; this does not imply an NP lower bound.
* A September ECCC report gives almost-everywhere near-cubic wire lower bounds for one language in `E^NP`, against depth-two SYM composed with threshold and threshold composed with threshold circuits ([ECCC TR26-167](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/167/)). Its result is for a restricted circuit model and wire complexity. The report also records an external comment raising concerns about presentation and relation to earlier work; I do not use it as a proved bridge to P vs NP.
* Lin, ECCC TR26-139 revision 2 (4 September), proves unconditional fine-grained AC0 size lower bounds for k-OV, k-XOR, and k-SUM by depth-zero projections from colored subgraph isomorphism. At fixed depth the bounds scale as n^{Omega(k)} for the stated parameter ranges, and the paper gives growing-k results for selected gate orientations. The targets and lower bounds remain in bounded-depth AC0; the report provides no transfer to general Boolean circuits or Gap-MCSP.
* Goldberg, Juvekar, and Kabanets give assumption-based NP-hardness results for ImpMCSP and PAC learning, while deriving subexponential circuit lower bounds from a combination of cryptographic and proof-complexity assumptions ([ECCC TR26-091](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/091/)). These are conditional results about different reductions and targets.
* Mazor and Pass show that, under obfuscation and one-way-function assumptions, suitable Gap-MCSP and Gap-MKtP variants are not NP-complete under witness-preserving Levin reductions ([ECCC TR24-053](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2024/053/)). This constrains one reduction style rather than ruling out the hardness-magnification route in Section 7.
* Karingula and Lovett give an ECCC proof of the Komlos vector-balancing bound: vectors of Euclidean norm at most one admit a signing with maximum-coordinate discrepancy at most 36 ([ECCC TR26-188](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/188/)). Proposition 12.10 applies it to the simultaneous majority-cover problem. Their report explicitly does not provide a polynomial-time signing algorithm, so this is an existence tool only.
* Golovnev and Gurumukhani introduce local decision trees and show that strong lower bounds for the non-oblivious model would imply major circuit lower bounds; their proved near-maximal results are for the weaker oblivious model ([ECCC TR26-195](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/195/)). A CNF's assignment-to-clause-satisfaction map is 3-local, but the paper does not lower-bound range membership for arbitrary such maps, which is the SAT problem itself.
* Li, Ren, and Zhong show that demi-bits generators contain exponentially many proof-complexity generators, with further consequences for restricted Range Avoidance settings ([ECCC TR26-196](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/196/)). This sharpens the generator side of the project's fiber/avoidance connection, but its applications require a hard generator or additional assumptions and do not yield an unconditional NP circuit lower bound.
* Kayal et al. give new operational characterizations of Boolean certificate complexity and quantum query measures ([ECCC TR26-206](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/206/)). This enriches local query-complexity tools, but does not bridge query lower bounds to worst-case SAT decision complexity.

The usable idea for this project is the Range-Avoidance-to-circuit-lower-bound connection, already included in the fiber/avoidance plan. The new results show that it currently reaches strong lower bounds for exponential-time oracle classes, not the explicit NP-versus-polynomial-circuit separation needed here.

---

## 15. Follow-up audit of the pedigree-polytope complexity bound

The 2025 membership paper's polynomial running-time estimate depends on a polynomial bound for the number of rigid pedigrees R_(k-1). The cited argument has a specific gap as written. Theorem 12 claims these pedigrees are pairwise adjacent. Theorem 13 then says that mutual adjacency makes them a simplex, and bounds their number by the dimension. But pairwise adjacency alone does not imply that a vertex set is a simplex, even among 0-1 polytopes. Maksimenko gives a 7-dimensional 2-neighborly 0-1-polytope with 14 vertices; all 14 vertices are pairwise adjacent, although a 7-simplex has only 8 vertices ([paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/1904.03638)). On a 2-neighborly polytope, the paper's stated "combinatorial polytope" property is vacuously true because there are no nonadjacent pairs to check. Thus that property alone does not justify the clique-to-simplex inference. This example is not a counterexample to the specific claimed R set for a pedigree polytope; it shows that an additional pedigree-specific argument is required. See Theorems 12-13 of [the 2025 preprint](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2507.09069) and the definition in Section 8.4 of [Arthanari's survey](https://riviste.unime.it/index.php/AAPP/article/download/AAPP.91S2A3/AAPP91S2A3).

There is a second issue in the written dimension step: Lambda_k(X), the set of all convex-decomposition weights representing one point, can have dimension much larger than the polytope. For a polytope with m vertices and affine dimension d, a full-support representation of a point in the relative interior has a local fiber of dimension m-d-1. Pedigree polytopes have (k-1)!/2 vertices but dimension only O(k^3). Thus the claimed general inequality dim(Lambda_k(X)) <= dim(conv(P_k)) is not valid without an additional hypothesis.

This is a gap in the stated proof, not yet a disproof of the cardinality bound. A possible repair is to prove that every pedigree in R_(k-1) has a positive weight fixed across all convex representations of X/k. That would imply affine independence: any affine dependence among those pedigrees would allow a sufficiently small perturbation of their positive weights while preserving the represented point, contradicting rigidity. The remaining proof obligation is to establish that every such convex representation induces a feasible solution of the exact flow system in which the rigid weights were defined. The current audit has not verified this implication, so the polynomial bound remains unproved here rather than disproved.

The public Lean formalization makes the dependency explicit. In [N_RigidCardinality.lean](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/TiruArt/Pedigree-Polytopes-Lean4/main/MembershipProject/Core/N_RigidCardinality.lean), cardinalitytheorem receives the hypothesis net.rigid.length - 1 <= tau n - (k - 3) and proves the cardinality conclusion with omega. It does not derive that dimension bound from mutual adjacency. Its corollary receives the same hypothesis. Since the paper uses the rigid-set bound to argue that each layered network has polynomial size, the Lean file verifies only the arithmetic consequence of the missing geometric bound, not that bound itself.

This is a substantial unresolved dependency in the advertised polynomial-time membership chain. It does not by itself refute the paper's separate mathematical arguments or establish P != NP. The positive result of this audit is narrower: we have isolated a precise theorem-level obligation that any proof of the running-time bound must discharge, and a candidate route through rigidity-induced affine independence that can be tested against the network construction.

### Proposition 15.1 - a flow-support bound that may replace the disputed simplex step

The polynomial bound on rigid pedigrees can be approached directly from the bounded transportation problem F_k. This argument uses the stated capacities in the 2025 paper and standard extreme-point geometry of network flows; it does not rely on mutual adjacency or on the dimension of Lambda_k(X).

Abstract form of the lemma: in a finite nonnegative transportation polytope with origin supplies and destination demands, let S be a set of origins such that every allowed arc leaving origin i in S has upper capacity equal to the full supply of i. At any extreme feasible flow, at most |S| + 2|D| arcs leaving S carry positive flow, where D is the destination set. The proof below gives this count. It is recorded as an elementary reusable lemma; no claim of literature novelty is made.

Let r_(k-1) be the number of rigid pedigrees supplied as origins to F_k. Split the origins into the residual edge origins U_k and the pedigree origins R_(k-1). Let D_k be the destination set. The paper gives each P in R_(k-1) supply mu_P and gives every arc leaving P capacity mu_P; residual-edge origins have at most one arc per destination, with link capacity C(L). See the construction of F_k and its capacities in [Sections 5.3-5.4 of the 2025 preprint](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2507.09069).

Choose a basic feasible flow f of F_k. In the subgraph consisting of arcs strictly between their lower and upper bounds, the underlying undirected graph is a forest: a cycle would permit a small alternating perturbation in both directions while preserving every origin and destination balance, contradicting extremality.

Consider an origin P in R_(k-1). Any positive outgoing arc at its upper bound carries mu_P, which is its entire supply, so at most one such arc can leave P. If P has any positive outgoing arcs strictly between bounds, it cannot also have an upper-bound positive arc. It must have at least two such interior arcs, since one sole interior arc would have to carry the full supply mu_P and would therefore be at its upper bound. The graph formed by these interior arcs from pedigree origins to destinations is a forest. In each component, if p is the number of pedigree origins and d is the number of destinations, every pedigree origin has degree at least two, while a forest has at most p+d-1 edges. Hence p <= d-1 and the component has at most 2d-2 such edges. Summing over components, at most 2|D_k| interior arcs leave pedigree origins. Therefore the number of positive rigid arcs from pedigree origins is at most |R_(k-1)| + 2|D_k|.

Each such rigid arc contributes at most one extended rigid pedigree to R_k. A rigid arc from a residual edge origin contributes at most one pedigree, and the number of possible residual-origin/destination arcs is at most |U_k||D_k|. Thus
r_k <= r_(k-1) + 2|D_k| + |U_k||D_k|.

Here |U_k| <= binomial(k-1,2) and |D_k| <= binomial(k,2), so the additive term is O(k^4). The initial network F_4 has at most three origins and six destinations, so r_4 <= 18. Iterating gives r_k = O(k^5). Summing these bounds over the layers keeps the layered-network size polynomial; the number of links at each stage is also polynomial. This supplies the kind of bound needed for the paper's later flow and linear-program dimensions, subject to the exact construction details below.

The key construction check is that R_k receives no more than one pedigree per positive rigid arc. The paper states this for residual origins by retaining P_unique(L) only when the maximum-flow path for link L is unique, and for a pedigree origin the extension by the destination edge is unique. It also merges duplicate pedigrees. These are precisely the rules used in the count above.

This argument repairs the running-time size bound if all of those stated construction rules are applied as written. It does not prove the separate membership characterization or the correctness of the underlying P=NP claim. Those still need a complete audit, especially the conversion from an arbitrary convex decomposition to the restricted-network flow representation and the necessity direction for the multicommodity-flow test.

The sufficiency proof normalizes commodity flows by v_s, so zero-flow commodities must be omitted before division. The 2025 preprint explicitly makes this restriction immediately before defining the normalized vectors (Section 7.2, its line 1637). I therefore withdraw the earlier concern that this repair was absent from the paper. A formalization should preserve the positive-flow restriction at the point where normalization is introduced; this does not affect the flow-support count above.

## 16. Follow-up on the pedigree size bound and an extension-complexity objection

This follow-up checks two separate issues raised while auditing the 2025 membership paper. The first strengthens the possible polynomial-size repair in Section 15. The second rejects an overly quick claim that the paper's input-dependent flow LP contradicts known extension-complexity lower bounds.

### 16.1 The flow-support recurrence gives a polynomial network bound

Write r_k = |R_k|. At stage k, the residual edge origins U_k number at most \(\binom{k-1}{2}\), and the destinations D_k number at most \(\binom{k}{2}\). The flow-support argument of Proposition 15.1 gives

\[
r_k \le r_{k-1} + 2|D_k| + |U_k||D_k|,
\qquad r_4 \le 18.
\]

The additive term is \(O(k^4)\), so summing the recurrence gives \(r_k=O(k^5)\). This count uses only the prescribed origins, capacities, and rule that each positive rigid arc contributes at most one pedigree. It avoids Theorems 12ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã¢â‚¬Å“13's clique-to-simplex and weight-fiber dimension steps.

The resulting network-size estimate is polynomial. Across all layers up to n, the pedigree nodes total \(O(n^6)\). At layer k, there are at most \((|U_k|+r_{k-1})|D_k|=O(k^7)\) transportation arcs, so the accumulated network has \(O(n^8)\) arcs. There are at most \(O(n^4)\) restricted-network max-flow calls per layer, each on a polynomial-size network. In the multicommodity LP, the paper assigns at most one commodity per network arc; even the conservative bound of one flow variable per commodity per network arc gives \(O(n^{16})\) variables and polynomially many constraints. Thus Proposition 15.1 can repair the *size* premise needed by the paper's polynomial-time analysis, if the construction rules and the separate membership theorems are correct.

This is now a concrete conditional proof of a previously missing size bound, not a proof that the membership algorithm is correct. It leaves the logical core untouched: in particular, the sufficiency and necessity arguments must still establish that their input-dependent flows exactly characterize membership, and the reduction from STSP optimization must be checked independently.

### 16.2 Why extension complexity does not refute an input-dependent LP test

An extended formulation of a polytope P consists of one polyhedron Q and one fixed linear projection \(\pi\) such that \(\pi(Q)=P\). The 2025 pedigree procedure does not present that object. For a queried point X, it constructs F_k, the restricted networks, the rigid sets R_k, and the later multicommodity LP using data obtained from X and earlier X-dependent flows. Its LP is a membership test specialized to X. A family of per-input LP feasibility tests is not automatically one fixed extended formulation of the whole pedigree polytope.

Rothvoss proves exponential extension complexity for the perfect-matching polytope and obtains an exponential lower bound for the TSP polytope by a reduction. This rules out a polynomial-size *fixed LP extension* of those polytopes. It does not rule out polynomial-time membership, separation, or optimization algorithms in general. The perfect-matching polytope is the standard witness to that distinction: weighted perfect matching is polynomial-time solvable, while its extension complexity is exponential. See [Rothvoss, ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã…â€œThe matching polytope has exponential extension complexityÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã‚Â](https://arxiv.org/abs/1311.2369) and [Edmonds, ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã…â€œMaximum matching and a polyhedron with 0,1-verticesÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã‚Â](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/jres/69B/jresv69Bn1-2p125_A1b.pdf).

Therefore, the third-party suggestion that ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã…â€œpolynomial-time membership implies a compact extended formulationÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã‚Â is not valid as stated. A genuine contradiction would require showing that the recursively constructed LPs can be replaced by one fixed polynomial-size system whose projection is the entire pedigree polytope. No such uniform formulation has been established. Conversely, rejecting this shortcut is not evidence that the pedigree algorithm works: it only removes this particular proposed objection. The paper's claimed route from membership to separation uses Maurras/GLS oracle reductions, which also do not produce a small extended formulation.

### 16.3 Current status of the candidate positive result

The O(k^5) rigid-set bound is the strongest new proof-level result in this follow-up. Its hypotheses are explicit and its derivation is independent of the disputed simplex inference. The bound makes every network and LP size in the described algorithm polynomial, with a conservative multicommodity dimension bound of \(O(n^{16})\). If the remaining characterization and optimization links are all correct, this would be significant evidence for P=NP; since that conclusion would overturn a central open problem, each link still requires independent proof review and machine-checked or otherwise reproducible validation. At present, the result is a conditional repair to the size analysis, not a P=NP proof or a resolution of P versus NP.


## 17. Audit of the public Lean formalization

**Audit date:** 23 September 2026  
**Result:** The public Lean repository does not currently provide a machine-checked proof of P = NP. Its main theorem is derivable only after assuming a custom axiom that directly asserts polynomial-time pedigree-polytope membership. The Lean MCF data structure also imposes a one-hot restriction on the target layer, unlike the general fractional-demand MCF in the paper. This is a concrete defect in the formalization and its claim of verification; it is not evidence for either P = NP or P != NP.

### The decisive complexity premise is an axiom

In `N_PEqualsNP.lean`, `QuickProtocol` is itself declared as an axiom, without a definition connecting it to an executable algorithm. The further axiom `tardos_strongly_polynomial` has the conclusion `QuickProtocol (LayeredPoint n)`. The main theorem then takes that proposition as an input to the Maurras and GLS steps, followed by the reduction from pedigree optimization to STSP and the standard NP-completeness implications. Therefore the formal proof assumes the key polynomial-time membership claim instead of deriving it from the proposed MCF construction. Citing Tardos does not discharge this obligation: the formal development must state the actual LP and prove that its feasibility decides membership in both directions, with polynomially bounded construction and bit complexity.

The repository's own README states that the necessity direction of the MCF characterization is an axiom and that a backup proof contains 16 `sorry` placeholders. Although a sufficiency direction could suffice for sound positive answers, it cannot by itself justify a membership decision procedure: completeness / rejection correctness must also be established. The separate axiom above bypasses that missing direction in the P=NP chain.

### The formal MCF forces a one-hot target layer

`N_LayeredNetworkTypes.lean` defines `MCFFeasible.src_val` as `X s.arc_head = 1` for every commodity. The same structure requires all coordinates of the target layer to be nonnegative and to sum to 1. It follows that every commodity head must be the same single triple: two distinct heads with value 1 would already contribute 2 to a layer sum of 1. Thus this structure cannot represent an input whose final layer has two or more positive coordinates.

In the paper, the final-layer demand is distributed among destinations, and each commodity's positive flow is a fractional quantity. The paper's later normalized vector `Y^s / v_s` has a unit vector at the final layer; that is a property of the commodity-normalized vector, not of the original input `X`. The Lean field places this unit-value condition on `X` itself. `N_YisinConv.lean` then calls the packability corollary on `X` and uses this condition to obtain a special one-destination case.

### Concrete n = 6 member excludes the encoded MCF

The 2025 preprint's Example 8 has x4 = (0, 3/4, 1/4), x5 = (1/2, 0, 0, 0, 0, 1/2), and x6 = (1/8, 1/8, 3/8, 0, 1/8, 2/8, 0, 0, 0, 0). The paper states this point is in conv(P6), gives a six-path witness, and identifies rigid F5 flows of 1/8 and 2/8. A separate min/max LP audit over the 60 enumerated vertices found exactly these two stage-5/6 pair flows fixed across all representations, leaving flexible mass 5/8. Every x6 coordinate is strictly below 1.

I independently enumerated the 60 valid n=6 pedigrees and verified an exact convex combination for this point. The support, with weights in eighths, is ((13,12,23), 2), ((13,12,34), 2), ((13,34,12), 1), ((13,34,23), 1), ((23,34,13), 1), and ((23,34,24), 1), where each triple lists the common edges inserted at layers 4, 5, and 6. This check is reproducible with the standard-library script [verify_pedigree_example8.py](verify_pedigree_example8.py).

For the paper's constructed network, zMax = 5/8 requires positive commodity flow, so the commodity list must be nonempty. But `MCFFeasible.src_val` requires X(s.arc_head) = 1 for every commodity, while every x6 coordinate in this member is at most 3/8. The Lean structure therefore cannot encode the paper's positive-flow MCF witness for this known member. This is a concrete completeness failure in the formalized input model, not a counterexample to the paper's mathematical theorem.

### Shared capacity coupling is also absent from the Lean type

The paper's MCF includes shared constraints summing the flow of all commodities through each node and arc (equations 28 and 30). In `MCFFeasible`, `flow_le_slack`, `slack_nonneg`, and `col_sum_le` are each quantified separately for one commodity; the structure has no corresponding sum-over-commodities bound by `node_cap` and no aggregate-flow equality of the paper's kind. `flow_is_zMax` constrains only the total scalar flow. Thus the encoded feasibility predicate is not the paper's MCF even apart from the one-hot target restriction.

### The Lean sufficiency theorem is only a special case

The comments in `N_Sufficiency.lean` outline assembling the witnesses for every commodity together with all rigid pedigrees. The actual theorem body obtains one commodity and returns the witness produced by `Y_s_in_conv`; it does not perform that assembly. With the one-hot condition above, the helper can certify a restricted single-destination situation. It does not formalize the stated general MCF sufficiency argument for fractional multi-destination inputs.

### What would be needed to repair the claim

A valid verification would need to define `QuickProtocol` as an algorithm with a clear input/output specification, encode the paper's fractional MCF without forcing `X(s.arc_head)=1`, and prove both directions of the membership characterization for the exact encoded network. It would also need to derive the runtime bound from that LP and show the separation/optimization reductions apply to the same polytope representation. Until those are supplied, the repository is useful as an evolving formalization scaffold, not a verified resolution of P versus NP.

### Primary sources checked

- [Author's Lean repository README](https://github.com/TiruArt/Pedigree-Polytopes-Lean4/blob/main/README.md), including its axiom inventory and characterization status.
- [N_PEqualsNP.lean](https://github.com/TiruArt/Pedigree-Polytopes-Lean4/blob/main/MembershipProject/Core/N_PEqualsNP.lean), especially `QuickProtocol`, `tardos_strongly_polynomial`, and the proof of `p_equals_np`.
- [N_LayeredNetworkTypes.lean](https://github.com/TiruArt/Pedigree-Polytopes-Lean4/blob/main/MembershipProject/Core/N_LayeredNetworkTypes.lean), especially `MCFFeasible.src_val` and `flow_is_zMax`.
- [N_YisinConv.lean](https://github.com/TiruArt/Pedigree-Polytopes-Lean4/blob/main/MembershipProject/Core/N_YisinConv.lean) and [N_Sufficiency.lean](https://github.com/TiruArt/Pedigree-Polytopes-Lean4/blob/main/MembershipProject/Core/N_Sufficiency.lean), for the use of the one-hot condition and the implemented theorem body.
- [The 2025 pedigree-polytopes preprint](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.09069), Section 7.2, Definitions 17 and Lemma 7, for the paper's normalized per-commodity vector and fractional MCF framework.


## 18. Late-September discrepancy update and a margin-normalized cover test

A search of current primary sources found a recent preprint by Guo, Fang, and Lu, *Vector Balancing via Directional Total Variation* (submitted 10 September 2026). Its Theorem 1.1 claims that any finite family of vectors of Euclidean norm at most one has a signing whose infinity norm is less than $K=3\sqrt{2\pi}$. The authors describe the proof as having been discovered by an automatic AI research agent. A 21 September ETH research-blog post by Afonso Bandeira gives a shorter exposition of the same result with the weaker constant $9\pi$. A further 20 September preprint claims a polynomial-time signing algorithm in a unit-cost exact-real-arithmetic model. These are new preprints, not a P-versus-NP result; the main $3\sqrt{2\pi}$ proof has not been independently audited in this project, and the algorithm paper's real-RAM operation count does not by itself provide a polynomial bit-complexity Turing algorithm. See [Guo, Fang, and Lu (2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11189), [Bandeira (2026)](https://randomstrasse101.math.ethz.ch/posts/komlos/), and [Guo, Fang, and Lu (2026), algorithmic preprint](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23540).

The Clay Mathematics Institute's current archive still lists P versus NP among the unsolved problems. No source located in this update supplies a proof of either $P=NP$ or $P\ne NP$. [Clay Mathematics Institute, unsolved archive](https://www.claymath.org/problem/unsolved/).

### Proposition 18.1 - weighted-margin Komlos criterion

Let $U$ be a finite universe, let $A$ be a finite set of anchors, and for each $a\in A$ let $\mu_a$ be a probability measure on $U$. Suppose each anchor is a bit string $a\in\{0,1\}^{N}$, and every coordinate has positive signed majority margin

$$
d_{a,i}=\sum_{z\in U}\mu_a(z)g_{a,i}(z)>0,\qquad
 g_{a,i}(z)=\begin{cases}1&z_i=a_i,\\-1&z_i\ne a_i.\end{cases}
$$

Define one vector $v_z\in\mathbb R^{|A|N}$ for each $z\in U$ by

$$
(v_z)_{a,i}=\frac{\mu_a(z)g_{a,i}(z)}{d_{a,i}},
\qquad
R=\max_{z\in U}\|v_z\|_2.
$$

Assume a vector-balancing theorem with constant $K$: every finite family of vectors of norm at most one has a signing with infinity norm less than $K$. If $KR<1$, then there is a partition $U=E\sqcup H$ such that, for every $a$ and $i$, both restrictions $\mu_a(\cdot\mid E)$ and $\mu_a(\cdot\mid H)$ give strict majority to the anchor bit $a_i$. Consequently, for the existential majority-support filters $\mathcal G_a$ of Proposition 12.8, $E,H\in\mathcal G_a$ for every $a$, while $E\cap H=\varnothing\notin\mathcal G_a$; one pair covers the entire family.

**Proof.** Apply the assumed signing theorem to $v_z/R$. It gives signs $\varepsilon_z$ with

$$
\left|\sum_{z\in U}\varepsilon_z\mu_a(z)g_{a,i}(z)\right|<KRd_{a,i}<d_{a,i}
$$

for every $(a,i)$. Write $E=\{z:\varepsilon_z=1\}$ and $H=\{z:\varepsilon_z=-1\}$. The signed anchor margin on $E$ is half the sum of the total margin and the signed discrepancy; on $H$ it is half their difference. The displayed strict inequality makes both positive. Thus the conditional measures on each part have every required coordinate majority. The definition of $\mathcal G_a$ then puts both parts in every filter, and their empty intersection is excluded by nontriviality. $\square$

This strengthens Proposition 12.10's uniform-margin condition $d_*>36R_0$, where $R_0=\max_z\|(\mu_a(z)g_{a,i}(z))_{a,i}\|_2$. Under the new $K=3\sqrt{2\pi}$ claim, the sharper condition is the direct heterogeneous-margin test

$$
\max_{z\in U}\sum_{a,i}\left(\frac{\mu_a(z)}{d_{a,i}}\right)^2 < \frac{1}{(3\sqrt{2\pi})^2}.
$$

If one uses only the simpler ETH exposition's constant, replace $3\sqrt{2\pi}$ by $9\pi$. The proof needs only the signing statement, not the algorithmic preprint.

### What the criterion tests

For the high-complexity Hamming-sphere measures from Proposition 12.4, suppose each measure is uniform on $m$ support points and each point belongs to at most $M$ anchor supports. If the maximum coordinate flip probability is $q<1/2$, then $d_{a,i}\ge1-2q$ and

$$
R\le\frac{\sqrt{MN}}{m(1-2q)}.
$$

The criterion succeeds whenever $m>(3\sqrt{2\pi})\sqrt{MN}/(1-2q)$. The existing sphere construction already has exponentially larger support than this threshold, so this recovers a known one-pair cover in a margin-sensitive form.

A finite sanity check is recorded in [experiment_weighted_margin.py](experiment_weighted_margin.py). It uses two anchors in $\{0,1\}^{16}$ and the radius-two Hamming balls around $0^{16}$ and $1^{16}$ as disjoint supports. Each support has 137 points, each coordinate margin is exactly $105/137$, and the normalized column radius is exactly $4/105$. Thus $3\sqrt{2\pi}R<1$; a seeded signing found on its first trial and exact integer checks confirmed all 32 side-wise coordinate majorities. This only checks the finite inequality and its partition interpretation on a toy set system; these points are not claimed to lie in the high-circuit-complexity promise set. The older $36R_0$ test would fail here, so this example demonstrates the practical gain from the sharper constant and margin normalization.

### Proposition 18.2 - a shared heavy core with a diffuse tail

This extends the one-heavy-atom split in Proposition 12.7 to any number of substantial atoms, provided they fit inside a common core of mass at most one half. Let $\{\mu_a:a\in A\}$ be a finite family of probability measures on $U$. Suppose a common set $S\subseteq U$ has mass $b_a=\mu_a(S)$ with $0<b_a\le1/2$ for every $a$. On the tail $T=U\setminus S$, define vectors

$$
v_z=(\mu_a(z)/b_a)_{a\in A},\qquad R=\max_{z\in T}\|v_z\|_2.
$$

If a vector-balancing theorem with constant $K$ applies and $KR<1$, then one pair violates every strict-majority filter $\mathcal F_a=\{W\subseteq U:\mu_a(W)>1/2\}$. To see this, sign the tail so that $D_a=\sum_{z\in T}\varepsilon_z\mu_a(z)$ satisfies $|D_a|<b_a$. Let $T_+$ and $T_-$ be the two sign classes, and set $E=S\cup T_+$ and $H=S\cup T_-$. Then

$$
\mu_a(E)=b_a+\frac{1-b_a+D_a}{2}>\frac12,\qquad
\mu_a(H)=b_a+\frac{1-b_a-D_a}{2}>\frac12,
$$

while $E\cap H=S$ has measure $b_a\le1/2$. So $E,H\in\mathcal F_a$ and $E\cap H\notin\mathcal F_a$ for every anchor measure.

A finite check is recorded in [experiment_heavy_core.py](experiment_heavy_core.py). It uses three measures, each with five private heavy atoms of mass $1/10$ (core mass exactly $1/2$) and a shared 100-point tail carrying mass $1/2$. The normalized tail radius is $\sqrt3/100$. The ECCC report TR26-188 gives the bound $K=36$; here $36R=9\sqrt3/25<18/25<1$, using $\sqrt3<2$. Thus the multi-atom core result and this example do not depend on the sharper September preprint constant. The seeded signing was found on the first trial; exact rational checks give both cover-set measures greater than $1/2$ and intersection mass exactly $1/2$ for all three measures. Since the filter uses the strict threshold $>1/2$, the intersection is excluded. This finite check probes the boundary case $b_a=1/2$ and is not a Gap-MCSP instance. See [Karingula and Lovett, ECCC TR26-188](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/188/).

### Corollary 18.3 - detecting the core by aggregate atom size

Write $w_z=(\mu_a(z))_{a\in A}$. For a threshold $\theta>0$, let $S_\theta=\{z:\|w_z\|_2>\theta\}$. If there is a $b_0>0$ such that

$$
b_0\le\mu_a(S_\theta)\le\frac12\quad\text{for every }a,
\qquad K\theta/b_0<1,
$$

then Proposition 18.2 applies: every tail atom has normalized vector norm at most $\theta/b_0$. With the ECCC bound $K=36$, it is enough that $\theta<b_0/36$. This gives a direct threshold test for families with several anchor-specific moderate/heavy atoms: take their union as the core when its mass stays in the same interval for every measure, then balance the low-aggregate-mass tail.

The heavy-core experiment passes this test at $\theta=1/100$: each private core atom has aggregate norm $1/10$, each shared tail atom has aggregate norm $\sqrt3/200<1/100$, and each measure assigns core mass exactly $1/2$. With $b_0=1/2$, the ECCC condition is $36\theta/b_0=18/25<1$. This explicitly shows the new criterion handles multiple substantial, anchor-specific atoms at the half-mass boundary. Consequently, any majority-measure family evading every threshold-core cover must satisfy the following for every $\theta$: if $\max_a\mu_a(S_\theta)\le1/2$, then either $\min_a\mu_a(S_\theta)=0$ or $\min_a\mu_a(S_\theta)\le36\theta$. Otherwise Corollary 18.3 covers it. This is a necessary condition for evading this particular sufficient criterion, not a general lower-bound condition.

### Example 18.4 - a high-promise heavy-atom stress test

The common-core discrepancy condition is sufficient, not necessary. In fact, there are valid majority-measure semi-filters above a low anchor in the Gap-MCSP promise for which every common core has normalized tail radius at least one, while a single explicit pair still violates all filters.

Let $N=2^n$ and partition the $N$ truth-table positions into three blocks $X,Y,Z$ whose sizes differ by at most one. Define three truth tables $x=1_X$, $y=1_Y$, and $z=1_Z$. For any fixed $0<\beta<1$, one can choose such a balanced partition with all three tables of circuit complexity at least $s_2=N^\beta$. There are $2^{N\log_2 3-O(\log N)}$ ordered balanced partitions. The number of low-circuit tables is at most $2^{O(s_2\log(n+s_2))}=2^{o(N)}$. For any fixed low-circuit table in one block, the number of completions into the other two blocks is at most $2^{2N/3+O(1)}$. A union bound over the three blocks leaves a partition for which all $x,y,z$ have complexity at least $s_2$. The constant-zero anchor $a=0^N$ lies in $Y_{n,\beta}$ for sufficiently large $n$.

Put two probability measures on these three high-complexity tables:

$$
\mu_1(x,y,z)=(49/100,49/100,2/100),\qquad
\mu_2(x,y,z)=(2/100,49/100,49/100).
$$

At every coordinate, exactly one of $x,y,z$ has bit one. For coordinates in $X,Y,Z$, respectively, the mass agreeing with $a_i=0$ is $(51/100,49/50)$, $(51/100,51/100)$, and $(49/50,51/100)$ under $(\mu_1,\mu_2)$. All are strictly greater than one half. Therefore the strict-majority filters $\mathcal F_j=\{W\subseteq U:\mu_j(W)>1/2\}$ are semi-filters above the same low anchor with respect to the bit-literal generators.

Nevertheless, the pair $E=\{x,z\}$, $H=\{y,z\}$ violates both filters:

$$
(\mu_1(E),\mu_1(H),\mu_1(E\cap H))=(51/100,51/100,2/100),\\
(\mu_2(E),\mu_2(H),\mu_2(E\cap H))=(51/100,49/50,49/100).
$$

All possible common cores of positive mass at most one half for both measures are the singletons. For $S=\{y\}$, the normalized tail radius is $R=\sqrt{1+(2/49)^2}>1$; for $S=\{x\}$ or $\{z\}$ it exceeds $24$. Thus the $K=36$ common-core bound fails for every such $S$. The aggregate-threshold sets are only $U$, $\{y\}$, or empty: $\|w_x\|_2^2=\|w_z\|_2^2=2405/10000$ and $\|w_y\|_2^2=4802/10000$. The middle case has core masses $49/100$ but threshold $\theta>49/100$, so $36\theta/(49/100)>36$ and Corollary 18.3 does not apply.

[experiment_correlated_heavy_atoms.py](experiment_correlated_heavy_atoms.py) checks all rational majority masses and all candidate common cores for the three-atom weight pattern. The balanced-partition counting argument above is what lifts the pattern to high-complexity truth tables; the script alone does not certify circuit complexity. This is a useful falsification test: heavy atoms and a large Komlos radius do not imply a difficult cover instance, because correlated atoms can cancel under one pair. Any lower-bound construction in this regime must control those cross-filter cancellations, not only atom sizes or per-column norms.

### Proposition 18.5 - small-support majority families have a short cover list

Let $\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_M$ be probability measures on $U$, each supported on at most $t$ points and with every atom of weight strictly below $1/2$. For each $j$, define $\mathcal F_j=\{W\subseteq U:\mu_j(W)>1/2\}$. Then the entire selected family has a cover list of at most

$$
L=\left\lceil 3^t(\ln M+1)\right\rceil
$$

pairs. For each measure, use the heaviest atom to construct a partition of its support into $A_j,B_j,C_j$ with $\mu_j(A_j)<1/2$, $\mu_j(C_j)<1/2$, and $\mu_j(B_j)\le1/2$. A uniformly random 3-coloring of $U$ agrees with this prescribed support labeling with probability at least $3^{-t}$. Whenever it agrees, the induced pair $E=\chi^{-1}(A\cup B)$, $H=\chi^{-1}(B\cup C)$ has $\mu_j(E),\mu_j(H)>1/2$ and $\mu_j(E\cap H)\le1/2$, so it violates $\mathcal F_j$. With $L$ independent colorings, the chance that any one of the $M$ filters is missed is at most $M\exp(-L3^{-t})\le e^{-1}<1$. Therefore some list covers the whole selected family.

For the Gap-MCSP anchor set, $M=|Y_{n,\beta}|\le2^{O(s_1\log(n+s_1))}=2^{O(N^\beta)}$. Thus one majority-measure witness per anchor, each supported on at most $t=\alpha\log_2N$ points, has a cover list of size at most $N^{\alpha\log_2 3+\beta+o(1)}$. This sharpens Proposition 12.6 for a selected family: the all-measures bound there pays for every possible support in $U$, while this bound pays only for the chosen $M$ witnesses. A packing intended to force $N^{1+\varepsilon}$ cover complexity cannot use this one-witness-per-anchor scheme when $\alpha\log_2 3+\beta<1+\varepsilon$.

This is only an upper bound for the selected majority-measure filters, not for all semi-filters in the definition of $\rho$. By itself, it does not exclude families with support sizes large enough that $3^t\ln M$ exceeds the desired cover threshold, or genuinely non-measure semi-filters. Proposition 18.9 later closes every uniform-support family of size at least three and every family with collision mass at most $1/48$, regardless of overlap or number of measures per anchor. Example 18.4 shows that merely increasing atom norms does not solve the problem: the supports must also resist common cover pairs.

The new criteria cover diffuse high-sphere measures, selected families with low support-overlap chromatic number, and multi-atom families admitting a common at-most-half-mass core with a sufficiently diffuse residual tail. Propositions 18.9 and 18.10-18.12 add the overlap-independent uniform/diffuse cover and close two non-measure constructions. They still do not cover every correlated heavy-atom family: a usable common core of mass at most one half may not exist, or its complement may contain atoms whose normalized vector load violates $KR<1$. Tiny margins in nonuniform measures with high collision, and arbitrary semi-filters beyond the tested constructions, remain open. No asymptotic cover lower bound or P-versus-NP consequence has been obtained.

### Proposition 18.6 - aggregate-load condition for one three-part cover

Let $\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_M$ be probability measures on a finite set $U$, and define the aggregate atom vector $w_z=(\mu_1(z),\ldots,\mu_M(z))$ and $\delta=\max_{z\in U}\|w_z\|_2$. Assume a signing theorem with constant $K$: every finite family of vectors of Euclidean norm at most one has signs whose signed sum has $\ell_\infty$ norm at most $K$. If

$$
13K\delta<1,
$$

then a single partition $U=A\sqcup B\sqcup C$ satisfies $\mu_j(A),\mu_j(B),\mu_j(C)<1/2$ for every $j$. Consequently, $E=A\cup B$ and $H=B\cup C$ satisfy $\mu_j(E),\mu_j(H)>1/2$ and $\mu_j(E\cap H)<1/2$ for every measure. With the $K=36$ bound stated in Karingula and Lovett's ECCC TR26-188, it is sufficient that $\delta<1/468$.

#### Proof

Apply the signing theorem to the vectors $w_z/\delta$ in any current block $S$ (the case $\delta=0$ is vacuous for probability measures). This divides $S$ into two parts such that, for every $j$, the difference between their $\mu_j$-masses has absolute value at most $K\delta$. Each child therefore has mass at most half its parent's mass plus $K\delta/2$. First split $U$ into two halves, then split both halves. The four resulting quarter-pieces each have mass at most $1/4+3K\delta/4$. Split one quarter-piece once more. Its two children each have mass at most $1/8+7K\delta/8$, leaving three unsplit quarter-pieces. Pair one eighth-piece with each of two quarter-pieces; use the third quarter-piece as the last part. The paired parts each have mass at most

$$
1/4+3K\delta/4+1/8+7K\delta/8=3/8+13K\delta/8
$$

under every measure, which is below one half when $13K\delta<1$. The remaining quarter-piece has mass at most $1/4+3K\delta/4<1/2$. Thus all three parts have mass strictly below one half, which gives the claimed violating pair. The Karingula-Lovett result is an ECCC technical report, so the numerical $1/468$ corollary inherits that result's current verification status; the deduction above is conditional only on its signing guarantee.

This is a family-level criterion with no common-core requirement. It covers, for example, many individually diffuse measures with largely disjoint supports, provided the squared masses that meet at each point sum to less than $1/468^2$. It fails for a shared atom of substantial mass, and it is only sufficient. The criterion uses the aggregate load across the selected family, so it becomes harder to satisfy as many measures reuse the same atoms.

The finite check in [experiment_small_aggregate_load.py](experiment_small_aggregate_load.py) uses 16 measures, each uniform on 4,096 points of a 16,384-point universe. Even in the worst case, a point belongs to at most 16 supports, so $\delta\le4/4096=1/1024<1/468$. A seeded 3-coloring was found, and exact integer checks verified all three part masses below one half for all 16 measures. This verifies one finite instance only; the signing argument proves the general criterion.

An exact search in [experiment_split_tree_threshold.py](experiment_split_tree_threshold.py) checks every full binary split-tree depth multiset with at most seven leaves and every grouping into three parts. Within this proof template, the best threshold is $k=K\delta<1/13$, attained already by the five-piece construction. For $m\ge8$ leaves, the sum of the leaf upper bounds is $1+(m-1)k$; three grouped parts each below one half would force $k<1/(2(m-1))\le1/14<1/13$. Thus adding more recursive two-way splits cannot improve the constant in this particular scheme. This does not rule out a direct three-way discrepancy theorem or another construction.

A simpler parameter form is useful for testing candidate witnesses. If every $\mu_j$ is uniform on a support $S_j$ of size $t$, and every point of $U$ lies in at most $d$ of the selected supports, then $\delta\le\sqrt d/t$. Hence the $K=36$ criterion gives a shared violating pair whenever $t>468\sqrt d$, regardless of the number $M$ of selected measures. More generally, if every atom is at most $\rho$ and support overlap is at most $d$, it suffices that $468\rho\sqrt d<1$. This is a conditional one-pair result: no argument currently constructs the required low-overlap, diffuse witness family for the Gap-MCSP anchors.

### Proposition 18.7 - support-intersection coloring bounds selected majority-measure covers

Let $\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_M$ be probability measures on $U$, each with finite support $S_j$ and every atom strictly below $1/2$. Let $G$ be their support-intersection graph: vertices are measures, and $ij$ is an edge exactly when $S_i\cap S_j\ne\emptyset$. If $\chi(G)$ is its chromatic number, then the selected majority filters $\mathcal F_j=\{W\subseteq U:\mu_j(W)>1/2\}$ have a cover list of at most $\chi(G)$ pairs.

#### Proof

By the support-partition argument in Proposition 12.6, for each $j$ there is a partition $S_j=A_j\sqcup B_j\sqcup C_j$ with $\mu_j(A_j)<1/2$, $\mu_j(C_j)<1/2$, and $\mu_j(B_j)\le1/2$. Properly color $G$ with $\chi(G)$ colors. Within one color class, the supports are pairwise disjoint, so merge the corresponding $A_j,B_j,C_j$ into three global sets $A,B,C$; assign points outside the class supports arbitrarily. For every measure in that class, its masses of the global parts equal its local masses. Thus $E=A\cup B$ and $H=B\cup C$ belong to every corresponding $\mathcal F_j$, while $E\cap H=B$ does not. One pair covers the whole color class, and one pair per color covers all selected filters.

If every support has size at most $t$ and each point belongs to at most $d$ selected supports, then $\Delta(G)\le t(d-1)$ and hence the cover list has size at most $t(d-1)+1$. In particular, pairwise disjoint supports need only one pair, with no aggregate-load or Komlos threshold.

The finite check in [experiment_support_graph_cover.py](experiment_support_graph_cover.py) uses five uniform three-point measures whose support graph is a path plus an isolated vertex. A proper two-coloring yields two pairs that exactly violate all five majority filters; the computed maximum point overlap is two.

Together with Proposition 18.6, this gives a two-regime upper bound. For uniform supports of size $t$ and maximum point overlap $d$, one pair suffices when $t>468\sqrt d$; without that condition, at most $t(d-1)+1$ pairs suffice by support-graph coloring. For nonuniform measures with maximum atom $\rho$, the one-pair condition becomes $468\rho\sqrt d<1$.

### Corollary 18.8 - high-sphere majority witnesses can be chosen disjointly

Fix $0<\beta<1$, let $N=2^n$, $s_2=N^\beta$, and $s_1=s_2/(cn)$ as in Proposition 12.4. Let $Y$ be the low-circuit anchor set and $U$ the high-circuit promise set. For a sufficiently large constant $K$, each candidate sphere

$$
V_a=\{z\in U:d_H(z,a)=k\},\qquad k=\lceil K s_2\rceil,
$$

has size $2^{\Omega(s_2n)}$, while $|Y|=2^{O(s_2)}$. A uniform point of $V_a$ disagrees with $a$ at any fixed coordinate with probability $q_n=O(N^{\beta-1})$. Let $t=t(n)$ be odd, at least three, and large enough that $N2^tq_n^{(t+1)/2}=o(1)$; either $t=\Theta(\log N)$ or a sufficiently large odd constant (depending on fixed $\beta$) works. Then one can choose pairwise disjoint supports $S_a\subseteq V_a$ of size $t$, each with coordinatewise majority exactly $a$. Their uniform measures define valid majority semi-filters above their anchors, and Proposition 18.7 gives a one-pair cover for the whole selected family.

#### Proof

Order the anchors and choose supports sequentially. Before processing $a$, fewer than $|Y|t=2^{O(s_2)}t$ points have been used, an $r_n=o(1)$ fraction of $V_a$. The remaining set still has size $2^{\Omega(s_2n)}$, and its coordinate disagreement probability is at most $q_n/(1-r_n)=O(N^{\beta-1})$. Draw $t$ independent points uniformly from this remaining set. For any coordinate, the probability that at least $(t+1)/2$ disagree with $a$ is at most $2^t(q_n/(1-r_n))^{(t+1)/2}$; a union bound over $N$ coordinates tends to zero. The probability of a repeated draw is at most $\binom{t}{2}/(2^{\Omega(s_2n)}-|Y|t)=o(1)$. A distinct sample with all coordinate majorities correct therefore exists, and excluding previously used points makes the supports pairwise disjoint.

The uniform measure on each $S_a$ gives every anchor literal mass strictly above one half, so it defines a majority semi-filter above $a$. Each atom has weight $1/t<1/2$. The support-intersection graph has no edges, hence chromatic number one and a one-pair cover follows from Proposition 18.7. The finite check in [experiment_disjoint_sphere_witnesses.py](experiment_disjoint_sphere_witnesses.py) uses five random anchors of length 4,096 and 469 distinct radius-16 flips per anchor. It verifies disjointness, all coordinate majorities, and one shared violating pair. The random flips are not certified high-complexity tables.

This shows that the natural repair of taking one sparse majority witness per anchor from its high-complexity Hamming sphere remains easy to cover, including logarithmic support sizes large enough that the exponent in Proposition 18.5 is no longer useful. It does not bound other measure choices, families with many witnesses per anchor, or arbitrary non-measure semi-filters.

### Proposition 18.9 - random three-color covers for selected majority filters

Let $\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_M$ be probability measures on a finite universe $U$. For each $j$, form the strict-majority semi-filter $\mathcal F_j=\{W\subseteq U:\mu_j(W)>1/2\}$. Suppose each measure satisfies at least one of these conditions:

1. $\mu_j$ is uniform on a support of size $t_j\ge3$; or
2. its collision mass $h_j=\sum_{u\in U}\mu_j(u)^2$ is at most $1/48$.

Then there is a list of at most $\left\lceil (\ln(M+1)+1)3^{47}\right\rceil$ pairs covering every selected filter. If all measures satisfy condition 2, the list size improves to $\left\lceil 2(\ln(M+1)+1)\right\rceil$. These bounds do not depend on how the supports overlap.

#### Proof

Color every point of $U$ independently and uniformly with one of three colors, and call the color classes $A,B,C$. Set $E=A\cup B$ and $H=A\cup C$. Then $E\cap H=A$. The pair covers $\mathcal F_j$ exactly when $\mu_j(E)>1/2$, $\mu_j(H)>1/2$, and $\mu_j(A)\le1/2$.

For any probability measure $\mu$, the three random masses have means $2/3,2/3,1/3$ respectively. Each has variance $(2/9)\sum_u\mu(u)^2$. Chebyshev's inequality gives probability at most $8h$ for each of the three bad events $\mu(E)\le1/2$, $\mu(H)\le1/2$, and $\mu(A)>1/2$. If $h\le1/48$, their union has probability at most $24h\le1/2$, so a random pair covers the filter with probability at least $1/2$.

For a uniform support of size $t\ge48$, $h=1/t\le1/48$, so the same bound applies. If $3\le t<48$, consider the one prescribed coloring of its support having $\lfloor t/2\rfloor$ points in $A$, one point in $B$, and the rest in $C$. It has probability $3^{-t}\ge3^{-47}$; on this event, $\mu(A)\le1/2$ and both $\mu(E)$ and $\mu(H)$ are strictly greater than $1/2$. Thus in every allowed case one independent random coloring covers each filter with probability at least $p=3^{-47}$; in the collision-bounded case use $p=1/2$.

Take $r=\lceil(\ln(M+1)+1)/p\rceil$ independent colorings. A fixed filter is missed by all $r$ with probability at most $e^{-pr}\le1/(e(M+1))$. A union bound over $M$ filters gives probability less than one that any filter is missed. Therefore some list covers them all. This proves the stated bounds.

#### Consequence and scope

For the Gap-MCSP anchor set, $M=2^{O(N^\beta)}$ for fixed $\beta<1$, so this gives a selected-family cover of size $O(N^\beta)=o(N)$ for one such measure per anchor, regardless of support overlap. More generally, the theorem applies to any finite number of selected measures per anchor, with $M$ equal to their total number. It closes the remaining uniform-support regime left by Proposition 18.5's $O(3^t\log M)$ estimate. It does not cover arbitrary semi-filters or nonuniform measures with collision mass above $1/48$; it is not an upper bound on the full cover number $\rho$.

### Proposition 18.10 - threshold block composition has a two-pair cover

Let $U_0=\{1,2,3,4\}$ and define three semi-filters over $U_0$ by upward closure from these minimal winning pairs:

Here, for example, $12$ abbreviates the set $\{1,2\}$.

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal F_0 &: \{12,34,13,24\},\\
\mathcal F_1 &: \{12,34,14,23\},\\
\mathcal F_2 &: \{13,24,14,23\}.
\end{aligned}
$$

For $k\ge1$, let $U=U_0\times[k]$. For an anchor $a=(a_1,\ldots,a_k)\in\{0,1,2\}^k$ and threshold $1\le q\le k$, define

$$
\mathcal G_a^{(q)}
=\left\{W\subseteq U:
\left|\{j: W_j\in\mathcal F_{a_j}\}\right|\ge q\right\},
\qquad W_j=\{u\in U_0:(u,j)\in W\}.
$$

These are semi-filters. The entire selected family $\{\mathcal G_a^{(q)}:a\in\{0,1,2\}^k\}$ has a cover of size at most two for every $k,q$.

#### Proof

Let $E_A$ have block $\{1,2\}$ in every coordinate and $H_A$ have block $\{3,4\}$ in every coordinate. Let $E_B$ have block $\{1,2,3\}$ everywhere and $H_B$ have block $\{1,2,4\}$ everywhere. For $a$, put $X(a)=|\{j:a_j\in\{0,1\}\}|$.

In a single block, both $\{1,2\}$ and $\{3,4\}$ belong to $\mathcal F_0,\mathcal F_1$ and neither belongs to $\mathcal F_2$. Also, their intersection is empty. Therefore $(E_A,H_A)$ covers $\mathcal G_a^{(q)}$ exactly when $X(a)\ge q$.

Both $\{1,2,3\}$ and $\{1,2,4\}$ belong to all three local semi-filters. Their intersection $\{1,2\}$ belongs to $\mathcal F_0,\mathcal F_1$ but not $\mathcal F_2$. Therefore $(E_B,H_B)$ covers $\mathcal G_a^{(q)}$ exactly when $X(a)<q$.

These two cases partition all anchors. The two pairs cover every selected semi-filter, so the cover number is at most two. $\square$

This exact test rejects the natural $q$-of-$k$ amplification of a nonweighted four-point filter. The script [experiment_threshold_block_filter.py](experiment_threshold_block_filter.py) checks both pair identities on all 73,812 anchor-threshold instances for $1\le k\le8$; the proof above covers all $k$. It also supplies a reusable falsification test for block constructions: before seeking a product lower bound, search for a pair whose coverage is one threshold event and a second pair whose coverage is its complement. It does not rule out other compositions or establish an upper bound for Gap-MCSP.

### Proposition 18.11 - complementary profiles defeat every monotone block aggregator

Use the same three local semi-filters $\mathcal F_0,\mathcal F_1,\mathcal F_2$ and $k$-block universe as in Proposition 18.10. Let $g:\{0,1\}^k\to\{0,1\}$ be any monotone Boolean function with $g(0^k)=0$ and $g(1^k)=1$. For anchor $a\in\{0,1,2\}^k$, define

$$
\mathcal G_a^g
=\left\{W\subseteq U:
g\left(\left(1[W_j\in\mathcal F_{a_j}]\right)_{j=1}^k\right)=1
\right\}.
$$

Then each $\mathcal G_a^g$ is a semi-filter, and the full family $\{\mathcal G_a^g:a\in\{0,1,2\}^k\}$ has a cover of size at most two.

#### Proof

Write $x_j(a)=1$ exactly when $a_j\in\{0,1\}$. For the pair $(E_A,H_A)$ from Proposition 18.10, the local membership vectors of both endpoints are $x(a)$, while every block of $E_A\cap H_A$ is empty, giving the zero vector. Hence this pair covers $\mathcal G_a^g$ exactly when $g(x(a))=1$.

For $(E_B,H_B)$, both endpoints belong to all three local filters, so their global membership vectors are $1^k$. Their intersection has local block $\{1,2\}$, which belongs to $\mathcal F_0,\mathcal F_1$ but not $\mathcal F_2$; its vector is $x(a)$. Thus $(E_B,H_B)$ covers $\mathcal G_a^g$ exactly when $g(x(a))=0$. The cases are complementary and cover every anchor.

Finally, monotonicity of $g$ and of each local semi-filter makes $\mathcal G_a^g$ upward closed. The empty set maps to $0^k$ and is rejected, while $U$ maps to $1^k$ and is accepted. Therefore it is a semi-filter. $\square$

This closes not only symmetric $q$-of-$k$ composition but every coordinatewise monotone aggregation of these local membership bits. Any block-amplification proposal using this local triple must change the anchor coupling or the local witness types; changing only the monotone aggregator cannot help. The result remains specific to this local triple and does not upper-bound the general Gap-MCSP cover number.

The script [experiment_threshold_block_filter.py](experiment_threshold_block_filter.py) also checks the complementary identities for all 13,971 anchor/function instances through $k=4$, including every monotone Boolean aggregator with the required boundary values. This is an implementation check; Proposition 18.11 proves the general statement.

### Proposition 18.12 - parity boxes cover bounded-coordinate certificate filters

Let $U\subseteq\{0,1\}^N$ be a promise set with

$$
|\{0,1\}^N\setminus U|<2^{N-r-1},
\qquad 1\le r\le N/2.
$$

For anchor $a\in\{0,1\}^N$ and $I\subseteq[N]$, write
$C(a,I)=\{z\in U:z_I=a_I\}$. Define the semi-filter

$$
\mathcal F_a^{(r)}
=\{W\subseteq U:\exists I\subseteq[N],\ |I|=r,\ C(a,I)\subseteq W\}.
$$

All certificates $C(a,I)$ are nonempty by the promise-size condition. The filter is above the usual literal generators, since for each coordinate $i$ one can choose an $r$-set $I$ containing $i$, and then $C(a,I)\subseteq C(a,\{i\})$. Nevertheless, the family $\{\mathcal F_a^{(r)}:a\in\{0,1\}^N\}$ has a cover of size at most four.

#### Proof
