Choose disjoint coordinate blocks $I,J\subseteq[N]$ of size $r$. For $p,q\in\{0,1\}$ define

$$
E_p=\{z\in U:\bigoplus_{i\in I}z_i=p\},
\qquad
H_q=\{z\in U:\bigoplus_{j\in J}z_j=q\}.
$$

For each anchor $a$, use the pair with $p=\bigoplus_{i\in I}a_i$ and $q=\bigoplus_{j\in J}a_j$. Then $C(a,I)\subseteq E_p$ and $C(a,J)\subseteq H_q$, so both endpoints belong to $\mathcal F_a^{(r)}$.

We show that $E_p\cap H_q$ contains no certificate $C(a,K)$ with $|K|=r$. Since $I$ and $J$ are disjoint and each has size $r$, the set $K$ cannot contain both blocks. At least one block, say $I$, has a coordinate not fixed by $K$ (the case of $J$ is identical). Among all cube points matching $a$ on $K$, exactly $2^{N-r-1}$ have parity on $I$ opposite to $a_I$: the unfixed coordinate makes parity balanced. By the promise-size bound, at least one such point lies in $U$. It belongs to $C(a,K)$ but not to $E_p$, so $C(a,K)\nsubseteq E_p\cap H_q$. This holds for every $K$ of size $r$, hence $E_p\cap H_q\notin\mathcal F_a^{(r)}$. The pair covers $\mathcal F_a^{(r)}$.

There are four choices of $(p,q)$, and every anchor selects one. Thus four pairs cover the entire family. $\square$

For the Gap-MCSP promise, the number of excluded low-circuit truth tables is $2^{O(N^\beta\log N)}$ for fixed $\beta<1$. Hence the displayed condition holds whenever $N-r-1$ exceeds that exponent; in particular it holds for $r=O(N^\beta\log N)$. This rejects the natural family generated by all matching subcubes of such codimension, despite its direct connection to the anchor literals. The proof needs the stated density bound on $U$; it does not cover arbitrary antichain filters or imply a four-pair bound on the full semi-filter cover number.

### Proposition 18.13 - singleton-witness filters have logarithmic covers

Let $S_1,\ldots,S_M$ be nonempty subsets of a finite universe $U$, each of size at least two, and define the hit filters

$$
\mathcal H_j=\{W\subseteq U:W\cap S_j\ne\emptyset\}.
$$

Then the selected family has a cover list of at most $\lceil2(\ln(M+1)+1)\rceil$ pairs, regardless of overlap among the $S_j$.

#### Proof

Choose a uniformly random cut $U=E\sqcup H$. It covers $\mathcal H_j$ exactly when $S_j$ meets both sides. The probability it fails to split $S_j$ is $2^{1-|S_j|}\le1/2$, so each independent cut covers each filter with probability at least $1/2$. With $r=\lceil2(\ln(M+1)+1)\rceil$ independent cuts, a fixed filter is missed with probability at most $e^{-r/2}\le1/(e(M+1))$. A union bound over the $M$ filters gives positive probability that every filter is covered.

If a hit filter is above an anchor $a\notin U$ with respect to all coordinate-literal generators, its witness set $S_a$ cannot be a singleton: a singleton $\{z\}$ meeting every literal generator must satisfy $z_i=a_i$ for every coordinate, hence $z=a\notin U$. Thus any family of singleton-witness anchored filters in the Gap-MCSP promise has an $O(\log M)$ cover.

This is a property-B coloring argument for singleton minimal witnesses. It does not extend to filters whose minimal members contain two or more points: a cut splitting a witness set does not put a whole witness in either side. It is not an upper bound on arbitrary semi-filter families or on $\rho$.

### Proposition 18.14 - disjoint bounded witnesses yield short cut covers

Let $\mathcal F_1,\ldots,\mathcal F_M$ be semi-filters over a finite universe $U$. Suppose each $\mathcal F_j$ has two disjoint inclusion-minimal members $S_j,T_j$, each of size at most $t$. Then the selected family has a cover list of at most

$$
\left\lceil 4^t(\ln(M+1)+1)\right\rceil
$$

pairs.

#### Proof

Choose a uniformly random cut $U=E\sqcup H$. The event $S_j\subseteq E$ and $T_j\subseteq H$ has probability $2^{-|S_j|-|T_j|}\ge4^{-t}$, since the two witnesses are disjoint. On this event, $E,H\in\mathcal F_j$ by upward closure, while $E\cap H=\emptyset\notin\mathcal F_j$. Thus the cut covers each selected filter with probability at least $4^{-t}$. With $r=\lceil4^t(\ln(M+1)+1)\rceil$ independent cuts, a fixed filter is missed with probability at most $1/(e(M+1))$; a union bound over all $M$ filters gives probability less than one that any filter is missed. $\square$

Consequently, any selected family of anchored filters whose minimal witnesses have bounded size and include a disjoint pair per anchor has a short cover, regardless of overlap between different anchors. A bounded-width witness construction can evade this proposition only if its minimal-witness hypergraph is pairwise intersecting for many anchors. Pairwise intersection alone is not enough for a lower bound: one still has to show that every endpoint pair is hard to cover across anchors.

### Proposition 18.15 - the p-biased fusion-defect identity

For a semi-filter $\mathcal F$ over a finite universe $U$, define its p-biased acceptance polynomial

$$
q_{\mathcal F}(p)
=\Pr_{W\subseteq U,\ \Pr[u\in W]=p\ {\rm independently}}[W\in\mathcal F].
$$

If $E,H$ are independent p-biased subsets of $U$, then

$$
\Pr[E,H\in\mathcal F,\ E\cap H\notin\mathcal F]
=q_{\mathcal F}(p)^2-q_{\mathcal F}(p^2).
$$

#### Proof

The events $E\in\mathcal F$ and $H\in\mathcal F$ are independent, so their joint probability is $q_{\mathcal F}(p)^2$. For each $u\in U$, membership in $E\cap H$ has probability $p^2$, independently across $u$, so $E\cap H$ is distributed as a $p^2$-biased subset and belongs to $\mathcal F$ with probability $q_{\mathcal F}(p^2)$. Upward closure implies $E\cap H\in\mathcal F$ only if both $E,H\in\mathcal F$. Subtracting gives the identity. $\square$

Write $\Delta_{\mathcal F}(p)=q_{\mathcal F}(p)^2-q_{\mathcal F}(p^2)$ and call this the p-biased fusion defect. For any finite $\mathcal F$, $\Delta_{\mathcal F}(p)>0$ for every $0<p<1$ exactly when $\mathcal F$ fails to be intersection-closed. Indeed, every ordered pair $(E,H)$ has positive p-biased probability; a violation contributes positive mass. An intersection-closed finite semi-filter is principal: the intersection of all its members is a nonempty member $S$, and $\mathcal F=\{W:S\subseteq W\}$. Conversely, a principal filter has defect identically zero.

For a selected family $\mathcal F_1,\ldots,\mathcal F_M$, if some common $p$ satisfies $\min_j\Delta_{\mathcal F_j}(p)\ge\delta>0$, then $O(\delta^{-1}\log M)$ independent p-biased endpoint pairs cover the family with positive probability. This supplies a distributional search criterion for arbitrary semi-filters. It does not provide a useful bound without a uniform lower bound on $\Delta$; pointwise positivity alone may be exponentially small in $|U|$.

Example: for $\mathcal F=\{W:W\cap S\ne\emptyset\}$ with $|S|=s$, $q_{\mathcal F}(p)=1-(1-p)^s$ and $\Delta_{\mathcal F}(p)=q_{\mathcal F}(p)^2-q_{\mathcal F}(p^2)$. At $s=2$ this is $2p^2(1-p)^2$. For the filter generated by two witnesses sharing a core of size $\ell$ and each having one private point, $q(p)=p^\ell(2p-p^2)$ and $\Delta(p)=2p^{2\ell+2}(1-p)^2$; its maximum is $\Theta(\ell^{-2})$. Thus pairwise-intersecting witnesses can make this natural random-pair distribution weak as the common core grows.

### Proposition 18.16 - a quantitative fusion-profile conjecture and its cover consequence

For a semi-filter $\mathcal F$ on an $N$-point universe, define its peak fusion profile

$$
\Psi(\mathcal F)=\max_{0\le p\le1}\Delta_{\mathcal F}(p).
$$

**Conjecture (inverse-quadratic fusion profile).** There is an absolute constant $c>0$ such that every nonprincipal semi-filter on an $N$-point universe satisfies

$$
\Psi(\mathcal F)\ge c/N^2.
$$

The scale is motivated by the two-minimum family with minimal members $C\cup\{x\}$ and $C\cup\{y\}$, where $|C|=N-2$. Its acceptance polynomial is $q(p)=p^{N-2}(2p-p^2)$, and its defect is

$$
\Delta(p)=2p^{2N-2}(1-p)^2.
$$

The maximum occurs at $p=(N-1)/N$ and equals $2(N-1)^{2N-2}/N^{2N}\sim 2e^{-2}/N^2$. This gives an upper example at the conjectured scale, but does not establish a lower bound for arbitrary semi-filters.

**Conditional consequence.** If the conjecture holds, every family of $M$ nonprincipal semi-filters on an $N$-point universe has a cover by $O(N^5\log(M+1))$ fusion pairs. Indeed, Russo's formula gives $|q'_{\mathcal F}(p)|\le N$ for monotone acceptance, hence $|\Delta'_{\mathcal F}(p)|\le4N$. A bias grid of spacing $\Theta(c/N^3)$ therefore contains, for each filter, a bias with defect at least $c/(2N^2)$. At each grid bias, independent endpoint pairs cover every assigned filter with that probability; $O(N^2\log(M+1))$ samples and a union bound cover the assigned subfamily. There are $O(N^3)$ grid biases. This bound is nonconstructive, polynomial in the explicit universe size, and potentially exponential in a succinct input parameter. It cannot be used as a P-versus-NP algorithm without a separate succinctness analysis.

**Finite evidence.** [experiment_fusion_defect_profiles.py](experiment_fusion_defect_profiles.py) enumerates all 7,579 nonempty antichains of nonempty subsets of a five-point universe, which correspond exactly to its semi-filters. On a bias grid of step $0.005$, the smallest observed peak defect is $0.0134217728$, attained by minima $\{0123,0124\}$; the exact peak for this family is $0.0134217728\ldots$. The corresponding minima on two, three, and four points are the same shared-core pattern. The script also checks the identity in Proposition 18.15 by direct ordered-pair enumeration for all 151 nonprincipal four-point semi-filters at $p=0.37$. Grid maximization is finite evidence, not a proof that these are global minimizers.

**Why this matters for the failures.** In the fusion-cover route, many constructions fail because accepted endpoints retain an accepted witness in their intersection. The defect measures precisely that loss under a natural random-pair distribution. A nonprincipal filter always has some positive defect, but that qualitative fact is insufficient: the defect may be only inverse-quadratic, and different filters may peak at different biases. The next mathematical question is whether the inverse-quadratic bound is universal, or whether a structured family can make every p-biased fusion experiment exponentially insensitive. Even a proof of the conjecture would only bound covers polynomially in $N=|U|$; it would not automatically cross the projectÃ¢â‚¬â„¢s succinctness or magnification requirements.

### Proposition 18.17 - every nonprincipal semi-filter has an OR face

Let $\mathcal F$ be a nonprincipal semi-filter on a finite universe $U$. Regard membership in $\mathcal F$ as a monotone Boolean function on subsets of $U$. Then there are distinct coordinates $i,j\in U$ and a fixed context $Z\subseteq U\setminus\{i,j\}$ such that, on the two-dimensional face

$$
Z,\quad Z\cup\{i\},\quad Z\cup\{j\},\quad Z\cup\{i,j\},
$$

membership is respectively $0,1,1,1$. Thus the restriction is the OR function $x_i\lor x_j$. Equivalently, a semi-filter is principal exactly when none of its two-dimensional faces restricts to OR.

#### Proof

Let $\mathcal A$ be the antichain of inclusion-minimal members of $\mathcal F$. Since $\mathcal F$ is nonprincipal, $\mathcal A$ has at least two members. Choose distinct $A,B\in\mathcal A$ minimizing $|A\cup B|$. Pick $i\in A\setminus B$ and $j\in B\setminus A$, and set $Z=(A\cup B)\setminus\{i,j\}$. The sets $Z\cup\{i\}$ and $Z\cup\{j\}$ contain $A$ and $B$, respectively, so both belong to $\mathcal F$.

If $Z$ belonged to $\mathcal F$, it would contain a minimal member $D\in\mathcal A$. Since $i,j\notin Z$, $D$ differs from both $A$ and $B$. But $D\cup A$ is a union of two distinct minimal members and is strictly smaller than $A\cup B$, because it omits $j\in B\setminus A$. This contradicts the choice of $A,B$. Hence $Z\notin\mathcal F$. Upward closure gives acceptance of $Z\cup\{i,j\}$. This proves the OR face. A principal filter is an AND of its core coordinates under every restriction, so it has no OR face. $\square$

**Product-bias corollary.** For coordinate biases $\mathbf p=(p_u)_{u\in U}$, let $q_{\mathcal F}(\mathbf p)$ be the acceptance probability under independent inclusion of each $u$ with probability $p_u$. For independent endpoints with these marginals,

$$
\Pr[E,H\in\mathcal F,\ E\cap H\notin\mathcal F]
=q_{\mathcal F}(\mathbf p)^2-q_{\mathcal F}(\mathbf p^{\circ2}),
$$

where $\mathbf p^{\circ2}=(p_u^2)_u$. This is the same proof as Proposition 18.15. On the OR face, fix the coordinates of $Z$ to $1$, coordinates outside $Z\cup\{i,j\}$ to $0$, and use bias $1/2$ on $i,j$. The defect is exactly $1/8$. The explicit pair $(Z\cup\{i\},Z\cup\{j\})$ covers $\mathcal F$.

This gives a small local certificate for each individual nonprincipal filter, but not one shared certificate for the whole anchored family. There are at most $\binom N2 2^{N-2}$ distinct OR faces on an $N$-point universe, so enumerating all their pairs gives an exponential universal cover. The open question is whether the anchored filters' OR faces have enough shared structure for a much shorter list, or can be arranged to behave like a large code of incompatible faces.

The construction and product-bias identity were checked for all 151 nonprincipal four-point semi-filters in [experiment_product_bias_fusion.py](experiment_product_bias_fusion.py); direct numerical identity checks across 1,992 filter/bias instances had maximum discrepancy $3.61\times10^{-16}$. The OR-face proof itself is finite and exact; these computations are consistency checks, not its proof.

The follow-up [experiment_anchor_or_faces.py](experiment_anchor_or_faces.py) extracts all OR faces induced by minimal-witness pairs in the five-anchor, six-point literal-cylinder toy. For certificate codimensions $r=1,2$, it finds respectively 36 and 15 distinct faces; the best single OR-face pair covers only 3/5 and 2/5 anchors. Yet an unrestricted endpoint pair covers all five in both cases, and that pair contains compatible (but different) OR-face certificates for every anchor. Thus requiring one shared canonical face is too strong: a global pair can hold several local faces at once. Counting or packing the faces alone cannot prove a cover lower bound; a general argument must also account for such simultaneous containment and for endpoint pairs that use broader, multi-coordinate witnesses.

The OR-face statement is a restriction/minor assertion for monotone Boolean functions. Related literature studies forbidden Boolean minors as a way to characterize monotone and threshold functions; see [Chi Wang, Ã¢â‚¬Å“Boolean minors,Ã¢â‚¬Â *Discrete Mathematics* 141 (1995), 237Ã¢â‚¬â€œ258](https://doi.org/10.1016/0012-365X(93)E0191-6). The exact principal-filter/OR-face dichotomy here is an elementary project lemma, and this limited source check is not a novelty claim.

### Proposition 18.18 - shared-core OR-face families reduce to graph coloring

Let $G=(V,E)$ be a finite graph with at least one edge, let $C$ be a fixed set disjoint from $V$, and for each edge $uv\in E$ define the semi-filter

$$
\mathcal F_{uv}=\{W\subseteq C\cup V:C\cup\{u\}\subseteq W\text{ or }C\cup\{v\}\subseteq W\}.
$$

The minimum number of fusion pairs covering all $|E|$ filters is exactly $\lceil\log_2\chi(G)\rceil$.

#### Proof

Every pair $(A,B)$ that covers $\mathcal F_{uv}$ must put $C$ in both endpoints and must split $u,v$ between $A\setminus B$ and $B\setminus A$; otherwise the intersection still contains one of the two generating witnesses. Thus each fusion pair induces a cut of $V$, and an edge filter is covered only if its edge crosses that cut. A list of $m$ cuts gives every vertex an $m$-bit side signature. Since every edge is separated by some cut, this is a proper coloring with at most $2^m$ colors, so $m\ge\lceil\log_2\chi(G)\rceil$.

Conversely, take a proper coloring with $\chi(G)$ colors and encode each color by $m=\lceil\log_2\chi(G)\rceil$ bits. Each bit defines a cut. For a cut $X,V\setminus X$, use endpoints $C\cup X$ and $C\cup(V\setminus X)$. Every graph edge separated by the cut gives a covered filter, with intersection exactly $C$. The coloring separates every edge in at least one bit, proving the matching upper bound. $\square$

Exhaustive checks of all 1,094 nonempty graphs on two through five vertices in [experiment_or_face_graph_coding.py](experiment_or_face_graph_coding.py) verify the exact identity. This tempting construction cannot yield a large fusion-cover lower bound: its cover number is at most $\lceil\log_2|V|\rceil$, even when the graph has many edges. It explains a specific collapse: local OR conflicts can be coded in parallel by vertex signatures. A surviving family must make the fixed contexts vary in a way that prevents such a shared coloring/cut representation, while also resisting higher-width witness pairs.

### Proposition 18.19 - a co-singleton family has exact quadratic cover number

Let $U$ have $N\ge2$ points. For each unordered pair $\{i,j\}\subseteq U$, define

$$
\mathcal F_{ij}=\{W\subseteq U:U\setminus\{i\}\subseteq W\text{ or }U\setminus\{j\}\subseteq W\}.
$$

Then every fusion pair covers at most one of these $\binom N2$ semi-filters, and the minimum cover number is exactly $\binom N2$.

#### Proof

If $(E,H)$ covers $\mathcal F_{ij}$, each endpoint must contain one of the two co-singletons, so it omits no point outside $\{i,j\}$ and omits at most one of $i,j$. Since $E\cap H\notin\mathcal F_{ij}$, the intersection must omit both $i$ and $j$. Therefore the endpoints are exactly $U\setminus\{i\}$ and $U\setminus\{j\}$, in either order. Such a pair identifies its unique index pair and cannot cover any other filter in the family. Conversely, that pair covers $\mathcal F_{ij}$ because its intersection is $U\setminus\{i,j\}$, which contains neither generating co-singleton. Thus precisely one pair per index pair is necessary and sufficient. $\square$

The exhaustive check [experiment_cosingleton_pair_filters.py](experiment_cosingleton_pair_filters.py) enumerates every ordered endpoint pair for $N=2,\ldots,8$ and confirms maximum pair coverage one. This is a genuine superlinear lower bound for an unrestricted family of semi-filters, and an explicit example of how anchor-dependent cores defeat the shared-core graph-coloring compression. It does not yet satisfy the projectÃ¢â‚¬â„¢s anchor condition: the co-singleton filters are not shown to contain all required bit-literal generators for their assigned low-circuit anchors. A P-versus-NP consequence would require an explicit embedding into those anchored filters with all size, promise, and circuit-complexity conditions checked. In the five-anchor, six-point literal-cylinder toy, [experiment_anchor_or_faces.py](experiment_anchor_or_faces.py) finds that all 30 tested co-singletons are already accepted by every canonical anchored filter for both $r=1,2$; naively adding them changes nothing.

### Finite test - complementary block-cylinder anchored filters

A direct attempt to satisfy the anchor condition while retaining two special witnesses is to partition the input coordinates into blocks $I,J$ and, for each low anchor $a$, use witnesses $S_a=\{z\in U:z|_I=a|_I\}$ and $T_a=\{z\in U:z|_J=a|_J\}$. Every literal generator is accepted because its coordinate belongs to one block, so the corresponding witness is contained in that literal slice. On the weight-two shell of four input bits with five anchors of weight at most one, all three unordered $2+2$ partitions are nonempty for every anchor. Exhaustive enumeration of all $3^5=243$ block assignments gives cover number one in six cases and two in the other 237. For five bits, the weight-two shell and six anchors, 12 seeded assignments of a $2+3$ split all had cover number one or two (three and nine cases respectively). [experiment_two_block_anchor_filters.py](experiment_two_block_anchor_filters.py) computes the exact endpoint-pair cover for each tested assignment.

The balanced version is also closed in the asymptotic high-complexity promise. When $N$ is even and $I,J$ partition the $N$ truth-table positions into two blocks of size $r=N/2$, the four pairs $(E_p,H_q)$ from Proposition 18.12 cover this family: for anchor $a$, choose $p=\bigoplus_{i\in I}a_i$ and $q=\bigoplus_{j\in J}a_j$. Then $E_p$ contains $S_a$ and $H_q$ contains $T_a$, so both endpoints belong to $\mathcal F_a$; the promise-density argument shows the intersection contains no matching $r$-coordinate certificate, in particular neither $S_a$ nor $T_a$. The density condition holds for fixed $\beta<1$ because the number of excluded low-circuit tables is $2^{O(N^\beta\log N)}<2^{N/2-1}$ for large $N$. So this valid anchored construction still has a four-pair cover at the project scale. The finite tests expose the toy collapse; the parity argument proves it for the balanced asymptotic case. Unequal-block variants remain open but need a new obstruction to parity boxes.

The stronger two-pair theorem below closes a broader full-cylinder version of this construction, including balanced and moderately unbalanced blocks; it also covers any fixed number of punctures per cylinder once the block completion counts dominate those punctures.

### Proposition 18.20 - two endpoint pairs cover dense two-cylinder filters

Let $U\subseteq\{0,1\}^N$ and write $L=2^N-|U|$. Partition the coordinates into nonempty blocks $I\sqcup J=[N]$, with $|I|=r$ and $|J|=s$. For patterns $p\in\{0,1\}^I$ and $q\in\{0,1\}^J$, define row and column cylinders

$$
R_p=\{z\in U:z_I=p\},\qquad C_q=\{z\in U:z_J=q\}.
$$

For any selected set of anchor pairs $(p_a,q_a)$, let $\mathcal F_a$ be generated by $R_{p_a}$ and $C_{q_a}$. If

$$
L<\min\{2^{r-1},2^{s-1}\},
$$

then two fusion pairs cover every selected filter.

More generally, suppose the two minimal witnesses are $S_a\subseteq R_{p_a}$ and $T_a\subseteq C_{q_a}$, each obtained by deleting at most $t$ points from its full cylinder. Then the same conclusion holds if

$$
L+t<\min\{2^{r-1},2^{s-1}\}.
$$

#### Proof

Choose distinguished coordinates $i_0\in I$ and $j_0\in J$. Give row pattern $p$ the two-bit code $\rho(p)=(p_{i_0},p_{i_0})$ and column pattern $q$ the code $\gamma(q)=(1-q_{j_0},q_{j_0})$. For every pair $(p,q)$, at least one of the two bit positions agrees: if $p_{i_0}=q_{j_0}$, the second bits agree; otherwise the first bits agree.

For bit position $b\in\{1,2\}$, define

$$
E_b=\{z\in U:\rho_b(z_I)=0\text{ or }\gamma_b(z_J)=1\},\qquad
H_b=\{z\in U:\rho_b(z_I)=1\text{ or }\gamma_b(z_J)=0\}.
$$

If the agreeing bit is $0$, then $E_b$ contains the row cylinder $R_p$ and $H_b$ contains the column cylinder $C_q$. If it is $1$, the roles reverse. Thus both endpoints belong to $\mathcal F_a$.

The intersection contains neither whole cylinder. For example, within a fixed row $p$, the points whose column code has the value opposite $\rho_b(p)$ are excluded from one endpoint. There are exactly $2^{s-1}$ full-cube points in that half-row; since fewer than this many points are outside $U$, at least one remains in $U$. The analogous half-column has $2^{r-1}$ points. With at most $t$ punctures, the strict condition $L+t<\min\{2^{r-1},2^{s-1}\}$ leaves at least one such point in each witness. Therefore $E_b\cap H_b$ contains neither generating witness, so it is not in $\mathcal F_a$. The two values of $b$ cover all anchors. $\square$

For the Gap-MCSP high-complexity universe with $L\le2^{O(N^\beta\log N)}$, this theorem applies whenever both block sizes exceed $O(N^\beta\log N)$, even if the blocks are unequal. It also rules out deleting any fixed number of points from those cylinders as a rescue. A two-cylinder construction can evade this result only when one block is $O(N^\beta\log N)$ or the witnesses are sparse enough to omit all of the code's opposite half-row/half-column completions.

The punctured-cylinder experiment [experiment_punctured_cylinder_anchor_filters.py](experiment_punctured_cylinder_anchor_filters.py) tested 500 fixed and 500 anchor-dependent block/puncture choices on the six-bit weight-two shell. Exact enumeration over all witness orientations gave cover number one or two in every trial. This finite result agrees with the theorem's collapse mechanism but is not needed for its proof.

A sparse-witness stress test, [experiment_sparse_block_certificates.py](experiment_sparse_block_certificates.py), used the eight-bit weight-two shell ($|U|=28$, nine anchors) and fixed $4+4$ blocks. It sampled 100 families at each witness size $t=2,3,4$. Every exact cover number was one or two; the smallest maximum single-pair coverage seen was $8/9$. Rather than enumerate all endpoint subsets, the script enumerates $3^M$ assignments per anchor (uncovered, first witness on the left, or second witness on the left). This is exact for filters with exactly two minimal witnesses: any pair covering such a filter must place its two witnesses on opposite endpoints, and shrinking the endpoints to unions of those witnesses preserves rejection of the intersection. These small random tests do not rule out structured sparse certificates.

### Proposition 18.21 - two-minimum cover search reduces to witness orientations

Let $\mathcal F_1,\ldots,\mathcal F_M$ be semi-filters, each with exactly two minimal members $A_j,B_j$. For any endpoint pair $(E,H)$ and every filter it covers, the two minima must be split across the endpoints: one lies in $E$ and the other in $H$. They cannot both lie in one endpoint, since the other accepted endpoint would then share a minimal member and their intersection would be accepted.

Consequently, enumerate $3^M$ assignments, giving each filter one of three states: unused, $A_j$ in the left endpoint and $B_j$ in the right, or the reverse. For each assignment, form the endpoints as the unions of the assigned minimal members and compute its actual coverage mask. This finite list exactly suffices to compute the maximum coverage of one pair and the family cover number. Given any arbitrary pair, orient the two minima for each filter it covers and discard all other filters; the resulting union endpoints are subsets of the original endpoints. Since the original intersection is rejected and the filter is upward closed, the smaller intersection is also rejected. The constructed pair therefore still covers all selected filters and may cover more. Conversely every enumerated orientation gives an actual pair, so the best cover obtainable from this list equals the unrestricted optimum.

The search is exponential in the number $M$ of selected filters but independent of enumerating all $2^{|U|}$ endpoints. It is exact only under the two-minimum hypothesis; larger antichains require enumerating witness choices or a separate compression theorem. The punctured and sparse-cylinder experiments use this reduction.

### Proposition 18.22 - an arbitrary pair covers cylinder anchors by two rectangles

Assume each selected filter is generated by a nonempty row cylinder $R_p$ and column cylinder $C_q$. For an endpoint set $W\subseteq U$, put

$$
\mathsf R(W)=\{p:R_p\subseteq W\},\qquad
\mathsf C(W)=\{q:C_q\subseteq W\}.
$$

For an arbitrary endpoint pair $(E,H)$, its covered anchor-pattern pairs are exactly the selected $(p,q)$ in

$$
\bigl(\mathsf R(E)\setminus\mathsf R(H)\bigr)
\times
\bigl(\mathsf C(H)\setminus\mathsf C(E)\bigr)
\quad\cup\quad
\bigl(\mathsf R(H)\setminus\mathsf R(E)\bigr)
\times
\bigl(\mathsf C(E)\setminus\mathsf C(H)\bigr).
$$

Indeed, both endpoints must contain opposite generators, and the intersection rejects the filter exactly when it contains neither whole row nor whole column. The displayed rectangles need not be subsets of the anchor relation: covering non-anchor pattern pairs has no cost. Therefore a standard biclique-cover lower bound on the anchor relation alone does not prove a fusion-cover lower bound. A successful use of this representation must enforce a soundness condition on the rectangles or exploit the constraints that row and column cylinders be fully contained in actual endpoints.

### Proposition 18.23 - a distance-sensitive literal-pair cover for two-minimum anchored filters

Let $U\subseteq\{0,1\}^N$, let $a_1,\ldots,a_M\notin U$, and suppose each semi-filter is generated by exactly two nonempty minimal witnesses $S_i,T_i$. Assume it is above its anchor: for every coordinate $k$, the literal slice

$$
L_{i,k}=\{z\in U:z_k=(a_i)_k\}
$$

belongs to $\mathcal F_i$. Then the family has a cover using at most $4N^2$ pairs, each endpoint itself a literal slice. More quantitatively, if every anchor is at Hamming distance at least $\Delta\ge1$ from every point of $U$, then it has a cover using at most

$$
4\left\lceil\frac{N(N-1)}{\Delta^2}(\ln(M+1)+1)\right\rceil
$$

literal pairs.

#### Proof

For each $i$ and coordinate $k$, select one of $S_i,T_i$ contained in $L_{i,k}$. Assign $k$ to $I_i$ if $S_i$ is selected and to $J_i$ if $T_i$ is selected, breaking ties arbitrarily. These disjoint sets partition $[N]$, and

$$
S_i\subseteq C(a_i,I_i),\qquad T_i\subseteq C(a_i,J_i),
$$

where $C(a,I)=\{z\in U:z_I=a_I\}$. Define the active coordinates

$$
X_i=\{d\in J_i:S_i\nsubseteq L_{i,d}\},\qquad
Y_i=\{c\in I_i:T_i\nsubseteq L_{i,c}\}.
$$

Every point of $S_i$ differs from $a_i$ on at least $\Delta$ coordinates. It agrees on $I_i$, so $|X_i|\ge\Delta$. Similarly, $|Y_i|\ge\Delta$. For any $c\in Y_i$ and $d\in X_i$, the pair $(L_{i,c},L_{i,d})$ covers $\mathcal F_i$: the first endpoint contains $S_i$, the second contains $T_i$, while $S_i\nsubseteq L_{i,d}$ and $T_i\nsubseteq L_{i,c}$. Their intersection therefore contains neither minimal witness and is rejected.

For the deterministic $4N^2$ bound, include every ordered coordinate pair $(c,d)$ and all four choices of endpoint signs. For the quantitative bound, sample $r=\lceil N(N-1)(\ln(M+1)+1)/\Delta^2\rceil$ ordered distinct coordinate pairs uniformly and include all four sign pairs for each sample. Since $I_i$ and $J_i$ are disjoint, each sample has probability at least $|Y_i||X_i|/(N(N-1))\ge\Delta^2/(N(N-1))$ of selecting a pair from $Y_i\times X_i$; among its four sign choices, one matches $a_i$. The chance a fixed filter is missed is at most $e^{-r\Delta^2/(N(N-1))}$. A union bound over $M$ filters leaves positive probability that none is missed. $\square$

For the project promise, patching the at most $d$ truth-table positions on which a low-circuit anchor and a table $z\in U$ differ gives a circuit for $z$ of size at most $\mathrm{CC}(a)+O(d\log N)$. With thresholds $s_1=N^\beta/(c\log N)$ and $s_2=N^\beta$, this implies $\Delta=\Omega(N^\beta/\log N)$. Since $M\le2^{O(N^\beta)}$, Proposition 18.23 yields

$$
\rho_{\text{two-min}}=O(N^{2-\beta}\log^2 N).
$$

This is sub-$N^{1+\varepsilon}$ when $\beta>1-\varepsilon$ (with a fixed exponent gap to absorb the logarithm). It narrows but does not close the regime of small $\beta$ or filters with three or more minimal witnesses. The estimate is an actual cover of the full two-minimum filters, not merely of the canonical endpoint strategy, because every constructed literal pair is a valid fusion pair.

The related experiment [experiment_variable_partition_sparse_witnesses.py](experiment_variable_partition_sparse_witnesses.py) used the full 8-bit cube minus nine anchors at pairwise distance at least three. It sampled 35 sparse witness families for each of six settings (block sizes $2+6$, $3+5$, or $4+4$, and witness sizes two or three). All 210 unrestricted exact covers had size one. The literal-slice-only cover sizes were two to five; by restricting endpoints to literal slices, the test isolates how arbitrary subsets of $U$ can compress coordinate-pair signatures. The toy universe is dense but is not a high-circuit-complexity promise, so these counts are finite evidence only.

This reduction is adjacent to established rectangle-hitting and biclique-cover work, including bicolored rectangular-family hitting sets and cover-free families; those results do not by themselves supply a lower bound against arbitrary fusion endpoints. See [Independent sets and hitting sets of bicolored rectangular families](https://arxiv.org/abs/1411.2311) and [Some new bounds for cover-free families through biclique covers](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.disc.2012.08.016).

### Proposition 18.24 - affine-grid filters still have a one-pair cover on the dense promise

Let $q=2^k>2$, and let $\pi:\{0,1\}^N\to\mathbb F_q^2$ read two disjoint $k$-bit coordinate blocks as field elements. Assume every partial assignment to at most $2k+1$ truth-table positions has a completion in $U$. Choose $X,Y\subseteq\mathbb F_q\setminus\{0^k,1^k\}$, where $0^k$ and $1^k$ denote the all-zero and all-one bit strings. For each $(x,y)\in X\times Y$, choose an anchor $a_{xy}\notin U$ whose projection is $(x,y)$. Generate $\mathcal F_{xy}$ by all anchor literals

$$
L_{xy,j}=\{z\in U:z_j=(a_{xy})_j\},\qquad j\in[N],
$$

and all line fibers $P_{xy,L}=\{z\in U:\pi(z)\in L\}$ for affine lines $L$ through $(x,y)$. Then these anchored semi-filters have a one-pair cover.

#### Proof

Set

$$
E=\{z\in U:\pi_x(z)\in X\},\qquad H=\{z\in U:\pi_y(z)\in Y\}.
$$

For each anchor $(x,y)$, $E$ contains the vertical line fiber through $(x,y)$, and $H$ contains the horizontal line fiber through it. Thus both endpoints belong to $\mathcal F_{xy}$. Their intersection is

$$
D=E\cap H=\{z\in U:\pi(z)\in X\times Y\}.
$$

The grid $X\times Y$ contains no complete affine line: a vertical line meets it in at most $|Y|<q$ points, a horizontal line in at most $|X|<q$, and every other line in at most $\min(|X|,|Y|)<q$. Hence every line fiber generator has a projection point outside the grid, and the completion assumption supplies a member of $U$ above that point. No line fiber is contained in $D$.

Each literal generator also has a member outside $D$. If its coordinate is in the $x$-block, choose an $x'\notin X$ from $\{0^k,1^k\}$ whose relevant bit matches $a_{xy}$, and keep $y'=y$. The $y$-block case is symmetric. For any other coordinate, take $x'=0^k\notin X$, keep $y'=y$, and fix that extra bit to its anchor value. In each case at most $2k+1$ bits are fixed, so the completion assumption gives a point of $U$ in the literal but outside $D$. Therefore $D$ contains none of the generating sets and is rejected by $\mathcal F_{xy}$. The same pair $(E,H)$ covers every anchor. $\square$

For the Gap-MCSP universe with $N=2^n$, fixed $\beta<1$, and $k=O(\log N)$, the completion assumption follows from $|\{0,1\}^N\setminus U|\le2^{O(N^\beta\log N)}<2^{N-2k-1}$. Anchors can encode $(x,y)$ in the selected $2k$ truth-table positions and be zero elsewhere; they have circuits of size $O(k\log N)=O(\log^2N)$, below $s_1=N^\beta/(c\log N)$ for large $N$. Taking $X=Y=\mathbb F_q\setminus\{0^k,1^k\}$ gives $(q-2)^2$ anchors, which exceeds $N^{1+\varepsilon}$ for any fixed $\varepsilon>0$ by choosing $k=c\log_2N$ with $2c>1+\varepsilon$. The construction therefore meets the anchor and promise-density conditions at the magnification scale but collapses to one pair. The finite verification [experiment_affine_grid_literal_collapse.py](experiment_affine_grid_literal_collapse.py) checks $q=4$, four anchors, 20 line generators and 32 literal generators over $U=\{0,1\}^8$ with four anchors removed; its intersection is nonempty but contains none of those generators.

This is a proof that this affine-grid incidence family fails as a lower-bound construction, including after all required literal generators are added. It is not a P-versus-NP result.

### Proposition 18.25 - affine line-pencil filters in every fixed dimension have an O(d) cover

Let $q>d\ge2$ be a prime power, and let $\pi:U\to\mathbb F_q^d$ read $d$ disjoint $k$-bit blocks, where $q=2^k$. Assume every partial assignment to at most $dk+1$ truth-table positions has a completion in $U$. For each anchor $p\in Y\subseteq\mathbb F_q^d$, generate $\mathcal F_p$ by all affine line fibers through $p$ and all matching bit-literal generators of an anchor $a_p$ with $\pi(a_p)=p$. Then the family has a cover of at most $d+1$ endpoint pairs.

Choose, for each coordinate $j\in[d]$, distinct levels $\gamma_{0,j},\ldots,\gamma_{d,j}\in\mathbb F_q$. For $t=0,\ldots,d$, define

$$
E_t=\{z\in U: \pi_j(z)\ne\gamma_{t,j}\text{ for }1\le j<d\},\qquad
H_t=\{z\in U: \pi_d(z)\ne\gamma_{t,d}\}.
$$

For a fixed anchor $p$, at most one index $t$ is excluded by each of the $d$ equations $p_j=\gamma_{t,j}$. Since there are $d+1$ indices, some $t$ satisfies $p_j\ne\gamma_{t,j}$ for every $j$. For this $t$, $E_t$ contains the line fiber through $p$ in direction $e_d$, and $H_t$ contains the line fiber through $p$ in direction $e_1$. Thus both endpoints belong to $\mathcal F_p$.

Their intersection $D_t=E_t\cap H_t$ contains no line generator. Given any nonzero direction $v$, if some $v_j\ne0$ for $j<d$, the line $p+\lambda v$ meets the hyperplane $x_j=\gamma_{t,j}$ for a suitable $\lambda$; otherwise $v_d\ne0$ and it meets $x_d=\gamma_{t,d}$. At the resulting projected point, fixing the $dk$ projection bits still leaves a completion in $U$, so the corresponding line fiber has a member outside $D_t$.

Nor does $D_t$ contain a matching literal generator. For a literal in projection block $j$, fix that whole block to $p_j$ and fix a different projection block $h$ to its blocker level $\gamma_{t,h}$; this uses $2k$ bits and gives a completion outside $D_t$. For a filler-bit literal, fix that bit and block 1 to $\gamma_{t,1}$, using at most $k+1$ bits. The density assumption supplies the required members of $U$. Hence $D_t\notin\mathcal F_p$, and the pair covers $\mathcal F_p$. The same $d+1$ pairs work for every anchor. $\square$

In the Gap-MCSP regime, taking $dk=O(\log N)$ makes the completion assumption follow from the excluded-set bound $2^{O(N^\beta\log N)}<2^{N-dk-1}$ for fixed $\beta<1$. Anchors hardwiring their $dk$ projected bits have circuit size $O(dk\log N)$, below the low-circuit threshold for fixed $d$. Even when $q^d$ exceeds the magnification anchor count, this family has cover number at most $d+1$; for fixed dimension this is constant. This closes affine line pencils in all dimensions as a route to a large fusion-cover lower bound. The result is construction-specific and does not bound arbitrary semi-filters or imply a P-versus-NP separation.

The finite checker [experiment_affine_pencil_higher_dimension.py](experiment_affine_pencil_higher_dimension.py) verifies the generator containments and the four-pair cover for every anchor of a $3$-dimensional $\mathbb F_4$ projection toy. The finite instance is illustrative; the theorem follows from the blocking-coordinate argument and the stated completion promise.

### Proposition 18.26 - affine r-flat pencils have a d-r+2 cover

Let $1\le r<d$, put $m=d-r$, and assume $q=2^k\ge m+2$. Let $\pi:U\to\mathbb F_q^d$ read $d$ disjoint $k$-bit blocks, and assume every partial assignment to at most $dk+1$ truth-table positions has a completion in $U$. For each anchor $p\in Y\subseteq\mathbb F_q^d$, generate $\mathcal F_p$ from every affine $r$-flat fiber through $p$ and every matching bit-literal generator of an anchor table projecting to $p$. Then $d-r+2$ endpoint pairs cover the family.

For each $t=0,\ldots,m+1$, choose distinct levels $\gamma_{t,j}$ in every coordinate and define $E_t$ to exclude the $m$ hyperplanes $x_j=\gamma_{t,j}$ for $1\le j\le m$, while $H_t$ excludes the single hyperplane $x_{m+1}=\gamma_{t,m+1}$. Every anchor avoids all $m+1$ blocker levels for at least one of the $m+2$ indices. At that index, $E_t$ contains the $r$-flat through $p$ parallel to $\operatorname{span}(e_{m+1},\ldots,e_d)$, and $H_t$ contains an $r$-flat through $p$ whose direction lies in the coordinate hyperplane $v_{m+1}=0$. Both endpoints are therefore accepted.

The intersection excludes the union of the $m+1$ blocker hyperplanes. Their common direction kernel has dimension $d-(m+1)=r-1$. Hence the direction space of any $r$-flat through $p$ contains a vector varying in at least one blocked coordinate; the flat meets that blocker hyperplane and is not contained in the intersection. Fixing the $dk$ projected bits at such a point and using density supplies a high-table member outside the intersection. A matching literal generator also escapes: fix its projection block to $p_j$ and a different one of the at least two blocked projection coordinates to its blocker level; for a filler bit, fix that bit and one blocked block. At most $dk+1$ positions are assigned, so density supplies the completion. Thus the intersection is rejected by every $\mathcal F_p$, and the $m+2=d-r+2$ pairs cover all anchors. $\square$

For fixed $d,r$ and $dk=O(\log N)$, the completion and low-circuit anchor arguments from Proposition 18.25 apply unchanged. The finite verifier [experiment_affine_plane_pencils_AG3_F4.py](experiment_affine_plane_pencils_AG3_F4.py) checks all 64 anchors, all 21 affine planes per anchor, all eight literals, and the three-pair cover over a dense $U\subseteq\{0,1\}^8$. This closes affine flat pencils as a broad geometric family; it does not address nonlinear incidence generators or general semi-filters.

### Finite test - affine point-pencil filters

The incidence-geometry experiment [experiment_affine_pencil_anchor_filters.py](experiment_affine_pencil_anchor_filters.py) constructs anchored filters with more than two minimal witnesses. In $AG(2,q)$, choose anchors on the parabola $y=x^2$, remove those points from $U$, and let each anchor filter be generated by all $q+1$ affine lines through it. One-hot bits for affine-form/value pairs provide the literal coordinates: the matching side for each bit contains an incident line. Thus the anchor condition holds exactly in the toy. For $q=3$ with three anchors, the orientation search finds a full-cover pair after 19 of 2,197 possible assignments. For $q=5$, it finds one after 604 of 923,521 assignments with four anchors, and after 1,197 of 28,629,151 assignments with five anchors. The five-anchor sample already uses every x-level, and the covering pair has a nonempty three-point intersection that contains no line witness. These explicit pairs certify cover number one; the search stops on finding them. The finite toy is not a high-complexity promise result.

### Finite checks of nonweighted witness attempts

Three exhaustive toy searches stress-tested minimal-witness antichains on a 4-bit universe with five anchors. In [experiment_coordinate_certificate_toy.py](experiment_coordinate_certificate_toy.py), the coordinate-cylinder family has a one-pair cover at certificate codimensions $r=1,2$. In [experiment_dense_witness_antichains.py](experiment_dense_witness_antichains.py), 20 seeded families generated by random half-subsets of literal slices had cover number one in 18 trials and two in two trials; every trial had a pair covering at least four of the five filters. In [experiment_affine_witness_antichains.py](experiment_affine_witness_antichains.py), 20 families using independent affine slices inside the literals all had a one-pair cover; the first trial's full-covering endpoints intersect in the single point $1001$. These exact finite results reject the tested toy constructions only. They provide no asymptotic bound and do not predict the cover number on the Gap-MCSP promise.

The new [experiment_literal_plus_sparse_witnesses.py](experiment_literal_plus_sparse_witnesses.py) exhausts all 4,096 ordered endpoint pairs for each family on the six-point weight-two universe with five anchors. It sampled 100 seeded families for each number (one, two, or three per anchor) and size (two or three points) of added inclusion-minimal witnesses. With one added witness per anchor, 88/100 two-point families and 96/100 three-point families had a one-pair cover; the others had exact cover number two. With two added witnesses, the counts were 97/3 and 99/1; with three, all 200 families had a one-pair cover. Families with exact cover two had maximum single-pair coverage four. The trend is finite and nonmonotone in general: extra witnesses sometimes poison the common intersection, but additional generators also give endpoints more ways to accept filters.

The follow-up [experiment_exhaustive_sparse_witness_assignments.py](experiment_exhaustive_sparse_witness_assignments.py) checks every $15^5=759{,}375$ assignment of exactly one two-point witness to each of the five anchors. It finds 685,272 assignments with a one-pair cover and 74,103 with a two-pair cover; every assignment has some endpoint pair covering at least four of five filters, so the exact cover number is always at most two. A selected 4-bit design extended to the 5-bit weight-two shell, with six anchors and one two-point witness per filter, also has exact cover two after checking all $2^{20}=1{,}048{,}576$ ordered endpoint pairs. Six seeded random witness assignments on that six-anchor shell all had one-pair covers. The finite obstruction is real but stalls at a constant cover. It is not evidence for an asymptotic lower bound.

Changing the shell gives a stronger anchor count but the same cover obstruction. On the 10-point 5-bit weight-three universe with all 16 anchors of weight at most two, [experiment_sparse_witness_weight3_n5.py](experiment_sparse_witness_weight3_n5.py) sampled 80 one-extra-2-point-witness families and exhaustively checked all 1,048,576 endpoint pairs for each. Seventeen had cover one and 63 had cover two; in the cover-two cases the largest pair covered 14 or 15 anchors. [experiment_sparse_witness_minimax_weight3.py](experiment_sparse_witness_minimax_weight3.py) used 30,000 sampled endpoint pairs only to guide a 32-start, four-sweep minimax search. Its selected family was then checked against every endpoint pair and had exact cover two, with maximum single-pair coverage 13/16. This is a better finite obstruction to one-pair fusion, but the maximum coverage bound only gives a cover lower bound of two; a complementary pair repairs the family.

The same principle was tested on the 15-point 6-bit weight-two shell with seven anchors. [experiment_shell_n6_sparse_witness_exact.py](experiment_shell_n6_sparse_witness_exact.py) checks all $2^{30}=1{,}073{,}741{,}824$ ordered endpoint pairs in bounded-memory chunks for a structured one-extra-witness family; it has exact cover two and maximum single-pair coverage five of seven. The accompanying 120 random-greedy and 24 minimax-greedy assignments were also exactly checked; their best result was cover two with maximum coverage four of six.

The new [experiment_cegis_sparse_witness_cover3.py](experiment_cegis_sparse_witness_cover3.py) formulates the next target more directly. Its Boolean variables select one special witness per anchor. Each exact two-pair cover generates clauses forbidding witness assignments that preserve the same endpoint pairs. At the latest 24 September checkpoint, the live search had passed 180 candidate iterations and generated 2,812 clauses without finding a three-pair family or proving none exists. This search is ongoing and its partial output is not a conclusion. The lesson from the completed finite searches is that reducing the largest one-pair coverage is insufficient: the real objective is to eliminate complementary coverage masks, so no two endpoint pairs jointly cover the anchors.

### 19. Research frontier cross-check as of 23 September 2026

The latest primary-source scan adds several relevant techniques and sharpens the project map. None supplies the missing general Boolean circuit lower bound or the promise fusion-cover lower bound.

* **Remote points:** Volk's ECCC TR26-203 (22 September 2026) gives a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm over the rationals achieving optimal remoteness $n-k$, and an improved finite-field guarantee $\Omega((n/\max\{k,\log n\})\log n)$. This directly updates the project's remote-point line. It does not overcome the full-span obstruction already proved in Section 9: the low-circuit singleton truth tables span the full truth-table space, so applying a remote-point algorithm to that span gives no hard table.
* **Range avoidance and near-maximum lower bounds:** Ren and Williams, ECCC TR26-118 (July 2026), prove a near-maximum $2^n/n$ circuit lower bound for $\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{prMA}}/_1$, using iterative win-win arguments, range avoidance, and PCP methods. This is an important neighboring result, but the target class has a promise-MA oracle and one bit of advice; no transfer to NP or the current Gap-MCSP cover target is supplied.
* **Monotone matching circuits:** Rao, ECCC TR26-129 (July 2026, revised 3 September), proves $\exp(\Omega(\sqrt n))$ monotone circuit lower bounds for bipartite perfect matching from a spread-matching lemma. The result concerns monotone circuits. A general polynomial-time algorithm for matching can use nonmonotone operations, so this does not imply $\mathrm{P}\ne\mathrm{NP}$.
* **Meta-complexity under assumptions:** Goldberg, Juvekar, and Kabanets, ECCC TR26-091 (June 2026), derive conditional NP-hardness for variants of implicit MCSP and full-support PAC learning under subexponential iO and proof-system assumptions, while also ruling out certain randomized Levin reductions. This suggests a cryptography/proof-complexity bridge worth monitoring, but it remains conditional and does not prove an unconditional lower bound for standard MCSP.
* **Wire lower bounds:** Dev Nag, ECCC TR26-167 (6 September), states near-cubic almost-everywhere wire lower bounds for two depth-two threshold circuit classes and an $\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{NP}}$ language. The ECCC page also records a substantive reader comment questioning the report's exposition and relation to prior work. Because the stated circuit models and the report's status do not give a general NP circuit lower bound, this is not used in any project inference pending clarification.
* **KomlÃƒÂ³s discrepancy:** Karingula and Lovett, ECCC TR26-188 (17 September), state the constant-$36$ signing bound used in Propositions 18.2-18.6 and give an elementary proof. The project uses it only as a vector-balancing lemma; even accepting it does not address the hard promise-cover lower bound.
* **Constructive KomlÃƒÂ³s signing:** Guo, Fang, and Lu, arXiv:2609.23540 (20 September), give a deterministic algorithm for a constant-discrepancy signing using polynomially many exact real-arithmetic operations, with runtime $O((mn^9+n^{10})\log(2+m+n))$. Substituting it into Proposition 18.6 makes the four recursive splits constructive on an explicitly supplied measure matrix. The paper's model is exact real-RAM, and the dimensions $M$ and $|U|$ can be enormous in the project application; this is not a polynomial-time SAT algorithm or a standard bit-complexity result.
* **Rank-two complex discrepancy:** Guillen and Kobzar, arXiv:2609.15071v2 (revised 21 September), prove a constant bound for rank-two vector discrepancy, equivalently allowing arbitrary unit-complex phases. Those phases are not restricted to three discrete colors, so the result does not directly provide the three-part strict-majority cover needed here. It is adjacent vector-balancing progress, not a P-vs-NP consequence.
* **A claimed topological collapse of counting:** M. Alasli's [arXiv:2603.22211](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22211) claims $\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{NP}\Rightarrow\#\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{FP}$. It does not provide a valid proof. Its Lemma 3.1 asserts that every function computed by a SAT algorithm is either obtainable from bounded-radius local information or distinguishes the cases $\beta_2=0$ and $\beta_2=2^{\Omega(N)}$. Those alternatives are not exhaustive: failure to be local does not imply that a function distinguishes those two topological regimes. The later inference that a polynomial-time SAT decider must compute a $\#\mathrm{P}$-hard topological distinguisher therefore has no justification. The 2026 paper's claimed antecedent also relies on Alasli's 2025 [arXiv:2508.13200](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13200), which says every 2-SAT solution cubical complex is contractible. Under the paper's own definition, $F=(x\lor y)\land(\neg x\lor\neg y)$ has exactly the two satisfying vertices $01$ and $10$, with no edge between them, so its complex is disconnected and not contractible. The exact check is in [experiment_topology_counterexample.py](experiment_topology_counterexample.py). These are direct proof defects, not a resolution of P vs NP.

These sources update the tool inventory, not the conclusion. The unresolved proof obligation remains a representation-independent construction of many semi-filters that no short list of fusion pairs can cover, or a different explicit lower-bound route with a complete transfer to $\mathrm{P}\ne\mathrm{NP}$.

### 20. Re-audit of the proof-search deadline project notes

The local ChatGPT project mirror contains a separate finite self-reference route, developed on 14 September 2026. Fix a sound proof system $T$ extending enough arithmetic and a concrete universal search program $U_T$ that accepts only verified $T$-proofs. The diagonal sentence $D_n$ says that $U_T$ does not output a proof of $D_n$ within $2^n$ steps. Under $\mathrm{Con}(T)$, the finite-halting formalization gives $\mathrm{time}_{U_T}(D_n)>2^n$, while a formalized finite nonhalting trace gives a $T$-proof of each individual $D_n$ with the baseline upper bound $2^{O(n)}$.

The project note proves a conditional implication: if these fixed sentences had sufficiently short $T$-proofs infinitely often, specifically $\liminf_n \log_2(1+s_n)/n=0$ for minimum proof lengths $s_n$, then $\mathrm{Con}(T)$ would imply $\mathrm{P}\ne\mathrm{NP}$. The proof uses $\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{NP}$ to search for a shortest proof with time polynomial in its length, then invokes the universal-search simulation. The required short-proof condition remains open; the known $2^{O(n)}$ baseline does not meet it, and no argument shows that $\mathrm{P}\ne\mathrm{NP}$ itself implies the condition.

The paired refutation note establishes only one-sided metamathematical limitations. It does not prove the short-proof condition false in the standard natural numbers. It shows that a proposed proof through a full bounded-consistency intermediate statement would inherit a large proof-length lower bound, but there is no known size-controlled reduction from arbitrary proofs of $D_n$ back to that intermediate statement. Its oracle comparison constructs a different proof system with an oracle and added axiom scheme, where short proofs coexist with missed deadlines. That comparison rules out a generic inference from soundness and universal search alone; it does not refute the original PA-family condition.

This route is a useful conditional proof-complexity project, not a breakthrough. Its status is unchanged: an explicit family of true, provable arithmetic sentences is available, but the subexponential proof-length upper bound needed for a P-versus-NP consequence has not been established. The mirror notes cite [Krajicek on algorithmic proof search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2104.04711), [Pudlak on finite consistency proof lengths](https://users.math.cas.cz/~pudlak/fin-con.pdf), and [Barak on proof search if $\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{NP}$](https://introtcs.org/public/lec_14_PvsNP.html).

The local project mirrors available to this audit still do not contain a complete export of all private ChatGPT project conversations. This section covers the specific proof-deadline notes present in the mirror; it does not claim to cover unexported chats.

### 21. Exact preservation closure and new finite stress tests (23 September 2026)

The cylinder-generated filter experiment was repaired and rerun with exhaustive enumeration. It does not produce a difficult family. A more general closure formulation emerged from the same check and gives an exact way to test proposed cover lists against all semi-filters, rather than only against majority-measure filters.

#### Proposition 21.1 - preservation closure characterizes covers of all semi-filters

Let U be finite, and for an anchor a let G_{a,1},...,G_{a,r} be the required nonempty generators. Let Lambda be a finite list of pairs (E,H) of subsets of U. Define C_a^0 to be the upward closure of the generators and recursively set

C_a^{m+1} = up(C_a^m union {E intersection H : (E,H) in Lambda, E,H in C_a^m}).

The process stabilizes because U is finite. The list Lambda covers every semi-filter above a if and only if the stabilized family contains the empty set.

#### Proof

Every semi-filter above a contains the generators and is upward closed. If it preserves every pair in Lambda, it also contains every set added at each closure step, by induction. Therefore, if the closure reaches the empty set, no such preserving semi-filter exists and Lambda covers all of them. Conversely, if the empty set is not reached, the stabilized family itself is a nonempty upward-closed family containing U, excluding the empty set, and preserving every pair in Lambda. It is a semi-filter above a, so Lambda does not cover all such filters. QED.

This reframes an all-semi-filter cover as a short set of intersection rules that forces the forbidden empty set from the anchor generators. It also provides a canonical counterexample to any proposed list that fails: the stabilized closure itself is a semi-filter preserving the list. The finite test experiment_toy_all_semfilters.py checks this equivalence on every pair and anchor in a four-point instance.

#### Corollary 21.2 - exact criterion for a one-pair cover

Let F_a range over all semi-filters above each anchor. A single pair (E,H) covers this whole family if and only if (i) E intersection H is empty, and (ii) for every anchor a, both E and H contain at least one required generator for a.

Indeed, the maximal semi-filter {W subset of U : W is nonempty} preserves every pair with nonempty intersection, so a universal pair must be disjoint. All semi-filters above a contain a set W exactly when W contains at least one required generator. These two facts prove necessity and sufficiency.

For the Gap-MCSP bit-literal generators, this criterion rules out a one-pair cover of the entire semi-filter family once every two-coordinate anchor cylinder intersects U. Fixing at most two of the N=2^n truth-table bits leaves at least 2^(N-2) completions, while the number of tables of circuit complexity below s2=N^beta is at most 2^(O(s2 log(n+s2)))=2^o(N). Thus every intersection of two required literal slices contains a high-complexity table. Any pair that is forced into every filter above an anchor has nonempty intersection, and the maximal semi-filter preserves it. This yields only the small bound rho >= 2; it distinguishes arbitrary semi-filters from the canonical-only one-pair result, but gives no magnification-scale lower bound.

#### Exact toy instance: arbitrary semi-filters require two pairs

Take U to be the four three-bit strings of weight at least two, and anchors to be the four strings of weight at most one. Exhaustively enumerating all 166 semi-filters on U, then retaining those above each anchor's three literal generators, gives 17 anchor-filter instances. Of all 256 ordered pairs (E,H), the best single pair violates only 16 of these filters. The exact minimum cover is two, witnessed by

(E1,H1)=({011,110},{101,111}), and
(E2,H2)=({101,110},{011,111}).

Each pair hits 16 filters and together they hit all 17. The missed filter for the first pair is the upward closure of the singleton sets {011} and {110} above anchor 010; it contains E1 but not H1, and it preserves that pair. The exhaustive script also verifies the closure criterion in all 1,024 pair-anchor cases. This is a concrete witness that arbitrary semi-filters behave differently from their canonical subfamily, but the universe is too small to imply an asymptotic bound.

#### Cylinder-filter stress test: rejected

For U the eleven four-bit strings of weight at least two and Y the five strings of weight at most one, define F_{a,k} as the upward closure of every nonempty cylinder in U that fixes between one and k coordinates to the anchor values. Full enumeration over all E,H subset of U finds a one-pair cover for every k=1,2,3,4. For k>=2, one disjoint pair is E={0110,1001}, H={0101,1010}; the intersection is empty. The result rejects this particular strengthening of canonical filters. It does not address arbitrary non-measure semi-filters.

#### Literature scan: 19-23 September 2026

* [Golovnev and Gurumukhani, ECCC TR26-195, Sumset Structure in Local Computation](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/195/) introduce local decision trees and show that strong lower bounds for the general model would imply circuit lower-bound consequences, with explicit near-optimal lower bounds for an oblivious restriction. This is a useful intermediate-model direction; the required adaptive lower bounds remain open and no SAT lower bound follows from the report.
* [Li, Ren, and Zhong, ECCC TR26-196, Many Proof Complexity Generators Inside One Demi-Bits Generator](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/196/) show that random output projections of a demi-bits generator yield proof-complexity generators with constant probability, and derive range-avoidance consequences in restricted settings, including an LPN-conditional result. This is adjacent to the project's proof-search and range-avoidance routes, but does not create the needed generator or prove an unconditional separation.
* [Kayal et al., ECCC TR26-206, Certification Complexity of Boolean Functions](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/206/) develop operational certification measures for query models and derive exact/zero-error quantum query lower bounds. These are query-complexity advances, not general Boolean circuit lower bounds.
* [Carmosino, Gupta, and Volkovich, ECCC TR26-207, Towards an Interesting VPSPACE-complete Problem](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/207) construct algebraic polynomial families tied to TQBF and the permanent. Small arithmetic circuits for their families imply strong class containments or a PSPACE subset NP^PIT collapse. The implications do not follow from P=NP alone and do not resolve the Boolean problem.

As a geometry control, `experiment_toy_shell_semfilters.py` uses the five 5-bit strings of weight exactly four and the six anchors of weight at most one. It enumerates the same 7,579 valid semi-filters on its five-point universe and finds a one-pair cover for all 11 anchored filter instances. The disjoint pair is E={01111,10111}, H={11011,11101}. In this shell, every anchor has a literal generator contained in each side. This small positive control confirms that the closure test distinguishes easy and harder finite promise geometries; it does not lift to the dense high-circuit set U, where all two-coordinate anchor cylinders remain nonempty.

#### Proposition 21.3 - sparse co-singleton shells have a one-pair cover

For n >= 2r+2, let U_n={1^n-e_i : i in [n]} be the n Boolean strings with exactly one zero, and let Y_{n,r} be all anchors of Hamming weight at most r. Partition [n]=I disjoint-union J with |I|,|J| >= r+1, and set E={1^n-e_i:i in I}, H={1^n-e_i:i in J}. Then (E,H) violates every semi-filter above every anchor in Y_{n,r}.

For each coordinate i, the anchor-matching literal slice in U_n is the singleton {1^n-e_i} when a_i=0, and U_n minus that singleton when a_i=1. Since each part has more than r points, it contains a point whose zero coordinate is outside the support of a. Thus each part contains a required generator for every anchor. The two parts are disjoint, so their intersection is empty and is excluded from every semi-filter. This proves the claim for arbitrary semi-filters, not just measures. The script `experiment_toy_shell_semfilters.py` checks the generator containments for 23 parameter pairs with n from 4 through 10 and exhaustively verifies the smallest n=5, r=1 instance.

This is a valid positive theorem about a sparse toy promise, and it identifies a sufficient geometric condition for a universal disjoint pair. Its crucial singleton-slice property fails for the real Gap-MCSP universe: every one- or two-coordinate slice contains exponentially many high-circuit tables. It therefore does not transfer to the target cover lower bound.

#### Proposition 21.4 - global anchor-support form of preservation closure

Let A be a finite anchor set, let each anchor a have a family G_a of required nonempty generators in a finite universe U, and let Lambda be a list of pairs (E,H). For each subset S of U, define A_S^t to be the anchors whose preservation closure contains S at stage t. Then

 A_S^0 = {a in A : some G in G_a is a subset of S},

 A_S^(t+1) = A_S^t union the union, over (E,H) in Lambda with E intersection H a subset of S, of (A_E^t intersection A_H^t).

For every t and S, this recurrence gives exactly the same membership as running the preservation closure separately for each anchor. It suffices to store S in the relevant family {empty set} union {E,H,E intersection H : (E,H) in Lambda}, which has at most 3|Lambda|+1 members. Consequently Lambda covers every semi-filter above every anchor in A exactly when A is contained in A_empty^infinity.

The proof is direct induction: a set S enters an anchor closure either because it already did, or because some listed pair has both endpoints in that closure and its intersection is a subset of S. Taking the anchor set of this condition turns the two endpoint requirements into A_E intersection A_H. This gives a compact exact checker for a proposed cover list without enumerating all 2^|U| subsets or all semi-filters. `experiment_anchor_support_closure.py` cross-checks it against direct closure on every one- and two-rule list in the four-point model (32,896 lists), and on 2,048 seeded multi-rule lists plus all single rules in the five-point model (3,072 lists total); all tested memberships agree. The support sets may still be exponentially large when A is the low-circuit anchor family, so this is a better representation of the obstruction, not a lower bound.

#### Fractional minimax stress test - no scalable witness found

On the four-point and five-point threshold toys, a numerical linear program searched for an anchor-balanced distribution on anchored semi-filters minimizing the largest mass captured by any ordered pair. The solver returned 3/4 and 9/11, respectively. In each instance its selected support contains one filter per anchor with equal mass; independently enumerating all pairs shows the exact maxima on those supports are 3 of 4 and 9 of 11 filters. The numerical LP values have no separately supplied exact dual certificate, and these finite values do not imply an asymptotic trend. In particular, they do not give the vanishing single-pair influence needed for a fractional-cover proof at magnification scale. The code is `experiment_toy_semfilter_minimax.py`.

#### Transfer test for local decision trees - only the baseline bound

Golovnev and Gurumukhani's ECCC TR26-195 local-decision-tree model looked relevant because sufficiently strong adaptive lower bounds imply circuit lower bounds. For Gap-MCSP itself, a direct counting argument gives only the trivial depth scale. If an Ã¢â€žâ€œ-local decision tree of depth d correctly separates low-circuit tables from tables of circuit complexity at least s2, fix any accepting low-circuit table y. At most dÃ¢â€žâ€œ truth-table positions are queried on its path. Every completion agreeing with y on those positions follows the same path. There are at most 2^{O(s2 log(n+s2))} tables with circuits below s2, so the subcube of completions must have size at most that count; otherwise it contains a high-circuit table, contradicting correctness. Thus the path touches at least N-O(s2 log(n+s2)) positions and dÃ¢â€žâ€œ >= N-O(s2 log(n+s2)). For s2=N^beta with beta<1, this is (1-o(1))N/Ã¢â€žâ€œ, essentially the model's trivial N/Ã¢â€žâ€œ baseline. It gives no superconstant depth overhead and does not activate the paper's circuit-lower-bound implication. The direct argument pinpoints the failure: high circuit complexity forces a large queried-coordinate set, but this model needs overhead beyond that baseline.

#### Proposition 21.5 - firing-rule circuits compile covers into promise separators

Let the pair list be \(\Lambda=\{(E_j,H_j):j\in[m]\}\), put \(K_j=E_j\cap H_j\), and let \(b_X(a)\) indicate that at least one required generator for anchor \(a\) is contained in \(X\). Track a Boolean flag \(F_j^t(a)\) saying that rule \(j\) has fired by round \(t\), starting from \(F_j^0=0\). For every endpoint set \(X\) and for \(X=\emptyset\), define

\[
A_X^t(a)=b_X(a)\ \vee\!\bigvee_{j:K_j\subseteq X}F_j^t(a),\qquad
F_j^{t+1}(a)=F_j^t(a)\vee(A_{E_j}^t(a)\wedge A_{H_j}^t(a)).
\]

Then \(A_{\emptyset}^m(a)=1\) exactly when the preservation closure for anchor \(a\) derives the empty set. Each fired rule contributes only its fixed intersection \(K_j\), and all larger sets are covered by the subset condition in \(A_X\). At most \(m\) rule flags can change from false to true, so \(m\) synchronous rounds suffice.

In the bit-literal setting, \(b_X(a)\) is an OR of at most \(2N\) input literals: include literal \(a_i=b\) exactly when the high-table slice \(B_{i,b}\) is contained in \(X\). Hardwire these containments. There are at most \(2m\) endpoint sets; computing all bases costs \(O(Nm)\) gates. Unrolling the recurrence for \(m\) rounds, with at most \(m\) rule flags and \(O(m)\) endpoint activations per round, gives a De Morgan circuit of size \(O(Nm+m^3)\) for \(A_\emptyset^m\).

If \(\Lambda\) covers every semi-filter above each low-circuit anchor, this circuit accepts every low-circuit table. For every high-circuit table \(z\), the principal semi-filter \(\{S\subseteq U:z\in S\}\) contains every matching literal slice and preserves every pair, so the closure cannot derive empty; the circuit rejects \(z\). Thus a size-\(m\) cover yields a nonuniform circuit separator of size \(O(Nm+m^3)\). The firing recurrence matches direct closure on all 32,896 one/two-rule four-point instances and all 3,072 single/seeded multi-rule five-point instances in `experiment_anchor_support_circuit.py`. This compilation is a useful converse view of the cover certificate, but its cubic overhead is too large to yield the required superlinear lower bound from the existing magnification threshold; it also assumes the hardwired endpoint-slice containments.

#### Proposition 21.6 - exact two-pair cover at the sparse-shell boundary

Let \(n=2r+1\) with \(r\ge1\), let \(u_i=1^n-e_i\) be the n strings with exactly one zero, add the all-ones string \(v=1^n\), and take anchors \(Y=\{a:\mathrm{wt}(a)\le r\}\). For anchor a, the matching coordinate generator is \(\{u_i\}\) if \(a_i=0\), and \(U\setminus\{u_i\}\) if \(a_i=1\). The minimum universal cover list over all semi-filters has exactly two pairs.

For the upper bound, partition coordinates into I,J with \(|I|=r+1\), \(|J|=r\), and use the disjoint pair \((\{u_i:i\in I\},\{u_j:j\in J\})\). Every anchor has at least r+1 zero coordinates, so its zero set meets I; it also meets J unless its zero set is exactly I. That single exceptional anchor is covered by a second disjoint pair \((\{u_p\},\{u_q\})\) for any two distinct p,q in I. Each chosen side contains a matching singleton generator, so the pair violates every semi-filter above the covered anchor.

For the lower bound, any one universal pair must be disjoint and have both sides contain a matching generator for every anchor (Corollary 21.2). For a fixed anchor, two matching generators can be disjoint only if they are zero-slices \(\{u_i\},\{u_j\}\) at distinct zero coordinates: two one-slices intersect at v, and a one-slice intersects every different zero-slice. Hence the index sets of singleton points lying in the two sides must each hit every \((r+1)\)-subset of [n], so each has size at least r+1. They are disjoint, requiring at least 2r+2 coordinates although n=2r+1. Contradiction. Thus one pair cannot cover all anchors, while the construction above shows two suffice.

The six-point experiment (`experiment_toy_all_semfilters_6point.py`, n=5,r=2) exhaustively finds maximum single-pair anchor coverage 15/16 and verifies the explicit two-pair cover. This exact boundary result concerns a sparse promise with singleton zero-slices. It does not transfer to Gap-MCSP, where every two-coordinate literal cylinder still contains exponentially many high-circuit tables.

#### Proposition 21.7 - proof-DAG leaves give a near-linear Gap-MCSP cover lower bound

Let a universal list \(\Lambda\) have m pair rules and fix any one low-circuit anchor a. If its preservation closure derives empty, choose the first rule that fires and inserts an empty fixed intersection. Trace proofs that its two endpoints belong to the closure: each endpoint is witnessed either by one initial literal generator or by one earlier pair rule whose intersection lies inside it. Memoizing rule witnesses gives an acyclic AND-DAG with at most m rule nodes and at most two antecedents per node. The DAG is connected to its root. If it has k rule nodes and L generator leaves, it has at most 2k edges, while connectedness requires at least k+L-1; hence L<=k+1<=m+1. By induction, the intersection of those leaf generators is contained in the root pair's empty intersection, hence is empty in U.

All leaves are matching bit-literal generators for the same anchor, so they fix a consistent set Q of at most m+1 truth-table coordinates. Their intersection is exactly the high-circuit promise restricted to the subcube matching a on Q. If \(2^{N-|Q|}>M_2\), where \(M_2\) is the number of truth tables with circuit complexity below s2, that subcube contains a high-circuit table and the intersection is nonempty, contradiction. The standard circuit-counting estimate gives M_2<=2^(O(s_2 log(n+s_2))). Therefore

\[
\rho_{\mathrm{GapMCSP}}(n,\beta)\ge N-\log_2 M_2-1
=N-O(s_2\log(n+s_2))-1.
\]

For \(s_2=N^\beta\) with any fixed \(\beta<1\), the error term is \(o(N)\), giving \(\rho\ge(1-o(1))N\). This is the first size-dependent all-semi-filter lower bound in this audit, but it remains linear and falls short of the \(N^{1+\varepsilon}\) magnification threshold. The proof-DAG leaf argument is capped at N by the number of truth-table coordinates, so any stronger result must use structure beyond the number of literal leaves and must account for the arbitrary endpoints. The experiment `experiment_proof_dag_leaf_bound.py` traces all 263,168 anchor/rule-list cases for all one- and two-rule lists in the four-point toy; all 45,606 empty derivations have empty leaf intersection and at most k+1 leaves for k proof rules. This finite check validates the trace implementation, not the asymptotic theorem.

The Clay Mathematics Institute still lists P vs NP as unsolved ([official problem page](https://www.claymath.org/millennium/p-vs-np/)). No positive P-versus-NP result has been obtained here. The strongest concrete outputs are exact preservation-closure and anchor-support characterizations, firing-rule compilation, the sparse-shell boundary theorem, the near-linear Gap-MCSP cover lower bound, and the exact $2^{O(r^*)}\operatorname{poly}(N)$ affine-control SAT algorithm. Sections 23-25 rule out broad binary and q-ary Reed-Muller feature-span candidates via one-pair covers. Section 26 derives a necessary endpoint blocker bound, without strengthening the pair-cover lower bound. Section 27 records a conditional ZFC route, but no corollary independence proof and no standard-model P=NP consequence. The required superlinear all-semi-filter cover lower bound and a general small-rank guarantee remain open.

### 22. Affine branch collapse: a parameter algorithm and Rank Vertex Cover (23 September 2026)

An accessible local ChatGPT-project mirror contains a separate line, Ã¢â‚¬Å“Exact branch collapse for SAT,Ã¢â‚¬Â dated 11 September. Its source files are treated as read-only. The note's exact derivations are included here so the project audit is not limited to the nine artifacts first collected. This is a restricted algorithmic result, not a P-versus-NP breakthrough.

#### Proposition 22.1 - projected affine controls give a $2^r\operatorname{poly}(N)$ SAT algorithm

Represent a circuit using XOR, NOT, and binary AND gates, and introduce one variable per wire. Put all XOR/NOT equations and the output-equals-one condition into an affine system $A$ over $\mathbb F_2$, temporarily leaving out the AND equations. For each AND gate choose one of its two input wires; let $B$ be the set of distinct chosen wires and define $r=\dim\pi_B(\operatorname{Sol}(A))$. If $A$ is inconsistent, reject. Otherwise enumerate the $2^r$ patterns in the affine projection. For each pattern, fix the chosen wires. Every AND equation $z=x\wedge y$ is then affine: it becomes $z=0$ if its chosen input is zero, and $z=y$ (or the other input) if that input is one. Gaussian elimination decides each resulting affine system. Thus SAT is decided in $2^r\operatorname{poly}(N)$ time, where $r\le |B|\le k$ for $k$ AND gates. For any supplied choice of controls, $r$ is computable by linear algebra. This includes the elementary $2^k\operatorname{poly}(N)$ bound obtained by choosing one input per AND gate and branching on its value.

This parameterized algorithm assumes a control set is supplied; Section 22.3 below gives an FPT method in the optimum rank to find one. It does not recover XOR structure when the input is an arbitrary CNF; the affine representation is supplied by the circuit basis. If the minimum projected control rank is $O(\log N)$, the combined control search and decision algorithm is polynomial, but no uniform logarithmic-rank bound is known for all circuits.

#### Proposition 22.2 - minimum projected control rank is NP-hard to find

Given a simple graph $G=(V,E)$ with at least one edge, create primary input bits $x_v$ and one AND gate $y_{uv}=x_u\wedge x_v$ for every edge. XOR all $y_{uv}$ values and require the circuit output to be one. In the affine system $A$, the primary input bits occur in no constraints: $A$ only constrains the AND-output wires through the XOR accumulator and output requirement. Therefore, for any selected control set $B$ of primary-input wires, $\pi_B(\operatorname{Sol}(A))=\mathbb F_2^B$ and its dimension is $|B|$.

Choosing one input of each AND gate selects one endpoint of each edge, so the chosen wire set $B$ is a vertex cover. Conversely, any vertex cover permits a choice of one endpoint for each edge. Hence the minimum possible projected rank is exactly the minimum vertex-cover size $\tau(G)$. The graph circuit is acyclic and every AND gate lies in the output cone. Thus exact minimization of $r$ is NP-hard, even for this restricted family. In particular, a graph of $k$ disjoint edges gives $r^*=k$ for a circuit with $O(k)$ gates; the parameter has no universal logarithmic upper bound. This only rules out treating optimal control selection as a free polynomial preprocessing step; it does not rule out good heuristics, approximation, or parameterized algorithms.

The independent finite check in [experiment_branch_rank_vertex_cover.py](experiment_branch_rank_vertex_cover.py) enumerates all 1,098 simple graphs on two through five vertices and all 59,808 control selections; every minimum projected rank agrees with brute-force vertex-cover number. The general equality follows from the reduction above, not from finite extrapolation. Classical NP-hardness remains compatible with FPT tractability when parameterized by the optimum rank; the next proposition makes that distinction explicit.

#### Proposition 22.3 - the control search is Rank Vertex Cover and is FPT in rank

First replace each AND gate with identical inputs, $z=x\wedge x$, by its equivalent affine equation $z=x$ and include that equation in $A$.
