Assume the resulting affine system is consistent, and write its solution set as $x_0+L$, where $L=\ker A\le\mathbb F_2^W$ and $W$ is the set of circuit wires. Choose a basis $v_1,\ldots,v_d$ of $L$. Associate to each wire $w$ the vector $c_w=((v_1)_w,\ldots,(v_d)_w)\in\mathbb F_2^d$. For every wire set $B$,

\[
\dim\pi_B(\operatorname{Sol}(A))=\operatorname{rank}\{c_w:w\in B\}.
\]

Construct a graph whose vertices are the wire indices and whose edges are the two distinct input wires of each remaining AND gate; duplicate input-pairs can be merged as edges. Choosing at least one endpoint of every edge is exactly choosing one control wire per remaining AND gate. Consequently, minimizing the projected affine rank is precisely Rank Vertex Cover for this represented binary matroid.

For completeness, a direct decision algorithm at rank budget $q$ keeps a basis for the span of already selected control vectors. If some gate edge $(u,v)$ has both $c_u,c_v$ outside the current span, branch by adding either endpoint. Each branch increases the span dimension by one. If no such edge remains, every gate has an endpoint in the span, so those endpoints form a vertex cover of rank at most $q$. Conversely, if a rank-$q$ cover exists, at every uncovered edge at least one endpoint belongs to that cover; choosing that endpoint maintains the invariant that the current span is contained in the cover's span. The recursion has depth at most $q$ and at most $2^{q+1}-1$ nodes. Iterative deepening finds a minimum-rank cover in $2^{O(r^*)}\operatorname{poly}(N)$ time.

Once this cover supplies one control per AND gate, Proposition 22.1 checks the $2^{r^*}$ affine projection patterns and solves each slice by Gaussian elimination. Thus the entire SAT algorithm, including control discovery, runs in $2^{O(r^*)}\operatorname{poly}(N)$ time. This closes the previous implementation gap when $r^*$ is small, while retaining an exponential worst case when $r^*$ grows.

#### Solver stress test and literature context

The mirror's solver combines Gaussian elimination, local gate-consistency propagation, exact branching, and an optional quadratic cancellation pass. I imported it read-only and independently checked 5,000 random affine/AND systems with 1-8 variables, up to 10 affine equations and up to 8 AND constraints. Both the quadratic and non-quadratic variants matched exhaustive assignment enumeration in all 10,000 comparisons: 1,788 systems were satisfiable and 3,212 unsatisfiable. The rank-cover brancher in [experiment_affine_control_rank_fpt.py](experiment_affine_control_rank_fpt.py) separately matched exhaustive minima for all 1,098 identity-matroid graphs through five vertices, all 16,384 pairs of simple four-vertex graphs and binary vector representations, and 1,000 random represented-matroid instances. The integrated affine-control SAT solver in [experiment_affine_control_rank_sat.py](experiment_affine_control_rank_sat.py) matched exhaustive SAT on 2,500 additional random small systems; it also solved shared-control examples with 8, 64, and 256 AND gates at projected rank one, using three branch nodes in each. These are finite implementation checks, not asymptotic evidence.

Rank Vertex Cover itself is established parameterized-complexity literature. Meesum, Panolan, Saurabh, and Zehavi use it in algebraic compression ([paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/1705.02822)); a 2024 ESA paper explicitly notes that Rank Vertex Cover is folklore FPT when parameterized by rank ([paper](https://drops.dagstuhl.de/storage/00lipics/lipics-vol308-esa2024/LIPIcs.ESA.2024.17/LIPIcs.ESA.2024.17.pdf)). The new project-level step is the identification of the affine projection parameter with that known problem and its composition with the affine-slice SAT algorithm; the FPT branching principle itself is not claimed as new.

The direction also connects to SAT work on backdoors and decomposition. Gaspers and Szeider combine strong backdoors with bounded treewidth under fixed structural parameters ([paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/1204.6233)); Johannsen studies parameterized hardness of detecting backdoors into tractable SAT classes ([paper](https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.3233/SAT-200125)). These are adjacent framework results, not direct evidence for a P-versus-NP separation.

The remaining issue is the value of $r^*$ on unrestricted circuits and whether useful native affine structure can be recovered from arbitrary SAT encodings. No universal small-rank theorem, polynomial-time all-SAT algorithm, or P-versus-NP consequence emerged. This is an exact parameterized algorithm for a structured slice of SAT, not a breakthrough resolving the open problem.

#### Related witness-isolation barrier (September 2026)

Ben Daniel's ECCC TR26-201 studies randomized polynomial-size pruning procedures that try to isolate one satisfying witness. Under indistinguishability obfuscation (iO), success at least $a/\log L$ on every nonempty affine-subspace input of description length $L$ implies $\mathrm{NP}\subseteq\mathrm{P/poly}$; iO together with nonuniformly secure one-way functions therefore rules out such an isolator. In a separate membership-query-only model, subexponential query budgets still permit only $\Theta(1/n)$ worst-case isolation probability on affine targets. This is directly relevant to proposals that would turn affine solution structure into a generic witness-picking routine, but it is not an unconditional P-versus-NP result and does not apply to the exact $2^{O(r^*)}\operatorname{poly}(N)$ decision algorithm above, which neither isolates a witness nor runs in polynomial time for general $r^*$. See [Ben Daniel, ECCC TR26-201](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/201/).

### 23. Reed-Muller feature-span filters have a one-pair cover (23 September 2026)

The affine-span filter candidate extends to a much larger feature space using low-degree Boolean polynomials. This gives a negative result for a broad algebraic family, not a P-versus-NP breakthrough.

Let $\Omega=\{0,1\}^N$, let $A=\Omega\setminus U$ be all tables below the high-complexity threshold $s_2$, and suppose $|A|\le M=2^{O(s_2\log(n+s_2))}=2^{o(N)}$ for $s_2=N^\beta$, fixed $\beta<1$. The low-circuit anchor set $Y=\{a:\operatorname{CC}_n(a)\le s_1\}$ is a subset of $A$. For an integer $d=d(N)<N$, let $\phi_d(x)$ be the vector of all squarefree monomials of degree at most $d$ evaluated at $x$, with dimension $D=\sum_{j=0}^d\binom Nj$. The proof below applies when $M<2^{N-1-d}$ and $D+1<2^{N-d}-M$; both inequalities hold for every $d=o(N)$. For fixed $d/N\to\alpha<1/2$, the second also holds whenever $H_2(\alpha)<1-\alpha$, where $H_2$ is binary entropy. The equality $H_2(\alpha_*)=1-\alpha_*$ occurs at $\alpha_*\approx0.22709$, so the estimate covers every fixed $\alpha<\alpha_*$. For each low-circuit anchor $a\in Y$, define
\[
\mathcal F_{a,d}=\{S\subseteq U:\phi_d(a)\in\operatorname{aff}_{\mathbb F_2}(\phi_d(S))\},
\]
with the affine hull of the empty set taken to be empty. These are semi-filters: they are upward closed and exclude the empty set.

Every required bit-literal generator $G_{i,a_i}=U\cap\{x:x_i=a_i\}$ belongs to $\mathcal F_{a,d}$. Otherwise an affine hyperplane separates $\phi_d(a)$ from $\phi_d(G_{i,a_i})$, yielding a nonzero degree-$d$ Boolean polynomial that is $1$ at $a$ and vanishes on all high-circuit tables in that coordinate slice. Its restriction to the slice is a nonzero word of $\mathrm{RM}(d,N-1)$, whose weight is at least $2^{N-1-d}$; its support would have to lie inside $A$, contradicting $M<2^{N-1-d}$. Likewise, $\phi_d(U)$ has the same affine span as $\phi_d(\Omega)$ because any nonzero degree-$d$ polynomial has weight at least $2^{N-d}>M$. The Reed-Muller minimum-distance formula is classical; see [Delsarte, Goethals, and MacWilliams (1970)](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0019-9958(70)90214-7).

Write $r=D-1$ for the affine dimension of the feature image. Every proper affine hyperplane misses at least $\delta=2^{N-d}-M$ points of $U$. Color each point of $U$ independently with one of two colors, forming a partition $U=E\sqcup H$. There are at most $2^{r+1}=2^D$ affine hyperplanes. For a specified color class and one hyperplane, the probability that all points outside it receive that color is at most $2^{-\delta}$. A union bound over both colors and all hyperplanes gives failure probability at most $2^{D+1-\delta}<1$, since $D=2^{o(N)}$ while $\delta=2^{N-o(N)}$. Thus a partition exists for which both $\phi_d(E)$ and $\phi_d(H)$ span the full feature space. Every low anchor's feature vector lies in each affine span, so $E,H\in\mathcal F_{a,d}$ for every $a\in Y$, while $E\cap H=\emptyset\notin\mathcal F_{a,d}$. One pair therefore covers the entire low-degree feature-span family.

The experiment `experiment_affine_span_filter_failure.py` checks the affine case on all 696 excluded sets of sizes one through three in a four-bit cube, with every literal slice and a disjoint full-span partition verified. The Reed-Muller experiment `experiment_low_degree_span_filter.py` uses the degree-two feature map on an eight-bit cube (37 monomials): it checks all 256 single-anchor excluded sets and 512 seeded excluded sets of sizes two or three. Every tested coordinate slice contains its anchor in feature span, and every tested high-table set admits a disjoint pair of full-span parts. The asymptotic claim follows from minimum distance and the probabilistic union bound, not from these finite checks.

This rules out affine and Reed-Muller feature-span filters satisfying the stated distance-versus-dimension conditions as sources of a cover lower bound. In particular it covers every $d=o(N)$ and every fixed degree fraction below $\alpha_*\approx0.22709$; the entropy cutoff is where this random-coloring proof stops, not a boundary for the filter family. Section 24 gives a stronger construction that covers every fixed degree fraction below $1/2$. Arbitrary semi-filters need not arise from a feature-space span, and constructing an uncovered semi-filter against every short pair list remains open.

### 24. Affine-orbit interpolation extends the feature-span cover below half degree (23 September 2026)

The random-coloring proof in Section 23 pays for all feature-space hyperplanes. A different pair construction avoids that cost and reaches the natural dimension range below half degree.

Let $\Omega=\{0,1\}^N$, let $A=\Omega\setminus U$ have size $M$, and let $0\le d<N/2$. Set $D=\sum_{j=0}^d\binom Nj$ and use the same degree-$d$ evaluation map $\phi_d$. Suppose
\[
2MD<2^N.
\]
Then the family $\mathcal F_{a,d}=\{S\subseteq U:\phi_d(a)\in\operatorname{aff}_{\mathbb F_2}(\phi_d(S))\}$, for any anchor set $Y\subseteq A$, has a disjoint pair $E,H\in\bigcap_{a\in Y}\mathcal F_{a,d}$, provided the required literal generators are included as in Section 23.

For the construction, define $B_0=\{x\in\Omega:|x|\le d\}$ and $B_1=\{x\in\Omega:|x|\ge N-d\}$. Each has size $D$ and they are disjoint because $2d<N$. The evaluation matrix of the monomial basis on $B_0$, indexed by subsets $S,T\subseteq[N]$ of size at most $d$, has entry $1[T\subseteq S]$. Ordered by inclusion, it is triangular with diagonal one, so $B_0$ is unisolvent: its feature vectors form a basis of $\mathbb F_2^D$. Complementation $x\mapsto x+\mathbf 1$ preserves the degree-$d$ polynomial space, so $B_1$ is unisolvent too. Every invertible affine map $T(x)=Lx+b$ also acts invertibly on that polynomial space, hence $T(B_0)$ and $T(B_1)$ remain disjoint unisolvent sets.

Choose $T$ uniformly from the affine group. For each fixed excluded point $a\in A$, $T^{-1}(a)$ is uniform on $\Omega$, so $\Pr[a\in T(B_0)\cup T(B_1)]=2D/2^N$. A union bound over $A$ gives probability at most $2MD/2^N<1$ that either image meets $A$. Thus some $T$ yields $E=T(B_0)$ and $H=T(B_1)$ inside $U$. Each feature set spans the full linear feature space. Since every feature vector has constant coordinate one, linear representations of $\phi_d(a)$ have coefficient sum one and therefore are affine representations. Consequently $E,H$ belong to every $\mathcal F_{a,d}$, while $E\cap H=\emptyset$ is excluded.

The condition $2MD<2^N$ also implies $M<2^{N-1-d}$, because $D\ge2^d$. Thus the literal-generator argument from Section 23 applies: an affine separator would restrict to a nonzero degree-$d$ polynomial on an $(N-1)$-dimensional slice, whose Reed-Muller weight is at least $2^{N-1-d}>M$. In the project regime $M=2^{o(N)}$, the condition holds whenever $d/N\to\alpha<1/2$, since $D=2^{H_2(\alpha)N+o(N)}$ and $H_2(\alpha)<1$. It also holds for $d=o(N)$. More generally, this proof applies whenever $2MD<2^N$ and $2d<N$; it makes no claim at or above half degree.

The experiment `experiment_affine_orbit_unisolvent_pair.py` checked 208 seeded excluded-set instances for $N=4$ through $7$ and degrees one or two. In every case it found disjoint transformed interpolation sets avoiding $A$, verified both feature matrices have full rank, checked every anchor lies in both affine hulls, and tested all matching coordinate-literal generators. The finite checks validate the implementation; the proof is the interpolation and affine-group argument above.

This strengthens the project-level negative result for low-degree feature-span candidates from the earlier entropy cutoff $\alpha_*\approx0.22709$ to every fixed $\alpha<1/2$. The ingredients are classical interpolation and Reed-Muller distance together with an affine-orbit averaging argument. The result rules out this candidate family as a source of a cover lower bound; it does not bound the full cover complexity, prove a circuit lower bound, or resolve P versus NP. The remaining challenge is still to produce an uncovered semi-filter outside this feature-span family, or a different route to a superlinear lower bound.

### 25. Transitive unisolvent orbits extend the filter obstruction to q-ary Reed-Muller spaces (23 September 2026)

The binary argument is an instance of a more general principle about equivariant feature spaces. Let $\Omega$ be a finite set of size $Q$, let $V\subseteq\mathbb F^\Omega$ be a $D$-dimensional function space containing the constant-one function, and let a group $G$ act transitively on $\Omega$ while preserving $V$ under pullback. Suppose $V$ has two disjoint information sets $B_0,B_1\subseteq\Omega$, each of size $D$ (evaluation on either set is an isomorphism from $V$ to $\mathbb F^D$). Let $A\subseteq\Omega$ have size $M$ and $U=\Omega\setminus A$. If $2MD<Q$, then some $g\in G$ sends both information sets away from $A$.

Indeed, for uniform $g\in G$ and fixed $a\in A$, transitivity makes $g^{-1}a$ uniform on $\Omega$. The chance that it lands in $B_0\cup B_1$ is $2D/Q$. A union bound over $A$ gives probability at most $2MD/Q<1$ that either $gB_0$ or $gB_1$ meets $A$. For a successful $g$, the sets $E=gB_0$ and $H=gB_1$ are disjoint subsets of $U$, and both feature images span the full $D$-dimensional space. Since the feature map includes the constant coordinate, linear representations of any anchor feature vector have coefficient sum one, so they are affine representations too. Thus every anchor lies in both feature affine hulls.

For the q-ary instantiation, take $\Omega=\mathbb F_q^N$ and $V$ to be the reduced polynomials of total degree at most $d$, with monomial basis $x^e$ for $e\in\{0,\ldots,q-1\}^N$ and $|e|\le d$. Its dimension is $D=|\{e:|e|\le d\}|$. Choose an ordering $\lambda_0,\ldots,\lambda_{q-1}$ of the field elements and an affine involution $\tau(x)=c-x$ such that $\tau(\lambda_k)=\lambda_{q-1-k}$. Such an ordering exists: for odd $q$ use the pairs under negation with zero in the middle; for even characteristic use any nonzero translation, whose orbits are pairs. Define
\[
B_0=\{(\lambda_{k_1},\ldots,\lambda_{k_N}):\sum_i k_i\le d\},\qquad B_1=\tau^{\times N}(B_0).
\]
The Newton polynomials $\prod_i\prod_{j<e_i}(x_i-\lambda_j)$ form a basis of $V$. Their evaluation matrix on $B_0$ is triangular with nonzero diagonal, since the entry for exponent $e$ and index vector $k$ vanishes unless $e_i\le k_i$ for every coordinate. Hence $B_0$ is an information set. The affine map $\tau^{\times N}$ preserves $V$, so $B_1$ is one too. If $2d<N(q-1)$, the index-weight ranges of $B_0$ and $B_1$ are disjoint.

For any anchor set $Y\subseteq A$, define the q-ary feature-span family by $\mathcal F_{a,d}=\{S\subseteq U:\phi_d(a)\in\operatorname{aff}_{\mathbb F_q}(\phi_d(S))\}$. If, in addition, $M<\delta_q(d,N-1)$, where $\delta_q$ is the minimum distance of the q-ary generalized Reed-Muller code on an $(N-1)$-coordinate slice, all matching coordinate-value generators belong to every $\mathcal F_{a,d}$. Otherwise affine separation would give a nonzero degree-$d$ polynomial whose restriction to the slice is nonzero at the anchor and zero on the high-table part of that slice, so its support would lie in $A$ and have size at most $M$, contradicting the minimum distance. If $d=a(q-1)+b$ with $0\le b<q-1$, then $\delta_q(d,m)=(q-b)q^{m-a-1}$ for $d\le m(q-1)$; this is the classical generalized Reed-Muller distance formula of Kasami, Lin, and Peterson and Delsarte, Goethals, and MacWilliams ([1970](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0019-9958(70)90214-7)). Under these two size conditions, one disjoint pair belongs to every anchored q-ary feature-span filter.

For each fixed prime power $q$ and degree fraction $d/(N(q-1))\to\alpha<1/2$, both conditions hold when $M=2^{o(N)}$. The dimension ratio $D/q^N$ is the lower-tail probability that a sum of $N$ independent uniform digits in $\{0,\ldots,q-1\}$ is at most $d$; Hoeffding's inequality makes this exponentially small below half the mean. The slice minimum distance is also exponential in $N$ for fixed $\alpha<1/2$. Thus the affine-orbit cover obstruction extends across finite fields and reaches every fixed sub-half degree fraction.

The experiment `experiment_qary_affine_orbit_pair.py` checks 387 finite-field cases over $\mathbb F_3$ and $\mathbb F_5$, with dimensions up to 21, and verifies disjointness, full feature rank, anchor affine-span membership, and matching coordinate-slice membership. It is a finite implementation check, not the asymptotic proof. This extension rules out a broader family of algebraic feature-span constructions as a source of the target cover lower bound. It does not produce an uncovered semi-filter, a circuit lower bound, or a P-versus-NP result; it identifies equivariance plus two small disjoint information sets as a further obstruction that any successful feature-based design must evade.

### 26. Minimum-support flats induce a blocking-set condition, but no pair-cover lower bound (23 September 2026)

This is the final check of the Reed-Muller feature-span branch before shifting to the unconventional-ideas ledger. Work over $\mathbb F_2^N$ with degree-$d$ features, where $0\le d<N$. For each anchor $a$ and set $E$, write $a\in\operatorname{Span}_d(E)$ when $\phi_d(a)$ lies in the affine span of $\{\phi_d(x):x\in E\}$.

#### Proposition 26.1 - endpoint sets must block minimum-support flats

If $a\notin E$ and $a\in\operatorname{Span}_d(E)$, then $(E-a)$ meets every $(N-d)$-dimensional linear subspace of $\mathbb F_2^N$. Consequently,
\[
|E|\ge 2^{d+1}-1.
\]

For any $(N-d)$-dimensional linear subspace $L$, choose independent linear forms $\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_d$ with common kernel $L$. The degree-$d$ polynomial
\[
p(x)=\prod_{j=1}^d (1+\ell_j(x-a))
\]
is the indicator of the affine flat $a+L$: it equals one at $a$ and vanishes outside $a+L$. If $E$ missed this flat, then $p$ would vanish on $E$ while $p(a)=1$, contradicting affine-span membership. Thus $E-a$ blocks all codimension-$d$ vector subspaces. On projectivizing the nonzero vectors, the Bose--Burton theorem gives the lower bound equal to the points of a projective $d$-flat, namely $2^{d+1}-1$. A $d$-dimensional vector subspace attains the bound because it intersects every $(N-d)$-dimensional subspace nontrivially.

The argument is only a necessary condition for the full feature-span test; it uses minimum-support indicators as a subfamily of all degree-$d$ separators. If $a\in E$, membership is automatic and the translated blocking condition is unnecessary.

#### Pair interpretation and limitation

For a pair $E,H$ already in the anchor's feature-span filter, failure of $E\cap H$ means that some degree-$d$ separator is one at $a$ and vanishes on $E\cap H$. A minimum-support flat through $a$ that misses $E\cap H$ would then meet $E$ and $H$ separately, giving a **minimum-flat split witness**. Such witnesses define a useful partial Ã¢â‚¬Å“split-flat shadowÃ¢â‚¬Â of the pair's coverage, but they are not known to capture every possible separator: a higher-weight polynomial may witness the failure even when no minimum flat does.

The blocking bound lower-bounds endpoint cardinality, not the number of pairs needed to cover anchors. It is consistent with the disjoint interpolation sets in Section 24, whose size is $D=\sum_{j\le d}\binom Nj$ and which give a one-pair cover under $2MD<2^N$. Thus the finite-geometry calculation closes this candidate route without strengthening the required superlinear all-semi-filter cover lower bound.

The exhaustive script [experiment_split_flat_blocking.py](experiment_split_flat_blocking.py) enumerates all blockers smaller than $2^{d+1}-1$ for $(N,d)=(2,1),(3,1),(3,2),(4,1),(4,2)$, checks all distinct codimension-$d$ kernels, and verifies that a $d$-flat attains the bound. All checks pass. The general claim follows from the Bose--Burton theorem, not from these small cases; minimum-weight affine-flat supports are classical for Reed--Muller codes. See [Bose and Burton](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0021980066800078) and [Leducq's treatment of the Delsarte--Goethals--MacWilliams theorem](https://arxiv.org/abs/1001.2554).

### 27. The finite-combinatorics/ZFC route remains conditional; its runtime bridge is model-sensitive (23 September 2026)

S. Gill Williamson's short preprint [On P=NP Either False or Independent of ZFC](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.00468) translates Friedman's Jump Free Theorem into a statement about families of finite functions and subset-sum instances. It proves a set version of the Jump Free theorem from the original Jump Free theorem, then states a subset-sum corollary and observes that a proof of $P=NP$ would imply the corollary. The paper explicitly **conjectures** that this corollary is independent of ZFC; it does not prove independence. Therefore this is a conditional metamathematical lead, not an independence result for $P=NP$. Even if the corollary were independent and the implication were formalized in ZFC, the consequence would be non-provability of $P=NP$ in ZFC (assuming consistency), not a proof that $P\ne NP$.

There is an input-model issue in the claimed connection to standard P=NP. The 2024 preprint allows arbitrary bijections $\Gamma\ni\gamma:\mathbb N\to\mathbb Z$ to encode finite-function values as integers, with no effectiveness or growth restriction. Its corollary measures the family by $p^k$ items and claims an $O(p^{kt})$ bound. The 2019 and 2021 papers likewise count a bound on the number of subset-sum comparisons and describe $p^k$ as instance size. In the standard binary Turing model, an instance has total encoding length $L_p$, including the bit lengths of all integer values, and $P=NP$ gives a polynomial bound in $L_p$. It does not by itself give $O((p^k)^t)$ unless a suitable uniform bound on $L_p$ in terms of $p$ is proved, or the intended model counts arithmetic operations on unit-cost integers. The cited constructions provide no such encoding bound. This leaves the claimed implication as stated under-specified in the standard model; it is not a refutation of the finite-combinatorial theorem.

Multiplicity is a convention issue, not a demonstrated flaw under the papers' formal set definitions. The 2019/2021 construction forms $H_p$ as a union of sets $\Delta_i$ and counts subsets of the distinct positive values. The 2024 note defines $F,H$ as sets and then separately says their cardinalities are $p^k$ "as multisets"; its regularity argument does remove the middle-block positions, so a list-based interpretation can preserve multiplicity if values are indexed by positions. The proof would need to state which representation is meant if the claimed $p^k$ multiset size is operationally relevant. The script [experiment_subset_sum_multiplicity.py](experiment_subset_sum_multiplicity.py) is only a convention demo: four distinct values with seven copies each yield 4,096 selectable subsets when treated as a list and 16 when treated as a set. It does not challenge the paper's set-based subset count.

This does not refute the finite combinatorial theorem or the possibility of a repaired implication. The missing work is to specify the encoding, prove a uniform bound on $L_p$ for the chosen sequence, and formalize the $P=NP\Rightarrow$ corollary proof. A direct proof of the corollary might avoid the Jump Free theorem; using the theorem to establish it does not make the corollary independent. The source's own abstract makes the independence status clear: it says the relevant statement is conjectured independent, and that this would conditionally obstruct a ZFC proof of $P=NP$.

The related [Subset Sum Instances in ZFC Limbo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2012.05385) gives the earlier enumeration argument and explicitly measures instance size by $p^k$. The 2019 paper's Theorem 6.7 and 2021 paper's Theorem 3.5 both count comparisons over distinct-value sets; the separate problem is reconciling their item/comparison measure with binary Turing input length. These papers provide a useful finite-combinatorics/foundations research direction, but no proof of either P-versus-NP outcome. The detailed attempt and status are in [ideas.md](ideas.md).

### 28. Audit of a claimed quantale/evidence proof of P != NP (23 September 2026)

The preprint [A Quantale-Weakness Route to P != NP via CD Evidence Normalization and Gauge-Buffered Locked Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.08814) (Ben Goertzel, v2 dated 22 April 2026) claims P != NP by deriving a lower bound on time-bounded conditional description length for a message uniquely read from satisfying SAT witnesses, then comparing it with the standard SAT self-reduction upper bound under P=NP. The upper-bound half is correct in outline: for a polynomial-time verifier, a polynomial SAT decider yields a polynomial-time search procedure by fixing witness bits one at a time, and a fixed projection reads the common message. It is conditional on having the promised sampled SAT family and uniform CNF construction.

The advertised lower-bound proof is not completed by the paper's construction appendix. The locked-core data are abstract, and the key properties are hypotheses: lock satisfiability and locked-message rigidity (D.7-D.8), balance of target coordinates (D.12), and the existence of construction-compatible message-opposite couplings (D.48). The sampler in Construction D.39 calls a lock sampler that already outputs a satisfying locked completion. Theorem D.54 explicitly assumes lock and buffer satisfiability, no public target tags, atom completeness, and compatible couplings before deriving the finite ensemble interface. The main-text Theorem 4.31 likewise cites the hidden-gauge product law and atom completeness as hypotheses (4.17 and 4.22), and derives its buffer estimate only under the separate buffer-contraction hypothesis 4.27. Appendix E assumes guarded remoteness and finite-geometry approximation (E.18-E.19); Appendix F states bounded readout overlap as Hypothesis F.36 and only says in a remark that it can be enforced by a suitable design. In the inspected construction appendix, these are not discharged by an explicit family of lock instances, buffers, and couplings.

There is a second, independent completeness obligation. The paper states that an observer may inspect the whole public SAT instance Y, while its primitive-read interface consists of declared template/surface reads, safe-buffer probes, and gauge-bearing literals. The observer-to-evidence theorem expands a decision tree over that chosen interface; it does not prove that every ordinary polynomial-time computation on the full bit string Y has a trace in this vocabulary with all target-relevant public computations neutral. The manuscript separately assumes atom completeness (4.22/D.33). A prohibition on a dedicated public target field does not itself rule out a global efficiently computable function of visible syntax. The finite script [experiment_public_neutral_leak.py](experiment_public_neutral_leak.py) finds an invertible 12-by-12 linear encoding over GF(2) such that no subset of at most three public bits determines any individual message bit, while Gaussian elimination on the full public string recovers all 12 bits. For each target coordinate it also constructs a coupling of opposite phases whose public strings differ in exactly one coordinate; this shows that disagreement under a chosen coupling can fail to reflect the predictive power of the full joint observation. This toy is not a counterexample to an interpretation of the paper's hypothesis that already forbids every polynomial-time public decoder; under that interpretation, however, the hypothesis contains the core hardness property that requires proof. If it forbids only declared tags or fixed template maps, it leaves ordinary computation of the public syntax outside the charge system.

More generally, because $B_j$ is a deterministic function of $Y$ on the support, the distributions $\mathcal{L}(Y\mid B_j=0)$ and $\mathcal{L}(Y\mid B_j=1)$ have disjoint supports and total variation one. The best information-theoretic test therefore distinguishes the phases perfectly by reading the whole string. This does not yield a polynomial-time predictor: the separating function may be computationally hard. It does show that the proof needs a computational hardness statement about the public encoding; calling its primitive fields neutral cannot replace that statement.

Hypothesis 7.5 exposes a precise coverage fork. It says every normalized neutral atom is pair-neutral under the opposite-message coupling. If the neutral public/template atoms collectively encode every bit of the public instance visible to a standard observer, pair-neutrality forces $Y(\omega^0)=Y(\omega^1)$. By the single-message promise this forces $B_j(\omega^0)=B_j(\omega^1)$, contradicting that the coupling is supported on opposite target phases. If instead the atoms record only a common public skeleton and omit some visible lock/template bits, the observer-to-evidence theorem has not shown how an ordinary machine reading those omitted bits is represented. Thus the proof needs either a precise full-input trace theorem that abandons zero-cost neutral coherence for phase-varying public bits, or a demonstrated observer restriction; the paper states that the observer has full access, so the latter would change its model.

Assessment: the manuscript gives a detailed conditional proof architecture and a plausible P=NP upper-bound leg, but the stated assumptions carry the central construction and observer-completeness burdens. It is not, as currently written, an unconditional proof of P != NP. This audit does not disprove P != NP or refute the conditional lemmas; it identifies the precise missing work: instantiate and prove the lock/buffer/coupling hypotheses, then prove semantic completeness for arbitrary polynomial-time observers without assuming their public computation is already hard. The independent idea ledger records a possible repair vocabulary based on pairwise semantic derivatives, while noting why the elementary derivative union bound is not yet useful.

There is a cryptographic benchmark for the missing sampler. If one fixed uniform witnessed generator $G(r)=(Y,W)$ had a rigid message $M(Y)$, then define $f(r)=Y$. An inverter that returns a seed $r'$ with $f(r')=Y$ could rerun $G(r')$ and recover a satisfying witness; rigidity forces its message to equal $M(Y)$. Thus average-case hardness of this message on a single fixed generator family would make $f$ one-way, under the standard input-length conditions. Grollmann and Selman characterize one-way-function existence by $P\ne UP$ ([SIAM Journal on Computing](https://doi.org/10.1137/0217018)); Liu and Pass connect mild average-case hardness of conditional time-bounded Kolmogorov complexity to one-way-function existence ([IACR ePrint 2021/513](https://eprint.iacr.org/2021/513)). This is only a benchmark, not a conclusion about the preprint: its lower bound chooses ensemble parameters after fixing an observer clock exponent, so it does not directly provide one fixed generator family that defeats every polynomial-time inverter.

### 29. Self-referential diagonalization: explicit tableau size and the succinct-input boundary (23 September 2026)

The unconventional-ideas ledger formalizes a direct sampler that tries to choose a $t$-bit message absent from every program of description length at most $at$ on a public input $Y$ of length $N$. The elementary counting step requires $0<a<1$: otherwise the number of candidate descriptions need not be smaller than the $2^t$ possible messages. With $0<a<1$ and sufficiently large $t$, enumeration costs about $2^{at}N^D$ steps for an $N^D$ clock, up to universal-simulation overhead. If $t=O(\log N)$ and is uniformly available to the sampler, this is polynomial in $N$, so the sampler itself gives a short polynomial-time public decoder at some fixed exponent. If $t/\log N$ grows without bound, enumeration ceases to be polynomial.

For the quine variant, an explicit Cook--Levin tableau cannot serve as its own short input if the diagonalizer deliberately pads every one of $2^{at+O(1)}$ simulated descriptions to $N^D$ steps. Its transcript then has $\Theta(2^{at}N^D)$ steps, and an encoding with an explicit row for each step has formula length at least a constant times that transcript. Setting the formula length equal to $N$ fails for superlogarithmic $t$. This is a no-go only for the padded explicit-tableau construction. If simulations are not padded, the clock upper bound alone does not imply a lower bound on actual transcript length; a different self-reference scheme may compress the relevant run.

Succinct encoding moves the question to a different language. Arbitrary succinct 3-SAT is NEXP-complete by the standard Cook--Levin tableau construction for nondeterministic exponential time; see Papadimitriou and Yannakakis, [A Note on Succinct Representations of Graphs](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0019-9958(86)80009-2). A generic polynomial-time compiler from arbitrary succinct formulas to ordinary SAT would imply NEXP subset NP, contradicting Cook's [nondeterministic time hierarchy theorem](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0022-0000(73)80028-5). This leaves open a special compiler for a highly structured family, which must be built and analyzed separately. Lawvere's fixed-point theorem provides useful language for the diagonal map but supplies no bounds on representation size or evaluation time; see [Lawvere, Diagonal Arguments and Cartesian Closed Categories](https://www.tac.mta.ca/tac/reprints/articles/15/tr15abs.html). The full parameter split and its caveats are in [ideas.md](ideas.md), Ideas 12--13. No P-versus-NP separation follows.

### 30. Kozen's subrecursive indexings: a nonstandard diagonalization lead (23 September 2026)

Dexter Kozen's 1980 paper [Indexings of Subrecursive Classes](https://doi.org/10.1016/0304-3975(80)90017-1) is a highly relevant foundations/complexity lead. The publisher-independent abstract states that it develops axioms for subrecursive indexings (programming systems/GÃƒÂ¶del numberings of classes such as polynomial-time functions), relates indexing structure to uniform simulation and diagonalization, and shows that if $P\ne NP$ is provable at all, it is provable by diagonalization. That abstract-level statement does not mean the paper proves the separation or supplies a concrete NP diagonal language. The exact theorem hypotheses and sense of Ã¢â‚¬Å“provable by diagonalizationÃ¢â‚¬Â still need examination in the full paper.

An independent reindexing argument clarifies why a conditional Ã¢â‚¬Å“diagonal proofÃ¢â‚¬Â can be obtained without finding a separation. Let $C$ be an effectively enumerable class of total Boolean functions on binary strings containing constants and closed under finite changes. Given any computable $g\notin C$, enumerate $C$ as $\psi_j$ and choose increasing code strings $e_j$ in length-lexicographic order where $\psi_j(e_j)\ne g(e_j)$. Such indices exist infinitely often, since finite disagreement would put $g$ in $C$. Assign $\varphi_{e_j}=\psi_j$; at every unreserved code $e$, assign the constant function $1-g(e)$. This is a computable surjective indexing and $1-\varphi_e(e)=g(e)$ for all code strings $e$. Taking $C=P$ and a hypothetical $g\in NP\setminus P$ yields an indexing whose diagonal is that witness. The construction is conditional on already having $g$; its selector uses $g$, and no polynomial evaluation bound follows. It is a concrete explanation of how an abstract Ã¢â‚¬Å“provable by diagonalizationÃ¢â‚¬Â claim can depend on a tailored GÃƒÂ¶del numbering. It does not establish that this numbering satisfies every axiom in Kozen's paper.

The ordinary clocked indexing gives a second precise benchmark. Define $U(p,x)$ by parsing $p=\langle M,1^k\rangle$ and accepting iff $M$ accepts $x$ within $|x|^k$ steps; let $\mathrm{UEVAL}=\{\langle p,x\rangle:U(p,x)=1\}$. Each fixed program section is in P, and the sections cover P. For total input length $m$, the encoded exponent $k\le m$, so universal simulation runs in at most $m^m=2^{O(m\log m)}$ time up to polynomial simulation overhead; UEVAL is in EXP. Conversely, for any $L\in EXP$ decided by fixed $M_L$ in time $2^{n^c}$, map $x$ to $\langle\langle M_L,1^{n^c}\rangle,x\rangle$. This is polynomial-size and $n^{n^c}\ge2^{n^c}$ for $n\ge2$, proving EXP-hardness. Thus UEVAL is EXP-complete. If UEVAL were in NP, then EXP=NP and, since P is strictly contained in EXP by the deterministic time hierarchy, P would differ from NP; see Hartmanis and Stearns, [On the Computational Complexity of Algorithms](https://doi.org/10.1090/S0002-9947-1965-0170805-7). This identifies a stronger target: proving the *full* universal evaluator is in NP already proves the desired separation. A useful diagonal proof would need a special restriction whose NP verification avoids that full evaluator collapse.

For the clocked evaluator, $D_U(p)=1-U(p,p)$ is itself a concrete diagonal language. It is in EXP and outside P by the direct self-code argument: if a clocked P machine with code $p_D$ decided $D_U$, then $D_U(p_D)=U(p_D,p_D)$ by correctness and $D_U(p_D)=1-U(p_D,p_D)$ by definition. It is also EXP-hard: hardwire any $L\in EXP$ input $x$ into a clocked program $M_x$ that ignores its input and decides $\overline L(x)$; a unary exponent polynomial in $|x|$ supplies a clock exceeding the EXP computation, so $D_U(\langle M_x,1^k\rangle)=L(x)$. Thus $D_U$ is EXP-complete and outside P. Its membership in NP is unproved; if it held, it would imply EXP=NP and hence P\ne NP. This gives the project a precise candidate language, not a solution.

The toy script [experiment_kozen_reindexing_toy.py](experiment_kozen_reindexing_toy.py) applies the custom numbering to eventually constant Boolean sequences with alternating target $g(e)=e\bmod2$. It checks the diagonal identity for all indices in a finite prefix and checks that selected base functions receive distinct reserved indices. This verifies only the finite implementation; the conditional infinite claim follows from the disagreement argument above. The full Kozen theorem remains unaudited: the publisher abstract was located, but attempts to access the journal full text through ScienceDirect returned HTTP 403, and no author-hosted paper copy was found in the checked catalog. That is an access limitation, not evidence for or against the theorem's application.

The current ledger records this as Idea 14. The next work is to obtain the full 1978 conference or 1980 journal text, state Kozen's axioms and diagonal theorem exactly, and check whether they permit a special NP-verifiable diagonal without encoding a pre-existing NP language outside P. A companion paper, Jones and Regan's 1992 [Diagonalization, Uniformity, and Fixed-Point Theorems](https://doi.org/10.1016/0890-5401(92)90040-M), derives fixed-point results for subrecursive classes using delayed diagonalization; it is a related lead, not an audited P-versus-NP result.

### 31. Clock ceilings, diagonal sweep, and a uniform witness budget (23 September 2026)

The new ledger Idea 15 formalizes a resource-profile version of the clocked diagonal. Let h(n) be a polynomial-time computable, nondecreasing, unbounded ceiling with h(n)<=n. On an index p=<M,1^k>, define U_h(p,x) by simulating M(x) for |x|^k steps only when k<=h(|p|), and set D_h(p)=1-U_h(p,p). Assume descriptions admit arbitrary ignored padding. Then D_h is outside P: if a polynomial-time machine M_D decided it with some fixed exponent d, choose k>=d and pad a code p=<M_D,1^k> until k<=h(|p|). The evaluator reproduces M_D(p), contradicting D_h(p)=1-U_h(p,p). The proof shows why a clock ceiling that grows without bound still diagonalizes against every fixed polynomial exponent.

The same construction does not put D_h in NP. For efficiently computable ceilings with h(n)<=n, its direct evaluator runs in n^{O(h(n))} time up to interpreter overhead; for h(n)=ceil(log n), this is quasipolynomial. An explicit deterministic computation tableau has at least one row per simulated step (the standard full tableau can be quadratic in the step count), so it is not a polynomial certificate when h is unbounded. This only rules out the literal tableau witness. A compressed NP certificate remains logically possible. For the unrestricted unary-clock evaluator from Audit 30, UEVAL is EXP-complete, so UEVAL in NP would imply EXP=NP and consequently P!=NP. For restricted h, membership of D_h in NP would itself yield P!=NP, but no verifier is known.

This sharpens the self-reference obligation: the computation index must represent arbitrarily large fixed polynomial exponents, while one NP verifier must have one fixed polynomial witness bound across all indices. Any escape must give a sound verification method uniform in the clock exponent without checking the entire n^k-step trace. This is a new formulation of the obstacle, not a proof that compressed certificates cannot exist.

A primary later source refines the Kozen-indexing lead. Bhaskar and Simonsen's 2023 paper on read/write factorizable programs says that one of Kozen's axioms is a polynomial-time transformation that takes codes for f and g and returns a code for f composed with g; the paper also says such axioms yield consequences including Kleene's second recursion theorem. This confirms an efficient structural requirement omitted by the conditional computable reindexing in Idea 14. We have not shown that the tailored numbering supplies polynomial-time composition, constant generation, or the rest of Kozen's axioms. The full original paper remains inaccessible in the checked publisher route (HTTP 403), so no exact claim about its complete theorem is made. See [Bhaskar and Simonsen, Read/write factorizable programs](https://doi.org/10.1017/S0956796823000023), Section 9, and Kozen's [publisher-independent abstract](https://research.ibm.com/publications/indexings-of-subrecursive-classes).

A padding rescue was also analyzed. Put a short code x inside a long input p=(x,padding), then try to use |p| as the polynomial budget for an explicit computation trace of M(x). The trace can indeed be polynomial in |p| if the pad is long enough. However, diagonalizing against a putative decider M_D requires relating M_D(p) to M_D(x). Padding-invariance of the language would give that relation and would let M_D be normalized to strip padding, but then the canonical short input x has the same diagonal truth value and must itself have an NP certificate of size polynomial in |x|. Declaring short x invalid avoids that certificate obligation only by losing the padding-invariance relation needed for the diagonal contradiction. Therefore padding by itself does not yield an NP diagonal; a short certificate for the canonical core remains the missing ingredient. This is a diagnosis of this padding construction, not a general no-go theorem.

Result: a uniform clock/certificate gap and a concrete audit requirement for the custom-indexing idea. No new separation or breakthrough. Next step: obtain a full Kozen text or reliable transcription, state all axioms precisely, and analyze the diagonal language under those efficient compiler assumptions. In parallel, search for an NP certificate for a restricted D_h that is not merely an explicit computation trace; if a proposed certificate exists, prove soundness and bound its size for all codes before running finite experiments.

### 32. Time-bounded incompressibility and the degree fixed-point failure (23 September 2026)

The new ledger Idea 16 tests a foundations/algorithmic-information route that is closely related to Juraj Hromkovic's work on time-bounded Kolmogorov complexity. Fix a polynomial clock f(n)=n^d and let K^f(x) be the length of the shortest program that prints x within f(|x|) steps. The threshold language Upper_f={(x,s):K^f(x)<=s}, for s<=|x|+O(1), lies in NP by guessing the short program and checking its bounded execution; Lower_f, expressing K^f(x)>s, is in coNP. Under P=NP, both are in P, so binary search computes K^f(x) in polynomial time for every fixed f. Hromkovic states this as a conditional alternative: P!=NP, or polynomial-clock time-bounded Kolmogorov complexity is deterministically computable in polynomial time for each such fixed clock. The report also proves conditional disjunctions involving deterministic versus nondeterministic compression, within its stated efficiently verifiable mathematics framework. See the [ECCC report, including revision 1](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2021/049/) and the later [published TCS article](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.tcs.2024.114747).

A fresh diagonal attempt uses prefix extension. For fixed f and s=n-2, define E_f(u,n) to ask whether there is an n-bit extension x of prefix u with K^f(x)>s. This is Sigma_2^P because it existentially chooses a completion and then asserts the coNP property Lower_f. If P=NP, the polynomial hierarchy collapses to P, so E_f is decidable in polynomial time. Counting guarantees a completion at the empty prefix, since fewer than 2^(n-1) programs have length at most n-2 while there are 2^n n-bit strings. Repeated prefix queries therefore produce an x_n with K^f(x_n)>n-2. A program containing the generator and n hardwired has description length O(log n).

This would contradict the chosen clock f only if the generator ran in time at most n^d. Let its actual polynomial runtime be n^{a_d}. The Sigma_2-to-SAT encoding and the assumed P algorithm can multiply exponents; P=NP places no useful upper bound on a_d relative to d. If a_d<d (or a concrete runtime bound fits f) for even one suitable fixed d, the generated string would have a description of length O(log n) running within f(n), contradicting its construction. But the argument gives no such fixed point: changing d changes the bounded-complexity language and may change its polynomial decider. This is the same uniform-exponent debt identified in Idea 15, now made explicit as an attempted Kolmogorov diagonal.

The source itself warns against overclaiming. Its Discussion explains that the argument cannot rule out an unbounded sequence of deterministic compression improvements, because the descriptions of the successive compressors may continue to grow. Thus neither its disjunction nor this prefix-search construction proves P!=NP. The candidate advance is a sharper obligation: prove a degree-neutral reduction or a self-referential bound that makes the generator run within its own fixed clock (for example, a_d<d for a polynomial exponent bound), or abandon the route. No such bound has been found.

### 33. Nondeterministic Kolmogorov complexity as a candidate NP-hard set (23 September 2026)

A fresh literature search found Goldberg, Hu, Lu, Lyu, and Oliveira's ECCC report [Synergies Between Complexity Theory and Nondeterministic Kolmogorov Complexity](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/215/), originally posted November 2025 and revised February 2026. Theorem 1.1 proves that for each fixed gamma>1/2, every dense language L contained in R_nKt[gamma n] is outside NTIME(2^{epsilon n}) intersect coNTIME(2^{epsilon n}) for some epsilon>0. Here R_nKt[gamma n] is the set of strings x whose nondeterministic time-bounded Kolmogorov complexity nKt(x) is at least gamma|x|. The same report notes that the estimation problem MnKtP is in P^NEXP and NEXP-hard under nonuniform reductions; it identifies a superpolynomial circuit lower bound for this problem as a breakthrough-level missing step.

This yields a precise conditional route to P!=NP. If there were a dense uniform language L in NP with L subset R_nKt[gamma n], then under P=NP it would be a P language. Every P language belongs to NTIME(2^{epsilon n}) intersect coNTIME(2^{epsilon n}), contradicting Theorem 1.1. A dense coNP subset would work as well. The proof task is therefore narrowed to producing an NP-verifiable certificate that each accepted string has no shorter nondeterministic program satisfying the unique-output condition.

The obstacle is the quantifier structure: low nKt complexity guesses a program and a time bound but must guarantee that every nondeterministic path outputs either the designated string or failure, while at least one path outputs it. Its complement is a global anti-description statement over all short programs and paths. The inspected paper does not provide a dense NP/coNP sublanguage of R_nKt; the lower bound concerns languages already contained there and gives no efficient membership test for the high-complexity property itself.

The NEXP-hardness result must be handled with its stated reduction model. If MnKtP had an NP upper bound, its nonuniform NEXP-hardness would imply NEXP subset NP/poly. Under P=NP this gives NEXP subset P/poly, but NEXP not subset P/poly is itself a major open circuit-lower-bound question; this does not currently yield a contradiction. The dense-NP-subset target is stronger and more direct, but no construction is known in this project. This is a research lead, not a breakthrough.

A foundations-oriented certificate route was considered. For each string x, form a succinct arithmetic/propositional assertion that no program/time description of cost below gamma|x| uniquely generates x, and define L_S to contain x when it has a short proof in a sound proof system S. If proof checking on this succinct statement is polynomial-time, then L_S is in NP; soundness gives L_S subset R_nKt[gamma n]. If, in addition, L_S is dense, Theorem 1.1 yields P!=NP. The construction has two independent open obligations: produce polynomial-size proofs for a dense set of high-nKt strings, and give a polynomial-time sound proof checker for the succinct exponential computation claim. The counting argument only shows that many such x exist; it gives no short proof for an individual x. Expanding the claim to an ordinary formula would itself take exponential size, so succinct-proof verification must be justified rather than assumed.

Next step: derive the exact uniform upper bound for R_nKt[gamma n], then try to construct a dense NP/coNP slice using an explicit anti-generator certificate. Any proposed slice must prove both density and high nKt complexity for every accepted string. A sampler that outputs a few high-complexity strings, or a nonuniform advice family, does not meet the condition.

### 34. Worst-case symmetry of information as a nonrelativizing separation target (23 September 2026)

Goldberg, Hu, Lu, Lyu, and Oliveira's ECCC report TR25-215 gives a direct conditional route to P!=NP through probabilistic nondeterministic time-bounded Kolmogorov complexity. Its Theorem 5.3 assumes that there is a constant c such that, for every sufficiently large n and every x,y in {0,1}^n,

    pnKt(x,y) >= pnKt(x) + pnKt(y | x) - log^c(n).

It concludes UNSAT is outside AMTIME[2^(log^d n)] for every d>=1. If P=NP, then PH=P, hence AM=P; UNSAT would then be in P and therefore in AMTIME[2^(log^2 n)], contradicting the theorem at d=2. The theorem's worst-case pnKt symmetry statement, if established, therefore proves P!=NP. This records a conditional sufficient theorem, not a proof of its hypothesis.

The report proves a weaker average-case result for every polynomial-time samplable distribution on equal-length pairs: with probability at least 1-1/k, a related inequality holds with loss O(log n + log k), and pKt(x) appears in the first term. This does not immediately imply worst-case symmetry. An attempted bootstrap was: under P=NP, search for a violating pair and turn the search into a sampler. Lemma 5.4 makes the obstruction explicit: testing a conditional pnKt threshold at budget m/2 enumerates all candidate program descriptions and uses SAT queries, requiring 2^{O(m)} time. The P=NP assumption can replace each SAT query with a deterministic polynomial-time computation but does not eliminate the 2^{O(m)} enumeration. Thus the violating pair is not known to be polynomial-time samplable, and the average-case theorem cannot be applied to it by this argument.

The proof of Theorem 5.3 offers a mechanism worth isolating. Assuming UNSAT has quasipolynomial AM proofs, the authors construct a sequence x_1,...,x_ell with conditional pnKt(x_i | x_1,...,x_{i-1}) at least m/2 for every i, while the joint sequence has a much shorter randomized nondeterministic time-bounded description. If a suitable pairwise symmetry inequality held along the sequence, telescoping would force the joint description to pay for all these increments and yield a contradiction. The unpaid sum is called Ã¢â‚¬Å“information debtÃ¢â‚¬Â in ledger Idea 18 as a mnemonic only. The derived violating pair need not have an efficiently samplable distribution.

More narrowly, write u_i=x_1||...||x_{i-1} and pad v_i=x_i with zeros to |u_i|. The telescoping only needs the inequality on these append pairs: pnKt(u_i,v_i) >= pnKt(u_i)+pnKt(v_i|u_i)-delta(n), where delta(n)=o(m) and O(log n) tuple/padding costs are absorbed. In the paper's parameter choice, the full theorem's log^c(n) loss is below the linear-in-m increment. This restricted target could conceivably use structure of the constructed sequence, but its tuples are still produced by an AMÃ¢Ë†Â©coAM oracle and an exponential sweep through candidate m-bit strings. No proof of the restricted statement is known here.

A second possible bridge is to generalize average-case SoI from polynomial-time samplers to slower, explicitly bounded samplers, with a loss charging the sampler's runtime or description. It would help only if that loss stays below the m/2 conditional pnKt increment for this construction. An arbitrary slow-sampler extension is not justified, and related time-bounded symmetry principles fail for other measures. This remains a conjectural direction, not an implication of the existing average-case theorem.

Variant and barrier checks matter. Hu, Manor, and Oliveira's 2026 ECCC report TR26-021 proves failure of symmetry for rKt and pKt, so those measures cannot simply replace pnKt in this route. TR25-215 Theorem 5.12 constructs an oracle and pairs with linear symmetry violations for each of rKt, pKt, nKt, rnKt, and pnKt. Therefore a proof of the unrelativized worst-case pnKt inequality would need a nonrelativizing ingredient.

Primary sources: [Goldberg et al., TR25-215](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/215/), Theorems 5.3 and 5.12; [Hu, Manor, and Oliveira, TR26-021](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/021/). The exact theorem statements and running-time enumeration are in the linked full reports. Next step: formalize the weakest restricted pairwise inequality needed by the telescoping proof, then determine whether it can hold for the sequence-generator pairs from Lemma 5.5 without enabling their exponentially long candidate sweep. Any proposed shortcut must account for the oracle barrier and the full quantifier over string pairs.

### 35. Under P=NP, randomized nondeterministic Kt descriptions have deterministic Kt descriptions

Ledger Idea 19 records a conditional simulation lemma derived from the definition of rnKt. Suppose rnKt(x) is witnessed by program ÃŽÂ  and time parameter t. On at least 2/3 of random tapes r of length t, all nondeterministic paths w of length t output either x or a failure symbol, and at least one path outputs x.

Define GOOD(ÃŽÂ ,1^t,r) by existentially choosing one successful w with output z and universally checking that every w' outputs either z or failure. Since bounded simulation is polynomial-time in the explicit input length |ÃŽÂ |+t, GOOD is in ÃŽÂ£Ã¢â€šâ€š^P. The prefix extension predicate asking whether some completion r of a given prefix is GOOD adds only an existential quantifier, so is also in ÃŽÂ£Ã¢â€šâ€š^P. Under P=NP, PH collapses to P; fixed polynomial algorithms therefore decide both predicates. Bit-by-bit search recovers a good r in poly(|ÃŽÂ |+t) time. Search-to-decision under P=NP then finds a successful w in the same polynomial scale, and simulating it outputs x.

The resulting deterministic program contains ÃŽÂ  and the binary encoding of t, so its description length is |ÃŽÂ |+O(log t); its runtime is poly(|ÃŽÂ |+t), whose logarithm is O(log t+log(|ÃŽÂ |+1)). Therefore Kt(x)=O(rnKt(x)) under P=NP. Conversely rnKt(x)Ã¢â€°Â¤Kt(x)+O(1) by using a deterministic program and ignoring the extra random and nondeterministic inputs, so the two measures would be equivalent up to constant factors under that assumption.

Applied to Lemma 5.5 of TR25-215, the sequence X=(xÃ¢â€šÂ,...,x_ell) with rnKt(X)Ã¢â€°Â¤2^{m^{1/(2d)}} would satisfy Kt(X)=O(2^{m^{1/(2d)}}) under P=NP. This is a description-complexity consequence, not a P-vs-NP contradiction. The deterministic program may take time 2^{O(2^{m^{1/(2d)}})}, which is superpolynomial in |X|Ã¢â€°Ë†2^{m^{1/d}}m. Moreover, a short joint Kt description does not imply that the sequence's conditional pnKt costs add. Hence the conversion does not make the sequence a polynomial-time sampler for the average-case theorem and does not prove a chain rule.

The proof depends on using unary 1^t inside the decision predicates: the P=NP algorithms run polynomially in t, which is permissible for Kt because only log runtime is charged. If one treated t's binary encoding as the full input to the collapse algorithms, the argument would be invalid. The construction has been checked at the quantifier level, but no separate formalization or machine-level implementation is claimed. Next step: determine whether any sequence construction relevant to the pnKt telescoping proof has a polynomially bounded t; otherwise this lemma remains a conditional description comparison only.

### 36. Fusion spectra and exact projective-incidence pilots (24 September 2026)

Idea 79 introduces a precise language for the endpoint-pair cover problem. For endpoint $S$, let $\sigma(S)$ be the anchors whose filters accept it. The fusion profile of $(E,H)$ is $\Phi(E,H)=\sigma(E)\cap\sigma(H)\setminus\sigma(E\cap H)$. The attainable nonempty profiles form the fusion spectrum, and the minimum endpoint-pair cover is exactly the set-cover number of that spectrum. A two-pair cover exists exactly when two profiles have union equal to the anchor set. Thus maximum profile size alone cannot diagnose whether a second pair repairs the first pair's misses.

The exact Fano-plane pilot [experiment_projective_fano_semifilter.py](experiment_projective_fano_semifilter.py) uses seven anchors/points, matching binary-coordinate generators, and all incident projective lines. It checks all 128 endpoint subsets and 16,384 ordered pairs. The combined filters have 60 nonempty profiles, maximum profile size four, and exact cover number three. Direct checks find no one- or two-profile cover and do find a three-profile cover. The coordinate-only subfamily has exact cover two. In this finite example, adding generators raises the cover number because it can make endpoint intersections newly acceptable as well as make endpoints acceptable.

The exact $PG(2,3)$ pilot [experiment_projective_pg2_3_semifilter.py](experiment_projective_pg2_3_semifilter.py) checks 8,192 endpoint subsets and 67,108,864 ordered pairs. With line generators, coordinate-value generators, or both, the cover number is two; the largest profile covers nine of 13 anchors. The exact comparison [experiment_incidence_coloring_vs_fusion.py](experiment_incidence_coloring_vs_fusion.py) finds fusion cover equal to weak chromatic number on four plane systems: $PG(2,2)$ gives 3, $PG(2,3)$ gives 2, $AG(2,2)$ gives 4, and $AG(2,3)$ gives 3. These matches prompted a coloring hypothesis, but the Fano three-cover does not persist even to the next projective plane and no general incidence theorem or P-vs-NP lower bound follows.

A proposed explanation was that fusion cover for line-generated filters equals weak hypergraph chromatic number. It matches the four projective/affine plane pilots but is false in general. The exact counterexample [experiment_hypergraph_coloring_fusion_counterexample.py](experiment_hypergraph_coloring_fusion_counterexample.py) uses five vertices and triples 012, 124, 023, 123, 024. It has a weak 2-coloring, every anchor has an individually covering endpoint pair, and yet its six-profile fusion spectrum has exact cover three. This rules out ordinary weak coloring as the needed invariant; it does not rule out a refined invariant that tracks endpoint intersections.

The dual weak-coloring variant also fails on this example. A two-coloring of its hyperedges can make every anchor incident to both colors, but each color class has the full vertex set as its shadow. Their intersection contains every witness edge, so it creates no fusion profile. This is **overlap contamination**: separate endpoint witnesses can regenerate an accepted generator inside the intersection.

For any finite upward filter family, an endpoint pair covering anchors $C$ can be normalized by choosing for each $a\in C$ a minimal witness $g_a\subseteq E$ and $h_a\subseteq H$, then replacing the endpoints with $E'=\bigcup g_a$ and $H'=\bigcup h_a$. Endpoint acceptance persists, and the intersection only shrinks; upward closure preserves rejection. Consequently every cover has a representative whose endpoints are unions of at most $|C|$ minimal witnesses per side. If anchor $a$ has $t_a$ minimal generators, its orientation choices are at most $1+t_a(t_a-1)$, since the two selected witnesses must be distinct. This generalizes Proposition 18.21's two-generator orientation reduction, although the resulting state space can still be exponential in the anchors.

The Fano fractional-cover optimum is exactly $19/10$: the dual weights $(2,2,3,2,3,3,4)/10$ are feasible on every profile, and a matching primal cover uses weights $(0.3,0.3,0.3,0.4,0.2,0.2,0.2)$ on profile masks $(30,45,51,75,84,100,112)$ in the script's anchor-bit order. Its integral cover is three, so this gives an explicit integrality gap and explains why the fractional dual misses the finite obstruction. For $PG(2,3)$, numerical optimization returns about 1.444 for line-only and 1.536 for combined filters; exact rational dual weights give feasible lower bounds $13/9$ and $106/69$. These are finite calculations, not an asymptotic proof. Any magnification route still needs a scale-dependent lower bound and a transfer theorem to circuit complexity.

### 37. Incident-edge filters and two-sided neighborhood splitting (24 September 2026)

Let $G=(V,E)$ and let anchor $v\in V$ accept endpoint $S\subseteq V$ iff $S$ contains an edge incident to $v$. A fusion profile $C$ is exactly an independent set admitting disjoint support sets $X,Y\subseteq V\setminus C$ such that every $v\in C$ has a neighbor in both $X$ and $Y$. For a covered $v$, the common endpoint intersection contains $v$ but no neighbor of $v$, forcing the endpoint witnesses into the two disjoint exclusive sides; conversely, endpoints $C\cup X$ and $C\cup Y$ realize such a profile. Thus the exact cover number is the minimum number of these two-sided-neighbor-splittable independent sets needed to cover $V$.

Every profile is independent, so $\tau(G)\ge\chi(G)$. If the minimum degree is at least two, every vertex can be covered individually by choosing two distinct neighbors, so $\tau(G)\le|V|$. The exact finite check [experiment_graph_vertex_filter_chromatic.py](experiment_graph_vertex_filter_chromatic.py) gives $\tau=\chi=5$ for $K_5$, $3$ for $C_5$, and $2$ for $K_{2,3}$. The six-cycle has $\chi(C_6)=2$ but exact fusion cover $3$: either bipartition class would require a 2-coloring of its opposite-side neighborhoods that splits each of three two-element neighborhoods, an odd-cycle constraint.

This is an exact graph baseline and a refined invariant beyond ordinary coloring. Its current realization has the same number of anchors and ground points, so its cover is at most $|U|$ and misses the project target of a superlinear lower bound. A coded-anchor lift could in principle provide many more anchors than ground points, but the matching literal generators can accept endpoints without carrying the proposed graph code; controlling those mixed profiles is open.

The direct overlay [experiment_literal_overlay_graph_filters.py](experiment_literal_overlay_graph_filters.py) tests a six-cycle on the 10-point weight-three 5-bit shell, with all matching literal generators and one 2-point witness per incident graph edge. Across 25 injections, exhaustive enumeration of all 1,048,576 ordered endpoint pairs per injection finds cover one every time. For the canonical injection, the winning pair covers four anchors using literals alone and gets the other two through edge witnesses. This rejects the unmodified graph lift at this scale; it is not an impossibility result for all coded constructions.

The larger coded experiment [experiment_graph_code_lift_weight3.py](experiment_graph_code_lift_weight3.py) puts 16 anchors on the same ten-point shell, uses a graph with a $K_4$ core and minimum degree two, and assigns distinct 2-point codewords as anchor tokens. Among 30 assignments checked against every endpoint pair, 28 have cover one and two have cover two; none has cover three. For the canonical code assignment, literals alone have a two-pair cover and one literal profile covers 15/16 anchors. The full winning pair uses matching literals on those 15 and one edge witness on the remaining anchor. The special witness repairs the exception left by the literal profile rather than increasing the cover.

### 38. Checkerboard fusion cover for coordinate-slice filters (24 September 2026)

Let $U=[q]^n$, where $q\ge2$ and $n\ge2$. For each anchor $a$ in any set $A\subseteq[q]^n$, let its upward filter be generated by the coordinate-value slices $G_{i,a_i}=\{x:x_i=a_i\}$. Partition the alphabet into nonempty proper sets $R,R'$ and $C,C'$. Define two endpoint pairs using coordinates 1 and 2:

$$
\begin{aligned}
E_1&=\{x:x_1\in R\text{ or }x_2\in C\},&
H_1&=\{x:x_1\in R'\text{ or }x_2\in C'\},\\
E_2&=\{x:x_1\in R\text{ or }x_2\in C'\},&
H_2&=\{x:x_1\in R'\text{ or }x_2\in C\}.
\end{aligned}
$$

For the first pair, the endpoint intersection is the union of the two off-diagonal quadrants $R\times C'$ and $R'\times C$; for the second it is the diagonal pair $R\times C$ and $R'\times C'$. Because all four symbol classes are nonempty and proper, neither intersection contains a full slice in coordinate 1 or 2; it also omits a quadrant from every later-coordinate slice. Thus both endpoint tests accept each anchor in the corresponding checkerboard profile, while the intersection rejects every coordinate generator. The two profiles partition $[q]^n$, giving exact fusion cover at most two for every anchor subset $A$. This is a general theorem about coordinate-slice filters on a product domain, independent of anchor count or code distance.

The exact checker [experiment_qary_coordinate_slice_covers.py](experiment_qary_coordinate_slice_covers.py) enumerates all endpoint pairs for $q=2,n=2$ and $q=3,n=2$: the exact cover is two, with six and 108 nonempty profiles respectively. It also checks the explicit complementary profiles for $n=3$, where their sizes are $4+4$ for $q=2$ and $12+15$ for $q=3$. The separate distance-three code-shell test [experiment_graph_code_lift_code_anchors.py](experiment_graph_code_lift_code_anchors.py) finds an even stronger accidental cover one using literals alone on its first assignment; across 100 assignments, the full graph-plus-literal filters all have exact cover one.

These profile covers apply only to the minimal filters generated by the coordinate slices. They do not cover every semi-filter above the anchors. The valid preservation-closure test [experiment_checkerboard_semifilter_closure.py](experiment_checkerboard_semifilter_closure.py) takes $A=\{0000,1111\}$ and $U=\{0,1\}^4\setminus A$; both anchors have a preserving semi-filter under the two checkerboard pairs. In general, when the checkerboard intersection $I=E\cap H$ is nonempty and an endpoint of the opposite pair contains neither a required matching slice nor $I$, the upward closure of the matching slices together with $I$ is a preserving semi-filter. The exact universal-cover test [experiment_universal_cover_q2_n3_sparse_anchors.py](experiment_universal_cover_q2_n3_sparse_anchors.py) takes $A=\{000,111\}$ and the other six words as $U$. It exhausts all 2,080 single-pair candidates, finds no universal one-pair cover, and finds a universal two-pair cover after inspecting 690,071 two-pair lists. Thus the exact cover on this sparse toy is two. The explicit rules work through a repair chain: one pair adds a nonempty intersection to closure, that new set enables a second rule, and the second intersection is empty. This is a finite demonstration that the closure calculus can create genuinely new effects beyond static fusion profiles.

In logical form, the universal cover is an anchor-indexed Horn refutation problem. Treat each subset $S\subseteq U$ as a proposition Ã¢â‚¬Å“$S$ is in the filterÃ¢â‚¬Â; upward closure gives free implications $S\Rightarrow T$ for $S\subseteq T$, and a pair contributes $(E\wedge H)\Rightarrow(E\cap H)$. The anchor's matching slices are the initial facts. A lower bound must show that every short rule list leaves at least one anchor whose least fixed point avoids $\emptyset$.

Every such refutation has a disjoint seed: $\emptyset$ can only enter the closure as the intersection of a listed pair with disjoint endpoints. All other rules relay nonempty intersections into endpoint supersets until both sides of a disjoint seed become derivable. In the exact sparse-cube cover, one rule is the disjoint seed and the other creates an intersection equal to one of its endpoints. This reframes the quantitative challenge as a seed-reachability problem over anchors, not simply a count of fusion profiles.

The small cube overlay [experiment_cube_graph_literal_overlay.py](experiment_cube_graph_literal_overlay.py) checks all 65,536 endpoint pairs for the 3-cube. Literal-only, edge-only, and combined families each have exact cover two. Here the graph-edge generators are subsets of coordinate slices, so adding them makes the literal generators redundant; this is a confounded overlay, not evidence that general additional generators preserve the checkerboard bound. The distance-three shell experiment provides the stronger mixed-generator test because its sparse graph tokens are inclusion-minimal relative to the literal slices.

This isolates the main structural failure of the coded graph lift. The graph controls which edge witnesses can coexist, and distance controls collisions among token codes, but neither controls the independent coordinate-slice layer. Its two-coordinate checkerboard profiles already cover the anchor space in complementary pieces for the *minimal* filters. Adding special generators can contaminate a selected checkerboard pair, yet a distinct mixed pair may repair the lost anchors; the shell experiment exhibits this directly. In the actual Gap-MCSP setup, filters above an anchor are required to contain every matching basis slice by Definition 19 of [Cavalar and Oliveira](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/033/download); omitting the literal layer is not an available escape. The full Gap-MCSP promise domain is not a product cube, and Proposition 12.3 gives a one-pair obstruction there for canonical filters. More fundamentally, the universal cover number ranges over every semi-filter extension, so profile covers of the minimal filters do not establish a bound on $\rho$. A lower bound must show that every short pair list leaves at least one anchor whose preservation closure avoids $\emptyset$. No P-vs-NP result follows from this finite and construction-specific analysis.

### 39. Directed-cycle relay and exact cover on a punctured cube (24 September 2026)

Take $U_n=\{0,1\}^n\setminus\{0^n,1^n\}$ and anchors $A_n=\{0^n,1^n\}$. For each cyclic edge $i\to i+1$, let $B_i=\{x\in U_n:x_i=0\text{ or }x_{i+1}=1\}$. Each $B_i$ contains a forced matching slice at both anchors: the zero-slice at coordinate $i$ for $0^n$, and the one-slice at coordinate $i+1$ for $1^n$. Consequently every semi-filter above either anchor contains every $B_i$.

Let $K_j=\bigcap_{i=1}^jB_i$. Use rule $(B_1,B_2)$ to add $K_2$, then use $(K_{j-1},B_j)$ for $j=3,\ldots,n$. At each stage the first endpoint has already entered closure and the second contains a forced slice. The constraints are $x_i\le x_{i+1}$ around a directed cycle; their only Boolean solutions are the two constant words. They are absent from $U_n$, so $K_n=\emptyset$ and the list of $n-1$ pairs covers every semi-filter above both anchors.

For the lower bound, suppose a closure derivation uses $m$ pair rules. Trace the endpoint derivations to an acyclic proof DAG. There are at most $m$ rule nodes, at most two antecedents per rule, and at most $m+1$ initial-generator leaves by the connected-DAG edge count. Their intersection must be contained in the root's empty intersection. At anchor $0^n$, any set of fewer than $n$ distinct zero-slices has nonempty intersection in $U_n$: set those coordinates to zero and one un-fixed coordinate to one. Thus at least $n$ leaves are required, giving $m\ge n-1$. The exact universal cover number is therefore $n-1$.

The explicit finite verification [experiment_cycle_relay_cover.py](experiment_cycle_relay_cover.py) confirms the closure sequence through $n=12$. The SAT ladder separately finds exact candidate covers at $n=3,4,5,6,7$ with $2,3,4,5,6$ rules; for $n=6$, four rules are UNSAT. The general proof explains the dimension pattern, so further SAT runs on this toy would add little.

**Relation to known theory and remaining task:** Cavalar and Oliveira prove that cover complexity is exactly cyclic intersection complexity, and Section 2.5 interprets fixed closure rules as cyclic monotone Boolean circuits ([paper](https://arxiv.org/html/2503.14117)). The relay certificate here is a concrete instance of that established correspondence. Its exact $n-1$ value saturates the projectÃ¢â‚¬â„¢s proof-DAG leaf bound and remains linear. The unresolved target is still cross-anchor sharing: determine whether the structured low-circuit anchors force more than $N$ rules in the actual Gap-MCSP cover problem.

### 40. Anchor activation signatures on low-weight promise toys (24 September 2026)

To probe the missing cross-anchor cost, take anchors $A_{n,1}=\{a:\operatorname{wt}(a)\le1\}$ and universe $U_{n,1}=\{x:\operatorname{wt}(x)\ge2\}$. Run [experiment_relay_cover_boolean_cube.py](experiment_relay_cover_boolean_cube.py) with all anchors explicitly listed. Its generic SAT encoding ranges over arbitrary endpoints $E_j,H_j\subseteq U$, unrolls the least fixed-point recurrence for at most $m$ firings, and independently checks SAT models over the support family consisting of $\emptyset$, endpoints, and rule intersections.

For $n=3$, it finds no one-rule list and verifies a two-rule cover. For $n=4$, three rules are UNSAT and four are SAT; the saved endpoint list in [experiment_weight_one_anchor_cover4.py](experiment_weight_one_anchor_cover4.py) is checked against the full 2,048-subset preservation closure for each of the five anchors, so the exact cover is four. The four-rule witness has initial fired-rule masks $\{0,2\}$ for anchors $0000,0010$, $\{2\}$ for $0001,0100$, and $\{0\}$ for $1000$. These masks route through different activation orders before reaching the same disjoint seed. For $n=5$, the four-rule search is UNSAT. A five-rule PySAT/CaDiCaL search with endpoint-swap, rule-order, and all coordinate-permutation symmetry breaking returned UNSAT. The symmetry actions preserve the instance, and the lex-leader encoding was exhaustively checked on all two-bit word pairs; no DRAT trace was saved. A six-rule SAT model is saved and verified in [experiment_weight_one_anchor_cover5_six_rules.py](experiment_weight_one_anchor_cover5_six_rules.py), which checks all six anchors using the exact support-set closure. Thus the SAT search gives computed exact value six for this toy. A general fallback upper bound of $\binom n2-1$ rules is explicit: each set $C_{ij}=\{x:x_i=0\text{ or }x_j=0\}$ is accepted by every weight-at-most-one anchor, and their intersection has weight at most one. The relay checker [experiment_weight_one_pairwise_relay.py](experiment_weight_one_pairwise_relay.py) verifies this for $n=3$ through $10$.

The two-extreme-anchor cycle does not carry over directly: its clause $x_i=0\lor x_{i+1}=1$ has no forced matching slice at a unit anchor with $x_i=1,x_{i+1}=0$. This explains exactly why the static shared cycle cannot cover the larger anchor family. The machine-found four-rule witness instead exploits anchor-dependent activation. Define $\sigma_\Lambda(a)$ as the set of rules whose two endpoints are each initially supported by a forced matching slice at $a$. Later rules fire when their endpoints are supported either initially or by intersections of fired rules. This signature-to-rule network is not a new framework: Cavalar and Oliveira's Section 2.5 already identifies fixed two-antecedent closure dynamics with cyclic monotone Boolean circuits ([paper](https://arxiv.org/html/2503.14117)). Our use is to inspect anchor-specific activation patterns in this low-weight instance.

The one-anchor proof-DAG bound gives $\rho\ge n-2$, while the next section gives an explicit shared prefix construction with $\rho\le2n-4$. Thus the threshold toy has only linear cover complexity. The target remains a lower bound for the actual Gap-MCSP anchors; any successful construction must defeat compact prefix-state summaries while respecting the arbitrary semi-filter extension and exact closure recurrence.


### 41. Prefix-state certificate for low-weight anchors (24 September 2026)

For $A_{n,1}=\{a:\operatorname{wt}(a)\le1\}$ and $U_{n,1}=\{x:\operatorname{wt}(x)\ge2\}$, let $Z_i=\{x\in U:x_i=0\}$ and define $A_i=\bigcap_{j\le i}Z_j$ and $F_i=\{x\in U:\operatorname{wt}(x_1,\ldots,x_i)\le1\}$.
