Start with one rule deriving $A_2=Z_1\cap Z_2$ and use the free upward superset $F_2=Z_1\cup Z_2$. For $i=3,\ldots,n$, use one rule for each update

$$A_i=A_{i-1}\cap Z_i\quad(i<n),\qquad F_i=F_{i-1}\cap(Z_i\cup A_{i-1}).$$

The two chains use $n-2$ rules each. For every anchor of weight at most one, $F_2$ contains one of its forced zero-slices. At step $i$, if $a_i=0$, the second endpoint contains the forced slice $Z_i$; if $a_i=1$, the anchor's earlier prefix is all zero and $A_{i-1}$ is derivable from its forced zero-slices. Hence every $F_i$ enters the preservation closure, and $F_n=\emptyset$ on $U$. This is a universal cover of size $2n-4$, not just a calculation for the canonical filters.

The support-set verifier [experiment_weight_one_linear_exact_certificate.py](experiment_weight_one_linear_exact_certificate.py) checks every anchor and the complete finite preservation closure for $n=3,\ldots,12$. For a lower bound, focus on anchor $0^n$. A proof-DAG using $m$ pair rules has at most $m+1$ generator leaves. If it used at most $n-2$ leaves, at least two coordinates would be absent among the forced zero-slices; the point with ones on those coordinates lies in $U$ and in every leaf, contradicting derivation of the empty set. Thus $m\ge n-2$ and

$$n-2\le\rho(A_{n,1},U_{n,1})\le2n-4,$$

which establishes $\Theta(n)$ growth. The SAT searches give exact values $2,4,6$ at $n=3,4,5$, matching the upper bound there, but do not prove the formula for larger $n$.

This is a compact threshold-state dynamic program, not a new circuit framework. Under complementing each coordinate, the target is threshold $n-1$, dual to threshold two; classical monotone-threshold circuit theory already contains the corresponding linear gate bounds, including the $2n-4$ gate-type bound for threshold two and its dual ([Sergeev](https://www.mathnet.ru/eng/dm1547)). The acyclic count does not settle whether cyclic relay rules can save gates in larger dimensions. More relevant to this project, the result shows why the low-weight family fails as a magnification candidate: despite $n+1$ anchors, one compact two-state prefix invariant routes all of them to the empty target.\n

The same state-machine construction applies to $A_{n,k}=\{a:\operatorname{wt}(a)\le k\}$ and $U_{n,k}=\{x:\operatorname{wt}(x)>k\}$ when $0\le k\le n-2$. For prefix states $S_{i,j}=\{x\in U:\operatorname{wt}(x_1,\ldots,x_i)=j\}$, use one rule for each valid branch in

$$S_{i,j}=(S_{i-1,j}\cap Z_i)\cup(S_{i-1,j-1}\cap O_i).$$

Every anchor of weight at most $k$ follows its prefix-count state, whose final state is empty on $U$. The number of rules is $R(n,k)=\sum_{i=2}^n(\min(i-1,k)+1+\min(i,k))\le(2k+1)(n-1)$. The same proof-DAG leaf argument at anchor $0^n$ yields $\rho\ge n-k-1$. Therefore all fixed-$k$ versions have $\Theta(n)$ cover complexity. [experiment_threshold_anchor_linear_cover.py](experiment_threshold_anchor_linear_cover.py) checks exact support closure for all $k\le n-2$ through dimension eight. Bounded-weight anchors are thus a closed toy family for superlinear search; future candidates must prevent this kind of small prefix-state refutation.


### 42. Cofactor gap-depth audit (24 September 2026)

The one-split rectangle identity in Proposition 12.1 invites repeated cofactor recursion. This section records exactly why the direct recursion fails to reach a trivial base case. Let $a$ and $b$ be low and high circuit-size thresholds. Restricting an input variable cannot increase circuit size, so a low parent of size at most $a$ has both cofactors at most $a$. Conversely, a selector/multiplexer combines two cofactor circuits using at most $C_0+C_1+3$ gates. Thus if a parent has size at least $b$, at least one cofactor has size at least $b' = \lfloor(b-3)/2\rfloor$. A promise separator for child gap $(a,b')$ can be applied to both children and their outputs ANDed: low parents give two low cofactors; high parents have at least one high cofactor, while the other may lie in the gap. The composition is correct on the parent promise.

After $d$ cofactor levels, the table length is $M_d=N/2^d$ and the inherited high threshold is approximately $b_d=b/2^d$, up to additive selector overhead. Thus the scale ratio $M_d/b_d$ remains approximately $N/b$. The gap closes after about $\log_2(b/a)$ levels, at which point $M_d$ is about $aN/b$. For $a=2^{\beta n}/(cn)$ and $b=2^{\beta n}$, this leaves $M_d\approx aN^{1-\beta}$ after only $O(\log n)$ levels. A decision tree gives an $O(M)$-gate circuit for every truth table of length $M$, so a trivial base requires $M=O(a)$, or depth

$$d\ge\log_2(N/a)=(1-\beta)n+O(\log n).$$

For fixed $\beta<1$, this is $\Omega(n)$, much deeper than the gap permits. A sharper generic truth-table circuit upper bound changes only lower-order terms; the depth remains linear. Therefore repeated use of the cofactor rectangle reaches a smaller unresolved gap-MCSP instance rather than solving it.

This is a no-go result for the naive recursion only. The possible new ingredient would be a decomposition whose circuit-combination cost grows more slowly than the number of cofactors, or an argument that handles intermediate cofactor complexities without deciding total MCSP. It explains a concrete compounding failure: promise rectangles are valid one level at a time, but the gap budget is consumed faster than the input table shrinks to a trivial size.

### 43. Sorting-network closure and the cross-anchor reuse bottleneck (24 September 2026)

#### Proposition 43.1 - every symmetric threshold toy has an $O(n\log^2 n)$ universal cover

Let $A_{n,k}=\{a\in\{0,1\}^n:\operatorname{wt}(a)\le k\}$ and $U_{n,k}=\{x:\operatorname{wt}(x)>k\}$, where $0\le k\le n-2$. Let $Z_i=\{x\in U_{n,k}:x_i=0\}$. Feed the set-indicators of $Z_1,\ldots,Z_n$ into a descending Batcher bitonic sorting network and pad with empty-set wires to $p=2^r$, $r=\lceil\log_2 n\rceil$. For each comparator on sets $E,H$, include the pair rule $(E,H)$; its intersection output is available by the rule and its union output is available by upward closure. At output position $n-k-1$, the network computes the predicate that at least $n-k$ input wires are nonempty at a point $x$, equivalently $\operatorname{wt}(x)\le k$. This output is empty on $U_{n,k}$. Every anchor in $A_{n,k}$ has at least $n-k$ zero coordinates, so its matching forced zero-slices make this monotone network's output enter its preservation closure. The pair count is

$$\frac{p r(r+1)}4=O(n\log^2 n).$$

Together with Proposition 21.7's single-anchor leaf bound on this toy, this gives

$$n-k-1\le\rho(A_{n,k},U_{n,k})\le\frac{p r(r+1)}4.$$

The implementation [experiment_threshold_sorting_cover.py](experiment_threshold_sorting_cover.py) independently checks the support closure for every $k\le n-2$ at dimensions 2 through 8. This rules out polynomially superlinear cover growth for the entire symmetric threshold family. It does not prove a bound for non-symmetric low-circuit anchor families.

#### The cross-anchor reuse bottleneck

For any cover list $\Lambda$, define the support of a carrier $X\subseteq U$ as the anchors whose closure derives $X$. The exact support recurrence is Proposition 21.4: initial support consists of anchors with a matching literal slice contained in $X$, and a rule $(E,H)$ adds its anchor-support intersection to carrier $E\cap H$ (then upward closure transfers that support to supersets). A successful universal cover derives the empty carrier for every low-circuit anchor.

Fixing a single anchor and tracing one empty proof gives a proof DAG with at most $m+1$ literal leaves for an $m$-rule list. Those leaves must fix a coordinate set $Q$ whose truth-table cylinder has no high-circuit completion. By circuit counting, $|Q|\ge N-o(N)$, which is the near-linear lower bound of Proposition 21.7. This argument counts proof resources for one anchor. The same rule node may be reused across proof DAGs for many anchors, so multiplying the single-anchor estimate by the number of anchors is invalid.

The next candidate quantity is **contextual reuse bandwidth**: the number of distinct anchor-dependent leaf patterns that can pass through one shared relay sub-DAG while still refining their near-full coordinate cylinders to empty. Raw support size is already useless on the toys: in the exact n=4 four-rule and n=5 six-rule certificates, each rule fires for every anchor, as recorded by the per-anchor round traces in the two independent closure verifiers. The proposed tradeoff must therefore concern proof alternatives and their actual shared antecedent sub-DAGs, not just the number of anchors in a rule's support. A proof could encode this in an anchor-tagged cylinder-intersection hypergraph or an entropy potential on carrier/proof-context pairs. Any such potential must handle arbitrary endpoint sets, mixed proof paths, and support overlap under the exact recurrence; a count of anchors or a selected-filter profile cover is insufficient. No quantitative contextual-reuse bound has been proved, so this is a research direction rather than a theorem.

#### Exact antichains of anchor-dependent proof contexts

For carrier $X$ and anchor $a$, let $\mathcal C_X(a)$ be the inclusion-minimal coordinate sets $Q$ whose matching literal generators derive $X$. Initialize with $\{i\}$ whenever $B_{i,a_i}\subseteq X$. For each rule $(E_j,H_j)$ with intersection $K_j$, every carrier $X\supseteq K_j$ receives all unions $Q\cup R$ with $Q\in\mathcal C_{E_j}(a)$ and $R\in\mathcal C_{H_j}(a)$, followed by inclusion-minimalization. For every subset $Q_0$ of available literal generators, $X$ is in the direct preservation closure generated from $Q_0$ iff some $Q\in\mathcal C_X(a)$ satisfies $Q\subseteq Q_0$.

The verifier [experiment_relay_proof_contexts.py](experiment_relay_proof_contexts.py) checks this equivalence for all $2^n$ generator subsets, all relevant carriers, and every anchor in the saved n=4 and n=5 universal covers. At the empty carrier, n=4 has four minimal contexts at $0000$ and one at each unit anchor, all fixing three coordinates; n=5 has five at $00000$ and one at each unit anchor, all fixing four coordinates. Every rule has support on all anchors, but its intersection carriers have only 7-10 anchor-context instances in these examples. This is finite evidence that context structure contains information discarded by support masks, not evidence of an asymptotic lower bound. Antichains can grow exponentially; the proposed use is mathematical accounting, not a claimed efficient procedure.

### 44. Linear-basis scrambling stress test (24 September 2026)

One way to change the mandatory-slice geometry without changing the promise's computational content is an invertible linear encoding $T:\mathbb F_2^N\to\mathbb F_2^N$. A transformed separator $C$ composes with $T$ to separate the original promise, with circuit size increased by at most the size of $T$; an inverse circuit is not needed for this direction. For a useful magnification transfer, $T$ must have size below the target lower-bound scale. A generic dense matrix costs $\Theta(N^2)$ XOR operations and is unsuitable when seeking near-quadratic or smaller improvements. Fast butterfly or sparse linear circuits are the relevant candidates.

The finite test [experiment_linear_basis_scramble_toy.py](experiment_linear_basis_scramble_toy.py) enumerates all 168 invertible linear maps in dimension three for $A=\{a:\operatorname{wt}(a)\le1\}$ and $U=\{x:\operatorname{wt}(x)\ge2\}$. With literal slices in transformed coordinates, 72 maps have an exact universal one-pair cover. For each remaining map, it exhaustively checks all 256 possible endpoint pairs as a rule and every ordered list of two rules; all 96 admit a universal two-rule closure. Thus the transformed toy has cover at most two for every basis, and has cover exactly two on the 96 bases without one-pair covers. The result is exact for this toy and says nothing asymptotic about Gap-MCSP.

The mixing-time result of [Ben-Hamou (2025)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.08185) puts a concrete cost on the naive route to a random basis: the elementary row-transvection walk mixes in $O(N^2\log N)$ row additions, each implementable as one XOR gate in the linear transform. The paper's alternative row-resampling chain mixes in $\Theta(N\log N)$ updates, but a dense row replacement costs $\Theta(N)$ XORs. Neither directly gives an $N^{1+o(1)}$-size transform. A useful construction would need fast sparse mixing or another explicit source of pseudorandom linear encodings.

The failure is informative: merely randomizing which parities count as coordinates does not remove the endpoint system's ability to adapt. A viable version would need a high-dimensional anchor family whose fast transform has a provable effect on all endpoint lists, not just a transformed low-weight toy. The transformed input promise still transfers a circuit lower bound to the original one by composition, provided the encoding circuit stays within the magnification slack. No such lower bound or useful transform has been found.

### 45. Sampled dimension-four basis changes (24 September 2026)

For the 4-bit toy, the low anchors $\{0,e_1,e_2,e_3,e_4\}$ and high universe $\{x:\operatorname{wt}(x)\ge2\}$ partition the whole Boolean cube. Thus a basis change sends the anchor simplex to $\{0,v_1,v_2,v_3,v_4\}$ and the high set to its complement, exactly matching the input model of the general PySAT relay-cover encoding. A fixed-seed sample of 20 matrices in [experiment_linear_basis_scramble_n4_sat.py](experiment_linear_basis_scramble_n4_sat.py) had first SAT rule counts 2 for two bases, 3 for six, and 4 for twelve. SAT witnesses pass the independent support-closure verifier; lower counts were rejected by solver status without saved proof traces.

The exhaustive orbit audit [experiment_linear_basis_scramble_n4_orbits.py](experiment_linear_basis_scramble_n4_orbits.py) enumerates all 840 unordered bases of $\mathbb F_2^4$ and quotients by the 384 coordinate-permutation/bit-flip automorphisms, yielding 14 representatives. Arbitrary-endpoint SAT search gives first-satisfiable counts 2 for one orbit, 3 for three, 4 for nine, and 5 for one. The exceptional representative has anchors $\{0,3,5,9,14\}$. Its five-rule endpoint list is stored in [experiment_basis_exception_contexts.py](experiment_basis_exception_contexts.py); direct closure matches the context-antichain recurrence for every subset of the four forced generators. Each anchor's only minimal empty context fixes all four coordinates, and every rule fires for every anchor. The orbit script's exact local certificate profiles show that this is the only class in which every anchor needs all four coordinates to make its cylinder lie inside the low set; every other class has at least one three-coordinate certificate. This is a finite correlation, not a proved cause. The four-rule UNSAT search has no proof trace, so the lower bound remains solver-derived. This closes the finite orbit census, not the high-dimensional route: a constant increase from four to five is not a magnification result.

### 46. Transversal form of proof contexts and independent four-rule check (24 September 2026)

For anchor $a$ and high universe $U$, let $\mathcal H_a=\{\{i:a_i\ne x_i\}:x\in U\}$. If a derivation of the empty carrier uses literal leaves indexed by $Q$, then $Q$ must hit every member of $\mathcal H_a$. If some $x\in U$ agreed with $a$ on all coordinates in $Q$, then $x$ would belong to every selected literal slice and, by induction over each intersection and upward-closure step of the proof, to every derived carrier, including the empty one. This contradiction proves that every root proof context is a high-free coordinate certificate. A context need not be an inclusion-minimal transversal: a smaller high-free coordinate set may not be derivable by the given endpoint network.

The new checker [experiment_anchor_transversal_audit.py](experiment_anchor_transversal_audit.py) explicitly enumerates the minimal transversals and compares them with exact proof-context antichains for three saved covers. In all three, they coincide for every anchor: the five-rule scrambled simplex in dimension four, the four-rule native weight-one cover in dimension four, and the six-rule native weight-one cover in dimension five. Every context is checked against every high point. This is evidence for a possible shared-selector view, not a general property or lower bound. It motivates Idea 92's Ã¢â‚¬Å“transversal compilerÃ¢â‚¬Â: a universal relay program must select at least one high-free transversal per anchor, and the prospective complexity measure concerns how one fixed network routes these anchor-dependent certificates. Any such measure must survive arbitrary endpoint sets and context-antichain pruning.

For algebraic bookkeeping, Idea 92 writes antichains as minimal monomials in an idempotent semiring: $P\oplus R=\min(P\cup R)$ for alternate derivations and $P\otimes R=\min\{p\cup r:p\in P,r\in R\}$ for paired premises. The carrier polynomial is the least fixed point of the matching-literal base terms plus all rule products whose intersections lie below the carrier. This is an exact reformulation of the existing context recurrence, not an algorithmic speedup; the antichain can be exponential.

An independent Z3 encoding of the exceptional simplex's four-rule problem returned UNSAT for anchors $\{0,3,5,9,14\}$, agreeing with the earlier PySAT/CaDiCaL search. The separate encoding expresses endpoint inclusion, anchor-slice support, four-round firing, and the disjoint-seed condition directly. Agreement reduces the risk of a shared CNF-translation bug but is not a portable proof certificate; the SAT-side proof trace also remains unavailable. I attempted to make Z3 export its proof object, but proof generation grew to about 14 GB of resident memory without completing; I stopped it and removed the incomplete artifact. This is a concrete tooling failure: quick UNSAT discovery does not imply cheap proof extraction. The normal cross-check script remains lightweight. The result remains a finite solver-derived lower bound, with no P-versus-NP transfer.

### 47. Geometry-only profiles for the dimension-five sparse-ball sample (24 September 2026)

Before the arbitrary-endpoint SAT search completes, [experiment_sparse_ball_transversal_profiles.py](experiment_sparse_ball_transversal_profiles.py) computes the minimal high-free coordinate certificates for each anchor in the same six seeded $GL(5,2)$ images of the weight-at-most-two ball. Every profile is explicit. In the native basis, 10 anchors have one minimal certificate, five have four, and the zero anchor has ten; all certificates have size three. In the five nonidentity samples, each anchor has between one and six minimal certificates, with minimum sizes between three and five. This supplies the geometry side of the Idea 92 comparison but does not determine which certificates any relay program can derive. The small counts also caution against using transversal-antichain cardinality alone as a scalable lower-bound potential.

The arbitrary-endpoint search was stopped on 25 September before the six-sample batch completed. Its process captured solver output internally and did not persist a witness or a final status; there is no SAT or UNSAT conclusion for the unfinished batch. Future proof-oriented work should use the existing geometry result as a falsification target and avoid restarting the full batch without a specific mathematical prediction.

### 48. Five-rule exact-closure cover for the five-bit radius-two ball (24 September 2026)

I ran a focused native-basis search using only the valid coordinate-permutation symmetries of $A=\{a:\operatorname{wt}(a)\le2\}$. For $n=3,4,5$, the first SAT rule counts are respectively 1, 2, and 5. At $n=5$, the SAT model is a five-rule cover of all 16 low anchors against the 16 high points $U=\{x:\operatorname{wt}(x)\ge3\}$; its endpoints and full closure traces are saved in [experiment_sparse_ball_native_n5_witness.py](experiment_sparse_ball_native_n5_witness.py) and [sparse_ball_native_n5_m5.txt](sparse_ball_native_n5_m5.txt). Direct support closure verifies every anchor. The solver returns UNSAT for one through four rules under the symmetry constraints, but no proof trace was saved, so the *minimum-five* claim is solver-derived; the five-rule upper bound is directly checked.

For the entire radius-two family in dimension $n$, a coordinate set $Q$ gives a high-free cylinder at anchor $a$ exactly when it fixes at least $n-2$ zero coordinates: the largest completion weight is $n-|Q\setminus\operatorname{supp}(a)|$. Hence minimum certificates have size $n-2$ and consist of zero coordinates. The n=5 witness's exact context antichains contain every such minimum certificate: 10 at the zero anchor, 4 at each weight-one anchor, and 1 at each weight-two anchor. This is a witness-specific fact, not a necessary condition for all covers.

The witness's five rule flags form a small cyclic monotone program. The sole disjoint rule's least-fixed-point firing set over the whole Boolean cube is exactly the radius-two ball; this is verified by [experiment_sparse_ball_native_equations.py](experiment_sparse_ball_native_equations.py). The observed counts $1,2,5$ are too few to imply a growth law and do not improve the asymptotic sorting-network upper bound. A separate $n=6$ search targeted five and six rules with valid $S_6$ symmetry; the five-rule CNF had 315,499 variables and 1,797,261 clauses. The long-running batch was stopped on 25 September without a newly persisted result. No P-vs-NP consequence follows from these finite toy results.

The n=5 cover also has a short structural completeness proof. Let $z_i$ indicate that anchor coordinate $i$ is zero. From the saved endpoint containments, rules 1 and 2 fire initially under $z_1z_2$ and $(z_1\vee z_2)z_4$, respectively; rule 4 fires initially under $z_0(z_1\vee z_2\vee z_4)$ and can also use rule 2's intersection. Rule 0 can use rule 1 on its left and rule 2 on its right. The empty-producing rule 3 can use rule 4 on its left and needs $z_3$, rule 0, or rule 1 on its right. For any anchor with at most two ones, split on $z_3$. If $z_3=1$, either $z_0=1$ lets rule 4 fire directly, or $z_0=0,z_4=1$ lets rule 2 then rule 4 fire, or $z_0=z_4=0$ forces $z_1=z_2=1$ and the chain 1,0,2,4 fires. If $z_3=0,z_0=1$, at least two of $z_1,z_2,z_4$ are 1; rule 4 fires, and either rule 1 fires or rules 2 then 0 do. If $z_3=z_0=0$, all of $z_1,z_2,z_4$ must be 1, giving rules 1,2,4. Each case fires rule 3. For soundness, a high point belongs to all its own matching slices and remains in every derived carrier, so it cannot lie in the disjoint rule's empty intersection. This proves the finite five-rule upper directly from endpoint containments and point preservation.

Deleting one endpoint pair at a time from this particular cover leaves respectively anchors $\{10,12,17\}$, $\{9,17,24\}$, and $\{1,3,5,9,10,12,17\}$ uncovered when rules 0, 1, and 2 are removed; removing either rule 3 (the disjoint seed) or rule 4 (its necessary relay) uncovers all anchors. This shows the witness has no locally redundant rule. It does not prove that every five-rule cover has the same dependency architecture.

### 49. A fixed transversal selector suffices, and the n=6 search reaches six rules (24 September 2026)

I extended [experiment_relay_cover_pysat.py](experiment_relay_cover_pysat.py) with an optional anchor-dependent mask of forced generators. Its default unrestricted encoding is unchanged; coordinate-permutation symmetry breaking is explicitly rejected when this mask is supplied. The new experiment [experiment_sparse_ball_canonical_selector.py](experiment_sparse_ball_canonical_selector.py) gives each radius-two anchor in dimension five only the first three zero-coordinate slices in coordinate order, then searches for a five-rule cover with no coordinate symmetry assumptions. SAT returns a witness, and the independent direct-closure verifier confirms that these three selected generators alone derive the empty set for all 16 anchors. The resulting endpoint list is saved in [experiment_sparse_ball_canonical_selector_witness.py](experiment_sparse_ball_canonical_selector_witness.py).

For this second witness, the unrestricted proof-context audit again finds every minimal high-free transversal, but exactly one root context per anchor lies within the prescribed selected triple. The restricted SAT instance independently confirms that the chosen context is sufficient. This demonstrates why the full root-antichain cardinality cannot itself be charged as necessary cover work: the cover only needs one valid anchor-dependent certificate. The candidate lower-bound target must be the complexity of *some* shared selector, with arbitrary endpoint advice allowed.

The selector's signed leaves are $(i,a_i)$, not only an unsigned coordinate set $Q$. For every low Gap-MCSP anchor, fixing all $N$ truth-table coordinates is trivially high-free; the difficulty is whether one fixed relay network can route these anchor-specific signs through its intersection states. Thus a lower bound cannot come merely from the existence or length of a high-free selector. The radius-two toy's lexicographic zero-selector is simple, and its five-rule witness demonstrates that such a simple selector can be implemented with compact feedback.

The separate n=6 radius-two run used valid $S_6$ symmetries and returned UNSAT at five rules; it then moved to six rules. A cover with fewer than five rules could be padded by the harmless pair $(U,U)$, so the solver status implies solver-derived $\rho\ge6$. The six-rule CNF has 382,420 variables and 2,172,134 clauses. The run remains active and no UNSAT proof trace exists for the five-rule instance. This finite lower bound is not a P-vs-NP result.

### 50. Failure anatomy: certificate geometry, selector choice, shared realization, transfer (24 September 2026)

The cumulative record points to a repeated structural mistake: several approaches paid for the wrong layer. The exact carrier-support recurrence is

$$S_0(X)=\{a:\exists i,\ B_{i,a_i}\subseteq X\},\qquad
S_{t+1}(X)=S_t(X)\cup\bigcup_{j:E_j\cap H_j\subseteq X}\left(S_t(E_j)\cap S_t(H_j)\right).$$

At the least fixed point, $S(\varnothing)$ must contain all anchors. For each $a$, every empty-carrier proof context is a high-free transversal of $\mathcal H_a=\{\{i:a_i\ne x_i\}:x\in U\}$. This gives a genuine necessary condition but only a per-anchor one. In the Gap-MCSP promise, all $N$ coordinates are always a high-free certificate, so certificate existence is not the hard step. The n=5 canonical-selector experiment then shows that one selected minimum transversal per anchor can suffice even when the full proof-context antichain has many members. In the earlier n=4 four-rule and n=5 six-rule weight-one covers, every listed rule fires at every low anchor, so raw rule support misses the cost. In contrast, the n=5 radius-two witness has intermediate rules that also fire on some weight-three anchors; its sole disjoint rule filters these false positives and fires exactly on the low ball. Neither fanout nor intermediate false-positive count alone supplies a lower bound. The remaining obligation is a bound on the shared realization of some signed selector by fixed endpoints under arbitrary mixed derivations.

This yields a four-layer checklist for future claims: (1) prove the certificate geometry, (2) account for the selector choice per anchor, (3) lower-bound the shared relay realization against arbitrary endpoints, and (4) calculate that the resulting scale transfers to an explicit circuit lower bound. Existing failures often stop at layer 1 or 2. The Hamming-threshold family fails at layer 4 because its symmetric function has an $O(n\log^2 n)$ sorting-network upper bound. Ã¢â‚¬Å“Selector realization debtÃ¢â‚¬Â is the project's name for the missing target, but currently it merely describes what a lower-bound theorem would need to quantify; without a nontrivial dual certificate it may just rename $\rho$.

There is also a technique-level explanation for why broad routes cannot be used naively. Baker, Gill, and Solovay exhibit oracles with both relativized outcomes, which rules out a fully relativizing proof ([original paper](https://doi.org/10.1137/0204037)). The Razborov-Rudich natural-proofs barrier applies conditionally to large, efficiently recognizable properties useful against general circuits ([paper](https://doi.org/10.1006/jcss.1997.1494)). Aaronson-Wigderson's algebrization barrier rules out another broad class of oracle-plus-arithmetization techniques ([paper](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/eccc-reports/2008/TR08-005/Paper.pdf)). These are limitations on proof methods under stated conditions, not proofs that P-vs-NP is impossible, independent of ZFC, or beyond future mathematics. The self-reference/proof-system work in this project has a separate repeated loss: formula size, verifier time exponent, proof cutoff, and theory quantifiers change during diagonalization, and no strict resource contraction has yet been obtained.

The current relay-cover framework must also be described with the right novelty boundary. Cavalar and Oliveira's 2025 result characterizes cover complexity via cyclic Boolean circuits ([paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.14117)). Thus the support-semiring and cyclic-circuit translations are useful audit tools, not a new general framework. Any advance here must use the specific low-circuit anchor family and its signed mandatory slices to prove an endpoint-robust selector-realization theorem.

At this checkpoint the unrestricted n=6 solver is active on six rules after UNSAT at five; the fixed canonical-transversal n=6 run is also active and silent. At the current process snapshot, the unrestricted n=6 six-rule process has used about 5,532 CPU seconds and 707 MB resident memory; the canonical-selector n=6 process about 3,641 CPU seconds and 186 MB; the normalized first-seed n=6 process about 166 CPU seconds and 755 MB. The transformed n=5 batch and one-two-point CEGIS process remain active at about 7,727 and 26,674 CPU seconds. All five Python processes responded to the latest poll and their CPU times increased; no solver output has arrived. Polls returned no new solver output, so none of those runs has a new SAT/UNSAT status. No process was restarted. If the canonical n=6 model returns SAT, verify its saved endpoint list and selected-generator closure independently; if UNSAT, that excludes only the chosen selector, not every possible one.

### 51. Explicit diagonal attempt against endpoint types and its padding failure (24 September 2026)

A fresh self-reference proposal starts from the fusion counting observation: a cover with $m$ pairs over a fixed $b$-element generator family is determined, for purposes of its universal output, by an endpoint/generator incidence pattern with $O(m(b+m))$ bits. Cavalar and Oliveira use this kind of description in their random-graph cover lower bound ([2025 paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.14117)). Directly enumerating these patterns costs $2^{O(m(b+m))}$, so it does not construct a target in the required uniform time.

I tested the obvious self-indexing repair: append a tag naming a pattern $\tau$, and define the target on that tag slice to be the complement of the output generated by $\tau$. A cover of the target would then fail on its own tag. If tag bits are omitted from the generator basis, however, generated sets ignore the tag, making the target unrepresentable for a trivial reason. If the tag bits are ordinary input coordinates, their two literal slices enlarge the basis from $b$ to $b+2L$. The uncompressed full incidence code then requires

$$L=2m(b+2L)+4m^2,$$

where the first term records endpoint-to-generator incidences and the second endpoint-to-endpoint incidences. The right side contains $4mL$, so this raw self-indexing scheme has no finite tag length for $m\ge1$. This is not a general impossibility for compressed diagonalization; it is a precise failure of the naive construction.

If one instead indexes only types over the original $b=2N$ coordinate slices, the raw tag length is $O(mN+m^2)$. With enlarged input length $M=N+L$, the resulting diagonal lower bound $m$ is at most $O(\sqrt M)$, below the superlinear cover lower bound needed by the project's Gap-MCSP magnification route. The only plausible escape is a proven promise-specific compression/normal form for endpoint types, or an implicit evaluator that avoids storing the type. Neither has been found. The distinction is important: counting proves typical hard targets, while explicit magnification requires a uniformly describable hard target within a sharply smaller computational budget.

This attempt supports the four-layer diagnosis in Section 50: the certificate and candidate enumeration are understood, but the explicit shared-realization lower bound and its transfer remain unpaid. The n=6 SAT processes were still active at the latest poll; no experimental status changed.

A further exact coding check rules out generic compression of every raw endpoint profile. In $U=\{0,1\}^N$, for each middle-layer coordinate set $S$ define $E_S=\bigcup_{i\in S}B_{i,0}$. Then $B_{j,0}\subseteq E_S$ iff $j\in S$, no $B_{j,1}$ is contained in $E_S$, and distinct equal-size $S,T$ produce incomparable endpoints. An ordered list of $2m$ distinct middle-layer sets therefore realizes $2^{\Omega(mN)}$ different endpoint-to-basis incidence profiles for $m$ below a fixed fraction of $\binom{N}{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}$. This proves that arbitrary raw profiles carry $\Omega(mN)$ bits in general. It does not show that all such profiles induce distinct target sets; any viable compression must quotient by equal fusion output or exploit structure of the particular promise.

### 52. Exact false-positive cancellation profiles across five relay covers (24 September 2026)

The diagnostic [experiment_disjoint_seed_error_profiles.py](experiment_disjoint_seed_error_profiles.py) traces closure across the full Boolean cube for five saved endpoint lists: native weight-one covers in dimensions 4 and 5, the dimension-four scrambled simplex, and both n=5 radius-two covers. Each list has exactly one disjoint seed. Its two premise-support sets intersect in exactly the low-anchor set and have no high-anchor overlap. Their individual support sizes and high spill counts are:

| Cover | Premise support sizes | High spill counts |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| n=4, weight one, 4 rules | 12, 6 | 7, 1 |
| n=5, weight one, 6 rules | 7, 13 | 1, 7 |
| n=4, scrambled simplex, 5 rules | 14, 7 | 9, 2 |
| n=5, radius two, native, 5 rules | 21, 22 | 5, 6 |
| n=5, radius two, canonical selector, 5 rules | 19, 23 | 3, 7 |

For these instances, every literal slice is nonempty, and the root support identity is $\Sigma_{\emptyset}=\bigcup_{j:E_j\cap H_j=\emptyset}(\Sigma_{E_j}\cap\Sigma_{H_j})$. The data make the finite cancellation mechanism concrete: each premise alone accepts some high anchors, but the pair does not. This is a finite pattern, not evidence that every optimal cover has one disjoint seed or a lower bound for Gap-MCSP. The script reuses the existing direct-closure routine and exhausts 16 or 32 points per instance; it is not an independent proof checker.
### 53. Two-seed n=5 cover reveals circular support inflation (24 September 2026)

I extended the PySAT encoding with an optional lower bound on the number of disjoint rules; the default encoding remains unchanged. [experiment_sparse_ball_multiple_disjoint.py](experiment_sparse_ball_multiple_disjoint.py) finds a five-rule cover of the n=5 radius-two promise with two disjoint seeds. The wrapper's direct closure verifier confirms all 16 low anchors derive empty. [experiment_disjoint_seed_error_profiles.py](experiment_disjoint_seed_error_profiles.py) finds that the first seed can start from 14 low anchors and the second from 9; their seedless-closure supports overlap on 7 low anchors and together cover all 16. The endpoint list is saved in [experiment_sparse_ball_multiple_disjoint_witness.py](experiment_sparse_ball_multiple_disjoint_witness.py).

This exposed a flaw in the first version of the support-profile diagnostic: in the full least fixed point, after one disjoint seed derives empty, upward closure can make the other seed's premises appear supported too. Final support masks then misleadingly suggest each seed independently covers all low anchors. The corrected **first-seed decomposition** removes every disjoint rule, closes under non-disjoint rules only, and measures each candidate seed against that closure. Every complete derivation of empty has a first disjoint rule; its two premises must already be in the non-disjoint closure. Conversely, any seed whose two premises are there immediately derives empty. Thus, when literal generators are nonempty, the low set is exactly the union of these zero-error first-seed intersections, with no high anchor in any term.

The tradeoff search [experiment_sparse_ball_seed_tradeoff.py](experiment_sparse_ball_seed_tradeoff.py) reports SAT for at least one and at least two disjoint seeds in five total rules, and UNSAT for at least three, four, or five. Coordinate permutation and rule symmetries preserve the number of disjoint rules, so the symmetry breaking is valid; no proof trace is available for the UNSAT outcomes, which remain solver-derived. This is finite structure only. It suggests the lower-bound target should charge the non-disjoint relay network for a small family of first-seed intersections that jointly cover the anchors, while allowing multiple seeds and all arbitrary endpoint choices.

[experiment_preseed_context_profiles.py](experiment_preseed_context_profiles.py) refines this test to minimal leaf contexts, with all disjoint rules removed. For each of the three n=5 radius-two witnesses, it checks every subset of literal generators, every listed carrier, and every anchor against direct closure; it also verifies that each combined seed context excludes every high point. Minimum first-seed context size is three throughout. Per activated anchor, minimal-context counts range from 1 to 10 for each one-seed cover, 1 to 8 for seed 0 of the two-seed cover, and 1 to 4 for seed 3. This is exhaustive finite verification of the context recurrence, not an asymptotic invariant.

### 55. Preseed proof-context antichains cross-check the seedless normal form (24 September 2026)

The new [experiment_preseed_context_profiles.py](experiment_preseed_context_profiles.py) computes minimal coordinate-leaf antichains after removing all disjoint rules. For each of three n=5 radius-two witnesses it checks every one of the $2^5$ selected-generator subsets, every carrier in the endpoint/intersection closure, and every cube anchor against direct non-disjoint closure. It also enumerates every combined premise context for every activated disjoint seed and verifies that no high point agrees with all its selected literals. Minimum combined context size is three. Context-count ranges are 1-10 for each one-seed witness, 1-8 for seed 0 in the two-seed witness, and 1-4 for seed 3. These exact finite checks distinguish the preseed certificate families from the larger, feedback-contaminated final fixed-point families; they do not establish a context-count lower bound.

### 56. The first-seed form refines the single-anchor leaf bound (24 September 2026)

Combining the first-seed normal form with the existing proof-DAG argument yields a sharper accounting identity. For a cover with $m$ rules and $d$ disjoint seeds, its non-disjoint relay core has $p=m-d$ rules. A first-seed proof for any low anchor uses at most $p$ core rules and one terminal seed; its connected binary proof DAG has at most $p+2$ literal leaves. The existing Gap-MCSP high-free-cylinder lower bound requires $N-o(N)$ leaves, so $p\ge N-o(N)-2$ and $m\ge N-o(N)-2+d$. This remains near-linear for small $d$ but explicitly charges additional disjoint outputs. It does not improve the asymptotic magnification threshold unless the seed count itself grows superlinearly or a stronger shared-core bound is found.

### 57. Flat certificate covers miss shared selector complexity (24 September 2026)

I tested a new intermediate object: the family of signed high-free subcubes produced as first-seed proof contexts. The script [experiment_sparse_ball_subcube_grammar.py](experiment_sparse_ball_subcube_grammar.py) independently reconstructs signed contexts from seedless closure and checks that each generated context excludes all high points. It also exactly solves the flat high-free cylinder cover problem for the n=4 and n=5 radius-two toys.

For $A=\{x:|x|\le k\}$ and $U=\{x:|x|>k\}$, the minimum high-free subcube cover is exactly $\binom nk$: each weight-$k$ anchor forces every one of its $n-k$ zero coordinates to be fixed to zero, and thus cannot share a high-free cylinder with another weight-$k$ anchor. One such cylinder per $k$-set covers the whole low ball. The measured values are 6 at $(n,k)=(4,2)$ and 10 at $(5,2)$. The exact n=4 two-rule, two-seed relay cover generates six distinct root contexts, four from each seed, and covers all 11 low points. The n=5 native, canonical-selector, and two-seed five-rule witnesses generate respectively 12, 15, and 15 signed root contexts; all are high-free and their unions cover the 16 low points.

This reveals why a flat certificate-count lower bound fails: a small shared program can generate many selector contexts. More generally, a bitonic sorting network gives an $O(n\log^2 n)$-rule relay cover for every Hamming threshold, although the flat cover number $\binom nk$ is exponential near the middle layer. At $n=64,k=32$, the latter is $1{,}832{,}624{,}140{,}942{,}590{,}534$, while the concrete Batcher network has 672 comparator rules. The construction is described and closure-checked on small dimensions in [experiment_threshold_sorting_cover.py](experiment_threshold_sorting_cover.py). Thus the first-seed antichain is a diagnostic, not a lower-bound measure that sees sharing.

The working term is **selector grammar**: a shared AND/OR derivation over signed literal leaves that generates high-free contexts, followed by terminal seed tests. This is a helpful normal form, but its support equations are already the monotone/cyclic-circuit formulation of relay covers; it is not claimed as a new invariant. The unpaid problem is a dual lower bound on the size of a shared grammar for the specific Gap-MCSP anchor family, robust to arbitrary endpoints, with a scale that transfers through magnification.

### 58. Co-occurrence graph blocks direct terminal splits (24 September 2026)

For two literal generators $B_g,B_h$, call them co-occurring if $B_g\cap B_h\cap U\ne\varnothing$. If a terminal seed has disjoint endpoints $E,H$, then it cannot have $B_g\subseteq E$ and $B_h\subseteq H$ for any co-occurring pair: their common high point would lie in $E\cap H$. The direct literal supports on opposite seed sides must therefore be anticomplete in this graph. This is an exact constraint on base-only contexts; it does not apply to compound contexts that a non-disjoint relay core has already produced.

On the Hamming threshold promise $A=\{x:|x|\le k\}$, $U=\{x:|x|\ge k+1\}$, every two zero slices co-occur once $n\ge k+3$, witnessed by the string with zeros in those two coordinates and ones elsewhere. At $(n,k)=(4,2)$ there are no co-occurrence edges among zero slices, and the exact two-seed witness uses cuts $\{0,1\}|\{2,3\}$ and $\{0,2\}|\{1,3\}$; their cross pairs cover all six minimal top-layer selectors. At $(5,2)$ the zero-slice graph is $K_5$. Any high-free context for a weight-two anchor must fix all three zero coordinates, so it cannot come from the two endpoints' base singleton leaves alone. The saved five-bit covers accordingly use core-generated premise contexts for their top-layer proofs. The same script checks the saved weight-at-most-one covers: their zero-slice overlap graphs are complete at n=4 and n=5 (6/6 and 10/10 edges), and their top-layer contexts also use a core-derived side; the relay sizes are four and six rules. These exhaustive toy checks confirm a mechanism, not a growth law. They explain why direct literal cuts stop working once high points realize every zero-coordinate pair, but give no growing lower bound because threshold functions have compact counting circuits.

I launched [experiment_sparse_ball_n6_two_seed.py](experiment_sparse_ball_n6_two_seed.py), a normalized, symmetry-reduced six-rule n=6 search requiring at least two disjoint terminal seeds, to check whether the n=5 multi-seed pattern persists. Session 76932 / PID 59596 is active and silent as of this audit entry. This runs alongside the original three n=6 searches and the transformed n=5 and CEGIS jobs. Preserve all sessions; any SAT witness must be checked in full closure and any UNSAT result remains solver-derived without a proof trace.

### 59. A separating-code upper bound for the one-zero high universe (24 September 2026)

For $U_n=\{x\in\{0,1\}^n:|x|\ge n-1\}$, every high point has at most one zero. Assign distinct $d$-bit labels to coordinates, where $d=\lceil\log_2 n\rceil$. For each bit position, make a disjoint rule whose endpoints are the unions of matching zero-slices on the two label classes. Each low anchor of weight at most $n-2$ has two zero coordinates with distinct labels, so one rule sees a matching generator on each side and derives the empty carrier; no high point triggers a rule. Therefore $\rho\le\lceil\log_2 n\rceil$ for this toy promise. In the restricted base-only cut construction, the bound is exact because every pair of coordinate labels must differ somewhere. This restricted lower bound does not apply to arbitrary relay endpoints.

The construction [experiment_near_full_cube_cut_cover.py](experiment_near_full_cube_cut_cover.py) directly verifies exact closure for every point in dimensions 2 through 12; the high universe has only $n+1$ points. For n=3 through 10, [experiment_near_full_cube_minimum_search.py](experiment_near_full_cube_minimum_search.py) finds solver-derived UNSAT for every smaller rule count, matching the constructed upper counts $2,2,3,3,3,3,4,4$. The raw solver outputs are saved as `near_full_cube_n*_m*_solver.txt`; no proof trace was produced. To keep symmetry reduction practical, I added `near_full_column_symmetry` to [experiment_relay_cover_pysat.py](experiment_relay_cover_pysat.py): sorting endpoint-membership columns of the $n$ single-zero points gives an $S_n$ orbit representative for this exact instance. [experiment_near_full_symmetry_crosscheck.py](experiment_near_full_symmetry_crosscheck.py) confirms identical SAT statuses to the original full-permutation symmetry encoding through n=7. This is a rigorous upper construction and finite lower-bound evidence for an easy threshold family, not progress on Gap-MCSP hardness. When the high set permits zeros on both sides of a cut, the direct endpoints overlap, so the one-zero argument does not extend as stated.

### 60. Endpoint signatures distinguish paired high witnesses (24 September 2026)

For the one-zero high universe $U_n=\{\mathbf1^n,u_1,\ldots,u_n\}$, any $m$-rule relay cover must give the points $u_i$ distinct membership signatures across its $2m$ endpoint sets. If $u_i,u_j$ have the same signature, then they also have the same membership in every rule intersection. For the low anchor with zeros exactly $i,j$, each matching zero-slice generator contained in a carrier is dominated by a matching generator of high anchor $u_i$: the $i$-slice is $\{u_i\}$, containment of the $j$-slice $\{u_j\}$ implies containment of $u_i$ by signature equality, and all other matching one-slices are identical. The initial carrier-support set for the low anchor is therefore contained in that for $u_i$, and the least-fixed-point support recurrence is monotone. This would force the forbidden high anchor to derive $\emptyset$. Thus $n\le2^{2m}$ and $m\ge\lceil\frac12\log_2 n\rceil$, even with arbitrary endpoint subsets and mixed relays.

This lower bound is within a factor of two of the cut construction and does not apply directly to Gap-MCSP. The proof also uses a special singleton-slice structure: each zero-slice generator is one high witness, so point membership determines generator containment. Gap-MCSP's literal slices contain many high tables, and matching point-membership signatures do not imply matching slice-containment flags. Its low hybrid anchors are also Hamming-near to the high witnesses, which the Gap-MCSP distance promise forbids. There is a scale ceiling as well: injectivity gives $m\ge\frac12\log_2|S|$ for witness family $S$, and $|S|\le2^N$ for truth tables of length $N$, so even the largest possible family gives only $m\ge N/2$; generally the bound is smaller. This falls short of the project's superlinear magnification target. This is a valid toy theorem and a clearer statement of the transfer obstacle, not a P-vs-NP breakthrough. The constructed endpoints' signatures are checked on n=2 through 12 in [experiment_near_full_cube_cut_cover.py](experiment_near_full_cube_cut_cover.py); the general signature argument is mathematical, while small exact minima remain solver-derived without proof traces.

### 61. Seed activation codes expose reciprocal multi-seed overlap (24 September 2026)

I tested whether terminal first-seed contexts from different anchors obey a cross-intersection bound. The initial size-bucketed diagnostic mixed contexts from different seeds and suggested no crossings. Preserving seed identity corrected the picture: within one seed, left and right contexts cannot share a high point because they are subsets of that seed's disjoint endpoints. This is only endpoint soundness. In the saved n=5 two-seed witness, a seed-0 left context and seed-3 right context share high point $10101_2$, while a seed-3 left context and seed-0 right context share $01011_2$. Thus the two seeds have reciprocal cross-overlap, ruling out seed ordering or pairwise independence as a general argument.

The exact first-seed decoder has a compact form. Remove every disjoint rule and close the literal generators using only the non-disjoint core. For each anchor $x$, let $L(x)$ be the disjoint seeds whose left endpoints are active in this closure and $R(x)$ those whose right endpoints are active. Then $x$ derives $\emptyset$ iff $L(x)\cap R(x)\ne\emptyset$: non-disjoint rules cannot produce the empty carrier, and the first disjoint rule that fires produces it immediately. I call $(L(x),R(x))$ the seed activation code.

[experiment_seed_activation_codes.py](experiment_seed_activation_codes.py) checks the decoder on six saved covers. The five one-seed examples use three or four distinct activation codes; the two-seed n=5 cover has 12 codes, five assigned to low-anchor classes and seven to high-anchor classes. [experiment_context_split_cross_compatibility.py](experiment_context_split_cross_compatibility.py) gives the explicit reciprocal high crossings. This language describes the final seed test but is only a re-expression of first-seed normal form: many low anchors collapse to one code, so code counts do not lower-bound the shared non-disjoint core.

### 62. Initial-support dominance becomes a carrier-cut cover (24 September 2026)

For each anchor $x$, let $S_x$ be the set of tracked carriers activated initially by its matching literal slices. If $x$ is low and $y$ high, validity requires $S_x\nsubseteq S_y$; otherwise monotonicity of the closure recurrence transfers the low derivation to $y$. Therefore every low/high anchor pair must be separated by some carrier predicate, and each carrier $C$ defines a rectangle $\{x\in A:C\in S_x\}\times\{y\in U:C\notin S_y\}$ covering the pairs it separates. There are at most $3m+1$ distinct carriers for $m$ rules.

The exact cover search [experiment_support_dominance_cut_cover.py](experiment_support_dominance_cut_cover.py) finds minimum necessary carrier-predicate set covers of 5, 5, 8, 6, 5, and 5 on six saved witnesses of sizes 4, 6, 5, 5, 5, and 5 rules. The largest single-carrier pair coverage ranges from 18/55 to 121/256. All six high universes also satisfy literal-slice noncontainment: no distinct literal slice is contained in another. Under that condition, the $2N$ literal slices provide coordinate cuts separating every distinct low/high pair: choose a coordinate where the anchors differ and the slice matching the low anchor. The script's exact minima using these literal cuts are 4, 5, 8, 5, 5, and 5.

The same condition holds for the actual Gap-MCSP promise at sufficiently large $N$. The high set is $Z_{n,\beta}=\{z:\operatorname{CC}(z)\ge s_2\}$; its complement has at most $2^{O(s_2\log(n+s_2))}=2^{O(N^\beta\log N)}=2^{o(N)}$ tables for fixed $\beta<1$. A face fixing any two coordinates has $2^{N-2}$ tables, so it contains a high-complexity point. This proves distinct literal-slice noncontainment and makes the $2N$ coordinate cuts a linear-size witness for the pair-separation relaxation on the target promise. They need not compose into a valid relay. Therefore pair separation alone cannot yield the desired superlinear lower bound; a viable invariant must charge the actual rule recurrence, core sharing, or seed composition.

### 63. Branching-program lower bounds lose the relay parameter (24 September 2026)

I tested whether known MCSP lower bounds against branching programs can bypass the missing De Morgan circuit lower bound. Each endpoint $C$ induces the clause $\phi_C(x)=\bigvee_{i:B_{i,0}\subseteq C}[x_i=0]\vee\bigvee_{i:B_{i,1}\subseteq C}[x_i=1]$ on an anchor. The vector of these $2m$ endpoint clauses gives the initial premise-state vector. The non-disjoint fixed-point closure and terminal-seed test are a fixed Boolean function $g_R$ of that vector, so the relay's acceptance factors exactly as $g_R(\phi_{E_1},\phi_{H_1},\ldots,\phi_{E_m},\phi_{H_m})$.

This gives a read-once oblivious branching program: scan the $N$ input bits, update the $2m$ clause-satisfaction flags, and let each final state accept or reject according to the precomputed closure. Its width is at most $2^{2m}$ and its size is $O(N2^{2m})$. This is valid for arbitrary endpoints and the whole cyclic core because the final state is labeled by the closure result; no explicit closure-time overhead is needed.

Cheraghchi et al. prove almost-quadratic size lower bounds for general branching programs computing total MCSP ([ICALP 2019](https://drops.dagstuhl.de/entities/document/10.4230/LIPIcs.ICALP.2019.39)); this transfers at most $m=\Omega(\log N)$. A later result proves a $2^{\widetilde\Omega(N)}$ lower bound for read-once co-nondeterministic branching programs for total MCSP ([Cheraghchi et al., STACS 2021](https://drops.dagstuhl.de/entities/document/10.4230/LIPIcs.STACS.2021.23)); even if the theorem applied directly to this promise, it would yield only $m=\widetilde\Omega(N)$. It does not directly apply because the relay is only required to separate the promised low and high sets. The existing $N-o(N)$ proof-DAG bound is stronger. Close this route as a source of superlinear cover bounds; reopen only if it yields a smaller state encoding or a promise-specific lower bound beyond the linear scale.

### 64. Clause shadows are dense-complete, but residue controls relay geometry (24 September 2026)

I separated each carrier's input-visible clause shadow from its remaining set geometry. For $C\subseteq Z$, let $\operatorname{sh}(C)=\{(i,b):B_{i,b}\subseteq C\}$. Its initial activation predicate is exactly the disjunction of the corresponding anchor literals. For any consistent selected set $S$, the carrier $C_S=\bigcup_{(i,b)\in S}B_{i,b}$ has no unintended literal in its shadow whenever $2^{N-|S|-1}>|\{0,1\}^N\setminus Z|$: for each omitted literal, the face satisfying it and falsifying $S$ has at least that many tables, so it contains a high table. Since $|Z^c|=2^{O(N^\beta\log N)}$ on the Gap-MCSP promise, this realizes all consistent clauses through width $N-O(N^\beta\log N)$ as initial endpoint tests.

The exhaustive audit [experiment_clause_shadow_completeness.py](experiment_clause_shadow_completeness.py) enumerates all 3^n consistent profiles for six saved finite witnesses. The excluded-table counts are 5, 6, 5, 16, 16, and 16; the respective density-safe maximum widths are 0, 1, 0, -1, -1, and -1. Every clause within a nonvacuous safe range is exact. Larger-width exactness varies by witness, as expected. The script also constructs a same-shadow residue witness: $\varnothing$ and a singleton high-point carrier both have empty shadow when every literal slice has size at least two, but the premise intersection $\{z\}\cap\{z\}$ is contained in the singleton consequent and not in the empty one.

Thus the branching-program state records the entire input-dependent activation but forgets static endpoint incidence. Arbitrary residual carrier elements can change $E\cap H\subseteq C$ without changing any clause flag, so a clause-only lower bound cannot control the actual decoder. The next useful theory must couple clause shadows to residual-intersection constraints, or prove a full-closure-preserving normalization that charges those residuals. Replacing carriers by the union of their shadow slices is not valid without such a lemma. This is a structural diagnosis and exact density result, not progress past the current $N-o(N)$ relay lower bound.

### 65. Normalize arbitrary endpoints into proof faces and point witnesses (24 September 2026)

I derived an exact normal form for the residual interaction layer. For each endpoint $C_j$, let $\mathcal D_j$ be the least family of consistent partial assignments generated by its contained literal slices and the relay rules: a rule with premises $a_r,b_r$ combines compatible contexts $P\in\mathcal D_{a_r}$ and $Q\in\mathcal D_{b_r}$ into $P\cup Q$ for every endpoint $j$ containing the original meet $M_r=C_{a_r}\cap C_{b_r}$. Every resulting high face $B_P$ is a subset of $C_j$.

Choose one high point in $M_r\setminus C_j$ for every false rule-to-endpoint inclusion, and one point in each nonempty rule meet. There are at most $2m^2+m$ selected points. Replace $C_j$ by the union of all generated faces $B_P$ and the selected points that originally belonged to $C_j$. Each replacement is a subset of its original endpoint. It preserves all literal-slice flags. It also preserves every inclusion bit: false inclusions retain their witnesses; true inclusions involving generated faces follow from context composition; true inclusions involving selected points follow from retaining their original membership. Shrinking preserves empty meets, and the selected point preserves every nonempty meet. Therefore the complete endpoint transition tensor and all anchor closures are unchanged.

[experiment_relay_context_normal_form.py](experiment_relay_context_normal_form.py) checks the construction on six saved covers. It verifies all rule-to-endpoint inclusion bits, meet nonemptiness, per-anchor endpoint closure, and empty-carrier acceptance across all 16 or 32 inputs in each toy. The generated context totals are 77, 118, 197, 260, 200, and 189; the point-witness counts are 5, 9, 7, 7, 7, and 6, all below $2m^2+m$.

This is a positive structural result about the relay model, not a P-vs-NP proof and not yet a superlinear lower bound. Context families may still have exponentially many faces, and the point witnesses are arbitrary high tables. It narrows the next target to the complexity of the shared context grammar together with its finite static witness layer; charging the residue by cardinality alone will fail because a single point can flip an inclusion bit.

### 68. Any Boolean circuit separator compiles to a linear-rule relay (24 September 2026)

This is the strongest structural connection found in this turn. Given any De Morgan circuit $C$ of size $s$ separating low anchors $Y$ from high universe $Z$, make a monotone dual-rail circuit on $y_{i,b}=[x_i=b]$ computing $C$ on valid paired inputs. For each gate $g$, set $E_g=\{z\in Z:g(\iota(z))=1\}$. Each original AND gate yields one positive AND and one negative OR gate; each OR yields one positive OR and one negative AND, so at most two monotone gates per original gate. Realize an AND node by the rule on its two child carriers, whose intersection is its gate carrier. Realize an OR node with two rules pairing each child carrier with the top carrier $Z$; the child carriers are subsets of the OR carrier. NOT gates just swap dual rails. A dummy output pair, when needed, names the final carrier. This uses at most $3s+1$ rules for fan-in-two De Morgan circuits.

For every $x\in Y$, matching literal-slice carriers start active and the relay derives each true gate; the output gate's carrier is empty because $C$ rejects all of $Z$, so the empty carrier is derived. For any fixed $z\in Z$, every initially active gate carrier contains $z$; rule firing preserves this because the active premises' intersection contains $z$ and each output carrier contains that intersection. Thus the empty carrier cannot activate on any high anchor, including through an internal disjoint gate. This proves the circuit-to-relay upper bound for arbitrary promise separators. The reverse simulation from Idea 107 gives a fan-in-two circuit separator of size $O(mN+m^3)$ from an $m$-rule relay. The two measures are therefore polynomially related on the same promise, and a superlinear relay lower bound would entail a superlinear general-circuit lower bound for Gap-MCSP.

The finite calibration [experiment_dualrail_parity_relay.py](experiment_dualrail_parity_relay.py) builds parity relays for $N=2,\ldots,6$. It verifies exact full closure on all $2^N$ anchors with $8N-7$ rules. Their core antichains produce exactly $2^{N-1}$ distinct width-$N$ high-free seed cubes, one for each odd input. This directly falsifies any proposed lower bound based only on the number of context cubes and confirms the linear circuit-to-relay compilation on a non-CNF function.

This does not solve P versus NP; it reveals that this route is closely tied to general circuit lower bounds, which explains why the relay-only combinatorics has resisted superlinear progress. Continue only with a circuit lower-bound idea specific to the Gap-MCSP promise or a nontrivial advantage from the relay side of the polynomial equivalence. The six long SAT/CEGIS searches were still live when this historical entry was written; they were later stopped or had exited without a new verified result.

### 66. The relay is a paired-literal Horn circuit; generic monotonicity stalls (24 September 2026)

On $2N$ variables $y_{i,b}$, an endpoint's initial activation is the monotone clause $\phi_j(y)=\bigvee_{(i,b)\in\operatorname{sh}(C_j)}y_{i,b}$. Removing terminal disjoint seeds, the core is a least fixed point of positive Horn implications: each round activates endpoint $j$ if it was active already or if both premises of a core rule are active and the rule meet is contained in $C_j$. At most $2m$ rounds are needed. A direct fan-in-two unrolling uses $O(mN+m^3)$ gates. On the paired inputs $y_{i,b}=[x_i=b]$, it computes exactly the relay's promise classifier.

The exact checker [experiment_dualrail_horn_model.py](experiment_dualrail_horn_model.py) evaluates every $2^{2N}$ dual-rail input in six saved covers, verifies monotonicity on all single-bit upward edges, and checks exact agreement with the anchor labels on every paired input. Its concrete upper bounds range from 132 to 350 fan-in-two gates for these small witnesses.

This does not yield a new lower bound. Any De Morgan circuit can be made monotone on paired positive/negative input variables with only constant-factor gate overhead, and substitution back gives the converse up to $N$ input negations. So generic monotone-circuit complexity on the paired code is essentially ordinary circuit complexity. Adding a monotone threshold wrapper extends the relay function to a monotone middle-slice function on $2N$ bits, but Austrin--Risse's result is a Sum-of-Squares proof-degree lower bound for refuting small monotone circuits, not a circuit-size lower bound ([CCC 2023](https://drops.dagstuhl.de/entities/document/10.4230/LIPIcs.CCC.2023.31)). The nearly quadratic MCSP lower bounds for arbitrary-basis formulas and general branching programs do not give a general circuit lower bound ([Cheraghchi et al., ICALP 2019](https://drops.dagstuhl.de/entities/document/10.4230/LIPIcs.ICALP.2019.39)). The only potentially useful gap is the relay's special Horn implication tensor, coupled to its endpoint clauses; a proof that forgets this special structure has reverted to ordinary circuit lower bounds.

This closes generic paired-slice monotonicity as a standalone path. Continue with Idea 106's context grammar and prove a bound that uses the actual incidence tensor, or find a promise-specific lower bound for that Horn model. The six long solver searches remain active and have not emitted new SAT/UNSAT results at this turn.

### 67. Dominance-pruned context frontiers give exact high-free seed cubes (24 September 2026)

The proof-context grammar has an exact antichain quotient. If $P\subset Q$, then $P$ matches every anchor matching $Q$ and its high face contains $B_Q$. In a binary context production, replacing $Q$ by $P$ preserves compatibility and produces the less restrictive union $P\cup R\subseteq Q\cup R$. So each endpoint's support is represented exactly by its inclusion-minimal contexts, computable directly without enumerating dominated terms.

Using first-seed normal form, I recomputed these frontiers using only non-disjoint rules. For any terminal disjoint seed $(E,H)$ and compatible contexts $P,Q$ at its endpoints, $B_{P\cup Q}=B_P\cap B_Q\subseteq E\cap H=\varnothing$. The anchor subcubes matching these contexts are therefore high-free, and their union equals the relay's accepted set. [experiment_seed_context_frontier.py](experiment_seed_context_frontier.py) checks this identity against full carrier closure for all 16 or 32 inputs in six saved witnesses. It finds 4, 5, 5, 10, 10, and 12 minimal seed cubes; all have empty high face. The context normalizer independently reduces full context totals 77, 118, 197, 260, 200, and 189 to minimal antichains of 19, 31, 35, 41, 40, and 47 while preserving rule tensors and closures.

The candidate lower bound is now a shared-grammar problem: the number of required high-free cubes is at most the sum of seed-frontier products $\sum_{(a,b)\in D}|\mathcal D_a||\mathcal D_b|$. Cube count alone has a $2^N$ ceiling and cannot establish a superlinear relay bound; a useful result must charge how a small Horn grammar generates and shares those frontiers. This is an exact finite-language reduction, not a Gap-MCSP lower bound.

### 69. A coordinate-query floor explains the missing superlinear term (24 September 2026)

Let $N=2^n$, and let $Z=\{f:\operatorname{CC}(f)\ge s_2\}$. Circuit counting gives $|\{f:\operatorname{CC}(f)<s_2\}|\le 2^{K}$ for some $K=O(s_2\log(s_2+n))$. Suppose a deterministic adaptive coordinate-query algorithm correctly distinguishes every table of complexity at most $s_1$ from every table in $Z$. Run it on a low table $f$, and let $Q$ be the distinct coordinates queried along that path. If $N-|Q|>K$, the cylinder of all completions agreeing with $f$ on $Q$ has more than $2^K$ members, so it contains a table of complexity at least $s_2$. That completion gives the same transcript and answer as $f$, a contradiction. Thus every low input forces at least $N-K=N-O(s_2\log(s_2+n))$ coordinate queries. In the magnification regime $s_2=N^\beta$ for fixed $\beta<1$, this is $N-o(N)$.

This theorem is only about deterministic query trees. It does not imply a superlinear Boolean-circuit lower bound: a circuit can inspect all $N$ input bits using $O(N)$ gates. It does explain why sublinear sampling, fixed local restrictions, and per-anchor sparse tests cannot by themselves resolve the promise. The magnification gap lies beyond the globality floor: a proof must show that combining almost all truth-table coordinates into the small-circuit-versus-high-circuit decision costs $N^{1+\epsilon}$ gates.

A patching calculation gives a related robustness estimate. If $f$ differs from a size-$s_1$ table $g$ on $t$ rows, circuit $g$ can be changed on those rows using $t$ equality tests of $O(n)$ gates each and a final XOR. Hence $\operatorname{CC}(f)\le s_1+O(nt+n)$. When $s_2-s_1\gg n$, any high table is therefore $\Omega((s_2-s_1)/n)$ rows from every low table. At the target parameters, the relative distance lower bound is only $\Omega(N^{\beta-1}/n)$. The class of size-$s_1$ circuits has description/VC dimension $O(s_1\log(s_1+n))=O(N^\beta)$, so the standard uniform-sampling estimate scales as $O(N\operatorname{polylog}N)$ and yields no sublinear test. This is an inference from the standard patching construction and sample-complexity bound, not a lower bound against every randomized test.

The working diagnosis is now **global integration debt**: the current proof-DAG, query, and pair-separation methods reach only the near-linear cost of exposing enough coordinates or leaves. The still-unpaid step is to prove superlinear circuit cost for integrating the global condition $\exists c\ (|c|<s_1)\ \forall i\ C_c(i)=f_i$, with the high side requiring $\forall c\ (|c|<s_2)\ \exists i\ C_c(i)\ne f_i$. I added a five-part audit vector $(L,A,U,H,T)$ to `ideas.md`: description length, semantic access, uniform construction, cross-anchor sharing, and transfer/magnification. This organizes project failure modes; it is not a new complexity measure.

An exact Skolemization makes the high-side bottleneck concrete: $f$ is high iff there is a total selector $S:\mathcal C_{<s_2}\to[N]$ with $C_c(S(c))\ne f_{S(c)}$ for every $c$. The first mismatching coordinate always defines such an $S$, so this is a reformulation, not a result. The uncompressed table has $2^{O(s_2\log(s_2+n))}$ entries. A direct uniform selector circuit evaluates each candidate circuit on the full table and costs about $N$ times a universal-circuit evaluation; verifying totality still expresses the universal quantifier over circuit codes. The concrete creative target is therefore a short, proof-carrying compression of this selector that does not call the original Gap-MCSP predicate. PCP/IOP, coding, and cryptographic methods are possible construction languages; none has yet met the size and verification obligations.

Current literature also supplies a conditional proof-complexity/cryptography connection worth tracking. Goldberg, Juvekar, and Kabanets' June 2026 ECCC report obtains conditional NP-hardness for variants of implicit MCSP and full-support learning from subexponential indistinguishability obfuscation and a no-infinitely-often-subexponentially-optimal proof-system assumption; the reductions are half-Levin, so they map SAT witnesses forward without efficiently recovering SAT witnesses from target witnesses. This assumption already implies $\mathrm{NP}\ne\mathrm{coNP}$, hence $\mathrm P\ne\mathrm{NP}$: $\mathrm{NP}=\mathrm{coNP}$ would give a polynomially bounded proof system and therefore a p-optimal one. Thus the report's result is conditional meta-complexity progress, not an unconditional escape from P-vs-NP; it also concerns implicit encodings, and its reduction code is nonconstructive at one step. A potentially reusable technique is to use witness non-recovery rather than a full Levin reduction. See [ECCC TR26-091](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2026/091/download/). The locality barrier relevant to magnification is analyzed by Chen et al. ([arXiv:1911.08297](https://arxiv.org/abs/1911.08297)); the exact Gap-MCSP-to-$P\ne NP$ threshold remains Oliveira, Pich, and Santhanam's theorem ([Theory of Computing](https://www.theoryofcomputing.org/articles/v017a011/)).

Validation: `experiment_arbitrary_promise_relay_compiler.py` stress-tests Idea 109 on 558 random finite promises for arbitrary AND/OR/NOT circuits of 2--5 inputs; all closures accept every selected low anchor and reject every selected high anchor. Of these, 446 have at least one empty high-universe literal slice, so the test checks the sparse-promise edge case. This supports the finite compiler implementation but is not a proof of the general construction. ### 70. Partial-function realization sharpens the selector-image floor (24 September 2026)

Idea 110's Skolem selector has an exact finite image invariant. For a high truth table $f$ and a low-circuit class $\mathcal C_{<s}$, let $\tau_s(f)$ be the minimum size of a row set $Q$ that intersects every disagreement set $\{x:C(x)\ne f(x)\}$ for $C\in\mathcal C_{<s}$. Every selector $S(C)$ induces such a set $Q=\operatorname{im}(S)$; conversely, any such $Q$ gives an abstract selector by choosing the first mismatch for each circuit. Thus $\tau_s(f)$ is exactly the minimum image size, without making the selector computable.

This is a specialization of the exclusion/specifying-set measures from exact learning (Angluin, *Queries Revisited*, ALT 2001). The standard exclusion-set convention may allow one low hypothesis to remain consistent; one extra row separates that hypothesis from the outside target, so our zero-consistency certificate size differs by at most one. We claim no new combinatorial measure here.

Andreev's partial Boolean function theorem gives a useful circuit-realization upper bound: a partial labeling on $q$ points of the $n$-cube has some total completion of size $O(q/\log(q+1)+n)$ in a fixed complete nondegenerate bounded-fan-in basis. Since $f|_Q$ has no completion below size $s$, it follows that every high table has $\tau_s(f)=\Omega(s\log s)$ when $s\gg n$. This is a distinct global certificate-size bound. Idea 110 separately lower-bounds the Hamming distance from one low table by $\Omega((s_2-s_1)/n)$; that distance estimate does not itself lower-bound $\tau_s(f)$. At $s=N^\beta$, $N=2^n$, the selector-image floor is $\Omega(ns)$, while the earlier estimate concerns a different quantity.

A matching best-case order follows from counting, not an efficient construction. There are at most $2^{O(s\log(s+n))}$ low circuits. On a fixed set of slightly more than $O(s\log(s+n))$ rows, fewer low circuits than labelings means some partial labeling is consistent with none. Any total completion is high and admits an abstract selector whose image is that row set. Hence, for $s\gg n$ and $s\log(s+n)=o(N)$, the minimum $\tau_s(f)$ over high tables is $\Theta(s\log s)$. This says nothing about the maximum over high tables, which may still be as large as $N$.

The verification side has a hard literature barrier. Partial MCSP asks whether any size-$s$ circuit agrees with a partial truth table on all defined rows. Hirahara proved partial MCSP NP-hard under randomized polynomial-time many-one reductions ([FOCS 2022 paper](https://ieee-focs.org/FOCS-2022-Papers/pdfs/FOCS2022-4Bu7jGV9xIcveUWYj3oWoi/551900a968/551900a968.pdf); [ECCC TR22-119](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2022/119/)). Given a proposed selector image $Q$, validity is exactly a NO instance of partial MCSP for $f|_Q$ at threshold $s-1$. Conversely, any partial instance $p$ becomes such a check by filling its undefined rows arbitrarily to form $f$ and taking $Q=\operatorname{dom}(p)$. Therefore checking a general exclusion set is coNP-hard under randomized reductions. Shrinking $Q$ does not by itself create an NP-verifiable certificate. A useful route must exploit additional structure relating $Q$ to the full table or introduce a proof system for this no-fit claim; neither has been found.

The finite formula enumeration was expanded and rerun with exact closure. For $n=3$, the minimum/maximum selector-image sizes for high tables at cutoffs 1 through 5 were $4/4$, $4/6$, $5/6$, $6/8$, and $7/7$ rows; four promise-gap tests had minimum/median/maximum $4/4/4$, $4/4/4$, $4/4/4$, and $5/5/5$. For $n=4$, exhaustive formula profiles at cutoffs 2 and 4 had respectively 314 low and 65,222 high tables with selector minimum/median/maximum $4/4/6$, and 3,302 low and 62,234 high tables with $6/6/8$.

The separate [shared-circuit enumerator](experiment_circuit_high_selector_toy.py) represents each state by its available truth-table signals, so arbitrary fanout and internal sharing are included. On $n=3$, it reproduces the formula function classes and selector profiles through four gates. On $n=4$, the circuit function classes equal the formula classes through four gates; the final layer has 998,524 distinct signal-set states. This is only a finite non-effect result: no sharing advantage appeared at these sizes, and it gives no general equivalence theorem.

The new fundamental obstruction is precise: short exclusion data can exist, yet checking it means ruling out every small circuit consistent with the labeled rows. This remains a universal circuit-synthesis question. The theorem lowers the certificate-existence floor and makes it asymptotically sharp for some high tables; it does not supply an efficient selector, verifier, or P-vs-NP separation.

Sources: Andreev, â€œThe complexity of realizing partial Boolean functions by schemes consisting of functional elements,â€ *Discrete Mathematics and Applications* 1(3), 251â€“261 (1991), [MathNet record and theorem abstract](https://www.mathnet.ru/eng/dm939); Angluin, â€œQueries Revisited,â€ ALT 2001, [Springer chapter](https://doi.org/10.1007/3-540-45583-3_3).
