### 71. Minimum selector images form punctured critical samples (24 September 2026)

If $Q$ has minimum cardinality among row sets that reject every circuit of size below $s$, then for every $x\in Q$ there is a low circuit agreeing with $f$ on $Q\setminus\{x\}$. It has to disagree at $x$. Thus the low-circuit trace on $Q$ contains every one-bit neighbor of $f|_Q$ but omits $f|_Q$. In SAT language, circuit synthesis on the full sample is unsatisfiable, while removing any one labeled example makes it satisfiable. This is a group-minimal unsatisfiable instance with explicit near-miss witnesses.

The associated partial circuit complexity lies in $[s,s+O(n)]$: the lower bound follows from the exclusion property; an upper bound patches a low circuit for any one-row deletion with an $O(n)$-gate equality test at the deleted row. This makes an inclusion-minimal selector image a threshold-critical partial function. Also, the counting construction from Section 70 can be pruned by deleting redundant rows; every remaining exclusion set still has $\Omega(s\log s)$ rows by Andreev, and its original size was $O(s\log s)$. Thus some high tables have inclusion-minimal critical samples of size $\Theta(s\log s)$, without an efficient construction.

The expanded exact toy verifies a concrete case: at $n=4$, formula cutoff $s=5$, the maximum selector image has eight rows. One example is truth table $0x03c0$ restricted to even-indexed rows $Q=\{0,2,4,6,8,10,12,14\}$. No formula of at most four AND/OR gates matches the restriction, every single-row deletion has a matching low formula, and the minimum formula completion has five gates. The script checks all high tables for the cutoff-4 profile and the punctured-star condition for this selected maximum example.

This does not produce a proof of non-realizability: the one-row near-miss circuits do not rule out an additional circuit matching every row. It suggests a proof-complexity target?derive a compact refutation of the full synthesis constraints from the critical boundary structure. Partial MCSP* is NP-hard under randomized reductions, so any generic certificate verifier remains blocked; a viable result would need a new invariant specific to these threshold-critical samples.

### 72. Trace-hole geometry exposes a small code-shaped obstruction (24 September 2026)

The follow-up to Section 71 tested whether the selected critical sample had an isolated missing labeling. It does not: for the even-row set $Q=\{0,2,4,6,8,10,12,14\}$ and target from $0x03c0$, the four-input, at-most-four-gate formula class realizes 206 of the 256 row labelings. The target is one of 50 missing patterns. This falsifies the unique-hole hypothesis for that sample.

I added [experiment_critical_selector_trace_geometry.py](experiment_critical_selector_trace_geometry.py), which exactly enumerates the 3,302 functions in the four-input De Morgan formula class of at most four AND/OR gates. It scans all 12,870 eight-row sets. The minimum number of omitted trace patterns is eight, attained by 74 row sets. For 10 of these 74 sets, the holes form an affine coset of a binary $[8,3,4]$ linear code with weight enumerator $1+6z^4+z^8$; for the other 64, they have eight holes but not this code geometry. I am not using a standard family name for the code. Thus the coding pattern is not a universal explanation even in this small formula model.

One code-shaped example is the even-parity input set $Q=\{0,3,5,6,9,10,12,15\}$ with target restriction $p=0x81$. The exact hole set is

$$H_5(Q)=\{0x18,0x24,0x42,0x7e,0x81,0xbd,0xdb,0xe7\}.$$

After translating by $p$, the holes are the span of $0x99,0xa5,0xc3$, with codeword weights $0$, six words of weight $4$, and one word of weight $8$. In particular, none of the one-bit neighbors of $p$ is a hole. The experiment reconstructs explicit low formulas for every one-row deletion: row 0 uses $x_0x_1x_2$; rows 3, 5, 6, 9, 10, 12 use, respectively, $(x_0x_1)\lor(\neg x_2\neg x_3)$, $(x_0x_2)\lor(\neg x_1\neg x_3)$, $(\neg x_0\neg x_3)\lor(x_1x_2)$, $(x_0x_3)\lor(\neg x_1\neg x_2)$, $(\neg x_0\neg x_2)\lor(x_1x_3)$, and $(\neg x_0\neg x_1)\lor(x_2x_3)$; row 15 uses $\neg x_0\neg x_1\neg x_2$. These witnesses have at most three gates.

The total table $f=0x8181$, whose one-input rows are $0,7,8,15$, extends that partial labeling. It has the five-gate formula $(\neg x_0\neg x_1\neg x_2)\lor(x_0x_1x_2)$ and the exact selector profile gives $\tau_5(f)=8$. This is a genuine maximum-selector example in the finite formula model, with the chosen eight-row trace omitting only eight of 256 patterns.

The global-minimum check is essential. One-row-deletion witnesses only show that $Q$ is minimal among subsets of itself. Across the 592 eight-row partial labelings from the 74 best traces that pass this deletion test, each has at least one total extension with selector size six; only 16 admit a total extension with selector size eight. The table $0x8181$ is one of those 16. This quantifies how a locally critical partial sample can lose global selector optimality when its unspecified rows are completed.

**Assessment:** this is an exact small-scale coding pattern and a useful new language for the experiments: the low class has a trace $T_s(Q)$ and an omitted-pattern set $H_s(Q)$. It is not an asymptotic circuit lower bound. A scalable route must explain why all low circuits miss the designated code-shaped target, exhibit an explicit full table, and rule out every alternative smaller selector. Distance or code structure alone does none of these tasks.


### 73. The code-shaped critical hole did not lift directly to five inputs (24 September 2026)

To test whether Section 72 was an artifact of four inputs, I added [experiment_five_input_trace_criticality.py](experiment_five_input_trace_criticality.py). It exactly enumerates all 147,136 five-input De Morgan formulas with at most five AND/OR gates and counts critical holes: missing trace patterns whose entire one-bit neighborhood is realized by low formulas.

On the 16 even-parity rows, the low class realizes 28,584 labelings and omits 36,952, but none of the holes is critical. Adding any of four tested odd rows gives 34,106 realized patterns and 96,966 holes, again with no critical holes. On seeded samples of 10 row sets for each size $q=12$ through $17$, and another 24 row sets at $q=17$, no critical hole appeared. A greedy procedure that chooses each next row to maximize the number of distinct formula traces reaches 22,848 patterns on 17 rows, also with no critical hole.

This is a strong falsification of the naive parity-slice/code-coset lift, not a theorem ruling out all five-input row sets. The greedy rule optimizes trace count locally, and the random samples cover only a tiny fraction of the row-set space. The evidence says the four-input example relies on a near-complete low-class trace: its 8-row restriction realizes 248 of 256 labels, whereas the tested five-input traces remain sparse or have many neighboring holes. Next work should seek a principled way to build a dense trace boundary, rather than assume that parity rows or random selection preserve the finite code pattern.


### 74. Scale-matched polarity budget gives an exact formula selector family (25 September 2026)

The fixed five-gate cutoff in Section 73 does not match the formula complexity of a growing target. I tested a scale-matched family and found an exact theorem for De Morgan formulas. This corrects the scope of Section 73: its negative result rejects the fixed-cutoff parity-row lift on the tested samples, not all larger-input critical-sample constructions.

For $m\ge3$, use $m+1$ input bits $(a,y_1,\ldots,y_m)$ and define

$$F_m(a,y)=1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad y=0^m\text{ or }y=1^m.$$

Formulas have signed literals at leaves and binary AND/OR gates, with no sharing. The exact formula complexity is $2m-1$. The endpoint-minterm formula gives the upper bound. For the lower bound, at $y=0^m$ each change $y_i:0\to1$ changes output $1\to0$, requiring a negative occurrence of $y_i$; at $y=1^m$ the same change switches output $0\to1$, requiring a positive occurrence. The formula therefore has at least $2m$ literal leaves and at least $2m-1$ binary gates.

Define

$$Q_m=\{(1,y):|y|\le1\}\cup\{(0,y):|y|\ge m-1\}.$$

This contains $2m+2$ rows. Its falling edges $(1,0^m)\to(1,e_i)$ and rising edges $(0,1^m-e_i)\to(0,1^m)$ force both signs of every $y_i$ in any matching formula. Thus $F_m|_{Q_m}$ is absent from all formulas with at most $2m-2$ gates.

For the matching selector lower bound, there are $2m+2$ low formulas, one for each endpoint-neighborhood vector $y$, that disagree with $F_m$ on exactly the two-row fiber $\{(0,y),(1,y)\}$:

* at $y=0^m$, use $\bigwedge_i y_i$;
* at $y=1^m$, use $\bigwedge_i\neg y_i$;
* at $y=e_i$, use $(\bigwedge_{j\ne i}\neg y_j)\lor(\bigwedge_j y_j)$;
* at $y=1^m-e_i$, use $(\bigwedge_j\neg y_j)\lor(\bigwedge_{j\ne i}y_j)$.

Every such formula has at most $2m-2$ gates. The disagreement pairs are disjoint, so any selector image against all formulas below size $2m-1$ needs at least $2m+2$ rows. The sample $Q_m$ takes one row from every pair and excludes the whole low class. Hence the exact formula selector image is

$$\tau^{\mathrm{formula}}_{2m-1}(F_m)=2m+2.$$

The verifier [experiment_antipodal_formula_selector.py](experiment_antipodal_formula_selector.py) checks the exact finite class and witnesses at $m=3,4$: respectively 3,302 and 662,176 low functions, selector sizes 8 and 10, and target tables `0xC003` and `0xC0000003`. The all-$m$ claim follows from the argument above, not from enumerating those cases. In particular, at $m=4$ this gives a scale-matched five-input, six-gate low cutoff, whereas Section 73 tested a five-gate cutoff.

**Model update.** The trace-hole mechanism can be generated by two ingredients: a polarity budget forcing both signs of each variable, and a packing of disjoint disagreement supports that lower-bounds every selector. This offers a simpler explanation for critical trace samples than treating code geometry as the source of the obstruction. The result concerns formulas and is only linear in $m$. It does not lower-bound general circuit size: the formula leaf-to-gate identity fails with fanout, and shared subcomputations may serve multiple obligations. A circuit analogue would need a gate-charging invariant that survives arbitrary sharing, and a P-vs-NP route would need a much stronger bound plus an established transfer.

**Transfer-scale audit.** Here $n=m+1$, $N=2^{m+1}$, target size $s=2m-1=\Theta(n)=\Theta(\log N)$, and selector size $2m+2=\Theta(\log N)$. This is far below the $s=N^\beta$ regime used by the project's hardness-magnification route. Therefore an all-$m$ theorem for this hand-built family, even if extended to circuits, would not satisfy that route's premise.

**Novelty status.** The polarity argument is elementary and resembles standard variable-occurrence lower bounds for formula size. The all-equal function itself is standard terminology; for example, KÃ¶hler-Schindler and Steif study it as a Boolean-function example in distributional complexity ([paper, Example 2.8](https://research.chalmers.se/publication/551459/file/551459_Fulltext.pdf)). That paper is not a source for the selector identity. The specific family/sample and exact selector statement have not been established as new in the literature; no novelty claim is made. The value here is a proved project lemma, a cleaner model of criticality, and a target for studying sharing. A focused literature search is still needed before any publication-level novelty statement.


### 75. Shared-circuit synthesis preserves the first two antipodal selector examples (25 September 2026)

The formula proof leaves open whether sharing defeats the boundary sample. I added [experiment_circuit_selector_antipodal.py](experiment_circuit_selector_antipodal.py), an exact bounded circuit-synthesis encoding. Circuits are topologically ordered and may use arbitrary fanout, free constants and signed input literals, binary AND/OR gates, unary NOT gates, and any available signal as output. The gate slots may be unused, so a $g$-slot satisfiable instance represents every circuit of at most $g$ gates. Any returned circuit is evaluated independently on all labeled rows.

For $m=3$, the solver reports UNSAT through four gates and SAT at five; for $m=4$, UNSAT through six and SAT at seven. The SAT witnesses are independently checked. The upper circuits are also supplied by the endpoint-minterm formula for $F_m$. The lower side remains Z3-derived: no proof trace has yet been exported and checked by an independent proof checker.

The selector lower bound $2m+2$ still follows analytically for circuits at threshold $2m-1$, because the $2m+2$ explicit witnesses from Section 74 are themselves circuits of size at most $2m-2$, and their two-row disagreement supports are disjoint. The solver result supplies the missing upper-bound fact that $Q_m$ excludes all circuits up to $2m-2$ for these two finite values of $m$. Thus the exact circuit selector image is 8 at $m=3$ and 10 at $m=4$, conditional only on trusting the finite solver UNSAT outputs. The total function's circuit size is also exactly $2m-1$ in these instances.

This finite extension is evidence that the formula sample may resist some sharing, but it does not explain why or establish the same claim for $m\ge5$. It remains linear and has no P-vs-NP consequence. The $m=5$ eight-gate search is unresolved: the partial-sample synthesis returned `unknown` at 30- and 120-second limits, and a separate 64-row total-function query returned `unknown` at 60 seconds. An instrumented five-second rerun reports Z3's reason as `timeout`. These are neutral observations, not lower bounds. The next step is a symmetry-reduced or proof-producing encoding, or a symbolic gate-charging argument for antipodal certificates that handles arbitrary sharing. Any claimed lower bound must also survive the general dual-rail simulation and meet a proven magnification threshold before it can become a separation route.


### 76. Independent CNF synthesis extends the circuit pattern through five variables (25 September 2026)

Z3's $m=5$ partial-sample search timed out. I built [experiment_sat_circuit_selector_antipodal.py](experiment_sat_circuit_selector_antipodal.py), a separate one-hot CNF encoding for topologically ordered circuits with arbitrary fanout, free signed input literals/constants, binary AND/OR, unary NOT, and an arbitrary output wire. A gate conflict budget distinguishes UNSAT from an inconclusive run; any SAT model is simulated independently on the sample.

The CNF encoding reproduces the small cases: at $m=3$, four gates is UNSAT and five is SAT; at $m=4$, six is UNSAT and seven is SAT. At $m=5$, the eight-gate instance has 614 variables and 8,928 clauses. Both CaDiCaL 1.9.5 and Glucose 4.2 report UNSAT with a two-million-conflict budget. No DRAT/LRAT proof has been emitted or independently checked, so the $m=5$ exclusion remains solver evidence, not a hand proof.

For threshold $s=2m-1$, the disjoint two-row formula witnesses from Section 74 force every circuit selector image to contain at least $2m+2$ rows. The solver-checked sample supplies the matching upper bound for $m=3,4,5$. Together with the endpoint-minterm circuit upper bound, this gives solver-derived exact circuit complexities $5,7,9$ and selector sizes $8,10,12$ for those three finite instances.

The next $m=6$ test asks whether any ten-gate circuit matches the 14-row sample. CaDiCaL exhausted a five-million-conflict budget without deciding it. Glucose's identical query was live when this entry was first written; it was subsequently interrupted at the user's direction after the scale mismatch was discussed. There is no $m=6$ conclusion. The finite sequence remains linear and far below magnification scale, so it is useful for testing a sharing invariant but not a P-vs-NP proof route. Do not extend the dimension sweep without a specific symbolic invariant or proof-producing objective.

### 77. Proof-first characterization by time-bounded witness description (25 September 2026)

For a fixed universal machine $U$, define $K_{\mathrm{SAT}}^t(\varphi)$ as the minimum code length of a program that, given $\varphi$, outputs a satisfying assignment within $t(|\varphi|)$ steps. Then

$$P=NP\iff \exists c,d\ \forall\varphi\in\mathrm{SAT},\ K_{\mathrm{SAT}}^{|\varphi|^d}(\varphi)\le c.$$

For one direction, a polynomial SAT decider yields a polynomial witness finder by variable-by-variable self-reduction, and the finder has a fixed code. For the other, enumerate the finitely many programs of length at most $c$, simulate them for the stated polynomial clock, and verify every proposed assignment; this decides SAT in polynomial time. This proves an exact equivalence, not a lower bound or a new separation.

The distinction from ordinary conditional Kolmogorov complexity is decisive: the lexicographically first satisfying assignment can always be computed by exhaustive search, so it has constant $K(w\mid\varphi)$ even when no polynomial-time finder is known. Any incompressibility proof relevant to P versus NP must be time-bounded and must lower-bound every valid witness program at a fixed polynomial clock. This is just the original search obstacle in resource-bounded-description form; a useful advance would need a structural argument that proves the bound without rebuilding the same time hierarchy by self-reference.

**Proof-first steering:** five long finite searches were stopped on 25 September 2026: the 16-anchor sparse-witness CEGIS, six sampled $GL(5,2)$ basis cases, and three $n=6$ radius-two relay SAT searches. They had produced no newly persisted result beyond the audit checkpoints recorded above. Their termination is not SAT/UNSAT evidence; the affected conclusions remain open or solver-derived at their last verified status. Further numerical work should be used only to falsify a specific lemma or check a witness.

### 78. Proof audit of a finite-substrate one-wayness argument (25 September 2026)

The June 2026 preprint [P versus NP: Computation as Counting over Finite Relational Substrate](https://www.preprints.org/manuscript/202606.1781) is explicitly marked not peer-reviewed on its posting page. Its Theorem 5 is a correct standard implication: a polynomial-time computable one-way function gives $P\ne NP$, since under $P=NP$ the NP relation $f(x)=y$ can be searched by self-reduction to find a preimage.

The unresolved step is its claimed one-wayness of $j\mapsto g^j\bmod P$. Square-and-multiply establishes that the forward map is polynomial time, and discrete logarithm is its inverse. The statement that no polynomial-time classical inverse exists is the substantive lower bound; the text calls it infeasible and uses it as the â€œscale-descent residue,â€ but the finite-field identities and finite runtime measurements listed in its reproducibility section do not prove a lower bound for all input lengths. If discrete-log hardness or one-wayness is assumed, the P-vs-NP implication is conditional and the assumption itself supplies the separation. No unconditional proof is obtained by changing the representation vocabulary.

There is also a standard-model transfer gap. The paper bounds its carrier family by $\ell\le\tfrac12\log_2\Omega$ while treating $\Omega$ as a fixed finite totality, then says asymptotic classes are recovered by dropping finitude. A fixed maximum input length gives finitely many instances, so any finite decision or inverse table can be hardcoded. Standard $P$ and $NP$ quantify over unbounded lengths with one uniform machine. An unbounded, uniformly encoded carrier family and cost-preserving transfer would be needed.

This audit is a proof-obligation check, not a refutation of the finite algebraic identities. The reusable distinctions are between a verified forward map, an inverse operation, and a proved lower bound on every uniform polynomial-time inverter; and between a finite resource horizon and an unbounded standard complexity-class family.

### 79. Backward implication map and hierarchy quantifiers (25 September 2026)

The active proof-first analysis is recorded in Section 2.1 of [New Model.MD](New%20Model.MD) and Idea 117 of [ideas.md](ideas.md). By Cook--Levin, $P=NP$ iff $SAT\in P$. Under $P=NP$, SAT self-reduction gives a polynomial witness finder; the same search-to-decision argument applies to polynomially balanced NP witness relations, implies $NP=coNP$, and precludes standard length-indexed one-way functions. These are consequences of the equality hypothesis, not a contradiction with current theorems.

The separation target has the quantifier order $\exists L\in NP\,\forall k\,L\notin DTIME(n^k)$. A nondeterministic time hierarchy theorem gives, for each fixed $k\ge1$, a language $L_k\in NTIME(n^{k+1})\subseteq NP$ outside $NTIME(n^k)$, hence outside $DTIME(n^k)$. This is only $\forall k\,\exists L_k$; no one language is thereby shown to lie outside every polynomial-time bound. The two statements cannot be identified by exchanging quantifiers.

The direct self-referential tableau sketch also has a concrete cost loop: encoding an $n^d$-step candidate run can produce a formula of length $M=\Theta(n^d)$, on which that candidate's clock is $M^d=\Theta(n^{d^2})$. That invalidates this unclosed construction, but does not rule out all fixed-point or diagonal methods. A different method must prove a uniform bound for the final encoding and clock.

Finally, $NP=coNP$ is equivalent to existence of a polynomially bounded Cook--Reckhow proof system for TAUT. Since $P=NP$ implies $NP=coNP$, equality gives such a system; separation alone does not show $NP\ne coNP$ or exclude it. Similarly, one-way functions imply separation, but are not known to follow from it. Incompleteness for one fixed theory is not an all-algorithms lower bound without an additional reduction.

**Status and research priority:** this is an exact implication/quantifier audit, not a proof of either answer. The previous finite searches remain stopped. Continue with symbolic fixed-point cost analysis and direct proof attempts; do not restart dimension sweeps without a theorem-level invariant.

### 80. ZFC arithmetization and a proof-complexity branch (25 September 2026)

Section 2.2 of [New Model.MD](New%20Model.MD) writes $P=NP$ as an existentially clocked SAT decider followed by a universal formula quantifier. Since SAT is decidable, its matrix is computable, so this has $\Sigma^0_2$ form; $P\ne NP$ has the dual $\Pi^0_2$ form. This is a logical classification, not evidence of ZFC provability or independence. Proof enumeration halts if it finds a derivation, but cannot certify independence by running for a finite time. No independence result for this sentence is known in the project.

Section 2.3 records the stronger proof-complexity route: $NP=coNP$ iff some Cook--Reckhow proof system for TAUT has polynomially bounded proofs. Therefore a lower bound against every proof system and every polynomial bound would imply $NP\ne coNP$ and hence $P\ne NP$. The quantified target is

$$
\forall\Pi\;\forall k\;\exists\tau\in TAUT\;\forall\pi\ (|\pi|\le(|\tau|+2)^k\Rightarrow \Pi(\pi)\ne\tau),
$$

where $\Pi$ ranges over valid polynomial-time proof systems. A fixed-system self-reference would still need a tautology truth proof and a closed formula-length/proof-bound calculation; a result for one formal theory or one proof system is insufficient for the all-systems statement.

**Research status:** this branch is a stronger target, not a new lower bound. Idea 118 adds the exact quantified proof obligation and the lesson that arithmetical classification and GÃ¶del incompleteness do not settle it.

### 81. Candidate-proof coverage bound for direct self-reference (25 September 2026)

The expanded proof-system synthesis in Section 2.3 of [New Model.MD](New%20Model.MD) incorporates the older FCP, recursive-jump, and fixed-point work in Ideas 27â€“45. The strongest direct tautology diagonal already in the record is Idea 45: under a suitable syntactic fixed point, a falsifying assignment would encode a sound-system proof of the same formula, so the output must be a tautology. The remaining issue is how much of the proof space its assignments cover.

Idea 119 formalizes that issue. If $m$-bit assignments encode candidate proof strings through $E$, and the self-referential formula is false exactly when $\Pi(E(a))$ is a proof of itself, soundness proves tautology and excludes proofs in $\operatorname{Range}(E)$. To exclude every proof string of length at most $L$ by this route, $E$ must cover all $2^{L+1}-1$ candidates or a separate proof-normalization theorem must show every valid proof has a representative in its image. Full coverage forces $m\ge L+1$. In the direct explicit formula interface, this costs $\Omega(L)$ formula symbols. The resulting lower bound is polynomially compatible with any Cook--Reckhow simulation because another system's proof must be long enough for its polynomial-time decoder to print the target formula.

**Proof and scope.** The tautology claim follows by contradiction from soundness. The coverage bound is the cardinality inequality $2^m\ge2^{L+1}-1$. The formula-size conclusion applies to the direct representation that explicitly reads the candidate bits; it does not rule out checker-specific normalization or a different non-relativizing lower-bound argument. Idea 119 therefore closes one architecture as a source of a superpolynomial jump, not the full proof-complexity route. The next target is a proved normal form for all short proofs or a non-relativizing recursive jump / finite-consistency lower bound.

### 82. Independence-transfer audit: subset sum and the Jump Free Theorem (25 September 2026)

Section 2.4 of [New Model.MD](New%20Model.MD) and Idea 120 formalize a narrow proof-theoretic route. For $A=(P=NP)$, an auxiliary sentence $C$, and $T=ZFC$, the valid rule is

$$T\vdash A\to C\quad\land\quad T\nvdash C\quad\Longrightarrow\quad T\nvdash A.$$

Williamson's 2024 arXiv preprint proposes a subset-sum corollary $C$ of a set-version $J$ of Friedman's Jump Free Theorem. It argues $J\to C$ and that $P=NP\to C$, but its abstract explicitly conjectures, rather than proves, that $C$ is independent of ZFC ([preprint](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.00468)). Moreover, $J\to C$ does not transfer $J$'s unprovability to $C$; that would require a reverse reduction, equivalence, or direct relative-consistency proof for $C$.

Even if the missing independence result were proved, the conclusion would only be $ZFC\nvdash P=NP$. It would neither establish $P\ne NP$ in the standard model nor show that ZFC cannot prove the negative answer. The encoding claim $P=NP\to C$ also needs a uniform bit-length and polynomial-exponent audit. This is therefore an instructive conditional status argument, not a separation route in its current form. The general lesson is to track proof-implication direction separately from computational reduction direction.

### 83. The canonical subset-sum corollary is provable in ZFC (25 September 2026)

Idea 121 sharpens the input-size audit and closes the corollary's independence route for a natural encoding in Williamson's [2024 preprint](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.00468). Fix $k\ge2$, take all three maps $\gamma_i$ to be the signed pairing bijection $\gamma(2j)=j$, $\gamma(2j+1)=-(j+1)$, and choose $E_p=\{0,\ldots,p-1\}$. By fullness, there is an $f_p\in S$ with domain $E_p^k$; by reflexivity its entire range lies in $E_p$. Therefore both lists have at most $p^k$ entries, each of absolute value at most $p$.

For either list, the sum of absolute values is at most $p^{k+1}$. The standard signed subset-sum dynamic program over attainable sums in $[-p^{k+1},p^{k+1}]$ decides target zero in $O(p^{2k+1}\operatorname{polylog}p)$ multitape bit operations. For fixed $k\ge2$, this is $O(p^{3k})$; after a single-tape simulation and absorption of logarithmic factors, $O(p^{6k})$ suffices. An extra state handles a nonempty-subset convention. Thus both sequences are solvable with one fixed exponent, and the corollary's existential witness statement is a ZFC theorem for this canonical triple. It uses fullness and reflexivity but not jump-freeness, P=NP, or the Jump Free Theorem.

This is a route-specific theorem, not a result about P versus NP. It shows the proposed auxiliary corollary is not independent for the canonical encoding. If the author intended arbitrary fixed bijections $\gamma_i$, the result above does not settle that variant: arbitrary bijections may inflate binary lengths, and P=NP only promises polynomial time in the actual encoded input length. The published statement must also specify whether the maps are computable, whether the sequence must be uniformly generated, and whether â€œsize $p^k$â€ counts only list entries or binary input bits. The next work is to keep the canonical case closed and formalize the exact arbitrary-map sentence only if its quantifiers can be recovered unambiguously.

### 84. Coupling derivatives, total variation, and full-input trace completeness (25 September 2026)

This extends the audit of Ben Goertzel's [quantale/evidence preprint](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.08814) in Sections 28 and 30 and Idea 11. The central distinction is now formalized by an exact finite lemma.

Let $\mu_0,\mu_1$ be phase distributions over finite world spaces and let $T$ be a finite-valued observation. Write $P_b=T_*\mu_b$. Then

$$
\min_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Couplings}(\mu_0,\mu_1)}\Pr_{(X_0,X_1)\sim\Gamma}[T(X_0)\ne T(X_1)]
=\operatorname{TV}(P_0,P_1).
$$

For any coupling, its probability of agreement on value $z$ is at most $\min(P_0(z),P_1(z))$, so disagreement is at least total variation. A maximal coupling of the two observation laws attains that bound; lift it to the world spaces by sampling from the conditional phase laws given the coupled outputs. Conversely, a fixed coupling can have larger disagreement than total variation: for identical uniform bit laws, the anti-diagonal coupling disagrees with probability $1$ while the phase laws are identical. Therefore a pairwise derivative under a fixed coupling is not itself a predictive-advantage measure.

For the preprint's rigid message ensemble, $B_j$ is a deterministic function of the public instance $Y$ on the support. Hence $\mathcal{L}(Y\mid B_j=0)$ and $\mathcal{L}(Y\mid B_j=1)$ have disjoint supports and total variation $1$. Every opposite-phase pair differs in at least one public input bit. This creates a completeness fork for the preprint's observer calculus: if neutral atoms cover every readable public bit, the asserted pair-neutrality of neutral atoms cannot hold on opposite-phase pairs; if the atoms cover only a common skeleton, Section 5.6's observer-to-evidence expansion still needs a theorem representing a standard polynomial-time machine that reads the omitted bits. Sections 5.6 and D.5 define a restricted primitive-read interface and assume atom completeness (4.22/D.33); the inspected v2 does not prove this interface simulates arbitrary ordinary Turing machines on the full public encoding. The SAT self-reduction sees the full $Y$, so a lower bound for the restricted interface does not yet contradict that upper bound.

Quantitatively, write $Y=(Y_1,\ldots,Y_L)$. For any coupling $\Gamma_j$ supported on opposite target phases, every pair differs on at least one coordinate, so

$$
\sum_{i=1}^{L}\Gamma_j[Y_i(X_0)\ne Y_i(X_1)]\ge1.
$$

If each bit read is classified as a neutral atom, the paper's neutral-coherence condition would set every summand to zero, a contradiction. If some varying bits are instead omitted from the neutral vocabulary, standard input readers still have access to them and the observer-to-evidence theorem must account for those reads. This makes the missing full-input coverage condition testable at the atomic level.

**Exact local-to-global stress test.** Let $n\ge4$ be even, $J$ the all-ones matrix over $\mathbb{F}_2$, and $A=I+J$. Since $J^2=nJ=0$ and $2J=0$ in this field, $A^2=I$. With uniform $M\in\{0,1\}^n$ and $Y=AM$, the full public string efficiently recovers every bit: $M_i=\bigoplus_{r\ne i}Y_r$. Yet for any set of at most $n-2$ coordinates, at least one input to that parity is unobserved, so each $M_i$ remains uniform conditioned on those coordinates. Bounded-coordinate leak tests therefore do not imply hardness against global polynomial-time computation. This example also explains the earlier 12-bit experiment exactly; it is not a SAT encoding or a separation.

The example also has a local 3CNF realization. For each sampled $Y$, construct a bounded-fan-in XOR circuit for $AZ=Y$ and Tseitin encode every XOR gate by a constant-size 3CNF equivalence gadget. There are $O(n^2)$ gates. Invertibility of $A$ fixes $Z=A^{-1}Y=M$ uniquely, and the gate auxiliaries are uniquely determined by the circuit, so every satisfying assignment has the same message. Yet the public RHS bits reveal $M$ by the explicit parity map in polynomial time. This family has local clauses and a rigid witness message without computational hiding. Adding a gauge lift $Z=X\oplus G$ only creates more witnesses; it does not impede decoding from $Y$. It is not a counterexample to the preprint's strongest no-public-decoder interpretation, but it shows that a ban on direct target literals or bounded-local leakage is insufficient. If the strong interpretation excludes this family, the excluded condition must be proved as a computational hardness property.

The correct computational quantity is size-bounded distinguishing advantage, for example

$$
\sup_{C:\,|C|\le s}\left|\Pr[C(Y)=1\mid B_j=1]-\Pr[C(Y)=1\mid B_j=0]\right|.
$$

For unrestricted tests this is total variation; for polynomial-size circuits it is computational indistinguishability. Establishing a negligible bound for a fixed, explicit sampler would itself be a major average-case hardness statement. The weak point in the preprint is therefore not the elementary pair-derivative identity but the missing proof that its restricted atom budget covers the full input-read power of every standard polynomial-time predictor.

**Literature cross-check.** Kabanets and Kolokolova's [revised ECCC TR25-089](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/089/) (Revision 1, March 2026) characterizes a sublinear-error chain rule for time-bounded conditional $K^t$ by the conjunction of an exponential-time circuit lower bound and a promise-gap $K^t$ problem in P. Under a cryptographic assumption making that promise problem NP-hard, the chain-rule claim is equivalent to $P=NP$. This is useful calibration for Idea 115: algorithmic-information language sharpens the missing hardness premise but does not discharge it unconditionally.

**Research status.** The coupling lemma and parity example are proved. The Goertzel article remains an incomplete proof as audited in Section 28: abstract lock/buffer/coupling hypotheses are not instantiated, and full-input observer completeness is an independent missing obligation. This does not refute the conditional mathematics or establish either answer to P versus NP. Continue only by defining a machine-complete full-input trace model and proving a nontrivial discrepancy bound for a fixed SAT-witness family; otherwise this claimed proof transfer is closed at the present gap.

### 85. Semantic advice elimination and the promise-preservation gap (25 September 2026)

Idea 123 audits a literature lead connecting unambiguous time hierarchies to a possible uniform hierarchy. Pervyshev's [ECCC TR05-054](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2005/054/) proves a strict polynomial hierarchy with one bit of advice for $UTime$, among other semantic classes. Hao Wu's [arXiv note](https://arxiv.org/abs/1711.11158) claims to remove constant advice for reasonable semantic models. Its main reduction fixes each constant advice string, treats the fixed-advice variants as members of $CTIME(t)$, applies a hypothesized unadvised-to-advised containment, and recombines the simulations.

Under the standard semantic advice definition, this fixed-variant step is not justified. Fortnow, Santhanam, and Trevisan define $L\in CTIME(t)/a$ using an advice sequence $s_n$ such that the machine meets its promise on every length-$n$ input with that selected $s_n$. They explicitly say the machine need not satisfy its promise for incorrect advice strings, distinguishing the definition from the Karp--Lipton convention. See their [primary paper](https://theory.stanford.edu/~trevisan/pubs/promise.pdf), lines 189â€“201 in the PDF text.

Formally, the premise supplies $\exists M,\alpha\;\forall n\;\forall x\in\{0,1\}^n:\mathrm{Promise}(M,x,\alpha(n))$ and correctness for those selected pairs. A fixed variant $M_s$ is eligible for a no-advice class theorem only if its promise holds for all $n,x$, which the advised premise does not provide. If $\alpha$ is eventually constant, finitely many exceptional lengths can be hard-coded; this does not cover sequences that vary infinitely often.

This yields a small lemma: if one valid advice sequence for $L$ is eventually constant, then $L$ is already in the corresponding uniform class. Replace the advised machine on the finitely many exceptional input lengths by a hard-coded truth table and use the stabilized fixed variant for all larger inputs. Hence any witness to $CTIME(t)/a\not\subseteq CTIME(t)$ must use advice that changes infinitely often in every valid realization. This does not make the advice computable.

For an advised unambiguous machine $M(x,s_n)$, fixing some other constant string $s$ gives $M_s(x)=M(x,s)$. At lengths where $s\ne s_n$, $M_s$ may have multiple accepting paths. It is then not a $UTime(t)$ computation, so the premise $UTime(t)\subseteq UTime(T)/A$ cannot be applied to it. Strong example: let $M(x,b)$ reject if $b=|x|\bmod2$, and otherwise branch into $\max(2,|x|)$ accepting paths. With advice $s_n=n\bmod2$, it validly recognizes the empty language in $UP/1$; each fixed-bit variant is ambiguous on infinitely many lengths. The same structural issue occurs for BPP when an incorrect advice string can cause error above the promised bound.

The selector wrapper after this step is plausible: if each fixed variant were valid, a uniform containment would give one lower-time advised simulator for each of the finitely many variants; a new machine could store the original $a$-bit selector plus all $2^a$ advice blocks and execute only the selected simulator. The exact missing premise is validity of every fixed variant. This keeps the diagnosis narrow: the issue is not the final finite table of advice blocks, but the semantic status of the entries put into that table.

The one-bit advice in Pervyshev's construction is substantive: its proof discussion says the advice certifies whether an enumerated candidate is a valid $CTIME$ machine and provides correct advice for a padded simulation input. These are global semantic facts. The construction therefore does not justify treating the advice sequence as a globally fixed or effectively recoverable bit; see [TR05-054, Section 3.2](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2005/054/).

For syntactic $NTime$, this particular issue disappears: every nondeterministic machine defines a language, so fixing advice preserves class membership. The distinction is therefore specific to promise-bearing models such as $UP$ and $BPP$, rather than a general failure of advice-selector wrappers.

This does not prove that Wu's hierarchy conclusion is false; it shows that the displayed advice-elimination proof does not establish it for standard semantic advice unless a separate promise-preservation lemma is supplied or the theorem uses a robust advice convention. The 2005 semantic hierarchy paper explains that a hierarchy in the stronger Karp--Lipton convention would imply an unadvised hierarchy, but the existing semantic hierarchy theorem uses the weaker selected-advice convention. As a status cross-check, the 2021 ECCC paper by Oliveira and Santhanam et al. still describes the unadvised hierarchy for $BPP$ as wide open ([TR21-039](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2021/039/)). The 2024 paper [TR24-049](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2024/049/) calls its theorem for the specialized class $O_2TIME$ the first uniform hierarchy theorem for a semantic class. This is not itself a direct result about $UP$ or $BPP$, but it further cautions that a general no-advice claim needs careful model-by-model verification.

There is a second, independent obstruction to a P-vs-NP consequence. Even if one had a valid unadvised hierarchy $UTime(n^b)\not\subseteq UTime(n^a)$ for every fixed $a<b$, this gives a family $\forall a\,\exists L_a$ separated at that exponent. It does not provide one language $L\in NP$ outside all $DTIME(n^k)$ clocks, which is what $P\ne NP$ requires. Under $UP=P$, each $L_a$ could have a deterministic polynomial exponent larger than $a$; deterministic time hierarchy already allows this behavior.

**Learning:** semantic validity is a proof obligation, not a property of a machine description or a transformation's syntactic form. Every advice-fixing or simulation argument must verify promise preservation on all inputs required by the target class. Keep this alongside full-input trace completeness from Section 84: both are failures of a proof model to cover the ordinary machine being ruled out, but they are logically distinct.

**Research disposition:** do not use the 2017 note to claim a $UP$ hierarchy or any P-vs-NP consequence under standard semantic advice without a repair. If revisiting this lead, first formalize the advice convention and prove that all $2^a$ fixed variants satisfy the promise; then separately address the per-exponent-to-all-polynomial-clocks quantifier gap. Neither obligation is solved here.

### 86. The global unambiguity promise is co-r.e.-complete (25 September 2026)

Idea 124 proves an index-set result that sharpens Section 85. Let $U_{\mathrm{valid}}$ contain explicitly polynomial-clocked nondeterministic machine descriptions with at most one accepting path on every input.

Its complement is r.e.: enumerate input strings and, for each, enumerate the finitely many clock-bounded paths; accept the complement when two distinct accepting paths are found. Thus $U_{\mathrm{valid}}\in\Pi^0_1$.

For hardness, given a deterministic machine $T$, construct $N_T$ which on $1^n$ simulates $T$ on blank input for $n$ steps. If $T$ has halted, branch into exactly two accepting paths; otherwise reject. Reject non-unary inputs. This is a polynomial-time nondeterministic machine with an effectively specified polynomial clock. If $T$ never halts, $N_T$ has no accepting paths anywhere and is unambiguous. If $T$ halts at step $r$, every $1^n$ for $n\ge r$ has two accepting paths. Therefore $T\in\overline{HALT}$ iff $N_T\in U_{\mathrm{valid}}$. This many-one reduction proves $\Pi^0_1$-hardness, hence completeness.

For every finite length cutoff $B$, a machine $T$ can be chosen to halt only after $B$ steps. Then its image has no accepting paths on inputs through length $B$ but is ambiguous at larger lengths. Finite tests can refute global validity with one counterexample; they cannot certify it without a proof covering all lengths.

**Implication for proof repair:** no total computable process can take arbitrary clocked nondeterministic machine descriptions and decide which ones satisfy the $UP$ promise. This blocks generic â€œenumerate and discard invalid machinesâ€ repairs. It does not block a construction that makes the specific machines valid by syntax, nor a mathematical proof of validity for a finite set of variants. It is a precise account of why the promise-preservation obligation in Section 85 cannot be assumed away, not a lower bound for SAT.

### 87. Proof-certified UP validity can miss true machine descriptions (25 September 2026)

Let $T$ be a consistent, recursively axiomatized theory extending enough arithmetic for Godel's second incompleteness theorem. Let $H_T$ search for a $T$-proof of contradiction and halt if it finds one. Apply the explicit clocked reduction from Section 86 to $H_T$, obtaining $(N_{H_T},p)$. The construction is formalizable in $T$ and yields

$$
\operatorname{Unambiguous}(N_{H_T},p)\leftrightarrow\operatorname{Con}(T).
$$

The intermediate statement is $\forall n\,\neg\operatorname{Halt}_T(n)$, where $\operatorname{Halt}_T(n)$ means the proof search finds a contradiction within $n$ simulation steps. A found contradiction yields two accepting paths on a sufficiently long unary input; if none is found, all inputs are rejected.

If $T$ is consistent, the machine is genuinely unambiguous, since $H_T$ never halts. But $T$ cannot prove this validity statement: together with the displayed equivalence, that would make $T$ prove its own consistency. For $T=ZFC$, this conclusion assumes ZFC is consistent.

This is a result about machine descriptions only. $N_{H_T}$ recognizes the empty language, which has an obviously valid deterministic representation. Thus the theorem does not show that proof-certified enumeration omits a UP language, and it gives no P-vs-NP separation. It shows that proof-based filtering is not an exhaustive enumeration of valid descriptions. The second incompleteness theorem is treated in Jech, [*On Godel's second incompleteness theorem*](https://arxiv.org/abs/math/9204207).

### 88. UP-completeness exactly characterizes language-level categorical enumeration (25 September 2026)

The machine-index result in Section 86 and the proof-theoretic result in Section 87 leave open whether a recursively enumerable *subfamily* of valid machine descriptions can still represent every UP language. Hartmanis and Hemachandra answer this exactly (1988, Lemma 2.7): UP has a polynomial-time many-one complete language iff there is a recursively enumerable list of clocked categorical machines whose languages cover UP. Here categorical means globally unambiguous on every input, and coverage is extensional: one listed description per language is enough.

**Complete language implies a list.** Fix a categorical machine for a UP-complete language. Compose it with an effective enumeration of clocked polynomial-time reductions. Each composition stays categorical, and every UP language is represented by one such composition.

**Covering list implies a complete language.** Let an effective enumerator output only valid clocked categorical machine descriptions. Build a universal NTM whose input contains a candidate description, an input x, a unary step budget 1^t, and a finite computation transcript verifying that the enumerator output that description. Deterministically check the transcript; reject if malformed. Otherwise simulate the certified machine on x for at most t steps, preserving its accepting paths. The runtime is polynomial in the whole input because the transcript and description are explicit and t is unary. The universal machine is categorical: uncertified encodings reject, while certified descriptions are all categorical. For any fixed listed machine N_i, hardwire one valid transcript and choose t equal to its explicit clock p_i(|x|). This is a polynomial-time many-one reduction from L(N_i) to the universal language. Since the list covers UP, the universal language is UP-complete.

This explains why Section 86 does not settle language-level enumeration. The full set of valid codes is non-r.e., but an r.e. *covering sublist* may exist; that is equivalent to UP-completeness. It also gives an exact conditional strengthening of Section 87: if UP has no many-one complete set, then for every sound, effectively axiomatized theory F, there is some language L in UP for which no machine accepting L has an F-proof of its global unambiguity. Otherwise enumerate all F-proved categorical descriptions; soundness and coverage would give an r.e. categorical list and hence a UP-complete set.

**Unique-SAT characterization.** Hartmanis and Hemachandra Theorem 2.1 proves

$$
P\ne UP\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \exists S\in P\;[S\subseteq\{F:\#SAT(F)\le1\}\ \land\ S\cap SAT\notin P].
$$

Forward: choose L in UP minus P and a clocked categorical N accepting it. A parsimonious Cook-Levin encoding F_{N,x} maps accepting paths one-to-one to satisfying assignments. Its canonical range S_N is polynomial-time recognizable because the encoding records N and x and has checkable syntax. Categoricity makes every member have at most one satisfying assignment. If S_N intersect SAT were in P, then L would be in P, contradiction. Reverse: deterministically test membership in S, then guess and verify a satisfying assignment. Outside S there are no accepting paths; inside S there is at most one. Hence S intersect SAT is in UP and, if not in P, witnesses P != UP. Since P subseteq UP subseteq NP, either such witness implies P != NP.

This is an exact target, not a lower bound. No current project construction has established all three obligations for a useful S: (1) S membership is in P, (2) every S formula has at most one satisfying assignment, and (3) S intersect SAT is not in P. Numerical samples cannot prove (2) universally or (3).

**Status and limits.** Hartmanis and Hemachandra described UP-complete-set existence as unknown; the 2024 MFCS paper still lists â€œUP has no many-one complete setsâ€ as a conjecture and constructs an oracle with no UP-complete sets even though NP = PSPACE. The oracle result is relativized and does not settle unrelativized UP-completeness. Sources: [Hartmanis and Hemachandra, Theoretical Computer Science 58 (1988)](https://doi.org/10.1016/0304-3975(88)90022-9); [Dingel, Egidy, and GlaÃŸer, MFCS 2024](https://doi.org/10.4230/LIPIcs.MFCS.2024.50).

**Next proof attempt:** audit every proposed unique-SAT family in the project against the three obligations above. The most promising candidate must be a uniform P-decidable family with a proof of global uniqueness and a lower-bound transfer to SAT on that family; otherwise it does not instantiate the characterization.
**Proof-complexity bridge (KÃ¶blerâ€“Messnerâ€“TorÃ¡n, 2003).** A p-optimal Cookâ€“Reckhow proof system for TAUT implies UP has a complete language, in fact for logspace many-one reductions. The mechanism is a proof-carrying universal simulator, not a bare enumeration of machine codes.

For a clocked machine M, unary time bound 0^t, and input-length cutoff 0^n, let CAT(M,0^t,0^n) assert that M has at most one accepting path on every input shorter than n, with paths cut off at t steps. Its complement has an NP witness: an input and two distinct accepting paths. Thus CAT is in coNP and reduces to TAUT. For a fixed categorical M with polynomial clock q, the canonical formulas Ï†_{M,q(n),n} are tautologies. Their sequence has a polynomial-time recognizable range; the paper's Lemma 6 constructs an auxiliary proof system with efficiently generated proofs for every tautology in this P-set. A p-optimal TAUT system p-simulates that auxiliary system, so there is a polynomial-time procedure producing h-proofs of Ï†_{M,q(n),n}.

Define the universal language to contain tuples (M,0^t,w,x) when h(w)=Ï†_{M,t,|x|+1} and M accepts x within t steps. The proof forces Ï† to be a tautology; soundness of the Cook reduction then certifies at most one accepting path on all inputs of length less than |x|+1, including x. The universal NTM therefore remains unambiguous on every input, even when arbitrary candidate machine codes are supplied. Given any L in UP, use its fixed categorical machine M, its polynomial clock q, and the efficiently produced proof for Ï†_{M,q(|x|+1),|x|+1}; the resulting polynomial-time map reduces L to the universal language. This fills the construction's key soundness gap: the proof certifies the promise exactly where the universal simulator uses it.

There is a direct sufficient route to P != NP here. If P=NP, then TAUT is in P, and h(Ï†)=Ï† for tautologies and a fixed tautology otherwise is a p-optimal proof system: any proof-system output is already a valid h-proof. Therefore a proof that TAUT has no p-optimal proof system would imply P != NP. The converse is not supplied by this argument. Also, UP lacking complete sets implies no p-optimal TAUT system by the 2003 theorem, but nonexistence of a p-optimal system alone does not prove UP lacks complete sets. See [KÃ¶bler, Messner, and TorÃ¡n (2003)](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0890-5401(03)00058-0), especially Theorems 10â€“11.

### 89. Worst-case one-way functions characterize $P\ne UP$ (25 September 2026)

Grollmann and Selman (1988) state that one-way functions exist iff $P\ne UP$. The notion is complexity-theoretic and worst-case: a total polynomial-time computable injective function $f$ with polynomially related input/output lengths, for which no polynomial-time total algorithm returns a preimage on every range point. This is weaker than cryptographic average-case one-wayness. Because $UP\subseteq NP$, proving such a function exists suffices for $P\ne NP$.

**Proof reconstruction, one-way function to separation.** Define $L_f=\{(u,y):\exists z\le_{ll}u, f(z)=y\}$ for length-lex order. On input $(u,y)$, let $m=|u|$ and guess exactly $m+1$ bits as a rank among the $2^{m+1}-1$ strings of length at most $m$; reject the unused rank, decode, then check $z\le_{ll}u$ and $f(z)=y$. Each $z$ has one computation path, and injectivity permits at most one accepting path, so $L_f\in UP$. If a P decider for $L_f$ existed, honesty would give a polynomial upper bound $N(|y|)$ on preimage length. Binary-search the unique preimage among strings of length at most $N(|y|)$ by asking threshold queries $(u,y)\in L_f$. There are $O(N(|y|))$ queries; thresholds have length at most $N(|y|)$, so the total computation is polynomial. This contradicts one-wayness and gives a language in $UP\setminus P$.

**Proof reconstruction, separation to one-way function.** Let $M$ be a clocked categorical recognizer of $L\in UP\setminus P$. On a string $z$, test whether it is a canonical complete accepting transcript for $M(y)$. Map valid transcripts to $1y$ and all other strings to $0z$. This map is total and polynomial-time. Tags separate the cases, and unambiguity ensures at most one valid transcript for each $y$, so it is injective. Clocking and inclusion of $y$ in its transcript make the input and output lengths polynomially related. A polynomial inverter, followed by checking whether the returned string maps to $1y$, would decide $L$, contradiction.

**Application and limit for the current algebraic lead.** This identifies the exact theorem-level debt of a discrete-log construction: it must be one uniform total honest injective function on bit strings, and it must have no polynomial-time inverse on all range points. A fixed finite modulus cannot witness asymptotic hardness. For a modulus-indexed family, the encoding must avoid exponent collisions modulo the group order and account for malformed inputs while retaining totality and injectivity. Even if these definition issues are repaired, the remaining worst-case inverse lower bound already implies $P\ne UP$ and hence the desired P-vs-NP separation. The 1988 theorem supplies no such lower bound for modular exponentiation. The abstract machine-transcript construction goes in the other direction and is conditional on an already existing $UP\setminus P$ language.

**Definition-level repair.** One uniform candidate can be made precise without asserting it is hard. Encode valid inputs as $1\langle p,g,C,x\rangle$, with odd prime $p$, canonical sorted complete prime factorization $C$ of $p-1$, a generator $g$ certified by $g^{(p-1)/q}\not\equiv1\pmod p$ for each prime divisor $q$, and exponent $0\le x<p-1$. Send this to $1\langle p,g,C,g^x\bmod p\rangle$; send every invalid string $s$ to $0s$. The factorization and primitive-root certificates are verifiable in polynomial time, the two tags prevent cross-branch collisions, primitive-root order prevents collisions between valid exponents, and retaining $p,g,C$ keeps bit lengths polynomially related. Every range output tagged 0 is inverted by stripping its tag; inverting a valid output tagged 1 is exactly discrete logarithm with the certified group data. Thus this total function is one-way iff those certified worst-case logarithms have no uniform polynomial-time algorithm. The only unpaid obligation for this candidate is now explicit: prove that inverse lower bound. Doing so would already prove $P\ne UP$ by the one-way-function characterization.

Source: [Grollmann and Selman, *Complexity Measures for Public-Key Cryptosystems*, SIAM Journal on Computing 17(2), 1988](https://epubs.siam.org/doi/10.1137/0217018). The journal abstract states the equivalence; the constructions above are reconstructed here to expose the exact proof obligations.

### 90. Synthesis: three representations of $P\ne UP$ (25 September 2026)

The leads in Sections 88â€“89 and Ideas 126â€“127 reduce to the same intermediate target:

$$
P\ne UP
\Longleftrightarrow\exists L\in UP\setminus P
\Longleftrightarrow\exists S\in P\;[S\subseteq\{F:\#SAT(F)\le1\}\land S\cap SAT\notin P]
\Longleftrightarrow\exists\text{ total honest injective P-computable one-way }f.
$$

The second equivalence is Hartmanisâ€“Hemachandra Theorem 2.1; the last is Grollmannâ€“Selman. All imply $P\ne NP$, but none follows from $P\ne NP$ by a known theorem. The inclusions $P\subseteq UP\subseteq NP$ alone leave open the configuration $P=UP\ne NP$. Therefore these apparently separate tracks are one sufficient branch, not independent evidence; their common unpaid obligation is a proof of a language in $UP\setminus P$. A direct SAT lower bound need not pass through UP and remains a distinct branch.

This limitation has a concrete relativized witness: Beigel, Buhrman, and Fortnow construct an oracle $A$ for which $P^A=UP^A\ne NP^A$, and state that satisfiability on formulas with at most one satisfying assignment is tractable relative to $A$ while $P^A\ne NP^A$. The oracle does not settle the ordinary classes, but it rules out proving the needed separation by an argument that relativizes and only establishes the $P$-versus-$UP$ gap. Source: [Beigelâ€“Buhrmanâ€“Fortnow, STOC 1998](https://doi.org/10.1145/276698.276737), [author-hosted paper](https://lance.fortnow.com/papers/files/newiso.pdf).

For the discrete-log candidate, the formula route can be instantiated without changing the hard question. From an injective polynomial-time $F$, form $L_F=\{(u,y):\exists z\le_{ll}u, F(z)=y\}$. A fixed-length rank guess gives one computation path per candidate $z$, so this is a UP language with at most one accepting path. A parsimonious Cookâ€“Levin encoding yields a P-recognizable formula range $S_F$ with at most one satisfying assignment per formula and $S_F\cap SAT$ equivalent to $L_F$. Also, $L_F\in P$ iff $F$ has a polynomial-time inverter: threshold queries recover the unique preimage in one direction, and direct inversion decides the threshold language in the other. This constructs an explicit unique-SAT candidate family from the algebraic map, but the third obligation ($S_F\cap SAT\notin P$) is exactly the missing inverse lower bound. The transfer changes representation only; it does not add hardness.

### 91. Positive diagonalization audit: polarity gap and monotone fixed points (25 September 2026)

A direct attempt to diagonalize against an effective list of globally categorical clocked machines runs into a polarity gap. For any hypothetical r.e. list of valid machines, an enumerator-certified universal simulator can include the candidate description, input, unary time budget, and an explicit transcript witnessing the enumerator's output. The simulator lies in UP: invalid certificates reject, and every certified machine has at most one accepting path. The direct diagonal language that accepts exactly when this universal simulator does not accept lies in coUP. There is no known general closure result that moves this complement into UP. If the unary budget is omitted, the list's machine-dependent polynomial exponents also prevent a uniform fixed polynomial simulation. Thus the simple complement diagonal has not produced a UP language.

A finite positive-witness self-reference scheme has a further exact limitation. Let a rule read a candidate machine's yes/no behavior on m selected inputs but use only positive accepting-path certificates. Its output vector defines a monotone map F:{0,1}^m -> {0,1}^m in the coordinatewise order. Every such F has a fixed point: set v_0=0^m and v_{t+1}=F(v_t). Since 0^m <= F(0^m), monotonicity makes the sequence ascending; the finite Boolean lattice forces stabilization at v=F(v). Therefore this restricted construction cannot force disagreement for every acceptance vector. This is not a theorem against all diagonalization: a machine may use genuinely negative or nonmonotone information, or a proof may use structure not captured by a finite positive-evidence map.

**Path-poisoning check.** Carleton et al. (arXiv:2606.20399, June 2026) show that assuming P=UP_{<=f(n)} collapses ambiguity from f(n)+O(1) to f(n), by removing the least accepting path on multi-accepting inputs and deciding auxiliary languages under the assumed collapse. Their method is conditional and does not decide absence of acceptance for an arbitrary categorical machine. Their Theorem 4 constructs oracle separations between constant ambiguity and each unbounded nondecreasing ambiguity bound up to 2^{n^{O(1)}}. This is a relativization filter on generic ambiguity-amplification attempts, not an unrelativized separation.

The positive-only template has an infinite fixed-point analogue. Order all languages by inclusion; $2^{\{0,1\}^*}$ is a complete lattice. A language transformer induced by an oracle computation that asks only positive membership queries is monotone, so Tarski's fixed-point theorem guarantees some $A$ with $\Phi(A)=A$. This prevents such a transformer from forcing disagreement for every possible oracle language. The fixed point may be undecidable or outside UP, so it is not a barrier to every UP-specific argument; a proof would need to show the fixed point belongs to the candidate class or introduce nonmonotone structure. This generalizes the finite Boolean-lattice iteration in Idea 129.

**Next attempt:** any self-referential UP construction must identify the source of negative information, demonstrate a fixed polynomial cost despite varying machine clocks, or replace bitwise diagonal disagreement with another invariant. Do not claim the monotone-map lemma rules out constructions outside its explicit positive-evidence model.

### 92. One-way permutations and two-sided unique witnesses (25 September 2026)

Homan and Thakur's Theorem 3.1 states that a worst-case total, honest, polynomial-time bijection with no polynomial-time inverse exists iff $P\ne UP\cap coUP$. Such a permutation implies $P\ne NP$: if $P=NP$, then $UP=coUP=P$. The theorem extends the Grollmannâ€“Selman one-way-function characterization from $P\ne UP$ to a permutation characterization of the stronger intermediate separation. It is an existence equivalence, not evidence that any particular arithmetic candidate is hard. Source: [Homan and Thakur, JCSS 67 (2003)](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0022-0000(03)00068-0).

The Beigelâ€“Buhrmanâ€“Fortnow oracle with $P^A=UP^A\ne NP^A$ also has $coUP^A=P^A$, so $UP^A\cap coUP^A=P^A$. The one-way-permutation characterization therefore does not evade the known relativizing barrier.

**Direct proof of the permutation-to-class direction.** Let $f$ be a polynomial-time honest permutation and let $B(n)$ bound the length of $f^{-1}(y)$ for $|y|=n$. Define $T_f=\{(u,y):f^{-1}(y)\le_{ll}u\}$. For membership, guess and verify the unique $z$ with $f(z)=y$ and $z\le_{ll}u$. For nonmembership, use the same unique preimage and check $z>_{ll}u$. Since $f$ is onto, every $y$ has a preimage; since it is one-to-one, both verifiers have at most one accepting witness. A fixed-width rank over all strings of length at most $B(|y|)$ gives one computation path per candidate. Thus $T_f\in UP\cap coUP$.

The language $T_f$ is in P iff $f$ has a polynomial-time inverse. Inversion decides $T_f$ by comparing the recovered preimage with $u$. Conversely, a $T_f$ decider supports binary search in length-lex order over $2^{B(|y|)+1}-1$ strings, using $O(B(|y|))$ polynomial-size threshold queries to recover the unique preimage. Therefore noninvertibility of $f$ gives an explicit language in $UP\cap coUP\setminus P$.

**Two-sided witness lesson.** For an injective, not necessarily onto function, failure of a threshold can mean either that the preimage is later or that no preimage exists; the second case has no preimage certificate. Onto-ness removes this ambiguity and makes the complement positively and uniquely verifiable. This is the exact reason the permutation route reaches $UP\cap coUP$, while the earlier generic injection route only guarantees UP.

**Finite-model-theoretic form.** GrÃ¤del's 1994 characterization gives another expression of this same target: $P=UP\cap coUP$ iff every first-order logic on finite structures that captures P has the weak definability principle. A total implicit definition selects exactly one relation on each finite input; that unique relation is a polynomial-size certificate for either side of a tuple query. A verifier checks the defining formula and the tuple's bit, while global uniqueness ensures unambiguity. A canonical fixed-length truth-table encoding gives one guess path per candidate relation. Thus a failure of the weak definability principle is a finite Beth-style nondefinability witness for the same class separation. It does not independently prove the separation; the unresolved task is to exhibit such a query and establish that no explicit formula in the chosen P-capturing logic defines it. [GrÃ¤del (1994)](http://www.logic.rwth-aachen.de/pub/graedel/Gr-mlcs94.ps).

For the arithmetic permutation, the implicit query can be encoded directly. On ordered structures containing $y$ and polynomially many witness cells, let a unary relation $W$ encode the canonical padded preimage; require $G(z)=y$, and force $W$ empty on malformed encodings. Polynomial-time evaluation of $G$ is expressible in FO(LFP) by Immermanâ€“Vardi. Onto-ness and injectivity give exactly one $W$ for every well-formed $y$ input, so this is a total implicit definition. An explicit FO(LFP) definition of $W$ would recover the entire inverse in polynomial time by querying each cell. Thus the one-wayness claim is exactly a failure of explicit definability in this representation, with polynomial padding a required proof obligation. [Immerman (1986)](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0019-9958(86)80029-8).

**Audit of the converse's path completion.** Homanâ€“Thakur start from a partial polynomial-time bijection $h:D\to\{0,1\}^*$ with P-decidable domain and complete it by coding, for each $w\in D$, the chain

$$
\cdots\to110w\to10w\to01h(w)\to001h(w)\to0001h(w)\to\cdots.
$$

The map sends $1^{k+1}0w$ to $1^k0w$ for $k\ge1$, sends $10w$ to $01h(w)$, and sends $0^j1z$ to $0^{j+1}1z$ for $j\ge1$; it fixes all other strings. Left-prefix vertices are indexed by $w\in D$; right tails cover every $0^j1z$ exactly once because $h$ is a bijection onto all strings. Hence these disjoint bi-infinite chains plus fixed points partition the entire domain and $f$ shifts each chain one step. Prefix changes affect length by at most one, and the cross-edge inherits honesty from $h$. The unique preimage of $01y$ is $10w$ where $h(w)=y$, so a polynomial-time inverter for $f$ would invert $h$. This independently checks the totality/injectivity/inversion-preservation mechanism used in the converse. It completes an existing hard partial map; it supplies no source of hardness on its own.

**Hard witness choice for an easy language.** Homanâ€“Thakur's Theorem 3.4 additionally characterizes one-way permutation existence by $Î£^*\in HiPermUP$, equivalently by an easy language $L\in P$ with a UPTM whose unique-witness map permutes $L$ but is not polynomial-time computable. This separates easy language membership from hard selection of a canonical witness. For $Î£^*$, compare a candidate string with the unique witness; either answer has the same accepting-path certificate, and a P decider for the threshold would recover the witness by binary search. This is an equivalent representation of the same $P\ne UP\cap coUP$ target, not a new lower bound.

**Certified discrete-log permutation.** Upgrade the candidate in Section 89 without asserting hardness. Let $V$ be the P-decidable canonical set of encodings $1\langle p,g,C,x\rangle$ for odd prime $p$, complete canonical factorization $C$ of $p-1$, certified primitive root $g$, and $0\le x<p-1$. Set

$$
G(s)=\begin{cases}
1\langle p,g,C,(g^x\bmod p)-1\rangle,&s=1\langle p,g,C,x\rangle\in V,\\
s,&s\notin V.
\end{cases}
$$

For each fixed $(p,g,C)$, the exponent values and shifted nonzero residues both range over $\{0,\ldots,p-2\}$, and the primitive-root map is a bijection. Parameters remain fixed, so valid blocks map bijectively to themselves; every invalid string is a fixed point. This makes $G$ a total permutation on all bit strings. Canonical encoding ensures honesty; AKS primality testing, factorization-product checks, primitive-root checks, and modular exponentiation are polynomial-time. A polynomial-time inverter for $G$ is equivalent to a uniform polynomial-time solver for all these certified discrete-log instances.

**Debt.** If this particular permutation were proved noninvertible, Homanâ€“Thakur would yield $P\ne UP\cap coUP$ and hence $P\ne NP$. Its missing inverse lower bound is not supplied by the characterization; the theorem only ensures some hard permutation exists if the class separation already holds. The immediate proof target is $T_G\in UP\cap coUP\setminus P$, but proving its hardness still requires a structural argument beyond restating worst-case discrete-log hardness.

### 93. Direct diagonalization against inverter clocks (25 September 2026)

Consider enumerating polynomial-time inverter candidates $I_i$ with clock exponents $k_i$ and constructing one polynomial-time permutation that alters its action to defeat each candidate. Let $n$ be inverter input length and $m$ the corresponding preimage length. One fixed honesty polynomial gives $m\le n^a$, and one forward clock $m^C$ therefore permits at most $n^{aC}$ work. Simulating $I_i$ through $n^{k_i}$ steps cannot fit for unbounded $k_i$. Padding the output to $N$ gives forward budget at most $N^{aC}$ while the inverter budget grows to $N^{k_i}$; padding only the preimage beyond the honesty polynomial fails the permutation requirement. Therefore the straightforward clocked-simulation template does not produce a single P permutation. A family $F_c$ that defeats adversaries up to exponent $c$ is not a single one-way permutation.

This is a resource and quantifier audit of one proposed diagonalization, not an impossibility theorem for diagonalization. Any viable variant must find a polynomially bounded local falsification certificate that avoids simulating a candidate through its own arbitrary clock, or use a different structural invariant. See [Idea 131](ideas.md).

### 94. Audit of the public-input evidence interface in the 2026 claimed proof (25 September 2026)

**Literature lead.** Ben Goertzel's arXiv preprint *A Quantale-Weakness Route to $P\ne NP$ via CD Evidence Normalization and Gauge-Buffered Locked Ensembles*, arXiv:2510.08814, currently v2 submitted 22 April 2026, claims $P\ne NP$. Its abstract says a time-bounded description lower bound for a unique message on a sampled SAT ensemble contradicts the constant-description SAT-search procedure available under $P=NP$. This version is much more elaborate than the v1 abstract initially screened in this project, so I audited the current text rather than relying on the earlier summary. [Current arXiv record and version history](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.08814).

**Sound upper-bound component.** The final conditional direction is standard: if $P=NP$, construct the CNF $F_Y$, decide satisfiability of each restriction, fix witness bits, and project a satisfying assignment to the common message. The construction and projection run in polynomial time if the asserted witness relation is polynomial-time and polynomially represented. This gives one fixed short program and one fixed polynomial clock on every sampler-support input.

**Required lower-bound bridge.** The claimed lower bound routes every ordinary observer's predictive advantage through a finite primitive-read trace. The main text then normalizes non-neutral trace leaves to safe-buffer or hidden-gauge atoms, while neutral public syntax contributes zero pairwise derivative. The proof's own structure identifies this as essential: Theorem 5.25 asserts an observer-to-evidence expansion only for observers â€œusing the primitive read interfaceâ€; Hypothesis 4.22 declares atom completeness; Hypothesis 7.5 declares every normalized neutral atom pair-neutral. Appendix D.54 makes lock satisfiability/rigidity, no-public-target-tags, atom completeness, and compatible message-opposite couplings assumptions to a construction theorem rather than deriving all of them from a single concrete instance.

**Sharper interface location from Definitions 5.23 and D.29-D.33.** Appendix D.5 says the public instance is fully visible and that the syntax discipline is not an access restriction. However, Definition 5.23 supplies only a finite list of primitive read operations, Theorem 5.25 expands observers that use these operations, and Lemma 6.6's instrumentation statement is likewise conditional on using that interface. Definitions D.29-D.31 map selected public template/surface symbols to neutral atoms; Hypothesis D.33 asserts completeness only for target-relevant primitives generated by the chosen interface. The proof does not supply a compiler from arbitrary reads of the full encoded $Y$ and arbitrary ordinary computations on those bits to the primitive interface with a controlled trace cost. If a complete-input read is added as an atom, it separates opposite-message fibers and cannot be pair-neutral; if some fields are omitted, coverage of the stated full-input observer class is missing. This pins down the interface debt independently of the padding-rate issue. [Current arXiv text](https://arxiv.org/html/2510.08814).

**Exact consistency lemma.** For a target coordinate $j$, the paper defines opposite-message fibers $\Omega_j^0,\Omega_j^1$ and a coupling $\Gamma_j$ between them (Definitions 3.5â€“3.6). It also claims a single-message property: all satisfying witnesses over one public input $Y$ project to the same $M(Y)$ (Theorem 4.31). Therefore, for every coupled pair,

$$B_j(\omega^0)=0,\quad B_j(\omega^1)=1\quad\Longrightarrow\quad Y(\omega^0)\ne Y(\omega^1).$$

Suppose the primitive interface represents every accessible symbol of the ordinary encoded input $Y$ by a normalized neutral atom and Hypothesis 7.5 applies to those atoms. Pair-neutrality gives equality of every symbol on the two endpoints, hence equality of the entire encoded $Y$, contradicting the displayed implication. Equivalently, because $B_j=b_j(Y)$ on the support, the distributions of the *full* $Y$ conditioned on $B_j=0$ versus $B_j=1$ have disjoint supports and total-variation distance exactly 1. This is compatible with computational hardness, but incompatible with treating the complete input as a collection of free pair-neutral observations.

This leaves a precise dichotomy. If all bytes and structures of $F_Y$ are exposed through neutral public-template/surface observations, neutral coherence is false for every opposite-message coupling with nonempty fibers. If the primitive interface exposes only a restricted public skeleton, then Theorem 5.25 has not shown that a usual polynomial-time machine reading all of $F_Y$ can be represented through that interface. If some of the varying public formula syntax is instead classified as safe or gauge evidence, the proof must establish that *those actual syntax observations* obey the max-qSSM or independent-hidden-gauge bounds; Remark I.41 explicitly says it does not claim all public CNF cells are qSSM-small. The current text asserts in Proposition I.40 that the SAT presentation adds no new target-evidence source because a clause mentioning a message variable is not itself an assignment. That does not settle the issue: an ordinary algorithm can compute a message from the global constraint system without reading a literal assignment as a public tag.

**Quantitative form.** Let $A(Y)=(A_1(Y),\ldots,A_q(Y))$ be any finite lossless representation of the full public input using deterministic public observations. For a probability coupling $\Gamma_j$ of the opposite message fibers, every pair has different $Y$ and therefore different $A(Y)$. Thus

$$
\sum_{i=1}^{q}\partial_{A_i}w_j(\Omega_j^0\times\Omega_j^1)
=\sum_{i=1}^{q}\Gamma_j[A_i(Y^0)\ne A_i(Y^1)]\geq1.
$$

So at least one full-input observation must carry nonzero derivative; a lossless interface cannot place all its components in the zero-cost neutral class. This is an information-accounting requirement, not a lower bound against all possible non-neutral charging schemes. Summing over $|S|=\Theta(t)$ selected message coordinates gives at least $\Theta(t)$ total bitwise disagreement mass for a complete input representation, which cannot support the claimed $o(t)$ derivative budget. The self-reduction's exact prediction is consistent with the derivative budget only if its phase-separating public reads are actually included and charged; doing so at this raw-input level costs linear aggregate mass.

Under the contradiction assumption $P=NP$, the bit-fixing SAT self-reduction is itself a deterministic polynomial-time observer of the full public formula and predicts every $B_j$ exactly. Any lower-bound interface used in the clash has to include that observer. Its exact trace must gain target information somewhere. A proof that calls every public read neutral, or simply defines the trace language so only safe/gauge reads can carry information, has not yet shown that ordinary computation on the full input obeys the restriction. This is a model-completeness gap; it does not imply that all evidence-trace or coupling methods are invalid.

This is not speculation about what the two sides of the contradiction can see: Remark 4.23 of the paper explicitly says the lower-bound observer and the hypothetical $P=NP$ self-reduction receive exactly the same public input $Y$. The paper's own input interface therefore needs to reconcile full formula access with pair-neutral zero-cost syntax.

**Learning / next proof obligation.** Record the â€œvisible-target incompatibilityâ€ principle: a target that is uniquely fixed by the visible instance cannot be pairwise invariant under opposite-target couplings. For future distributional lower bounds, explicitly map every input bit, derived predicate, and adaptive query of a standard Turing machine into the charged transcript. Then test the interface against the hypothetical algorithm supplied by the assumed collapse. Zero-cost atoms must be pointwise invariant only after verifying they do not collectively contain the target; absence of a literal target tag is insufficient. See [Idea 132](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.15](New%20Model.MD).

**Status:** the preprint's advertised $P\ne NP$ conclusion is not established by the audited interface as written. The missing/contradictory public-input accounting is a fatal proof gap unless repaired by a concrete ensemble and a complete ordinary-machine simulation with valid charges.
