This is not a counterexample to the proposition $P\ne NP$ and not a proof of $P=NP$.

### 95. Gauge-fixing shows sampler entropy is not search-witness entropy (25 September 2026)

The current preprint version adds a hidden gauge lift: conditioned on quotient $z$, it samples $g$ uniformly and sets $x=z\oplus g$ (Appendix D, Definition D.17 / Lemma D.18). The verifier depends on $z$ for its lock, buffer, and message constraints and includes the equation $x\oplus g=z$. Its gauge action sends $(x,g,z,\xi,M)$ to $(x\oplus s,g\oplus s,z,\xi,M)$ and preserves $Y$, the verifier relation, and the message (Definition 4.14 / Lemma 4.15).

**Canonical-gauge lemma.** For any satisfying witness $W=(x,g,z,\xi,M)$, set $s=g$. Then

$$x'=x\oplus g=z,\qquad g'=g\oplus g=0,\qquad z'=z,$$

and $W'=s\cdot W$ is still a satisfying witness with the same message. Therefore for every support input $Y$, the CNF $F_Y$ remains satisfiable after adjoining all unit clauses $\neg g_v$. This assertion does not need $P=NP$; it follows from the paper's gauge-invariance lemma and sampler-support satisfiability.

At the relation level, substituting $g=0,x=z$ makes the gauge equation tautological; recomputing or dropping its Tseitin auxiliaries yields the quotient verifier on $(z,\xi,M)$. A quotient witness extends to a full canonical-gauge witness in polynomial time, and any full witness can be gauge-transformed to this slice and projected. Hence witness search for the gauge-lifted relation and the quotient relation are polynomial-time interreducible with message preservation; the gauge lift adds no search hardness.

Assuming $P=NP$, a deterministic polynomial-time search algorithm can exploit this fact. Build $F_Y\wedge\bigwedge_{v\in V}\neg g_v$ and run bit-fixing SAT self-reduction. The added formula has polynomial size because $|V|=\mathrm{poly}(|Y|)$; all restrictions remain satisfiable along a standard witness-search path. The returned witness has $g=0$ and its message projection is the same unique $M(Y)$. Thus the hypothetical class-collapse algorithm never needs to predict the sampler's random gauge vector.

This exposes a second semantic gap in the proposed evidence budget. The hidden-gauge product law is a distributional statement about the sampler's representative $g(\omega)$. But the SAT search can select a different valid representative $g'(Y)=0$. If a normalized gauge atom $G(v,\gamma)$ is evaluated on the sampler world, the search's output bit is not evidence that $g_v(\omega)=\gamma$. If the atom is instead evaluated on the search-produced witness, its gauge coordinates are algorithm-chosen and do not obey the sampler's product-uniform law. The proof must justify which object its atom denotes and prove that target-prediction success requires correct information about the sampler's gauge.

This distinction matters because the target $M$ is explicitly invariant under the gauge action. Uniform entropy of a nuisance representative cannot alone imply hardness of its invariant quotient: the symmetry gives an exact canonical section on satisfying witnesses, namely $g=0$, $x=z$. The hard search may still be to find $z$ satisfying the global lock, but the entropy of $g$ does not pay that debt. Corollary 5.21 says the CD-ENF syntax has no free rule replacing a gauge orbit by $g=0$ unless gauge literals are charged. That formal rewriting restriction does not prevent the actual SAT search algorithm from restricting its CNF to the satisfiable $g=0$ slice; the observer-completeness argument must cover that ordinary computation.

There is also a direct quantitative separation under the contradiction assumption. If $A(Y)$ is the SAT search restricted to $g=0$ and $\widehat g(Y)=0^{|V|}$, then $A$ returns the correct $M(Y)$ on every support input, while the probability that $\widehat g$ equals the sampler's $g$ is $2^{-|V|}$ conditional on $Y$ (Hypothesis 4.17 with no prior gauge-bearing evidence). Thus no general implication from exact message-prediction success to accurate prediction of the sampled gauge can follow from the product law alone. Any valid gauge-rank route must establish a stronger, specifically trace-dependent implication and reconcile the two meanings of â€œwitnessâ€ above.

**Cross-check and scope.** This is independent of, but compounds, the public-input coverage issue in Section 94. It does not prove the ensemble has an easy message or refute all entropy methods. It proves the exact symmetry reduction and shows that the stated sampler entropy is not automatically entropy of the witness selected by an algorithm. Any repair must lower-bound complexity of the quotient/message after allowing arbitrary representative choice, or establish a new target-sensitive invariant that survives canonical gauge fixing. See [Idea 133](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.16](New%20Model.MD). Source: [current arXiv text, Lemma 4.15, Lemma D.18, Corollary 5.21](https://arxiv.org/html/2510.08814).

### 96. Clock-specific lower bounds versus one uniform sampler (25 September 2026)

A parameterized lower bound can be useful even when its constants depend on an algorithm's polynomial exponent. For a contradiction from $P=NP$, assume a SAT-search algorithm with one fixed exponent $D_*$, then instantiate the ensemble and its lower bound for that $D_*$. Goertzel's v2 explicitly uses this order: Lemma 11.12 and Remark 11.18 fix the alleged solver clock before selecting the ensemble. This dependence is not, on its own, a flaw in that contradiction.

A stronger conclusion would be to combine the separate clocked ensembles into one fixed polynomial-time sampler that is hard against every polynomial-time decoder. The paper's Section 12.2 gives an observer trace bound $C_Q(D)m^{q_D}$, and Section 12.6 uses $q_D$ to choose the safe radius. This is a bound on the encoded cost of a $D$-clock observer, not a runtime bound for generating the ensemble. Hypothesis 12.3 asks for a polynomial geometry instantiation for each fixed $D$ and target error exponent $B$, preserving polynomial-time sampling, but it does not specify a sampler exponent $a(D,B)$ uniform in those parameters or a rate comparing $a(D,B)$ with $D$.

**Padding rate lemma.** Suppose a construction for hardness clock $d$ starts with a public instance of length $m$, can be sampled in time $m^{a_d}$, and defeats every decoder of time at most $m^d$. Pad its input to length $N=m^{r_d}$, preserving the message and allowing a decoder on the padded instance to be simulated on the base instance. If one length-indexed sampler is to run in time $N^C$ for a fixed $C$, then $a_d/r_d$ must be bounded, aside from uniform index-selection and padding costs. A decoder clock $N^k$ transfers to a base clock $m^{r_d k+O(r_d)}$. If every sufficiently large selected block must exclude every fixed $k$, one needs $d/r_d\to\infty$ and hence $d/a_d\to\infty$. If only arbitrarily large hard blocks are required, an effectively selectable subsequence with unbounded $d/r_d$ may suffice, so unbounded $d/a_d$ is the corresponding rate test. If $a_d=\Omega(d)$, neither padding-only version makes the translated hardness clock unbounded. If $a_d=o(d)$, the stronger rate test is only a first filter; uniform effectiveness and threshold control remain to be proved.

This calculation does not establish a general uniformization impossibility: a construction could use a different assembly method or a local hardness invariant not requiring simulation through the full clock. Nor does missing rate information weaken the paper's claimed fixed-clock contradiction by itself. For the separate one-way-function route, however, the project must not turn per-clock ensembles into one hard sampler without supplying the uniform rate and construction. The current text states no such rate. See [Idea 134](ideas.md#idea-134---a-rate-condition-for-padding-clock-specific-hard-ensembles) and [New Model, Section 2.17](New%20Model.MD).

**Learning / next move.** Maintain the distinction between specializing to the one clock in a contradiction and uniformizing over all clocks. If the latter is attempted, obtain a concrete bound $a(D,B)$ for sampler generation, specify how $D,B$ are selected as a function of input length, and test the padding inequalities before claiming a single OWF or uniform hard generator. This branch is secondary to the preprint's independently identified full-input coverage and gauge-quotient gaps.

Source: [Goertzel, *A Quantale-Weakness Route to $P\ne NP$*, current arXiv HTML, Sections 11-12](https://arxiv.org/html/2510.08814).

### 97. Uncharged machine indices collapse class-relative witness complexity (25 September 2026)

Audited Fabio F. G. Buono, [*Witness Complexity of Short Descriptions: A Cryptographic Perspective*, arXiv:2606.31370v1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.31370v1), especially Definition 2.6 and Theorems 4.4 and 4.7. The definition minimizes over near-shortest descriptions $d$ and arbitrary $M\in\mathbf P$ satisfying $M(d)=x$, charging $T_M(d)$ but neither $|M|$ nor a requirement that the same machine serve multiple $x$.

**Direct bound.** Let $N=|x|$. Fix any near-shortest $d$, so $|d|\leq K(x)+c_0\leq N+O(1)$. A machine $M_x$ can hardcode $x$, ignore its input, and print $x$ in $O(N)$ steps. The description of $M_x$ can grow with $N$; membership in $\mathbf P$ is a per-machine property, and the definition does not charge the machine's code. Hence for a model-dependent constant $C$,

$$
N\leq\gamma_{\mathbf P}(x)\leq C(N+1).
$$

The lower bound holds because the standard output-tape Turing machine must write all $N$ output symbols; the source uses the same convention when asserting $\gamma(x)\geq n$ for an $n$-bit output in Example 5.2. Thus $\gamma_{\mathbf P}(x)=\Theta(|x|)$ for every nonempty $x$. This is a direct two-sided theorem, not an asymptotic conjecture or computational observation. It makes any claim of superpolynomial $\gamma_{\mathbf P}(x)$ impossible on a family whose output length is polynomially bounded in the parameter.

**Theorem 4.4 (conditional separation) audit.** The theorem claims an infinite SAT-indexed family with $\gamma_{\mathbf P}(x_\varphi)$ superpolynomial in $|\varphi|$ under $P\ne NP$. Its construction has $x_\varphi=(1,y_\varphi)$, where $y_\varphi$ is an assignment on the explicitly represented variables, so $|x_\varphi|=O(|\varphi|)$ in the standard formula encoding. The direct bound above gives $\gamma_{\mathbf P}(x_\varphi)=O(|\varphi|)$ for every formula. Separately, the proof assumes that absence of one fixed decompressor that succeeds over the full hard subfamily implies large pointwise minima. Formally, $\neg\exists M\,\forall\varphi\,Q(M,\varphi)$ does not imply $\forall\varphi\,\forall M\,\neg Q(M,\varphi)$; the pointwise minimum permits $M_\varphi$. Thus Theorem 4.4's conclusion is incompatible with Definition 2.6 under the paper's stated size regime.

**Theorem 4.7 (characterization) reverse-direction audit.** The premise gives pointwise witnesses to the existential minimum: for each satisfiable $\varphi$, some $x_\varphi,d_\varphi,M_\varphi$ with a polynomial runtime bound. The reverse proof states that this â€œmeansâ€ there is a fixed $M^*$ independent of $\varphi$, then restricts search to descriptions $\langle\varphi,\pi_{M^*}\rangle$. Neither fixed-machine uniformity nor this canonical form for $d_\varphi$ is in the premise. An arbitrary existential near-shortest description is not known to be constructible or enumerable in polynomial time. The forward proof on source lines 471-476 expressly allows $M_{c'_w}$ to hardcode an instance-dependent pad, confirming that the definition permits decompressor descriptions of growing size.

For SAT under a verifier that permits ignored certificate padding, the pointwise right-hand condition can be met without deciding SAT: pad a valid assignment to polynomial length, use counting to select a Kolmogorov-rich suffix, and for each resulting certificate use its own printer. This makes $\gamma_{\mathbf P}=O(|x_\varphi|)=\mathrm{poly}(|\varphi|)$ by direct construction, but supplies no uniform algorithm to select the suffix or machine. It is therefore a counterexample to the intended inference from the premise to a uniform search algorithm, not a resolution of $P$ versus $NP$.

For a quantitative counting check, let the verifier accept witnesses of fixed length $3q(n)$ by checking the original witness prefix of length at most $q(n)$ and ignoring the remaining bits. For a fixed valid prefix there are at least $2^{2q(n)}$ suffixes. Fewer than $2^{q(n)}$ strings total have Kolmogorov complexity below $q(n)$, so some accepted padded witness has $K\geq q(n)=\Omega(n)$. Its length is polynomial in the SAT instance length.

The unsatisfiable side can be represented by a fixed-form polynomial-length string and printed by its own instance-dependent machine. Thus, for this padded verifier, the entire SAT-side right-hand condition is satisfied pointwise without deciding SAT. If Theorem 4.7's biconditional were valid with this interpretation, it would imply $P=NP$ unconditionally. The paper's reverse proof does not establish that implication; its uniformization step is the missing theorem, not a consequence of the pointwise definition.

**Proof obligation / reusable filter.** Any algorithm-minimum measure used to prove a uniform class separation must disclose whether machine descriptions are charged, whether one machine is fixed across all inputs, how the machine and description are selected, and what clock is uniform. First test the measure with a per-object constant printer. Then distinguish a uniform decompressor from pointwise machine existence and distinguish a fixed decompressor from a uniform method for finding its input description. See [Idea 135](ideas.md#idea-135---uncharged-per-instance-decompressors-erase-the-search-cost) and [New Model, Section 2.18](New%20Model.MD).

The paper's own Conjecture 4.18 explicitly adds a polynomial-time map from instances to rich witnesses, describing this as a uniformity condition beyond Theorem 4.7's pointwise premise. This makes the distinction visible in the source, but does not repair the reverse proof: that proof uses a fixed machine and canonical description without assuming the conjectured map. Also, merely charging polynomial machine-code length would not block a printer whose code stores a polynomial-size output; the cost model must rule out hardcoding at the scale of the claimed lower bound.

This gives a reusable three-level test: (i) objectwise machines/descriptions $\forall x\exists(M_x,d_x)$ collapse under hardcoding; (ii) a fixed machine $\exists M\forall x\exists d_x$ still leaves potentially exponential search over descriptions; (iii) a fixed polynomial-time selector for $d_x$ makes the representation uniform and can support an algorithmic consequence. Do not infer level (iii) from either of the first two levels. Merely bounding $|M_x|$ by a polynomial also leaves the constant-printer attack intact for polynomial-size outputs.

### 98. The proposed uniform rich-witness map conflicts with Kolmogorov monotonicity (25 September 2026)

Buono's Conjecture 4.18 asks for a polynomial-time computable map $f_L$ with $K(f_L(w))=\Omega(|w|)$ for every $w\in L$, uniformly across instances. This cannot hold for SAT as stated.

For every fixed computable $f$, a constant-size wrapper turns any shortest program for $w$ into a program for $f(w)$, so $K(f(w))\leq K(w)+O_f(1)$. Consider the satisfiable formulas $\varphi_m=\bigwedge_{i=1}^m(x_i\lor\neg x_i)$, with distinct tautological clauses. Their encoding lengths $n_m$ grow at least linearly and at most polynomially in $m$, but a program given $m$ in binary prints the whole formula, so $K(\varphi_m)=O(\log m)=O(\log n_m)$. A fixed P-time $f_{SAT}$ therefore has $K(f_{SAT}(\varphi_m))=O(\log n_m)$, contradicting the required $\Omega(n_m)$.

The contradiction is to the conjecture's universal uniform-map requirement, not to the pointwise counting claim that each satisfying instance has some rich padded witness. Those pads can be selected separately by existence, but a fixed computable selector cannot output one for every input, because $K(f(w))$ is at most $K(w)+O(1)$. Conditional complexity does not solve this: $K(f(w)\mid w)=O(1)$ for computable $f$.

This also sharpens the witness-model implications. Theorem 4.7's forward direction uses a noncomputable choice of a rich pad for each input; that is permissible in a pointwise existence theorem but cannot be upgraded to Conjecture 4.18 on all inputs. A possible repair is to restrict to an explicit high-$K$ SAT family, but a hardness-preserving reduction to such a family would be a new proof obligation. Merely asserting that high-$K$ formulas exist does not prove SAT hard on them.

See [Idea 136](ideas.md#idea-136---kolmogorov-complexity-cannot-rise-under-a-uniform-map), [New Model, Section 2.19](New%20Model.MD), and [Conjecture 4.18 in the source](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.31370v1).

### 99. Random padding turns high-K promise tractability into NP subset BPP (25 September 2026)

This is a derived consequence of the high-K uniform-witness issue in Section 98. Define $SAT_{pad}=\{\langle\varphi,r\rangle:\varphi\in SAT\}$ with an efficiently parsed pair encoding of length $|\varphi|+|r|+O(\log(|\varphi|+|r|))$. Its projection to $r$ is computable, so $K(\langle\varphi,r\rangle)\geq K(r)-O(1)$.

Fix $0<\delta<1$. Assume a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm $A$ is correct for $SAT_{pad}$ on every well-formed string $z$ satisfying $K(z)\geq\delta|z|$, with arbitrary behavior outside that promise. Choose $\beta\in(\delta,1)$. By counting self-delimiting programs, a uniform $N$-bit string $r$ satisfies $K(r)\geq\beta N$ except with probability at most $2^{-(1-\beta)N+O(1)}$. For a formula $\varphi$ of length $n$, take $N=Cn$ for sufficiently large constant $C$. The output length is $n+N+O(\log N)$; choosing $C$ so $\beta N-O(1)\geq\delta(n+N+O(\log N))$ ensures that $\langle\varphi,r\rangle$ lies in the promise whenever $r$ is incompressible enough. Sampling $r$ and running $A$ therefore gives a BPP algorithm for SAT with exponentially small error.

Composing with a polynomial-time reduction from any $L\in NP$ proves $NP\subseteq BPP$. Alternatively, choose one high-K string $r_n$ for each input length by counting and put it in polynomial advice; then a deterministic advice machine appends it to the reduced formula and applies $A$, proving $NP\subseteq P/poly$ directly.

This consequence does not give $P=NP$. It shows that an algorithm for the high-K promise slice is already a strong randomized/nonuniform upper bound for all NP. Conversely, proving the high-K slice is not in P would separate P from NP, but no argument for that lower bound follows from counting alone. The key distinction is that random padding is a randomized reduction and a fixed incompressible pad is advice; neither is a uniform deterministic selector of Idea 136's kind.

See [Idea 137](ideas.md#idea-137---random-padding-links-high-k-slices-to-bpp-and-p-poly) and [New Model, Section 2.20](New%20Model.MD).

### 100. High-K access mode and machine-sensitive oracle boundaries (25 September 2026)

This section records a fresh literature link and tests whether known Kolmogorov-random oracle power can strengthen the high-K promise result.

Three interfaces must be distinguished. A **promise solver** is correct on high-K inputs but does not know whether arbitrary strings satisfy the promise. **Advice** supplies one selected string per length. A **membership oracle** answers whether any queried string belongs to a high-complexity set. These are not interchangeable. In particular, the high-K padded-SAT solver of Section 99 implies $NP\subseteq BPP$ through random padding and $NP\subseteq P/poly$ through fixed advice; neither construction simulates arbitrary oracle membership.

The classic oracle results are substantial but concern the third interface. Allender et al. show $EXP=NP^{R_{Kt}}$ and $PSPACE=ZPP^{R_{Ks}}\subseteq P^{R_K}$ for their resource-bounded complexity sets and stated reductions ([ECCC TR02-028](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/eccc-reports/2002/TR02-028/)). These results cannot be cited as a solver for the promise slice. The oracle machine can query the semantic property of chosen strings; the promise solver cannot.

Milovanov's 2026 result supplies a sharper warning about the oracle interface itself. For some universal plain decompressor $U$, the halting problem is not in $P^{R_{C_U}}$, with $R_{C_U}=\{x:C_U(x)\geq|x|\}$. The paper notes an earlier result giving a different universal $U$ for which the halting problem is in this class ([arXiv:2605.16261](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16261)). Because universal plain complexities vary by additive constants, the zero-slack cutoff at exactly $|x|$ is sensitive to machine choice. This does not mean every high-K promise is equally sensitive: if a reduction produces strings with $K(z)\geq(\delta+\epsilon)|z|$ while the promise requires $K(z)\geq\delta|z|$, its $\epsilon|z|$ margin absorbs any fixed additive constant for sufficiently long strings. The bare predicate at the $\delta|z|$ boundary can still vary between machines.

The high-K promise implication also composes with recent meta-complexity and cryptographic results. Hu, Lu, and Oliveira prove $MINnKT\in BPP$ iff $NP\subseteq BPP$ ([ECCC TR25-203](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/203/)). Therefore a P solver for the promise slice would imply $MINnKT\in BPP$ via Section 99, but this is a transitive consequence, not a Karp reduction from SAT to $MINnKT$. Further, $NP\subseteq BPP$ gives a randomized inversion procedure for any proposed one-way function: encode existence of a preimage extending each prefix as an NP decision query and recover a preimage bit by bit. Hence existence of one-way functions would rule out the promise solver, conditionally. Since one-way-function existence is open, this is calibration rather than an unconditional lower bound.

**Learning:** every future algorithmic-randomness argument should specify the triple $(\mathcal K,\mathsf{access},\mu)$: complexity measure, promise/advice/oracle access mode, and threshold margin. A proof that silently changes one coordinate has changed its computational assumption. In particular, no oracle power follows from counting high-K strings or from random padding.

A deterministic polynomial-size list does not repair the random-padding reduction while preserving the promise. For a simple satisfiable family $\varphi_n$ with $K(\varphi_n)=O(\log n)$, each candidate in a uniformly generated list of $poly(n)$ pads is computed from $n$ and an $O(\log n)$-bit list index. Thus every resulting pair $z_{n,i}$ has $K(z_{n,i})=O(\log n)$ but length $\Omega(n)$, so none meets a fixed linear high-K promise for all sufficiently large $n$. This blocks the direct strategy of replacing random pads with a polynomial-size list whose every call to $A$ is promised. It does not block derandomization that obtains correctness outside the promise through a separate theorem.

For machine invariance, distinguish the threshold from slack above it. The bare set $\{z:K(z)\geq\delta|z|\}$ may vary on inputs within an additive constant of its boundary when the universal machine changes. If a reduction guarantees $K(z)\geq(\delta+\epsilon)|z|$, an additive loss of at most $c$ is absorbed for $|z|\geq c/\epsilon$. The access-triple margin records whether the proof has this stronger guarantee.

See [Idea 138](ideas.md#idea-138---randomness-access-channels-and-threshold-instability), [New Model, Section 2.21](New%20Model.MD), [Allender et al., TR02-028](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/eccc-reports/2002/TR02-028/), [Milovanov, arXiv:2605.16261](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16261), and [Hu, Lu, and Oliveira, TR25-203](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/203/).

### 101. Fixed-polynomial-clock Kolmogorov randomness as a coNP target (25 September 2026)

This lead comes from Hirahara's *Unexpected Hardness Results for Kolmogorov Complexity Under Uniform Reductions* (ECCC TR20-050). Fix $t(n)=n^q$ and define $R_t=\{x:K^t(x)\geq|x|-1\}$, where $K^t$ minimizes program length subject to printing the output in the fixed time bound. The complement has a polynomial-size witness (a short program) and polynomial-time verifier (bounded simulation), so $R_t\in coNP$. Counting gives members at every length. Therefore $R_t\notin P$ would imply $P\ne NP$.

The paper proves a stronger P-immunity theorem when $t(n)=n^{\omega(1)}$: there is no infinite P subset of $R_t$. Its proof assumes such a subset is decided by a P machine $M$ with runtime $n^a$, fixes an $(n-m)$-bit prefix from a member, and searches all $2^m$ suffixes for another accepted member. Taking $m=C\log n$ saves enough description length to put the output below the randomness threshold; the search costs $n^{a+C+O(1)}$, which eventually fits any super-polynomial $t$.

For a fixed polynomial clock $n^q$, this only excludes decider exponents $a<q-C_0$. It implies a degree lower bound $a\geq q-O(1)$ for any P decider if one exists, but not non-membership in P. If $q$ is fixed, a possible decider may have a larger polynomial exponent; choosing a new $q$ after inspecting it changes the language, and no uniform bound on the exponent as a function of $q$ is known. Thus the known P-immunity theorem cannot be promoted to a P-vs-NP proof by fixing a sufficiently large polynomial clock.

The additive constant in that degree estimate is encoding-dependent. A tighter derivation (Idea 141) separates the generic proof for an arbitrary infinite P subset from the full target language. With plain K and an end-marked program, a fixed suffix of $m=O(1)$ bits pays for the fixed code header, yielding runtime $O(n^a\log n)$ and ruling out $a<q$. For prefix-free K applied to an arbitrary infinite P subset, encoding $n$ with a self-delimiting code of length $h(n)=\log n+O(\log\log n)$ yields $O(n^{a+1}\operatorname{polylog}n)$ search time, ruling out $a\leq q-2$ and giving the generic floor $a\geq q-1$. Kraft's inequality explains why that all-length code covering every prefix cannot have constant overhead.

For a P decider of the **full** $R_{n^q}$ language, counting guarantees an accepted string at every length. Restrict to $n=2^k$, encode $k$ rather than $n$ in a prefix-free code of length $h(k)=\log k+O(\log\log k)$, and choose suffix size $m=h(k)+C$. The search then uses $O(\log n\operatorname{polyloglog}n)$ suffixes, so its total runtime is $n^a\operatorname{polylog}n$. If $a<q$, the output is an accepted string with a program of length at most $n-2$ within clock $n^q$, contradiction. Thus the exact language has degree floor $a\geq q$ under either K convention. This does not extend to P-immunity of arbitrary infinite subsets, whose occupied lengths need not include $2^k$, and still leaves decider degrees $q$ or higher possible.

Hirahara's related result links non-trivial SAT algorithms to immunity of Levin-$Kt$-random strings (Theorems 3.10â€“3.11), including Williams-style SAT algorithms for uniform circuit classes. This provides a real technique connection, but Levin $Kt$-randomness is not known to be in coNP. The central unresolved bridge is to preserve polynomial-clock NP verification and establish a lower bound strong enough to defeat every polynomial exponent.

See [Idea 139](ideas.md#idea-139---the-fixed-clock-randomness-frontier), [Idea 144](ideas.md#idea-144---sparse-output-lengths-remove-the-prefix-free-degree-tax-for-the-full-target), [New Model, Section 2.22](New%20Model.MD), and [Hirahara, ECCC TR20-050](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2020/050/download/).

### 102. Random-string hitting lists do not supply a subclock selector (25 September 2026)

Korten and Santhanam's CCC 2025 paper [*How to Construct Random Strings*](https://doi.org/10.4230/LIPIcs.CCC.2025.35) constructs short-seed pseudorandom generators under strong time-hierarchy-style hardness assumptions against NP-oracle algorithms with advice. Their generator range is a list guaranteed to intersect the dense coNP set of strings with $K^{T(n)}(x)\geq n-1$. Concatenating the list gives a deterministic string with a lower time-bounded complexity bound without identifying the good seed. Theorem 23 gives conditional polynomial-time constructions with $K^{n^k}(x)\geq n/\operatorname{polylog}(n)$ in its stated regime; this is not an unconditional construction and does not place the construction time below the tested clock.

**Derived list-to-degree lemma.** Fix $q$ and suppose a uniform list generator outputs at most $n^b$ strings of length $n$ in time $n^g$, with at least one member in $R_q=\{x:K^{|x|^q}(x)\geq |x|-1\}$ for infinitely many $n$. If $D$ decides $R_q$ in time $n^a$, scan all list elements with $D$ and output the first accepted, using $0^n$ if no element is accepted. On every length where the list hits, this yields an element of $R_q$ in time $n^{\max(g,a+b)+O(1)}$. The resulting program has length $O(\log n)$, so if $q>\max(g,a+b)+O(1)$ it prints that element within the defining clock and contradicts its membership. This is a valid degree lower bound, not a separation for fixed $q$: an unknown decider may have a larger exponent.

There is a matching description barrier. A candidate from a polynomial-size list has a description consisting of the list generator, the length, and the $O(\log n)$-bit index. Therefore an enumeration taking $T'(n)$ with $T'(n)\log T'(n)=o(T(n))$ makes every listed element $K^{T(n)}$-compressible to $O(\log n)$ bits. A list containing a $K^T$-random element cannot be generated substantially faster than its own compression clock. Under $P=NP$, the coNP predicate can be tested to select the good item, but the known construction and test bounds do not yield a sub-$T$ description. This explains exactly why the proposed list-plus-selector bridge stops.

The paper supplies two further boundaries. Theorem 46 equates polynomial-time NP-oracle construction of near-maximally $K^{\operatorname{poly}(n)}$-random strings with polynomial-time NP-oracle construction of exponentially hard truth tables. Theorem 48 rules out a specified relativizing black-box range-avoidance reduction between exponents separated by more than one. The result is a method barrier in that formal black-box model, not an impossibility for nonrelativizing proofs. The useful next target is an input-structure-sensitive way to find an accepted suffix from the decider itself, or a direct lower-bound invariant not based on constructing a hard string.

**Learning:** a randomness-based proof must budget list construction, list cardinality, membership testing, selection, and the Kolmogorov output clock independently. A dense target property provides existence, and a hardness-based PRG provides a list, but neither pays for recognition within the same clock. This diagnosis aligns with the chain-rule audit: time-bounded information additivity can conceal both a circuit lower bound and a promise-gap meta-complexity algorithm.

See [Idea 140](ideas.md#idea-140---hitting-lists-selector-time-and-the-randomness-clock), [New Model, Section 2.23](New%20Model.MD), and Korten-Santhanam, [full CCC 2025 text](https://drops.dagstuhl.de/storage/00lipics/lipics-vol339-ccc2025/html/LIPIcs.CCC.2025.35/LIPIcs.CCC.2025.35.html).

### 103. Encoding-sensitive sharpening of the fixed-clock suffix proof (25 September 2026)

This is a proof refinement of Section 101, not a new separation. The earlier estimate set suffix length $m=C\log n$ and paid $2^m=n^C$ search cost. Re-derive the program code with the Kolmogorov convention stated explicitly:

* **Plain K:** With an end-marked program, the search routine can infer $n$ from the encoded prefix length if suffix length $m$ is a fixed constant. Choose $m$ larger than the fixed description header by at least two bits. The program hardcodes an $(n-m)$-bit prefix from an accepted member of the infinite P subset, searches the constant number $2^m$ of suffixes, and outputs an accepted extension. Its length is at most $n-2$, and an efficient universal simulation takes $O(n^a\log n)$ when the decider takes $n^a$. Hence a decider of degree $a<q$ is impossible for the fixed clock $n^q$.
* **Prefix-free K:** Include a self-delimiting code for $n$ of length $h(n)=\log n+O(\log\log n)$ and set $m(n)=h(n)+O(1)$ so the total program length is at most $n-2$. There are $2^{m(n)}=n\operatorname{polylog}(n)$ suffixes, so the search takes $n^{a+1}\operatorname{polylog}(n)$. It fits in $n^q$ when $q\geq a+2$, ruling out $a\leq q-2$ and giving $a\geq q-1$.

For a generic prefix-free construction covering every length and every possible prefix, the logarithmic cost is forced by Kraft: at each n, the $2^{n-m(n)}$ prefix codes of length $n-m(n)+h(n)+O(1)$ use total Kraft mass $\Theta(2^{-h(n)})$. The sum over n must converge, so $h(n)$ cannot be constant. This yields degree floor $q-1$ for an arbitrary infinite P subset. For the full language, however, [Idea 144](ideas.md#idea-144---sparse-output-lengths-remove-the-prefix-free-degree-tax-for-the-full-target) uses the all-length density to encode $k$ on $n=2^k$ and restores the degree floor $q$ for prefix-free K as well. Both bounds leave degree q and higher unaddressed. The implications are direct compression arguments and do not depend on P=NP.

The derivation assumes an efficient universal simulation with logarithmic time overhead, as in the standard model used for time-bounded K in the cited literature. For a universal machine with a larger simulation slowdown, adjust the exponent accordingly. The formal distinction also prevents silently importing a plain-K program-length trick into a prefix-free proof.

See [Idea 141](ideas.md#idea-141---the-suffix-search-exponent-is-a-coding-convention-cost), [Idea 144](ideas.md#idea-144---sparse-output-lengths-remove-the-prefix-free-degree-tax-for-the-full-target), [New Model, Section 2.22](New%20Model.MD), and Hirahara, [ECCC TR20-050](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2020/050/download/).

### 104. Black-box lower bound for accepted-suffix search (25 September 2026)

This audits the remaining generic speedup idea from Sections 101 and 103. Fix prefix $u$ and regard the decider only as an oracle $f(v)=M(uv)$ on $m$-bit suffixes, with a promise that some suffix is accepted. For the family of singleton oracles $f_z(v)=1$ exactly when $v=z$, a deterministic algorithm making fewer than $2^m-1$ distinct all-negative queries leaves at least two possible hidden locations and cannot guarantee an accepted output. Hence the worst-case query complexity is $\Omega(2^m)$. If $z$ is uniform, any randomized algorithm making $r$ queries succeeds with probability at most $(r+1)/2^m$, so constant success requires $\Omega(2^m)$ queries as well.

For prefix-free K, the coding refinement requires suffix length $m=\log n+O(\log\log n)$, making this $n\operatorname{polylog}(n)$ oracle calls. Thus neither black-box random sampling nor adaptive query ordering removes the extra degree in the generic compression proof. The lower bound applies only when the decider is exposed as an oracle: a source-level algorithm may exploit structure specific to the correct decider for $R_q$. The exact unpaid step would be to prove such structure yields faster search, or to replace the compression invariant entirely.

This closes only the black-box acceleration branch. It does not prove that the actual decider behaves like a singleton oracle, and it does not rule out non-black-box diagonalization. See [Idea 142](ideas.md#idea-142---black-box-suffix-search-cannot-beat-exhaustive-search) and [New Model, Section 2.24](New%20Model.MD).

### 105. Direct SAT liar self-reference fails its tableau-size recurrence (25 September 2026)

Test a diagonal proposal against a hypothetical polynomial-time SAT decider $A$: construct a formula $F_A(z)$ satisfiable iff $A(z)$ rejects, then try to choose $z=F_A(z)$. A standard full Cook-Levin tableau for a $T(n)$-step Turing-machine computation uses $\Theta(T(n)^2)$ local space-time records in its direct rectangular encoding. If $A$ runs in $T(n)=n^d$, writing the indexed CNF explicitly takes $O(T(n)^2\log T(n))=n^{2d}\operatorname{polylog}(n)$ bits. Feeding that larger formula back to $A$ makes the next tableau still larger; at the length level the recipe asks for a fixed point of a superlinear polynomial map. Padding increases, rather than closes, the recurrence.

This identifies why â€œapply the recursion theoremâ€ is incomplete: program-index self-reference has no automatic same-size guarantee for a CNF formula that must explicitly encode the solver's computation. A succinct circuit representation would make the object a succinct-SAT instance, outside the promise that $A$ decides ordinary SAT. The tested argument therefore does not refute a correct SAT algorithm. It only closes the direct full-tableau liar construction; another encoding or a different semantic diagonal remains logically possible.

There is also a constant-size anti-answer map: run $A(z)$ and output a fixed contradiction when it accepts, or a fixed tautology when it rejects. Then $SAT(F_A(z))=1-A(z)$, but if $A$ is correct this map sends the tautology to the contradiction and the contradiction to the tautology. It has a 2-cycle and no fixed point. Thus the alternate construction removes the tableau size bill but does not provide the self-consistent formula needed for a contradiction; recursion-theorem self-reference for programs does not force a raw string fixed point.

**Next proof obligation:** specify a semantic interface to $A$'s behavior and prove (i) the resulting map has an admissible fixed point or another contradiction-producing invariant, (ii) its representation fits inside the final ordinary SAT instance, and (iii) its verifier is sound and complete for the intended opposite answer. A fixed-point theorem should be invoked only after both existence and size are established. This is the concrete extension of the exponent/fixed-point audit in [New Model, Section 2.25](New%20Model.MD), linked to [Idea 143](ideas.md#idea-143---a-liar-formula-needs-a-size-controlled-fixed-point).

Ko's primary 1991 paper studies time-bounded program-size problems and reports oracle dependence for certain variations ([SIAM J. Comput. 20(5)](https://doi.org/10.1137/0220059)). This supports treating relativization as a real method boundary for the broader area, but it does not assert oracle behavior for the exact fixed-clock language $R_{n^q}$.

### 106. Sparse-length codes sharpen the prefix-free bound for the full fixed-clock set (25 September 2026)

This refines Sections 101 and 103 and [Idea 144](ideas.md#idea-144---sparse-output-lengths-remove-the-prefix-free-degree-tax-for-the-full-target). It is important to distinguish a decider for the whole language $R_{n^q}$ from a decider for an arbitrary infinite subset $S\subseteq R_{n^q}$.

Fix integer $q\ge2$ and assume $D$ decides the full $R_{n^q}$ language in time $O(n^a)$. Counting gives an accepted string at every length: among the $2^n$ strings, fewer than $2^{n-1}$ can be printed by programs of length below $n-1$. Restrict to $n=2^k$. Let $e(k)$ be a self-delimiting code for $k$ of length $h(k)=\log k+O(\log\log k)$. Construct a fixed prefix-free decompressor $V$ whose descriptions are $e(k)u$, with $|u|=n-m(k)$ and $m(k)=h(k)+C$ for a fixed constant covering the universal-machine compiler and a two-bit margin. It computes $n=2^k$, scans the $2^{m(k)}$ suffixes, runs $D$ on each extension, and outputs the first accepted one.

The domain is prefix-free: codes $e(k)$ for different $k$ are prefix-free, and for each $k$ the literal tail $u$ has one fixed length. Choose $u$ to be the prefix of any member of $R_{n^q}$ at length $n$. The search halts, and its output is also in $R_{n^q}$. Under a universal prefix-free machine, the description length is $h(k)+(n-m(k))+O(1)\leq n-2$. The number of trials is $2^{m(k)}=O(k\operatorname{polylog}k)=O(\log n\operatorname{polyloglog}n)$, so the total print time, including writing the $n$-bit output, is $O(n+n^a\operatorname{polylog}n)$. For integer $q\ge2$ and any $a<q$, this is below $n^q$ for sufficiently large $n$, contradicting the output's membership in $R_{n^q}$. Therefore any P decider for the full language has degree at least $q$, for plain or prefix-free K under the stated efficient-simulation model.

This does not improve the generic P-immunity proof for arbitrary infinite $S$. Such an $S$ may contain strings only at a length set for which no simple subsequence like $2^k$ is available; the proof cannot assume a member at each selected length. The result also leaves a degree-$q$ decider possible, so it remains a quantitative lower bound rather than a P-vs-NP separation.

**Updated proof obligation:** attack the exact-clock boundary $a=q$ or find a different invariant. Do not use the generic prefix-free $q-1$ floor as the best bound for the full $R_{n^q}$ language; retain it only for arbitrary infinite subsets where the all-length abundance is absent. See [New Model, Section 2.22](New%20Model.MD) and checklist item 34.

### 107. Kraft leaves an unbounded factor in black-box exact-clock suffix search (25 September 2026)

Fix integer $q\ge2$. Let $n_j$ be any computable unbounded sequence of lengths and $e(j)$ a prefix-free code for its index, of length $h_j$. In a one-prefix compressor, a description has the form $e(j)u$ with $|u|=n_j-m_j$. To be at least two bits shorter than an output of length $n_j$, after the fixed universal-machine overhead, it needs $m_j\ge h_j+C$. Exhaustive suffix search then considers at least $2^{h_j+C}$ candidates.

Kraft gives $\sum_j2^{-h_j}\le1$, so $h_j$ is unbounded and in fact tends to infinity along the sequence. The query lower bound of Section 104 supplies a singleton-accepting oracle for which a black-box selector must inspect essentially every candidate. If each membership call may consume $\Theta(n_j^q)$ time, the method cannot guarantee that its total runtime remains at most $n_j^q$. Choosing sparser output lengths makes the overhead slower-growing but still unbounded.

This closes only the black-box, exhaustive-suffix version of the equality case. It does not establish that the actual decider for $R_{n^q}$ has singleton-oracle behavior, nor preclude shared computation visible from its code or a different coding/invariant. A proof of $P\ne NP$ cannot be claimed from this bound. The next meaningful step is a non-black-box lemma that exploits a required structure of any correct decider, or a new argument that avoids per-suffix calls. See [Idea 145](ideas.md#idea-145---prefix-free-coding-leaves-an-unbounded-search-factor-at-the-exact-clock-boundary) and [New Model, Section 2.26](New%20Model.MD).

### 108. P=NP supplies a selector, but not one below the fixed clock (25 September 2026)

Fix $q\ge2$. Since the full $R_q=\{x:K^{|x|^q}(x)\ge |x|-1\}$ is in coNP, under $P=NP$ it has a P decider $D_q$. Define $E_q$ on a unary length $1^n$ and a prefix $u$ to mean that some $n$-bit extension of $u$ is accepted by $D_q$. This language is in NP: the extension is a polynomial-size certificate and the decider runs in polynomial time. Under $P=NP$, $E_q$ is in P. Greedily choose a bit that preserves existence of an accepted completion; this constructs a P-time selector $G_q(1^n)\in R_q$ for every $n$.

The program for this fixed selector, given a self-delimiting code for $n$, has description length $O(\log n)$. Measure its runtime on the same fixed reference universal machine used for $K^{n^q}$, including simulation and output. If it were at most $n^q$ for infinitely many sufficiently large $n$, it would contradict $K^{n^q}(G_q(1^n))\ge n-1$. Thus no implementation with reference-machine exponent below $q$ can be the selector; degree $q$ with a sufficiently large leading constant remains possible. This makes the search-to-decision consequence precise but does not yield a contradiction: the assumption $P=NP$ gives polynomial time with no exponent bound below $q$ for $E_q$.

The 2026 coding result for Levin $Kt$ ([Hu, Lu, and Oliveira, ICALP 2026 / ECCC TR25-211](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/211/)) is tied to $EXP\ne BPP$ and is not a theorem about the hard-clock measure $K^t$. The separate chain-rule characterization by Kabanets and Kolokolova has additional circuit-lower-bound and promise-gap premises ([ECCC TR25-089, Revision 1](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/089/)). These results help locate the frontier but do not close the selector-degree gap. See [Idea 146](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.27](New%20Model.MD).

**Search exponent check.** Suppose one SAT algorithm runs in time $N^d$. A direct rectangular Cook-Levin implementation gives the previous bound $n^{4qd^2+1+o(1)}$, but classical reductions from nondeterministic time T to 3SAT use size $T\operatorname{polylog}T$. Using those reductions, the complement verifier for $R_q$ yields a P decider $D_q$ in $n^{qd+o(1)}$ time. The verifier for $E_q$ runs $D_q$ once; its quasilinear SAT reduction and the same SAT algorithm decide one query in $n^{qd^2+o(1)}$. The n-1 adaptive prefix queries yield a selector in $n^{qd^2+1+o(1)}$: the counting bound gives more than half of all n-bit strings in R_q, so both first-bit halves contain a member and the first bit needs no query. Since d >= 1 for explicit SAT input, this remains above n^q, but it is a substantially sharper generic upper bound than the direct tableau estimate. The remaining linear-query cost is not a lower bound against code-aware batching; the precise open target is to share or batch the related extension computations, or avoid extracting all n bits. See Idea 154 and [Jahanjou, Miles, and Viola, Section 1.2](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2013/099/revision/3/download/).

### 109. Dense pseudodeterminism creates a PP output-decoding gap (25 September 2026)

Theorem 1.1 of Chen, Lu, Oliveira, Ren, and Santhanam says that every dense P property has a randomized polynomial-time pseudodeterministic constructor: there is a canonical accepted string $x_n$ at every length, the algorithm emits only $x_n$ or $\bot$ except with probability $2^{-n}$ on every length, and it emits $x_n$ with probability at least $1-2^{-n}$ on infinitely many lengths ([arXiv:2305.15140](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.15140)). Under $P=NP$, the fixed-clock language $R_q=\{x:K^{|x|^q}(x)\ge|x|-1\}$ is in P and has density at least $1/2$, so the theorem applies to it.

This does not immediately derandomize the constructor under $P=NP$. For bit position $i$, let $p_{n,i}$ be the probability that its output is an $n$-bit string with bit $i=1$. The threshold language $M_B=\{(1^n,i):p_{n,i}\ge1/2\}$ is in PP. On every length where $B$ succeeds with probability at least $1-2^{-n}$, $M_B$ recovers bit $i$ of $x_n$: its acceptance probability is at least $1-2^{-n}$ for a 1-bit and at most $2^{-n}$ for a 0-bit. On other lengths, the theorem gives no bound on $p_{n,i}$, so it supplies no promise gap for deciding $M_B$ in BPP. The fact $P=NP$ collapses BPP to P does not apply to an arbitrary PP threshold predicate.

Under the stronger assumption $PP=P$, bitwise evaluation would produce a deterministic polynomial-time generator agreeing with $x_n$ on infinitely many lengths. Its degree is still unconstrained, so the fixed-clock contradiction still requires a degree below $q$. The theorem does imply, conditionally on $P=NP$, that infinitely many members of $R_q$ have randomized polynomial-clock description length $O(\log n)$ but deterministic $K^{n^q}$ complexity at least $n-1$. This is a separation between description measures, not between P and NP; fixing the random seed can cost polynomially many bits.

**Learning:** distinguish existential projection from majority projection. Prefix self-reduction asks an NP question and under $P=NP$ provides a polynomial selector of unspecified degree (Section 108). Decoding an infinitely-often randomized canonical output asks for a PP threshold, and its success guarantee on only infinitely many lengths is insufficient for BPP derandomization. A possible route would need an all-length promise gap, a special NP certificate for the output-majority predicate, or a construction that avoids decoding the canonical output. Any candidate still needs an exponent below $q$. See [Idea 147](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.28](New%20Model.MD).

### 110. P=NP does not relativize to a PP collapse (25 September 2026)

Aaronson, Ingram, and Kretschmer's Theorem 10 constructs an oracle $A$ such that $P^A=NP^A\ne BQP^A=P^{\#P^A}$ ([CCC 2022 paper](https://drops.dagstuhl.de/entities/document/10.4230/LIPIcs.CCC.2022.20)). This implies $PP^A\ne P^A$. The identity $P^{\#P^A}=P^{PP^A}$ relativizes: polynomially many PP threshold queries recover a #P count by binary search, while a PP query is a threshold question about a #P count. If $PP^A=P^A$, then $P^{PP^A}=P^A$, contradicting the theorem. In the same oracle, $BPP^A=P^A$ because $BPP^A\subseteq PH^A$ and $P^A=NP^A$ collapses $PH^A$.

This is a precise oracle separation between the quantifier interfaces in Sections 108-109. Existential SAT-style search and bounded-error randomness collapse with $P^A$, but majority/counting power does not. Hence no proof that relativizes can infer $PP=P$ merely from $P=NP$, and the generic majority-output extraction of the pseudodeterministic constructor cannot be justified by that collapse alone.

**Limit:** the oracle does not establish that the specific predicate $M_B$ for the unrelativized fixed-clock language is outside P under $P=NP$. It neither proves nor refutes $P=NP$. It says a successful use of this branch needs a feature of the actual $R_q$ decider or constructor that does not survive arbitrary oracle relativization, or a route avoiding the PP threshold. Any P-vs-NP argument must still resolve the selector exponent gap.

**Learning:** classify each backward-implication step by its quantifier. An existential projection is exposed to NP collapse; a majority projection is exposed to PP and can remain hard after $P=NP$ in relativized worlds. This gives a concrete novelty requirement rather than a generic instruction to "derandomize." See [Idea 148](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.29](New%20Model.MD).

### 111. Existential seed search avoids majority decoding but not the clock gap (25 September 2026)

Assume $P=NP$ and fix $q\ge2$. Then $R_q$ has a P decider $D_q$. Apply the dense-property theorem to obtain randomized polynomial-time $B$ and canonical $x_n\in R_q$ on infinitely many good lengths. Let $r(n)$ be a polynomial upper bound on B's random-bit use and define $W_q(1^n,s)=1$ iff deterministic simulation of $B(1^n)$ on tape $s$ outputs a length-$n$ string $y$ accepted by $D_q$. Under the assumption, this is a P-verifiable relation.

The prefix-extension language $F_q(1^n,p)$, true iff some $r(n)$-bit tape extending prefix $p$ satisfies $W_q$, is in NP and hence in P under $P=NP$. Greedy self-reduction finds a successful tape whenever one exists. On every good length, B outputs $x_n\in R_q$ with probability at least $1-2^{-n}$, so such a tape exists. The resulting deterministic polynomial-time transducer outputs some member of $R_q$ on infinitely many lengths. It need not recover the canonical $x_n$; this avoids the PP majority predicate in Sections 109-110.

The degree remains unpaid. The P algorithm for $F_q$ can have any polynomial exponent, and the compression argument only contradicts a selector whose reference-machine time is below $n^q$. The direct prefix-extension selector of Section 108 already produces a member at every length with an uncontrolled degree, so this seed construction has not improved the separation route. A useful follow-up would prove a sub-$q$ bound for this structured seed search; the accounting must include random-tape length, B simulation, the $R_q$ decider, Cook-Levin encoding, SAT-decider exponent, and all prefix queries.

**Learning:** the PP barrier is specific to extracting the canonical output. For the existence of any high-K member, an existential search over random tapes suffices under $P=NP$. The remaining bottleneck is the exact clock exponent, not the quantifier type. See [Idea 149](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.30](New%20Model.MD).
### 112. Exact query cost for a near-certain successful seed (25 September 2026)

Fix length $n$ and an $r$-bit seed space. Let $f:\{0,1\}^r\to\{0,1\}$ accept at least a $1-2^{-n}$ fraction. Then the number of rejected seeds is at most $b=\lfloor2^{r-n}\rfloor$. A deterministic membership-query algorithm needs exactly $b$ worst-case queries to output an accepted seed. For the upper bound, query $b$ distinct seeds; if all reject, no other seed can be rejected, so output an unqueried seed. For the lower bound, if the algorithm makes $t<b$ queries and receives all rejections, mark its output rejected as well if it was not queried. At most $t+1\le b$ rejections are used, so this defines a promised oracle on which the algorithm fails. A uniform random seed, in contrast, succeeds with probability at least $1-2^{-n}$. If $r<n$, then $b=0$ and every seed is accepted; if $r\ge n$, the query bound is $2^{r-n}$.

For Idea 149, define $f(s)=1$ iff $B(1^n;s)$ emits a length-$n$ string accepted by $D_q$. On every good length the canonical output is valid with probability at least $1-2^{-n}$, so this oracle satisfies the promise. Density alone gives a polynomial query search only when $\max(0,r(n)-n)=O(\log n)$; if $r(n)-n=\omega(\log n)$, its exact black-box query cost is super-polynomial. The dense-property theorem guarantees polynomial running time but does not state the random-bit bound needed for the short-list shortcut ([Chen et al., Theorem 1.1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.15140)).

**Limit:** this is a lower bound only for deterministic algorithms that use $f$ as a membership oracle. It does not constrain a code-aware procedure that exploits the structure of $B,D_q$, or uses the NP existential-prefix predicate $F_q$ available under $P=NP$. It shows that near-certain randomized success by itself is insufficient; a proof needs a random-tape bound close to $n$, a structural shortcut, or a different selector. Even then, the total reference-machine runtime must be below $n^q$ to contradict $R_q$ membership. See [Idea 150](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.31](New%20Model.MD).

**Literature distinction:** Chattopadhyay, Dahiya, and Mahajan prove $D_{dt}(S)=O(psD_{dt}(S)^3)$ for total search problems and analyze pseudo-deterministic query lower bounds for PromiseFIND1 ([MFCS 2023, Theorem 3.2](https://drops.dagstuhl.de/entities/document/10.4230/LIPIcs.MFCS.2023.34)). Those results measure canonical-output pseudo-determinism and total-search relations. The present $b$-query theorem is an elementary adversary lower bound for deterministic any-witness search under a dense-set promise; the randomized one-query strategy is not pseudo-deterministic, and the constructor is infinitely often successful. It is complementary, not a replacement for their results.


### 113. One-bit advice can be searched away existentially, but not majority-decoded for free (25 September 2026)

Chen et al.'s Corollary 3.2 supplies a polynomial-time randomized constructor with a one-bit advice sequence \(\alpha_n\). On the advised input, it emits a canonical string with probability at least \(1-2^{-n}\), and that string lies in the dense easy property on infinitely many lengths. The corollary does not give a uniform procedure for generating \(\alpha_n\).

For the fixed-clock target \(R_q\), assume \(P=NP\). If only any valid output is required, include both the advice bit and a random tape as existential witnesses, then verify the output with the P decider for \(R_q\). The resulting prefix-extension relation is in NP and hence P. Check whether any witness exists, output a fixed default if not, and otherwise use self-reduction; this gives a total transducer that finds a valid output on every length where one exists. The theorem supplies such lengths infinitely often. This eliminates the advice sequence for existential search, but reproduces Idea 149 and inherits its uncontrolled polynomial degree. The direct prefix selector in audit Section 108 is stronger on coverage: it finds a member at every length under the same assumption.

If instead we decode the canonical output, each bit is a threshold question about a randomized machine's output distribution. Its promise gap is known only for the selected advice values, not for every advice input. Thus the threshold is not automatically a total BPP language, and \(P=NP\Rightarrow BPP=P\) cannot be invoked as a generic derandomization. The one-bit corollary therefore adds no clock bound and does not close the promise-majority interface from Sections 109-110.

**Learning:** constant-size advice can be eliminated by finite existential branching when verifying an any-witness output; this does not eliminate it from a majority predicate merely by considering both values. The remaining proof target is a source-specific search procedure with reference-machine time below \(n^q\), or a total bounded-error reformulation of canonical decoding derived from the actual constructor. See [Idea 151](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.32](New%20Model.MD).


### 114. Time hierarchy pinpoints why fixed-degree diagonalization is insufficient (25 September 2026)

Enumerate polynomially clocked nondeterministic machines \(N_i\) and deterministic machines \(D_j\). Their agreement predicate \(E(i,j,x)\) is decidable by bounded simulation. Hence the class equality can be written

$$
P=NP \iff \forall i\ \exists j\ \forall x\ E(i,j,x),
$$

and separation as \(\exists i\ \forall j\ \exists x\ \neg E(i,j,x)\). To prove \(P\ne NP\), one must establish a single NP machine whose language disagrees with every polynomially clocked deterministic candidate. A diagonalization against only degree \(c\) proves at most that one language is outside \(\mathrm{DTIME}(n^c)\).

The deterministic time hierarchy gives \(L_c\in\mathrm{DTIME}(n^{c+1})\setminus\mathrm{DTIME}(n^c)\) for each fixed \(c\), since \(n^c\log n=o(n^{c+1})\). This is an unconditional strict hierarchy inside P. Under \(P=NP\), every \(L_c\) is also in NP, so the collapse still permits unbounded polynomial degrees across languages. Consequently, neither the class equality nor a generic search-to-decision conversion can supply one exponent that works for every NP verifier.

This clarifies the failed diagonalization pattern: simulating a finite set of candidate deciders costs a polynomial whose degree depends on their maximum clock; increasing the finite set without bound destroys the fixed polynomial clock of the proposed NP diagonal language. Fixing a degree misses machines above it. Padding does not change this quantifier issue unless a construction proves a same-language simulation bound, which none of the tested padding arguments provides.

For the fixed-clock branch, Idea 144 independently shows that a P decider for \(R_q\) must have degree at least \(q\). Thus increasing \(q\) raises both the target clock and its necessary decision degree; it does not outrun the unknown degree supplied by \(P=NP\). The viable target remains a non-generic argument that defeats all deterministic polynomial clocks for one fixed NP language. The all-machine form is syntactically \(\Pi^0_3\). NP-completeness of SAT gives an equivalent \(\Sigma^0_2\) form for \(P=NP\), and hence a \(\Pi^0_2\) form for its negation. These classifications record quantifiers only and make no claim of ZFC independence.

Source: Hartmanis and Stearns, [On the Computational Complexity of Algorithms](https://doi.org/10.1090/S0002-9947-1965-0170805-7), which establishes the deterministic time hierarchy used above. See also [Idea 152](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.33](New%20Model.MD).


### 115. Odd-negation cycles give a precise liar-proof obligation (25 September 2026)

For a correct SAT decider \(A\), an odd cycle of ordinary formulas satisfying \(\operatorname{SAT}(\phi_i)=1-A(\phi_{i+1})\) yields \(b_0=1-b_0\). The semantic contradiction is valid. Idea 143's constant anti-answer map instead gives a two-cycle with alternating, consistent truth values. A k-state tag does not force an odd orbit: the combined state update (tag,b) -> (tag+1 mod k,1-b) has period 2k when k is odd. More generally, every actual cycle of a map that flips SAT has even length. Recursion-theorem self-reference does not itself provide a raw-string fixed point.

Encoding each $A(\phi_{i+1})$ computation as a rectangular Cook-Levin tableau expands the formula at each edge, so this direct finite cycle does not close. The quadratic blowup is specific to that encoding: classical reductions encode nondeterministic time T in 3SAT size T polylog(T) (Jahanjou, Miles, and Viola, Section 1.2). Even this sharper generic reduction still grows from m to m^{d+o(1)} when the decider runs in m^d time for d>1; it does not rule out code-aware fixed-point methods. A viable attempt must separately establish odd semantic parity, ordinary-formula closure, and construction within one fixed NP clock. This closes the direct tableau template only, not all self-reference.

See [Idea 153](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.34](New%20Model.MD).

### 116. Quasilinear reductions sharpen the fixed-clock selector accounting (25 September 2026)

The standard rectangular Cook-Levin tableau is not the best generic reduction from nondeterministic time to SAT. Classical simulation-based reductions produce a 3SAT instance of size $T\operatorname{polylog}T$; Jahanjou, Miles, and Viola summarize this result and its lineage in Section 1.2 ([ECCC TR13-099 revision 3](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2013/099/revision/3/download/)). Thus the quadratic size estimate in Sections 108 and 115 applies to the direct rectangular encoding, not to every Cook-Levin reduction.

For fixed q >= 2 and a SAT decider running in N^d time, the first quasilinear reduction gives a decider for R_q in n^{qd+o(1)} time. The prefix-extension verifier runs that decider once; reducing it a second time and calling SAT costs n^{qd^2+o(1)} per question. The n-1 adaptive questions in the generic selector give total time n^{qd^2+1+o(1)}; the first bit is free because both first-bit halves contain an accepted string by density. As d >= 1 for explicit SAT input, this remains above the n^q fixed clock. It improves the previous direct-tableau bound n^{4qd^2+1+o(1)} but does not yield the needed contradiction.

The corrected learning is that generic encoding size was not the only apparent degree tax: after using a quasilinear generic reduction, the sequential witness-extraction queries remain (n-1 exactly, n asymptotically). This is not a query lower bound for the concrete E_q instances and does not rule out shared computation. Gharibian and Kamminga summarize that generic classical SAT search needs Theta(n) NP queries under ETH, via enumeration of all possible o(n)-bit answer transcripts; this is only a conditional benchmark, applies to arbitrary SAT inputs, and cannot be invoked under the P=NP assumption being tested. The next target is a proved batch-search bound for this related family, or a compression invariant that avoids full bitwise selection. See Idea 154 and [New Model, Section 2.35](New%20Model.MD). Source for the query benchmark: Gharibian and Kamminga, [BQP, meet NP, Section 1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.03943).

### 117. A fast near-certain high-K constructor needs at least twice the output length in randomness

Fix q. Let B(1^n) use r(n) random bits and output any member of R_q with probability at least 1-2^{-h(n)} on infinitely many lengths. Suppose every deterministic fixed-tape run of B fits within n^q steps on those lengths. Then r(n) >= n+h(n)-O(log n).

Choose k=n-C log n for a sufficiently large constant C. Each k-bit string s gives a U-program of length k+O(log n)<n-1: store n and s, pad the tape with zeros, simulate B, and print the result. No such padded tape can be successful, since it would output an x in R_q with K_U^{n^q}(x)<n-1. Hence at least 2^k tapes fail. The success probability bound allows at most 2^{r(n)-h(n)} failures, yielding r(n)>=h(n)+k. If r(n)<k, every tape has a short description and positive success probability already contradicts R_q membership.

On the dense-property theorem's successful lengths h(n)=n, so a constructor whose complete run fits below the fixed clock would need at least 2n-O(log n) random bits. This is consistent with polynomial randomness and gives no contradiction; P=NP does not bound the constructor's time exponent below q. A conditional proof target is a constructor obtained from P=NP with error 2^{-n}, 2n-\omega(\log n) random bits, and fixed-tape runtime below n^q. The dense-property theorem supplies no such tight bounds. The lemma applies to any-member search and does not require canonical pseudodeterminism. It explains why near-certain success alone cannot identify a short successful seed.

Source context: Chen et al., [Polynomial-Time Pseudodeterministic Construction of Primes, Theorem 1.1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.15140); project derivation: [Idea 155](ideas.md).


### 118. Randomized high-K descriptions must pay in random bits

Let R_q be the length-n strings with K_U^{n^q}(x) >= n-1. A randomized program with code length L that, on input 1^n, uses r(n) random bits and outputs an R_q member with probability at least 1-2^{-h(n)}, for h(n) >= 1, must satisfy L+r(n) >= n+min(h(n),r(n))-O(log n), provided every fixed-tape simulation fits the n^q clock. To see this, choose k=max(0,n-L-C log n) for a sufficiently large constant C, encode n and a k-bit tape prefix, pad the rest with zeros, and simulate. A successful padded tape would describe an R_q member below the n-1 K threshold, so all 2^k such tapes fail. If r<k, any full tape is already a short description, contradicting positive success. If h<=r, the failure bound 2^{r-h} then gives L+r>=n+h-O(log n). If h>r, no tape may fail, contradicting k>0; thus k=0 and L>=n-O(log n). These cases give the stated bound with min(h,r).

This makes explicit the randomness hidden by short randomized Kolmogorov descriptions: an O(log n)-bit randomized program for a high-K output still needs linearly many coins at constant success, or about 2n coins at error 2^{-n}, if it runs under the matching K clock. Chen et al.'s dense-property theorem supplies error 2^{-n} on infinitely many good lengths, and its randomized-K corollary counts the program bits but not the coins ([Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.15140)).

This is not a contradiction under P=NP. Applying the theorem to R_q gives a polynomial-time constructor, but its exponent depends on the decider for R_q, and no bound places that exponent below q. The lower bound applies only after clock alignment. The new proof obligation is to align the dense constructor with its own high-K clock or prove an equally efficient randomness removal; neither follows from pseudodeterminism or a short randomized code alone. See Idea 156 and [New Model, Section 2.37](New%20Model.MD).

### 119. The dense-property construction's degree depends on the property decider

For fixed q under P=NP, let D_q decide R_q in n^a_q time. The fixed-clock argument gives a_q>=q. Chen et al.'s Theorem 1.1 applies to R_q, but the proof defines tau from the running-time exponent of A_Q, then lambda=c(tau+1)kappa and beta>=100lambda, and sets n_(i+1)=n_i^beta. Its reconstruction bound is (d+n)M^(c rho); with M=n_(i+1) and d'_i<=2n_i^c+n_i^tau, the displayed branch runs in roughly n_i^(tau+beta*c*rho), before other polynomial overheads.

Therefore the theorem certifies a polynomial-time constructor whose analyzed exponent depends on the decider degree, but it does not certify that the constructor fits the matching n^q K-clock. This is not a lower bound on the actual runtime: the algorithm may have a faster implementation or the analysis may be sharpened. It means only that the theorem's published polynomial-time guarantee cannot be used as the sub-clock step needed for the contradiction. Choosing q after the fact changes R_q and its decider.

This quantifies the clock-alignment gap left by Idea 156. A proof would need a resource-preserving dense-property constructor for R_q or a selector that avoids full use of D_q. Source: Chen et al., [Theorem 1.1, proof in Section 3](https://arxiv.org/html/2305.15140v2); see [Idea 157](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.38](New%20Model.MD).


### 120. Conjunction batching improves the supplied-list selector, not list generation

Fix q and assume a SAT decider runs in N^d time. For each supplied candidate x_i, a quasilinear reduction gives a 3CNF F_i of size n^(q+o(1)) satisfiable iff x_i is not in R_q. Conjoining F_i over an index block S gives a formula satisfiable iff every candidate in S is outside R_q. If a list of m candidates is promised to contain an R_q member, binary splitting finds one with O(log m) SAT calls. The formula sizes decrease geometrically, so the total time is O((m n^(q+o(1)))^d). For m=n this is n^((q+1)d+o(1)), improving the n^(qd^2+1+o(1)) generic prefix-selector exponent from audit Section 116 when d>1, and matching it at d=1. It remains above n^q because d>=1.

The input-list promise cannot be omitted. Density alone does not guarantee that a polynomial-size list intersects R_q. Moreover, a fixed enumerator E that outputs m=poly(n) candidates in n^c time for c<q gives each item K_U^{n^q}(x_i)<=|E|+O(log n+log m)=O(log n), using E, n, and a self-delimiting index; thus each item has K_U^{n^q}<n-1. Thus a uniformly generated sub-clock list contains no R_q member. The batched selector is a verified improvement conditional on an already supplied list, while construction of a guaranteed list is the unresolved part.

Learning: the SAT-query count can be compressed by bundling low-K certificates, but the same batching does not compress the information needed to obtain a high-K candidate. A useful next result would give a short, structured implicit family guaranteed to intersect R_q and enable a hit to be located without enumerating it. See [Idea 158](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.39](New%20Model.MD).

### 121. Fixed-clock high-K construction is one-bit range avoidance

Fix q and length n. The number of programs of length below n-1 is 2^{n-1}-1. Index them by all but one of the 2^{n-1} strings z, use the remaining index as a dummy, and let C_{n,q}(z) simulate the indexed program for n^q steps. Return its output if it halts with exactly n bits; otherwise return a fixed n-bit default. Then every output outside Range(C_{n,q}) has no description of length below n-1 within the clock, so it lies in R_q. Pigeonhole guarantees such an output because C_{n,q} maps 2^{n-1} inputs to 2^n outputs.

This is standard circuit AVOID with stretch one. A source-checked universal simulation gives size O(n^q log n), sharper than the earlier n^{O(q)} upper bound; this upper bound still does not certify a circuit below the n^q clock. General AVOID is complete for APEPP. Validating a candidate missing output says that all 2^{n-1} circuit inputs differ from it, hence is coNP in the explicit circuit description. If P=NP, this test is in P and the total search is in FP, and a solver taking S^d on a standard gate-list encoding of length S=O(n^q(log n)^2) gives O(n^{qd}(log n)^(2d)) after composition. This does not certify a subclock bound even at d=1; it is an upper-bound limitation, not a lower bound on the special family.

The proof-complexity link is precise but adjacent: Ren, Santhanam, and Wang, Theorem 6.6, characterize FNP algorithms for general constant-stretch AVOID by the existence of propositional proof systems breaking every nonuniform proof-complexity generator of constant stretch. Their high-K reduction builds a short-program range circuit, using a conditioned K variant. This supports the project's fiber/proof-system synthesis, but it neither gives an NP lower bound for our fixed-clock family nor transfers hardness from that family to general circuits.

A concrete sufficient target is the unary family L_q={(1^n,y): y is outside Range(C_{n,q})}. It is in coNP because the complement supplies z and evaluates C_{n,q}(z) in polynomial time. Proving L_q not in P already implies P != NP; the stronger L_q not in NP would also imply NP != coNP. Neither is achieved here; these restate the missing lower-bound debt. The failed shortcut was to invoke APEPP or general AVOID under P=NP and expect subclock computation: the generic composition gives no bound below n^q, even with the sharper circuit size. The next proof attempt must either establish a subclock special-purpose avoidance algorithm under P=NP or prove that this explicit range-exclusion language has no NP certificates.

**Sources:** Ren, Santhanam, and Wang, [*On the Range Avoidance Problem for Circuits*, ECCC TR22-048](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2022/048/) and [full paper, Sections 6.1-6.2](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2022/048/download/); compare the existing fiber model in [New Model, Sections 2.2-2.3](New%20Model.MD).

### 122. Dual weak pigeonhole links AVOID to bounded-arithmetic proof extraction

For a circuit or PV function f mapping n-bit inputs to ell-bit outputs with ell>n, dWPHP asserts that some ell-bit y is absent from the range. Ordinary counting proves existence. A feasible construction algorithm must find y; a bounded-arithmetic proof of the universal statement can carry stronger algorithmic consequences.

Ilango, Li, and Williams apply the KPT witnessing theorem. A proof of dWPHP for circuit evaluation in TPV yields a constant-round Student-Teacher procedure: the student proposes y, and if y is in the range, the teacher returns a preimage x. This is a deterministic polynomial-time AVOID procedure with O(1) circuit-inversion oracle queries. Their Theorem 21 rules such a procedure out assuming JLS-secure iO and coNP not infinitely often in AM. Corollary 23 therefore gives TPV not proving dWPHP for every constructive ell(n)<=poly(n), ell(n)>n, and hence APC1 strictly extends PV1 under those assumptions.

This sharpens the project's logic model: truth by finite counting, provability in PV1, and polynomial-time witness extraction are separate. ZFC proves finite pigeonhole existence; the conditional PV1 nonprovability result is not a ZFC independence statement. It also does not settle P vs NP: the assumptions are unproved and the general AVOID theorem does not establish hardness for the specific C_{n,q} family. In fact, P=NP implies coNP=P, and P is contained in AM, so the premise coNP not in i.o.AM used for the bounded-arithmetic separation would fail. A separate result in the same paper says polynomial-time AVOID on suitable polynomial-size circuits plus subexponentially secure iO implies coNP is contained in NP; this alone is consistent with P=NP.

**Learning:** the promising foundational question is whether a proof of the high-K range-avoidance instance in a weak, resource-sensitive theory forces a selector that is too strong. The cited KPT argument handles all circuits conditionally but only through a constant number of inversion queries. Transferring it to the explicit universal-program family or removing its cryptographic assumptions remains open. See [Idea 160](ideas.md), [New Model, Section 2.41](New%20Model.MD), and [Audit Section 5.8](P_VS_NP_PROJECT_AUDIT_2026-08-24.md#58-pigeonhole-proofs-and-dual-weak-pigeonhole).

**Source:** Ilango, Li, and Williams, [*Indistinguishability Obfuscation, Range Avoidance, and Bounded Arithmetic*, ECCC TR23-038](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2023/038/), especially Theorems 18, 20-21 and Corollary 23.

### 123. Pippenger-Fischer tightens the universal evaluator size

The earlier range-avoidance note used the safe but loose estimate n^{O(q)} for the universal evaluator circuit. A sharper construction is available. Let T=n^q. For each program p of length j<n-1, assign the (n-1)-bit index z=1^(n-2-j)0p; the all-ones index is the sole dummy. This is a bijection because the first zero determines j and the remaining suffix is exactly p. Decoding has O(n^2) circuit size.

A T-step multitape computation can be represented by log-space-uniform Boolean circuits with O(T log T) gates, by Pippenger-Fischer/Koucky. Shalunov's ECCC TR25-078 gives a direct exposition: its recursive circuit maps an initial tape configuration to the entire final configuration, so the bound applies to the multi-output universal evaluator, not only a single acceptance bit. Extracting the n output bits costs O(T) more gates. Since q>=2, the decoder is absorbed. Thus the gate count is O(n^q log n), while a standard gate-list encoding has length S=O(n^q(log n)^2).

Under P=NP, a generic Avoid solver with time S^d on a standard gate-list encoding of length S=O(n^q(log n)^2) yields a selector in O(n^(qd)(log n)^(2d)). Even the d=1 analysis only certifies O(n^q(log n)^2), not a subclock bound; d>1 gives a weaker generic bound. The material insight is that the broad exponent mismatch can be reduced to a logarithmic overhead plus the unknown decision/search degree, but a generic solver still must receive the explicit circuit. Any subclock breakthrough needs a code-aware avoidance procedure for the implicit universal evaluator or a different compression invariant.

This is an upper bound on the circuit representation, not a lower bound ruling out an O(n^q)-size specialized evaluator. Nor does it show that AVOID on this structured family needs the full general solver cost. The proposed special-purpose route remains open.

**Source:** Shalunov, [*Improved Bounds on the Space Complexity of Circuit Evaluation*, ECCC TR25-078](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/078/download/), Theorem 2.2 and Appendix B; original result: Pippenger and Fischer, [*Relations Among Complexity Measures*](https://doi.org/10.1145/322123.322138).


### 124. The 2026 Kt coding equivalence does not close the fixed-clock route

Hu, Lu, and Oliveira's ICALP 2026 paper proves equivalences between weak/non-trivial coding for deterministic Levin Kt and EXP != BPP, and between the corresponding nondeterministic Kt coding and NEXP != BPP. The paper is directly relevant to the project's broader premise that a compression theorem can encode a complexity lower bound. Its complexity measure, however, is Kt(x)=min(|p|+ceil(log t)), not the fixed-clock plain measure K_U^{n^q}(x).

The difference is essential for the current NP target. The complement of R_q={x:K_U^{n^q}(x)>=n-1} has an NP certificate p of length below n-1, verified by simulating for n^q steps. Kt can use a program whose runtime is superpolynomial in n; a saving in |p|+log(t) does not ensure |p|<n-1 within n^q steps. For example, a hypothetical code of length n-2sqrt(n) and runtime 2^sqrt(n) has Kt<n by sqrt(n) but is invisible to the R_q verifier for every fixed q at large n. Therefore the published Kt coding result cannot be substituted for the clock-sensitive compression needed to contradict P=NP.

The exact fixed-clock bounds remain compatible: under P=NP, each fixed R_q is in P; the existing compression argument rules out decider degrees a<q; and a SAT algorithm of degree c would give a generic upper bound near n^(qc+o(1)) via a quasilinear NP-to-SAT reduction. This does not show R_q is in P without the assumption, and the interval is consistent with P's unbounded polynomial degrees. The required target is R_q not in P for one fixed q, or a resource-preserving selector consequence of P=NP that contradicts the degree floor. P-immunity would suffice but is stronger than required.
