Altering q after learning the decider's exponent is invalid because it changes the language.

**Learning:** code length, computation time, and verifier class form a coupled resource budget. Superpolynomial clocks support known immunity arguments against every P decider, but the direct short-program verifier then loses its polynomial-time guarantee and no longer establishes coNP membership. Polynomial clocks preserve NP verification but only yield a degree-sensitive diagonal argument. A proof must bridge this frontier without silently replacing fixed-clock K by Kt.

**Source:** Hu, Lu, and Oliveira, [*Equivalence Between Coding and Complexity Lower Bounds*, ICALP 2026, Theorems 1 and 3](https://drops.dagstuhl.de/storage/00lipics/lipics-vol374-icalp2026/html/LIPIcs.ICALP.2026.110/LIPIcs.ICALP.2026.110.html); full version [ECCC TR25-211](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/211/).



A final implication check closes the tempting contradiction: if P=NP, then PH=P, so BPP=P; deterministic time hierarchy gives EXP != P, hence EXP != BPP. The paper's coding property therefore follows from P=NP via its equivalence. It cannot by itself refute the collapse assumption. A useful proof would need a further theorem that converts this Kt coding conclusion into a polynomial-clock K_U^{n^q} description for R_q.

The direct comparison is also checkable. On a common universal machine, if x is in R_q at length n, any program producing it either has length at least n-1 or runs longer than n^q. Therefore Kt_U(x)>=min(n-1,q log n), which equals q log n for sufficiently large n. Under P=NP, R_q is nonempty and in P, so FNP=FP supplies a polynomial-time selector of some degree b; its output has Kt_U at most O((b+1)log n) on a time-efficient fixed machine. Comparing requires b to grow linearly with q up to fixed machine-model constants. Generic search-to-decision does not bound b in terms of a membership-decider exponent, so this is not a contradiction. The Hu-Lu-Oliveira weak-coding bound n^epsilon is weaker than the selector's O(log n) description and supplies no stronger clock bound.


### 125. The fixed-clock exponent floor does not accumulate into a separation

For each fixed q, R_q is a coNP language. Under P=NP it has a polynomial-time decider, but the exponent can depend on q. The established compression argument excludes degree a<q. In the other direction, a SAT decider of degree d and a quasilinear Cook-Levin encoding give a generic upper bound n^(qd) polylog(n)^(O(1)). Both estimates scale with q, so they are compatible with P=NP; P has languages requiring arbitrarily large fixed polynomial degrees.

This is a quantifier issue. The statement "for every fixed q there is a decider whose degree is at least q" does not yield "there is a fixed q for which no decider exists." Choosing q after examining a decider changes the language. Making q an input also makes the n^q verification clock superpolynomial in the combined input length for unbounded q, so that uniform family is not covered by P=NP.

Korten and Santhanam's 2025 result conditionally constructs strings with K^(n^k)(x) >= n-poly(log^[d](n)) infinitely often and gives uniform Range Avoidance algorithms in specified parameter regimes. It is a relevant explicit-construction connection, but it does not provide our exact n-1 threshold together with a clock below n^q; the theorem gives no constructor degree below k, and the circuit stretch regime must be accounted for. Its general output-time bound forces near-n^k runtime to construct strings hard for a T=n^k clock. The route currently yields no separation. More precisely, if a uniform circuit maps r bits to m bits and evaluation fits within T, every range output has K^T at most r+O(log n). A lower bound m-Delta on a constructed output proves avoidance only when m-Delta>r+O(log n); the paper's poly(log^[d] n) deficit cannot cross that threshold for our one-bit stretch.

**Learning:** the required target is R_q not in P for one fixed q. P-immunity is a sufficient but unnecessarily strong target. Future arguments should preserve the fixed q and state the verifier, decider, selector, and description clocks in one resource ledger.

**Source:** Korten and Santhanam, [*How to Construct Random Strings*, ECCC TR25-104](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/104/), Theorem 12 and Section 4.4.


### 126. Multi-string chain rule is a precise separation target, with a cryptographic obstruction

Let CR be the conditional multi-string K^t chain rule with sublinear total-length error. A proof audit of Kabanets-Kolokolova's ECCC TR25-089 revision 1 gives the following implication map.

First, P=NP implies CR. For each fixed C>1, the predicate that a string x has a description of length at most |x|-|x|/C, conditional on y and running within unary clock t, is in NP. Under P=NP it is decidable in P. Conditional Kolmogorov counting ensures it rejects at least half of random x for every fixed y, so it is the required natural property. The paper's Theorem 7.6 supplies the exponential circuit lower-bound conjunct under P=NP via Kannan's theorem; Theorem 7.1 then gives CR. Therefore a proof of CR failure with a linear-in-N deficit on arbitrarily large tuples would imply P!=NP.

Second, CR implies that standard cryptographic one-way functions do not exist. Theorem 5.2 constructs from CR a P-computable natural property for K^t with usefulness n-n/D for any constant D. Set D=10. A polynomial-stretch PRG from any standard cryptographic OWF has n-bit outputs generated from n^epsilon-bit seeds, epsilon<1, so every output has K^t<n-n/D at a polynomial clock. The property accepts all PRG outputs and rejects at least half of uniform strings, contradicting PRG security. Hence standard cryptographic OWF existence implies CR failure, conditionally; such OWFs are not known to exist. Appendix B's more quantitative two-string theorem uses the stronger error delta=o(n^{1/3}) and yields deterministic polynomial-time inversion almost everywhere. Do not conflate that threshold with the multi-string o(N) result.

The naive PRG seed-plus-chunks argument did not itself prove conditional-K lower bounds: ordinary PRG security does not test coNP predicates. A refinement does give an explicit conditional witness constructor. Fix any proposed chain rule with right-hand clock p(t), hidden coefficient a in O(ell log t), and sublinear error e(N,t). Choose D with PRG seed length N^alpha < N-N/D-eta N, and C>2D so e(N,t)≤N/C for all sufficiently large N. Set t=N^k, then partition the N-bit generator output into ell≈N/(c log t) blocks of b≈c log t bits. Let S=floor((N-N/D+a ell log t+N/C)/ell), choosing c large enough that S≤b-2log ell-O(1).

Define A(x)=1 iff some block has K^{p(t)}(x_i|x_1...x_{i-1})≤S. Enumerating all fewer than 2^(S+1)=t^{O(c)} programs and simulating each for p(t) makes A polynomial time. By conditional-K counting and a union bound, A accepts only O(1/ell) of uniform x. A secure PRG fools A, so it rejects almost all generator outputs. For any rejected output, every block has K^{p(t)}>S, giving a conditional sum greater than N-N/D+a ell log t+N/C. Yet the whole output has K^t≤N^alpha+O(log N). Since e≤N/C and the seed saves eta N bits relative to N-N/D, this tuple violates the proposed chain inequality by a linear amount. Sampling seeds until A rejects is an expected-polynomial-time conditional witness construction. The PRG output length can be chosen/truncated to a block-compatible length; polynomial stretch and security are preserved.

This sharpens the earlier finding: OWF existence does not merely imply abstract chain-rule failure; it lets us efficiently find a violating tuple for each proposed clock and constants. The logarithmic block length is the crucial step that makes the local K test polynomial-time. The result remains conditional on OWFs, which are not known to exist.

The unconditional counterexample obligation is still: for some fixed D>1, defeat every proposed polynomial clock and cutoff at arbitrarily large lengths with a linear deficit after O(ell log t). No finite tuple settles it. A proof without cryptographic assumptions must build the same low-joint/high-conditional structure directly or derive it from a weaker premise that already implies P!=NP.

**Learning:** the failed direct PRG approach exposed an important parameter mismatch; shrinking blocks to O(log t) resolves that mismatch conditionally. This is now a concrete construction path, but its only source of pseudorandom outputs is the unproved OWF premise. Continue by testing whether the key blockwise property can be forced from P!=NP or a purely diagonal argument without assuming OWFs.

**Sources:** Kabanets and Kolokolova, [ECCC TR25-089, revision 1, Theorems 5.2, 7.1, 7.6 and B.1](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/089/revision/1/download/); Huang, Ilango, and Ren, [ECCC TR23-046](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2023/046/). See also [Idea 164](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.45](New%20Model.MD).



### 127. Do not convert the cryptographic obstruction into P = UP

The tempting chain-rule implication
\[
CR\Rightarrow\text{no one-way functions}\Rightarrow P=UP
\]
does not go through. Kabanets-Kolokolova's natural-property argument rules out standard cryptographic OWFs by contradicting secure PRGs: it gives a polynomial-time distinguisher and therefore excludes average-case inversion hardness for uniform inputs. Grollmann-Selman instead characterize \(P\ne UP\) by a worst-case honest injective function with no deterministic polynomial-time inverter that succeeds on every range input. Almost-everywhere inversion does not imply inversion on every input. Thus the first implication does not rule out the Grollmann-Selman witness and does not imply \(P=UP\).

Valiant-Vazirani gives \(P=UP\Rightarrow NP\subseteq RP\), which alone is not a known proof of \(P=NP\). In the CR setting \(BPP=P\) as well, so if CR really implied \(P=UP\), then \(NP\subseteq RP\subseteq BPP=P\) would finish. The issue is that the audited cryptographic argument does not imply \(P=UP\).

The verified unconditional consequences of CR in the stated parameter regime are: \(E\not\subset io\text{-}SIZE[2^{o(n)}]\), hence \(BPP=P\); the corresponding conditional \(GapMcK^tP\) promise problem is in P; and no standard cryptographic OWFs exist. If that exact gap problem is additionally NP-hard under randomized polynomial-time reductions, then \(NP\subseteq BPP=P\), giving \(P=NP\). The reverse implication \(P=NP\Rightarrow CR\) is known. So the chain-rule equivalence to \(P=NP\) depends on the NP-hardness bridge; CR's cryptographic consequence by itself does not supply it.

**Learning:** track worst-case/average-case, deterministic/randomized, uniform-family/single-function, and all-input/almost-all-input quantifiers on every crypto arrow. “No OWFs” is too ambiguous to be used in a proof. Continue at the exact missing obligation: unconditional NP-hardness of the relevant gap problem or a direct SAT reduction respecting its clock and additive gap.

**Sources:** Kabanets-Kolokolova [TR25-089 rev. 1](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/089/revision/1/download/), in particular Theorems 2.2, 4.1, 5.2, 6.2, 6.5 and Appendix A; Grollmann-Selman [1988](https://epubs.siam.org/doi/10.1137/0217018); Huang-Ilango-Ren [TR23-046](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2023/046/download/). See also [Idea 165](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.46](New%20Model.MD).



### 128. Conditional collapse from witness-encryption hardness and CR

A more exact version of the gap-hardness bridge is available in Kabanets-Kolokolova Theorem 6.4 (from Huang-Ilango-Ren). Assume subexponentially secure witness encryption for NP. It gives a randomized-reduction NP-hard promise problem distinguishing, for \(|x|=n\), \(|y|=\mathrm{poly}(n)\), and polynomial \(t_H\),
\[
K^{t_H(n)}(x\mid y)\le \sqrt n
\quad\text{from}\quad
K^{2^{n^2}}(x\mid y)\ge n-O(1).
\]

Under CR, the conditional-gap characterization gives a polynomial-time promise solver with yes condition \(K^t(x\mid y)\le s\), no condition \(K^{\tau(t)}(x\mid y)>s+n/C\), for a fixed polynomial \(\tau\) and constant \(C>1\). Query the HIR instance with \(s=\lceil\sqrt n\rceil\), \(t=t_H(n)\). Yes instances meet the yes promise. On no instances, \(\tau(t_H(n))\) is polynomial in \(n\) and eventually below \(2^{n^2}\), so
\[
K^{\tau(t_H(n))}(x\mid y)\ge K^{2^{n^2}}(x\mid y)\ge n-O(1)>\lceil\sqrt n\rceil+n/C.
\]
Thus the CR gap solver decides the HIR promise problem. Its randomized NP-hardness reduction implies \(NP\subseteq BPP\). CR also implies \(E\not\subset io\text{-}SIZE[2^{o(n)}]\), hence \(BPP=P\); therefore CR plus subexponentially secure witness encryption for NP implies \(P=NP\). The converse \(P=NP\Rightarrow CR\) is unconditional, so the two are equivalent under this assumption.

This is conditional progress only. The outstanding proof debt is to remove the witness-encryption assumption from the NP-hardness bridge or to refute CR directly from \(P\ne NP\). The argument's type conversions are now explicit: randomized reduction gives BPP; the chain rule's circuit lower bound collapses BPP to P; polynomial \(\tau(t_H(n))\) fits beneath the HIR \(2^{n^2}\) no-clock; and \(n-O(1)\) exceeds the gap threshold for large n.

**Sources:** Kabanets-Kolokolova [TR25-089 revision 1](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/089/revision/1/download/), Corollary 2.17 and Theorems 6.2, 6.4, 6.5; Huang-Ilango-Ren [TR23-046](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2023/046/download/).



### 129. Direct reductions need witness-dependent compression

A simple invariant rejects a common proposed reduction template. Let a randomized reduction output a conditional \(K^t\) instance \((x,y,1^s,1^t)\). If a fixed program \(q\), with \(|q|\le s\), computes \(x\) from \(y\) within \(t\) on every output, then all outputs satisfy \(K^t(x\mid y)\le s\). Such a reduction cannot send unsatisfiable formulas to promised NO instances. In particular, if y contains the source formula and all random coins needed by an efficient reconstruction of x, then both YES and NO outputs are easy.

A viable reduction needs witness-dependent compression: the reduction generates \((x,y)\) without the witness, but a short conditional program can recover x when a satisfying witness exists, while no such short program works on unsatisfiable inputs even at the gap solver's enlarged clock. HIR achieves this conditionally using witness encryption. This is not a no-go theorem for all direct reductions; it only rules out constructions that expose an efficient reconstruction in the conditional input in both cases.

For each candidate, count the program bits and clock, test if the same reconstruction works in both promise cases, and prove high conditional K at \(\tau(t)\). Do not call an output “random-looking” a lower bound: the no-instance claim is a statement about every short program relative to the actual condition y.

**Learning:** a plausible fresh construction must implement a witness lock without relying on an unproved primitive, or derive that lock from a separate theorem. The formal obligation is now sharper than “find a clever encoding.”




### 130. Direct diagonalization fails its witness-length budget

For a formula of length \(m\), constructing an \(n\)-bit string outside every output of programs of length at most \(s\) by exhaustive enumeration costs \(2^{s+1}\mathrm{poly}(m,n,t)\). When \(n\) and unary \(t\) are polynomial in \(m\), that is polynomial-time only if \(s=O(\log m)\). A straightforward YES-side program that hardcodes a general satisfying assignment needs \(\Omega(m)\) bits. The direct exhaustive diagonalization template therefore cannot simultaneously fit the entire program enumeration and the witness encoding into a polynomial-time reduction.

This is only a rejected template. Padding the output polynomially does not make \(2^s\) polynomial when \(s\) grows linearly with the padded output; random sampling can make high K likely in the NO case but does not produce the witness-dependent compression required in the YES case. If the reduction publishes the random seed in y, the output becomes short in both cases; if it hides the seed, a satisfying witness must unlock it.

**Learning:** account jointly for source size, target size, program cutoff, reduction running time, and the enlarged no-case clock. “Count short programs” and “hardcode the witness” each work separately but clash at these parameters.

Related: [Idea 168](ideas.md) and [New Model, Section 2.49](New%20Model.MD).



### 131. Isolation does not make the unique witness efficiently decodable

Valiant-Vazirani can reduce a satisfiable formula to one with a unique satisfying assignment with inverse-polynomial probability. Encoding that assignment by rank among all \(m\)-bit strings takes m bits. Encoding rank 1 among satisfying assignments is short, but a decoder must search the assignment space to find that first witness, taking exponential time by the direct method. The gap clock t is unary encoded, so supplying an exponential clock makes the reduction output exponential; a polynomial clock is too short for that enumerative decoder.

This rejects the direct rank-compression patch to the witness-length conflict. It does not rule out a different polynomial-time witness-recovery mechanism or a non-enumerative compression theorem.

**Learning:** uniqueness is a cardinality statement, not an efficient selector. Check both code length and decoding time.




### 132. HIR's missing ingredient is witness-gated list hiding

HIR samples a uniform message m and outputs \(x=m\), \(y=\operatorname{WE}(1^\lambda,\varphi,m;r)\). A satisfying witness lets the conditional program decrypt m using y and φ, giving the low-K YES case. In the NO case, list-security ensures m is unlikely to occur in the list of all outputs of programs of length at most \(n-105\) running for \(2^{n^c}\) steps on y; hence m has near-maximal conditional K with high probability.

This identifies what the conditional cryptographic assumption contributes: the reduction can efficiently sample a hidden message, yet only a satisfying witness unlocks its short description. Randomness alone proves high K on typical NO inputs but does not make the message witness-decodable on YES inputs. A public encoding of φ and the sample seed makes it easy in both cases.

**Learning:** an unconditional replacement must establish both witness-gated recovery and NO-case list-hiding for all short programs. That is the precise construction obligation left by this route.

**Source:** Huang-Ilango-Ren [TR23-046, Theorem 3.1](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2023/046/download/).



### 133. The 2026 algorithmic chain rule does not directly inherit HIR's hardness reduction

Hirahara--Nanashima, ECCC TR25-092 revision 1, give an algorithmic proof of a time-bounded chain rule. Under \(GapK^t\text{-vs-}K\in pr\text{-}BPP\), an efficient procedure outputs per-prefix information bounds \(k_i\) with \(k_i\ge q^{\tau(t)}(x_i\mid x_{<i})\) and \(\sum_i k_i\le q^t(x)+mO(\log t)\), with high probability. This constructive budget allocation supports their universal inductive-inference theorem.

The hardness types do not match HIR. The newer gap is unconditional, with YES \(K^t(x)\le s\) and NO plain, unbounded \(K(x)>s+c\log(t|x|)\). HIR uses conditional \(K^t(x\mid y)\) and a NO lower bound at one finite clock \(K^{2^{n^2}}(x\mid y)\ge n-O(1)\). Since \(K(x)=\inf_T K^T(x)\le K^T(x)\), high complexity at one finite clock does not imply high plain K. Also, deleting y from the YES description can require paying its description length and generation time. Thus no direct parameter substitution transfers HIR hardness to the newer gap.

**Next proof obligation:** any claimed bridge must show how it removes side information without overrunning the YES description budget, strengthens the finite-clock lower bound to the target plain-K statement (or changes the target theorem to the finite-clock variant), and preserves the time model of the randomized \(q^t\) chain rule. Without those steps, the new paper strengthens conditional meta-complexity tools but yields no P-vs-NP separation or collapse.

The direction of implication is consistent with \(P=NP\): \(P=NP\Rightarrow DistNP\subseteq AvgP\subseteq AvgBPP\), and the report proves \(DistNP\subseteq AvgBPP\Rightarrow GapK^t\text{-vs-}K\in pr\text{-}BPP\). The resulting inductive-inference theorem therefore cannot by itself refute \(P=NP\).

**Sources:** Hirahara--Nanashima [TR25-092 revision 1](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/092/revision/1/download/), Definitions 2.1/4.3 and Lemma 5.1; Kabanets--Kolokolova [TR25-089 revision 1](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/089/revision/1/download/); Huang--Ilango--Ren [TR23-046](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2023/046/download/).



### 134. Under P = NP, the fixed-clock range circuit yields near-maximum E circuit hardness

Let \(N=2^m\) and fix q large enough for the universal-machine evaluation below. Use \(C_{N,q}:\{0,1\}^{N-1}\to\{0,1\}^{N}\) to enumerate programs of length below \(N-1\) and simulate each for \(N^q\) steps. It has \(G=O(N^q\log N)\) gates and standard gate-list input length \(S=O(N^q(\log N)^2)\); any missing output \(y_N\) satisfies \(K_U^{N^q}(y_N)\ge N-1\) (Ideas 159/161).

If \(P=NP\), then \(\mathrm{PH}=\mathrm{P}\). For a prefix u, deciding whether it has an extension y with no preimage under C is a \(\Sigma_2^P\) predicate. Query this predicate one bit at a time to find \(y_N\); this gives a deterministic range-avoidance algorithm polynomial in S. Define an m-input Boolean function \(f_m\) with truth table \(y_{2^m}\). The construction costs \(\mathrm{poly}(S)=2^{O(m)}\), so \(f\in E\).

If a circuit D of size \(s\) computes \(f_m\), one program can encode D and print its N-bit truth table. Its code length is \(O(s\log s+\log N)\); for \(s\le cN/m\), choosing c sufficiently small makes this less than \(N-1\). Evaluating D over all N inputs takes \(N^{O(1)}\) time when \(s\le N\); choose q to dominate that fixed exponent. Then the program halts within \(N^q\), placing \(y_N\) in the range of \(C_{N,q}\), contradiction. Therefore \(C(f_m)=\Omega(2^m/m)\) for all sufficiently large m.

This is a conditional derivation of the E lower-bound conjunct in the KK characterization, with a concrete near-maximum bound. It does not contradict \(P=NP\): the collapse supplies the range-avoidance algorithm, and E has enough time to run it on a circuit whose size is polynomial in the truth-table length. The output construction need not fit under the K clock; only the hypothetical short-circuit-to-program simulation must fit.

**What this sharpens:** Idea 161's failure to find an \(R_q\) element in less than \(N^q\) steps blocks a direct \(P\ne NP\) contradiction, but not a conditional E circuit lower bound. Reparameterizing from output length N to Boolean input length \(m=\log N\) changes which clock is relevant.

**Sources:** project [Ideas 159/161/173](ideas.md); Chen, Hirahara, and Ren [*Symmetric Exponential Time Requires Near-Maximum Circuit Size*](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.12912); Kabanets--Kolokolova [ECCC TR25-089 revision 1](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2025/089/revision/1/download/).



### 135. The witness-encryption premise does not imply separation

Under the standard definition, witness-encryption security only constrains encryptions of NO instances; correctness is required when a witness exists. If \(P=NP\), encryption can decide whether \(\varphi\) is satisfiable, reveal the tagged message on YES instances, and output fresh message-independent randomness on NO instances. The NO-instance ciphertext distributions are identical across messages, so security is perfect.

Therefore WE does not itself imply \(P\ne NP\). The conditional implication WE + CR \(\Rightarrow P=NP\) is not vacuous by definition and supplies no unconditional separation.

Related: [Idea 171](ideas.md), [New Model, Section 2.52](New%20Model.MD).


### 136. The range-avoidance search bound does not beat its own clock

For the fixed-q evaluator \(C_{n,q}\), a missing n-bit output has \(K_U^{n^q}(y_n)\ge n-1\), while the gate count is \(G=O(n^q\log n)\) and the standard gate-list length is \(M(n,q)=O(n^q(\log n)^2)\). Under \(P=NP\), the prefix-extension decision predicate lies in P. If its fixed decider has degree d in the explicit input length, bitwise search costs at most \(O(nM^d)=n^{qd+1}(\log n)^{2d}\). This generic bound cannot be less than the incompressibility clock \(n^q\), since \(qd+1<q\) is impossible for \(d\ge1\). Even granting a direct FP selector of degree \(d'\ge1\), the generic bound \(M^{d'}\) would require \(qd'<q\).

This rules out the proposed *generic exponent comparison* as a route to \(P\ne NP\); it does not rule out a family-specific algorithmic insight, and it does not imply a lower bound on the AVOID decision problem. The K clock directly constrains a uniform generator that starts from n, not an AVOID algorithm measured from receipt of the full explicit circuit description. The conditional E circuit lower bound in §134 remains consistent with \(P=NP\).

Replacing the explicit gate list by a short generator does not retain the same PH predicate: at the empty-prefix query, for n exponentially larger than the generator description, the quantified y and x are exponentially long, placing the succinct prefix-extension predicate in the exponential hierarchy (in \(\Sigma_2^{EXP}\) under the standard encoding). \(P=NP\) collapses PH, not this exponential hierarchy by any known implication. Unary padding can restore PH membership, but does not relate the resulting fixed-q decider degree to q; padding to \(n^q\) matches the input length to the K clock.

**Next logical target:** a route must avoid asking an AVOID selector to outrun its incompressibility clock. Investigate either a SAT proof-theoretic property incompatible with \(P=NP\), or an NP diagonalization that handles all fixed polynomial exponents under one verifier. The second target faces the precise uniformity obstacle that the candidate P degree is arbitrary while an NP verifier has one fixed polynomial bound.

**Sources:** [Idea 174](ideas.md); [New Model, §2.55](New%20Model.MD); the general AVOID/circuit-lower-bound connection in [ECCC TR24-076](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2024/076/).


### 137. A source-hiding condition for reductions to the K-gap promise

Suppose a randomized reduction \(R(\varphi;r)=(x,y,1^s,1^t)\) runs in polynomial time \(T_R\), and NO instances require \(K_U^{\tau(t)}(x\mid y)>s+\Delta(|x|)\). If y lets a description q reconstruct \(\langle\varphi,r\rangle\) in time \(T_0\), a fixed wrapper runs R and prints x. If the composed clock fits \(\tau(t)\), then \(K_U^{\tau(t)}(x\mid y)\le |q|+O(1)\), contradicting NO whenever \(|q|\le s+\Delta-O(1)\). Even without y, storing \(\varphi,r\) yields a description of length \(|\varphi|+|r|+O(\log(|\varphi|+|r|))\) if R fits the NO clock.

Thus on good NO-reduction tapes, y must leave the source-and-randomness pair conditionally incompressible past the target threshold for every decoder whose composition fits the larger NO clock. This quantifies the witness-hiding requirement in Idea 167. It explains why simply including the formula and random tape in y fails, but it neither implies a one-way function nor rules out every unconditional SAT reduction.

**Use:** for each proposed reduction, calculate source length, random-tape length, reconstruction time, \(T_R\), \(s+\Delta\), and \(\tau(t)\), then test the composition inequality before claiming the NO promise. See [Idea 175](ideas.md), [Idea 166](ideas.md), and [New Model, §2.56](New%20Model.MD).

### 138. The K-gap reduction must spend enough random bits

For a randomized reduction on an \(m\)-bit formula with fixed tape length \(\rho\), a program can hard-code the formula and tape, ignore y, and rerun the reduction to print x. If this fits the promised NO clock, then
\[
K_U^{\tau(t)}(x\mid y)\le m+\rho+O(\log(m+\rho)).
\]
Thus an NO cutoff \(k=s+\Delta(|x|)\) requires \(m+\rho+O(\log(m+\rho))>k\). For \(k\gg m\), the reduction needs \(\rho\) at least \(k-m-O(\log k)\), up to machine-dependent coding constants. A near-\(n\) NO threshold on an \(n\)-bit target with \(m=o(n)\) therefore requires almost \(n\) random bits if the NO clock covers the reduction.

In particular, for a deterministic reduction the random tape has length zero, so a cutoff gap \(k-m=\omega(\log m)\) rules out every promised NO output once the reduction fits the clock. A randomized reduction with insufficient tape length is ruled out on every tape by the same description argument.

HIR meets this floor directly: its target is a uniformly sampled \(n\)-bit message, so the reduction's tape contains those \(n\) bits before encryption randomness. This is a parameter consistency check, not a new hardness theorem. HIR's list-security premise does the additional work of ensuring that the side information does not provide a short conditional program for that message. See [Idea 176](ideas.md), [New Model, Section 2.57](New%20Model.MD), and the HIR [revised ECCC report](https://eccc.weizmann.ac.il/report/2023/046/revision/1/download/).

### 139. Exact separator target and an invalid complement-side count (25 September 2026)

OPS magnification needs a slightly superlinear ordinary circuit lower bound for Gap-MCSP in its fixed-$\beta$ non-membership regime. On $\Gamma=Y\sqcup Z$, the literal-slice discrete complexity $D(Y\mid\mathcal B)$ represents the De Morgan separator; fixed-basis circuits convert with constant-factor overhead. Thus a lower bound on acyclic $D$ is the minimum direct target, while a superlinear cyclic-cover bound on $\rho$ is only a stronger sufficient route.

The valid low-anchor trace gives $\rho(Y,\mathcal B)\ge N-\log_2M_2-1=N-o(N)$. I attempted a symmetric count for target $Z$ and claimed that a subcube disjoint from $Y$ has size at most $M_1=|Y|$. This inference is invalid: the cube can contain gap and high tables. Therefore the proposed $\rho(Z)\ge N-\log_2M_1-1$ and $D\ge2N-o(N)$ conclusions are withdrawn. Section 140 records the corrected DNF-interpolation bound, which gives only $\rho(Z)=\Omega(s_1/n)$.

**Method update.** Continue on the direct acyclic target. The semi-filter route has no missing representation bridge; the open work is a new superlinear lower bound against arbitrary circuits. See [research/CURRENT_STATE.md](research/CURRENT_STATE.md), [research/OPEN_OBLIGATIONS.md](research/OPEN_OBLIGATIONS.md), and [frontier reassessment](research/FRONTIER_REASSESSMENT_2026-09-25.md).
### 140. Retraction of the symmetric fusion count; high-anchor interpolation only (25 September 2026)

The attempted complement-side counting argument in Section 139 contains a gap. For a high anchor $a\in Z$, the first empty intersection of matching literal leaves must be disjoint from the low set $Y$. It does **not** follow that all points in that subcube have complexity at most $s_1$: the subcube may contain gap or high tables. Therefore the assertion that its size is at most $M_1=|Y|$, the bound $\rho(Z,\mathcal B)\ge N-\log_2M_1-1$, and the derived total circuit lower bound $2N-o(N)$ are withdrawn.

A valid replacement is a DNF interpolation argument. If the trace fixes $q$ distinct truth-table positions to a prescribed pattern, that pattern extends to a Boolean function by taking the OR of one minterm for each prescribed-1 position. This uses $O(qn)$ fan-in-two gates and matches the prescribed bits exactly. If $q\le s_1/(C n)$, the extension is a low-circuit table in the subcube, contradicting disjointness from $Y$. Thus the high-anchor trace needs $q=\Omega(s_1/n)=\Omega(N^\beta/n^2)$ fixed positions, so $\rho(Z,\mathcal B)=\Omega(N^\beta/n^2)$.

The literal-complement duality then adds only this sublinear quantity to the OR-side complexity. Together with the valid low-anchor bound it leaves the total lower bound at $N-o(N)$, not $2N-o(N)$. The mistaken argument and its correction are now recorded in [research/CLAIM_LEDGER.md](research/CLAIM_LEDGER.md), C-13. The exact remaining target is unchanged: an arbitrary acyclic circuit lower bound exceeding $N^{1+\epsilon}$ in the OPS regime.

### 141. Adaptive fusion closure game and cross-anchor potential (26 September 2026)

For a semantic pair list \(Q=((E_i,H_i))_{i=1}^q\), write \(T_i=E_i\cap H_i\), and let \(x_i(w)\) record whether \(T_i\) enters the least upward closure of anchor \(w\)'s matching literal slices under the rules in Q. With \(a_i(w)=1\) when a matching slice is contained in \(E_i\), \(b_i(w)=1\) for \(H_i\), and \(P_i=\{j:T_j\subseteq E_i\}\), \(R_i=\{j:T_j\subseteq H_i\}\), the exact recurrence is
\[
x_i^{(t+1)}(w)=
\left(a_i(w)\vee\bigvee_{j\in P_i}x_j^{(t)}(w)\right)
\wedge
\left(b_i(w)\vee\bigvee_{j\in R_i}x_j^{(t)}(w)\right).
\]
The least fixed point is reached after at most q strict rounds. It derives empty exactly when an active rule has empty intersection; otherwise the least closure is a proper semi-filter preserved by Q. Each seed predicate is a clause over signed truth-table coordinates, while the semantic containment network remains unrestricted. See C-67 and C-68.

For partial Q, let \(\delta_Q(w)\) be the minimum number of extra pairs required to derive empty. It is at most \(N-1\) and one-pair-Lipschitz. This supplies an exact local potential but no cross-anchor bound. C-09 defeats raw pair-to-anchor counting. Fixed-family fractional rounding, conditional on the project \(8N+1\) ceiling, does not cover the global adaptive quantifier: one selected filter per anchor is insufficient, because a pair may add a nonempty consequence and change the surviving closure. C-69 and C-70 record these facts and the remaining source-proof gap.

The recurrence was compared to explicit upward closure on 204,160 one-rule and seeded two-rule toy cases, and the known sparse-cube universal cover was recovered. This validates only the finite state encoding. The adaptive marginal theorem, a realizable superlinear integrality gap, a universal \(O(N\operatorname{polylog}N)\) cover, and the superlinear \(\rho\) bound remain open. Full frontier: [research/FUSION_CLOSURE_GAME_2026-09-26.md](research/FUSION_CLOSURE_GAME_2026-09-26.md).

### 142. Two-wise richness forces bootstrap depth (26 September 2026)

For fixed \(\beta<1\), the high side contains a table in every two-coordinate cylinder: each cylinder contains \(2^{N-2}\) truth tables, while the number \(M_2\) of tables of complexity at most \(s_2=N^\beta\) is \(2^{o(N)}\). Consequently an empty-intersection rule cannot activate from the initial literal slices alone. If its E and H sides were each supported by matching slices, the same coordinate would give either a common nonempty slice or inconsistent anchor bits; distinct coordinates have a high table in their joint cylinder, contradicting disjoint endpoints.

If empty first activates at round t, unfold the least-fixed-point derivation as a binary AND/OR proof tree and select one literal at each seed leaf. There are at most \(2^t\) leaves; their common cylinder must avoid every high table, so at least \(N-\log_2M_2\) distinct coordinates are fixed. Thus \(t\ge\log_2(N-\log_2M_2)=\log_2N-o(\log N)\). This is a proof-depth lower bound only; it does not strengthen the total pair-count lower bound. See C-72.

### 143. Static additive closure-distance potential is refuted (26 September 2026)

Let \(\delta_Q(w)\) be the fewest additional pairs needed to derive empty from partial list Q. Although \(\delta_Q(w)\le N-1\), is one-pair-Lipschitz, and starts at \(N-o(N)\) for every low anchor, no static probability measure on anchors can make every pair's expected initial decrease \(o(1)\).

Choose a minimum literal-slice certificate \(S_w\) for each low anchor. Its size \(d(w)\ge d_0:=\lceil N-\log_2M_2\rceil\). Averaging over coordinate pairs, some \(\{k,\ell\}\) belongs to \(S_w\) for \(\mu\)-mass at least \(\theta=\binom{d_0}{2}/\binom N2=1-o(1)\). One of the four anchor patterns \((w_k,w_\ell)=(b,c)\) has mass at least \(\theta/4\). Write \(G_{k,b}=U\cap\{z:z_k=b\}\). The pair of literal slices \((G_{k,b},G_{\ell,c})\) precomputes the intersection of two leaves of the chosen certificate. It reduces \(\delta\) by exactly one on every anchor in that class, using the remaining \(d(w)-2\) leaves; the one-pair Lipschitz bound makes the improvement exact.

Thus \(\sup_p\mathbb E_\mu[\delta_\varnothing-\delta_{\{p\}}]\ge1/4-o(1)\) for every \(\mu\). This falsifies the proposed route from a static weighted \(\delta\)-sum to a superlinear lower bound, not the distance itself. The successor potential must account for shared intersection states once, rather than charging a common first rule separately to many anchors. Full proof: C-73.
